  17. Summary

The kernel is, above all, a manager of addresses. Every file cache page, every userspace stack, every interrupt stack, every DMA buffer, and every kernel text byte becomes useful only after the processor can translate one address into another quickly and correctly. The core ideas are old—segmentation, paging, caches, and TLBs—but Linux 7.0.12 applies them very differently from Linux 2.6. The decisive shifts are 64-bit address spaces, five-level software abstractions, per-CPU page-table state, KPTI hardening, and the need to explain two primary architectures rather than one.

Memory addressing in Linux 2.6 was usually studied through the i386 lens. That was natural in 2005. A Linux 2.6 kernel on a 32-bit Pentium-class system had to care deeply about segment limits, PAE corner cases, and the awkward divide between low memory and high memory. Linux 7.0.12 still knows all of that history, but the mainstream paths are now x86_64 and ARM64. On x86_64, segmentation survives mostly as a compatibility shell around a flat address space, while paging has grown to four or five levels. On ARM64, there is no segmentation at all; the architecture presents a pure translation-table model, separated into user and kernel halves by hardware.

This chapter explains how the hardware and the kernel cooperate. We start with the three classic address types, move through x86_64 segmentation and ARM64's lack of it, then examine paging hardware, Linux page-table abstractions, physical memory models, virtual memory layouts, and the modern defenses and optimizations built on top. Chapter 3 will revisit several of these mechanisms from the process perspective, Chapter 8 will study how Linux allocates and reclaims physical memory, and Chapter 9 will show how an individual process address space is built, modified, and torn down.

Memory Addresses

Every instruction a CPU executes — every load, every store, every instruction fetch — involves an address. But "address" is a dangerously overloaded term. The number embedded in a machine instruction, the number the operating system manages, and the number that actually selects a byte in physical DRAM can all be different values related by hardware translation steps. Confusing them is one of the most common sources of bugs in kernel and driver code.

The Linux kernel inherits three traditional terms for these addresses:

  • Logical address — the raw address produced by the instruction stream. On x86, this is a segment selector plus an offset (CS:0x1050). On ARM64, the concept does not exist — the instruction directly produces a virtual address.
  • Virtual (linear) address — the flat address after any segmentation processing. This is the address that the kernel's page tables map to physical memory. Every user-space pointer and every kernel pointer (with the exception of a few early-boot paths) is a virtual address.
  • Physical address — the electrical signal that the memory controller sends to the DRAM chips. This is the "real" address of a byte in hardware. The kernel must know physical addresses when programming DMA engines, setting up page tables, or communicating with firmware.

On modern 64-bit Linux, the distinction between logical and virtual addresses has collapsed: x86_64 sets all segment bases to zero (making logical = virtual), and ARM64 has no segmentation at all. The practical reality is therefore a two-level system: virtual addresses used by all software, and physical addresses used by hardware. The translation between them — performed by the Memory Management Unit (MMU) through hierarchical page tables — is the central mechanism that enables process isolation, demand paging, memory-mapped I/O, and the kernel's ability to present each process with its own private address space.

Understanding these address types precisely matters for several practical reasons:

  • Page-table programming — the kernel must convert between virtual and physical addresses when building or modifying page tables. Functions like virt_to_phys(), phys_to_virt(), and __pa() / __va() exist specifically for this, and using them incorrectly (e.g., on a vmalloc address) produces silent corruption.
  • DMA setup — device drivers must give hardware the physical (or bus/IOMMU) address of buffers, not the virtual address. The DMA mapping API (dma_map_single(), dma_map_page()) handles this translation, but driver authors must understand why it is necessary.
  • Debugging — when reading an oops, a page-fault address, or a memory dump, knowing whether a number is a virtual address (which process's page tables?) or a physical address (which DRAM bank?) determines how to interpret it.

The rest of this section walks through the translation pipeline, compares the x86_64 and ARM64 approaches, and explains why segmentation survives in name but not in practice.

The Two-Stage Translation Pipeline

To understand how the three address types relate, it helps to think of memory translation as a two-stage pipeline built into the CPU hardware. The pipeline exists for two fundamental reasons: isolation (each process sees its own private address space and cannot accidentally — or maliciously — access another process's memory) and abstraction (the kernel can present a contiguous, uniform address space regardless of how fragmented or small the physical RAM actually is).

On x86, the pipeline has two distinct hardware stages. Stage 1 (Segmentation) converts a logical address (segment selector + offset) into a virtual address by adding the segment's base. Stage 2 (Paging) converts the virtual address into a physical address by walking the page tables. On ARM64, Stage 1 does not exist — the instruction directly produces a virtual address — so the pipeline has only one active stage.

Here is the complete path an address follows from the moment a program references memory to the moment the physical RAM chips are accessed:

Three Address Types

The pipeline diagram above shows the translation flow, but each box deserves a closer look. The three address types differ not just in value but in who produces them, who consumes them, and what guarantees they carry. A logical address is meaningful only to the CPU's instruction decoder; a virtual address is meaningful to the kernel's page-table infrastructure; and a physical address is meaningful to the memory controller and DMA hardware. Mixing them up — for example, handing a virtual address to a DMA engine, or treating a physical address as a kernel pointer — is a category of bug that compiles silently and may appear to work on some configurations before crashing on others.

The following subsections define each type precisely, with both the formal hardware definition and a practical analogy to help the intuition.

1. The Logical Address — The Program's View

When your software program is running, it does not know (or care) where it actually lives in physical RAM. It operates inside its own illusion of memory.

In Intel architecture, a logical address is made of two parts: a Segment Selector and an Offset. It looks something like CS:0x1050 (Code Segment, offset 0x1050).

The analogy: imagine you are in a hotel. The logical address is "Floor 3, Room 12." The program only knows its local environment — it has no idea which building or city it is in.

The CPU's Segmentation Unit takes this logical address and performs the first stage of translation. It looks up the base address of the segment (e.g., the Code Segment starts at linear address 0x00000000) and adds the offset to it.

2. The Virtual (Linear) Address — The Operating System's View

Once the Segmentation Unit finishes combining the segment base and the offset, it produces a single, flat, continuous number. Intel calls this the Linear Address. Most modern operating systems — including Linux — call it the Virtual Address.

A virtual address is a flat 32-bit or 64-bit number (e.g., 0x00001050). This is the address space that the operating system manages. Virtual addresses form a contiguous range that may be far larger than the physical RAM installed, and the kernel can map, unmap, and rearrange the correspondence between virtual pages and physical frames as needed.

The analogy: this is the global street address: "123 Main Street, Unit 312." It is a complete, flat address, but the apartment might not physically be occupied right now — it could be swapped out to the hard drive.

This virtual address is immediately handed off to the second stage of the pipeline: the Paging Unit (the MMU — Memory Management Unit).

3. The Physical Address — The Hardware's View

The Paging Unit takes the virtual address and looks it up in a map called a Page Table (which is maintained by the operating system). The result is the actual, electrical address used by the memory controller to access the silicon memory chips (DRAM) or hardware device registers (MMIO).

The analogy: the exact GPS latitude and longitude coordinates of the building.

If the page table says that virtual address 0x00001000 (a 4 KB page) is currently stored at physical address 0x1F001000, the MMU performs the substitution. The final physical address is sent across the motherboard's memory bus to fetch the data.

The Modern Reality: Stage 1 Is Essentially Dead

The reason this three-address scheme feels overly complicated is that Stage 1 (Segmentation) is essentially dead in modern 64-bit computing. Modern operating systems — including 64-bit Linux and Windows — bypass Intel's segmentation hardware by setting all segment bases to zero:

Because  base(0) + offset = offset,
the Logical Address and the Virtual Address are the exact same number.

We really only use the Paging Unit (virtual → physical) today. But the Intel manuals still define the Logical step because the hardware technically still processes it, and because two special segment registers — FS and GS — do retain nonzero bases for specific purposes (thread-local storage and per-CPU data).

On ARM64, the story is even simpler: there is no segmentation stage at all. The address computed by the instruction stream is already a virtual address, and it is sent directly into the translation-table walk machinery.

Key takeaway: On modern Linux (both x86_64 and ARM64), the practical distinction is between virtual addresses (what the kernel and user programs work with) and physical addresses (what the hardware uses). The logical address is an x86 historical artifact that survives only in the FS/GS bases, privilege level checks, and the Intel architecture manuals.

How Paging Translates a Virtual Address to a Physical Address

Since segmentation is effectively a no-op in modern systems, the real work of address translation falls entirely on paging. Every time a CPU core fetches an instruction, loads data, or writes to memory, the hardware Memory Management Unit (MMU) intercepts the virtual address and converts it to a physical address before the memory bus ever sees it. This happens transparently — application code (and most kernel code) never issues a physical address directly.

A naïve design would use a single flat table with one entry per page. On a 48-bit address space with 4 KB pages, that table would need 236 entries (roughly 64 billion) — 512 GB of memory per process, most of it empty. The solution adopted by every modern architecture is a hierarchical (multi-level) page table: instead of one giant array, the translation is broken into several small tables (each fitting in one page frame), linked together in a tree. Levels that map unused address ranges simply do not exist, so a process that uses only a few megabytes of virtual memory needs only a handful of page-table pages, not billions of entries.

The tradeoff is walk latency: each translation now requires multiple memory reads — one per page-table level — before the CPU can access the data it actually wants. Hardware compensates with the TLB (Translation Lookaside Buffer), a fast associative cache that stores recently used virtual-to-physical mappings. On a TLB hit (the common case), the MMU delivers the physical address in a single cycle with no table walk at all. Only on a TLB miss does the hardware walk the full page-table hierarchy. Understanding this walk is essential because it explains page sizes, huge-page optimizations, TLB-flush costs, and the entire kernel page-fault path covered later in this chapter.

Here is how the walk works, step by step:

  1. The virtual address is split into index fields and a page offset. Each index field selects one entry from a page-table level.
  2. The MMU reads the page-table base register (CR3 on x86_64, TTBR on ARM64) to find the root table's physical address.
  3. The MMU uses the first index field to look up an entry in the root table. That entry contains the physical address of the next table.
  4. The MMU repeats this process for each table level, each time using the next index field to select an entry.
  5. The final table entry contains the physical base address of the target page frame (a fixed-size block of physical memory, typically 4 KB).
  6. The MMU concatenates this physical base with the page offset from the original virtual address to produce the final physical address.

The same principle applies to ARM64 — only the names change. TTBR replaces CR3, and the levels are called L0/L1/L2/L3 instead of PGD/PUD/PMD/PTE. The page offset is still taken directly from the low bits of the virtual address and concatenated with the physical frame base found in the last-level descriptor.

Two important optimizations make this fast enough for real-world use:

  • TLB caching — the Translation Lookaside Buffer stores recent virtual→physical mappings, so most accesses skip the page-table walk entirely (see the TLB section later in this chapter).
  • Large pages — the walk can terminate early at the PMD level (2 MB pages) or PUD level (1 GB pages) by setting the _PAGE_PSE flag. The larger offset field from the VA is used directly, eliminating one or two levels of indirection.

64-bit virtual-address widths

A 64-bit general-purpose register does not imply a 64-bit implemented virtual address space. A full 64-bit address space would contain 264 bytes — 16 exbibytes — far more than any machine can physically address today or in the foreseeable future. Implementing the page tables for that space would be wasteful: each additional page-table level multiplies walk latency and memory overhead for zero practical benefit. So every 64-bit architecture implements only a subset of the full range, and the hardware enforces a rule on the remaining bits to prevent software from silently using addresses that would become ambiguous if the range were later extended.

On x86_64, the rule is called canonical addressing: the unused high bits must all be copies (sign-extensions) of the most significant implemented bit. For 48-bit virtual addresses, bits 63:48 must equal bit 47; for 57-bit (LA57), bits 63:57 must equal bit 56. Any address that violates this rule triggers a #GP (General Protection) fault — the CPU refuses to even attempt the translation. This creates a large non-canonical hole in the middle of the address space: the low-half canonical addresses (starting from 0x0000…) are used for userspace, the high-half (starting from 0xFFFF…) for the kernel, and the gap between them is unreachable. The split is a hardware-enforced boundary that Linux exploits for user/kernel isolation.

ARM64 uses a different but equivalent mechanism: TTBR0 translates addresses whose top bits are zero (userspace), and TTBR1 translates addresses whose top bits are one (kernel). The hardware selects the translation table base register based on the top bit of the virtual address, achieving the same user/kernel split without a canonical-address exception — an address that falls in neither range simply generates a translation fault.

The practical impact for kernel developers is that constants like PAGE_OFFSET, VMALLOC_START, and TASK_SIZE all depend on the implemented VA width. Linux 7.0.12 determines this at boot on x86_64 (detecting LA57 support) and at compile time on ARM64 (via CONFIG_ARM64_VA_BITS). The subsections below detail the specific widths each architecture supports.

x86_64

Linux 7.0.12 supports two mainstream virtual-address widths on x86_64:

  • 48-bit virtual addresses with four-level paging.
  • 57-bit virtual addresses with five-level paging, also known as LA57.

The kernel encodes the active width through runtime variables rather than one compile-time constant. arch/x86/include/asm/page_64_types.h defines:

#define __VIRTUAL_MASK_SHIFT    (pgtable_l5_enabled() ? 56 : 47)
#define DEFAULT_MAP_WINDOW  ((1UL << 47) - PAGE_SIZE)

The apparent off-by-one is intentional. If 48-bit addresses are implemented, bit 47 is the sign bit, so the highest low-half canonical user address is (1UL << 47) - PAGE_SIZE. If five-level paging is active, bit 56 becomes the sign bit. The kernel exposes the top-level geometry through pgdir_shift and ptrs_per_p4d in arch/x86/include/asm/pgtable_64_types.h.

The documentation file Documentation/arch/x86/x86_64/mm.rst summarizes the same rule in prose: 48-bit and 57-bit virtual addresses are supported, and bits 63 down to the most significant implemented bit must be sign-extended. That rule creates the large non-canonical “hole” between user and kernel regions when addresses are displayed as unsigned 64-bit values.

ARM64

ARM64 is more flexible. Linux 7.0.12 supports several virtual-address sizes, chosen by configuration and limited by page size and CPU features:

  • 39-bit VA spaces with 4 KB pages and three translation levels.
  • 48-bit VA spaces with 4 KB pages and four translation levels.
  • 52-bit VA spaces on hardware with Large Virtual Address support, typically paired with 64 KB pages.

The basic selection lives in arch/arm64/include/asm/memory.h:

#define VA_BITS         (CONFIG_ARM64_VA_BITS)
#define _PAGE_OFFSET(va)    (-(UL(1) << (va)))
#define PAGE_OFFSET     (_PAGE_OFFSET(VA_BITS))

The ARM64 memory-layout documentation in Documentation/arch/arm64/memory.rst explains an important compatibility detail: for 52-bit userspace support, the kernel may still hand out 48-bit user addresses by default unless an application opts in with a sufficiently high mmap() hint. That preserves compatibility with software that assumed the old 48-bit limit.

From Instruction to DRAM: Architecture Pipelines

The two-stage pipeline described above — segmentation then paging — is the general x86 model inherited from the i386. But the two architectures that this book focuses on, x86_64 and ARM64, implement the pipeline in strikingly different ways. Understanding these differences is important because they affect how Linux sets up the translation machinery at boot, how the kernel switches address spaces on context switch, and which hardware knobs are available for performance tuning (PCID, ASID, memory attributes).

On x86_64, both stages nominally exist, but Stage 1 (segmentation) is almost entirely vestigial. Long mode forces the base of CS, DS, ES, and SS to zero, so for the vast majority of memory accesses the logical address is the virtual address. The only exceptions are FS and GS, which retain nonzero base registers used by glibc for thread-local storage and by the kernel for per-CPU data. Stage 2 (paging) does all the real work: a 4-level or 5-level page-table walk produces the physical address.

On ARM64, there is no segmentation stage at all — the architecture was designed in the post-segmentation era. Instructions emit virtual addresses directly, and the MMU performs a single translation-table walk. A key architectural feature is the dual TTBR design: bit 55 of the virtual address selects between TTBR0_EL1 (user mappings) and TTBR1_EL1 (kernel mappings), giving each a completely independent page-table root. This eliminates the need to copy or share page-table entries between user and kernel — a simplification that has security implications (see KPTI discussion later).

The diagrams below show each pipeline end-to-end, from the instruction that generates the address to the physical bus transaction that fetches the data.

x86_64 pipeline

  Instruction (mov, call, push...)
       │
       ▼
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ Stage 1: SEGMENTATION UNIT                             │
  │  • Implicit or explicit segment register (CS, DS, SS)  │
  │  • In Long Mode: base = 0 for CS/DS/ES/SS              │
  │  • FS and GS may have nonzero bases (TLS, per-CPU)     │
  │  • Result: base + offset = VIRTUAL (LINEAR) ADDRESS     │
  └───────────────────────┬────────────────────────────────┘
                          ▼
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ Stage 2: PAGING UNIT (MMU)                             │
  │  • CR3 holds page-table root (+ PCID)                  │
  │  • 4-level walk: PGD → PUD → PMD → PTE → page frame   │
  │  • Or 5-level walk with P4D when LA57 is active        │
  │  • Checks Present, R/W, U/S, NX bits                   │
  │  • Result: PHYSICAL ADDRESS                            │
  └───────────────────────┬────────────────────────────────┘
                          ▼
            Cache → Memory Controller → DRAM

Because all standard segments have base 0 in long mode, Stage 1 is a no-op for most accesses. The Intel documentation still distinguishes logical from linear addresses, but in practice the segmentation hardware has been reduced to a narrow compatibility and privilege mechanism.

ARM64 pipeline

ARM64 skips Stage 1 entirely — there is no segmentation unit:

  Instruction (ldr, str, bl...)
       │
       ▼
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ TRANSLATION TABLE WALK (MMU)                           │
  │  • Bit 55 of VA selects TTBR0_EL1 or TTBR1_EL1        │
  │    - bit 55 = 0 → TTBR0 (user mappings)               │
  │    - bit 55 = 1 → TTBR1 (kernel mappings)              │
  │  • Walk levels (L0→L1→L2→L3 for 4KB/48-bit)           │
  │  • Checks AP, UXN, PXN, AF bits                        │
  │  • Memory attributes via MAIR_EL1 + AttrIndx           │
  │  • Result: PHYSICAL ADDRESS                            │
  └───────────────────────┬────────────────────────────────┘
                          ▼
            Cache → Interconnect → DRAM

The ARM64 documentation states this split plainly: Documentation/arch/arm64/memory.rst notes that bit 55 selects TTBRx, that the swapper_pg_dir contains only kernel mappings, and that the user page directory contains only user mappings. This single rule replaces a considerable amount of x86 segmentation lore.

Memory arbiters and DMA, briefly

Everything discussed so far — segmentation, paging, TLBs — describes how the CPU translates addresses. But the CPU is not the only device that reads and writes main memory. On a modern server or SoC, dozens of hardware engines — network controllers, storage adapters, GPU compute units, display pipelines, camera ISPs, crypto accelerators — all access DRAM independently of the CPU. They issue their own address transactions on the system bus, and those transactions must be arbitrated, translated, and protected just like CPU accesses. A kernel developer who ignores this reality will write drivers that corrupt memory or silently read stale data.

The fundamental problem is that these devices do not see the CPU's virtual address space. A pointer value like 0xFFFF8880_12345000 is meaningful only inside the CPU's MMU — if a driver hands that address to a NIC's DMA engine, the NIC will interpret it as a raw bus address and access the wrong physical location (or trigger a fault if an IOMMU is present). The kernel must therefore convert between CPU virtual addresses, CPU physical addresses, and DMA (bus) addresses — three distinct namespaces that happen to overlap on simple platforms but diverge on anything with an IOMMU, memory-mapped I/O windows, or non-identity bus-to-physical mappings.

At a high level, the hardware path from any bus master to DRAM involves three components:

  • The memory controller turns physical addresses into DRAM row, column, bank, and channel operations. On multi-channel systems it also interleaves addresses across channels for bandwidth.
  • The interconnect or memory arbiter decides which master reaches memory next: CPUs, GPUs, NICs, storage controllers, display engines, and so on. On ARM64 SoCs this is typically an AMBA/AXI interconnect; on x86 servers it is the ring or mesh bus connecting cores, LLC slices, and PCIe root complexes.
  • DMA controllers and bus-mastering devices issue their own memory transactions, often using I/O virtual addresses (IOVAs) that an IOMMU (Intel VT-d, AMD-Vi, or ARM SMMU) translates separately from the CPU MMU. The IOMMU provides the same two benefits for devices that the CPU MMU provides for processes: address isolation (a buggy device cannot corrupt arbitrary memory) and address remapping (scatter-gather DMA can appear contiguous to the device even when the physical pages are fragmented).

Linux exposes this through the DMA mapping API (dma_map_single(), dma_map_sg(), etc.), which returns a dma_addr_t — the address the device should use, not the CPU. Drivers must never pass a virt_to_phys() result directly to hardware unless they are certain no IOMMU or bus offset exists. This chapter focuses on the CPU's view of address translation; Chapter 13 will revisit DMA, IOMMUs, and the DMA mapping API from the device-driver side.

Why Linux still keeps the old names

A reasonable question at this point is: if segmentation is a no-op and every pointer the kernel touches is already a virtual address, why does the Linux source code — and every textbook, mailing-list post, and commit message — still distinguish "logical," "virtual (linear)," and "physical" addresses? Why not just say "address" and be done with it?

The answer is that the names track responsibilities, not just numerical values. Each address type answers a different question: who produced this value? (the instruction decoder, the segment unit, the MMU, or the DMA subsystem) and who can consume it? (the segmentation unit, the paging unit, the memory controller, or a device). Collapsing the names collapses the distinctions, and that leads to bugs. A developer who treats a kernel virtual pointer as a physical address and passes it to a DMA engine will see silent data corruption. A developer who ignores the FS/GS segment base will miscompute a TLS variable's location. The terminology exists to prevent exactly these category errors.

The distinction also matters when reading architecture manuals. Intel's SDM uses "logical address" and "linear address" as precise, formally defined terms — the entire protection model (privilege levels, segment limits, access-rights checks) is specified in terms of those stages. ARM's Architecture Reference Manual defines "virtual address" and "physical address" as inputs and outputs of the translation-table walk, with "intermediate physical address" for two-stage translation under hypervisors. If you cannot map Linux's terminology onto the hardware manual's terminology, you cannot reason about what the hardware actually does on a given access — and you cannot debug translation faults, protection violations, or IOMMU misconfigurations.

Even if x86_64 flattening makes logical and virtual addresses numerically equal most of the time, Linux still has to care about:

  • selector values in trap frames (which segment was active when the fault occurred?),
  • CPL and privilege changes on x86 (ring 0 → ring 3 transitions check CS and SS),
  • TLS bases in FS_BASE (userspace thread-local storage depends on a nonzero segment base),
  • per-CPU bases in GS_BASE (the kernel's fast per-CPU variable access relies on swapgs),
  • canonical-address rules (a user pointer in non-canonical space must be rejected, not dereferenced),
  • page-table layout (the kernel programs different structures for 4-level vs 5-level paging),
  • TLB invalidation (flushing the wrong scope — local vs global vs ASID-tagged — causes stale translations),
  • and the distinction between CPU virtual addresses and bus-visible physical ones (DMA requires explicit translation).

The names are historical, but the concepts remain operational. If a kernel developer says that a bug occurs "before paging but after segment-base application," that sentence still means something concrete on x86_64 — it locates the problem between the GDT lookup and the CR3-based page walk, narrowing the debugging search space immediately.

Table 2-1. Address forms on x86_64 and ARM64
Concept x86_64 ARM64 Linux consequence
Logical address Selector + offset semantics still architecturally defined Usually just the instruction's virtual address Mostly relevant on x86 entry code and TLS/per-CPU handling
Virtual address Linear address after segmentation Address sent to translation tables What Linux stores in pointers and VMAs
Physical address Result of page walk Result of table walk Used by caches, interconnect, and DMA-visible memory
Implemented width 48 or 57 bits 39, 48, or 52 bits Affects layout macros, page-table depth, and canonicality
User/kernel split Canonical halves; KPTI may duplicate top level TTBR0/TTBR1 split in hardware Shapes context-switch and TLB rules

On x86_64, the term linear address is architecturally precise. In Linux source code and in this book, virtual address is usually the more practical name.

Segmentation on x86_64

Segmentation is where the modern x86 story differs most dramatically from the old i386-centric model of Linux 2.6. The hardware still exposes segment registers, selectors, descriptors, privilege levels, and task-state structures, but Linux uses them in a sharply reduced, carefully curated way. Long mode did not delete segmentation; it made most of it boring, and therefore easy to forget—until one needs to understand TLS, swapgs, or privilege transitions.

In the original 8086, segmentation was the only address-translation mechanism — there was no paging. A program's address space was carved into 64 KB segments, and every memory reference combined a 16-bit segment register with a 16-bit offset to form a 20-bit physical address. The 80286 added protection (privilege rings, access-rights checks, segment limits), and the 80386 extended segments to 32 bits and layered paging on top. For over a decade, x86 operating systems had to deal with both translation stages as fully active, interacting mechanisms.

When AMD designed the x86_64 (AMD64) extension, they made a deliberate architectural decision: in 64-bit long mode, the base and limit fields of CS, DS, ES, and SS are ignored for address calculation. The CPU treats every instruction offset as a flat 64-bit virtual address with no segment relocation. This single decision eliminated the complexity that had plagued i386 OS design — no more 16-bit and 32-bit segment gymnastics, no more far pointers, no more LDT per process. But the decision was selective, not total: privilege checks via CS.RPL survived (they are how the CPU knows ring 0 from ring 3), the FS and GS registers kept their programmable bases (essential for thread-local storage and per-CPU data), and the TSS remained necessary for stack switching on privilege changes and interrupt delivery.

For kernel developers, the practical consequence is a split personality. Most of the time segmentation is invisible — a kernel pointer is a virtual address, full stop. But at the boundaries — syscall entry, interrupt gates, context switch, TLS setup, swapgs — the segment machinery surfaces and demands precise attention. The subsections below walk through exactly what Linux 7.0.12 sets up, what it ignores, and what still breaks if you get it wrong.

Segmentation in Long Mode

Intel and AMD define long mode as the 64-bit operating mode of the processor — the mode Linux runs in on every x86_64 system. Long mode actually contains two sub-modes: 64-bit mode (used for the kernel and 64-bit applications) and compatibility mode (used to run legacy 32-bit binaries under a 64-bit kernel). The segmentation behavior differs significantly between them, and understanding the distinction is essential for anyone who reads the kernel's entry/exit code or the GDT setup.

In 64-bit mode, the processor enforces a sweeping simplification: the base address of CS, DS, ES, and SS is treated as zero regardless of what the GDT descriptor says. Segment limits are not checked for these four registers. The CPU still reads the descriptor to extract the DPL (Descriptor Privilege Level), the L (Long) bit, and the D (Default operand size) bit — these control privilege level and instruction decoding — but address relocation is gone. The FS and GS registers are the sole exception: their bases remain fully programmable via the MSR_FS_BASE and MSR_GS_BASE model-specific registers (or via WRFSBASE/WRGSBASE instructions on newer hardware). This exception is not accidental — it was designed specifically to support thread-local storage and per-CPU data without per-thread page-table changes.

In compatibility mode, segmentation behaves exactly as it did on the 80386: bases, limits, and access-rights checks are all fully active. This is how a 64-bit Linux kernel runs 32-bit IA-32 user programs — the application's segment descriptors work as they always did, while the kernel itself stays in 64-bit mode. The transition between the two modes happens automatically when the CPU loads a code segment with the L bit clear (compatibility) or set (64-bit).

The crucial fact for day-to-day kernel development is simple: x86_64 long mode uses a flat segment model for ordinary code and data references.

In practice:

  • CS, DS, ES, and SS exist, are loaded, and are checked for privilege and type.
  • Their usable bases are effectively forced to zero in long mode.
  • Their limits are largely ignored for 64-bit code and data.
  • Only FS and GS retain meaningful programmable base values for ordinary address generation.

This means that most C code in the kernel can act as if a pointer is just a virtual address. The hidden segment-descriptor cache still exists in the CPU, but it no longer contributes interesting relocation for everyday references. Linux embraces that flat model aggressively. The GDT entries for the main kernel and user segments all use base 0 and limit 0xfffff. The architecture keeps the machinery; Linux keeps the setup minimal.

What did not disappear is privilege information. The current privilege level, or CPL, is still derived from the low bits of CS. On Linux:

  • Ring 0 is kernel mode.
  • Ring 3 is user mode.

Trap handling, syscall/sysret, iretq, and many security checks still depend on this distinction. A saved cs field in a pt_regs frame is not decorative; it tells the entry code whether the interrupted context was user or kernel mode. Several x86 helper routines rely on exactly that test.

Segment registers that still matter

Even though long mode neutralises most segmentation for address calculation, the six segment registers are not vestigial bits of silicon. The CPU reads them on every instruction fetch (CS), every stack operation (SS), every explicit data access (DS/ES), and every FS/GS-prefixed load or store. What changed is what the CPU does with them: instead of relocating addresses through a base, it mostly extracts metadata — privilege level, operand-size default, and (for FS/GS) a base offset that is applied after the flat 64-bit address is formed. The table below summarises each register's original role versus what Linux 7.0.12 actually uses it for today.

The six architecturally visible segment registers remain, each with a specific name and historical function:

Table 2-2. x86 Segment Registers and Their Modern Linux Usage
RegisterFull NameOriginal Function (16/32-bit)Modern Linux (x86_64) Function
CS Code Segment Points to the segment containing program instructions Selects kernel (__KERNEL_CS) or user (__USER_CS) code descriptor; its RPL field encodes the Current Privilege Level (0 = kernel, 3 = user). Critical for privilege transitions.
SS Stack Segment Points to the segment containing the current program stack Must match the privilege level of CS. Loaded automatically on interrupt/syscall entry. Used for stack-switch semantics on privilege changes.
DS Data Segment Points to the segment containing global and static data Effectively inert in long mode (base forced to 0). Still needs a valid selector in compatibility and entry paths.
ES Extra Segment General-purpose data segment, used by string instructions (movs, stos) Effectively inert in long mode. Required by legacy string instructions but base is always 0.
FS Additional Segment General-purpose (no predefined role in hardware) User-space TLS base — glibc stores the thread pointer here. The base is set via MSR_FS_BASE or the arch_prctl(ARCH_SET_FS) system call.
GS Additional Segment General-purpose (no predefined role in hardware) Kernel per-CPU data base — the kernel stores its per-CPU offset here. On syscall/interrupt entry, swapgs swaps MSR_GS_BASE and MSR_KERNEL_GS_BASE so the kernel can access per-CPU data immediately.

Linux uses them asymmetrically. CS matters for privilege and execution mode. SS matters for stack semantics on privilege transitions. DS and ES are mostly inert in long mode but still need sane values in entry and compatibility paths. FS is the standard user-space TLS base. GS is the standard kernel per-CPU base, reached through swapgs on entry and exit.

That division of labor is one of the best examples of architectural sediment in the modern kernel. A mechanism invented for segmented memory survives as the transport for two very contemporary ideas: thread-local storage and per-CPU data.

Fast Access to Segment Descriptors

Every memory reference on x86 involves a segment register — even when the programmer does not name one explicitly. A mov rax, [rbx] instruction implicitly uses DS; a push uses SS; an instruction fetch uses CS. If the CPU had to look up the segment descriptor in the GDT (a table in main memory) on every such access, segmentation would add an extra memory read to every single instruction — an unacceptable performance penalty. The hardware solves this with a simple cache.

To speed up the translation of logical addresses into linear addresses, the processor provides a nonprogrammable register (also called the descriptor cache or hidden part) for each of the six programmable segment registers. Every time a segment selector is loaded into a segment register, the corresponding segment descriptor is automatically fetched from memory (from the GDT or LDT) and stored in the matching hidden register. From that point on, all address translations involving that segment can proceed without accessing the descriptor table in main memory — the processor reads the cached descriptor directly from the hidden register.

  Visible Part          Hidden Part (Descriptor Cache)
  ┌──────────────┐     ┌─────────────────────────────────────┐
  │   Segment    │     │  Base Address  │  Limit  │  Attrs   │
  │   Selector   │────▶│  (from GDT/   │  (from  │  (type,  │
  │   (16 bits)  │     │   LDT entry)  │  desc)  │  DPL, P) │
  └──────────────┘     └─────────────────────────────────────┘
       cs, ds,              Loaded automatically when the
       ss, es,              selector changes; used for all
       fs, gs               subsequent translations

Accesses to the GDT or LDT are necessary only when the contents of a segment register change — which on modern x86_64 Linux happens only at:

  • Privilege transitions — syscall/sysret, iretq, interrupts (CS and SS are reloaded).
  • Context switches — the kernel may update FS and/or GS bases when switching between user threads.
  • Compatibility mode entry — 32-bit user-space code on a 64-bit kernel uses different selectors.

In long mode, because CS/DS/ES/SS all have base 0 and the CPU ignores limits for 64-bit code, the hidden parts are effectively constant and boring for those four registers. The descriptor caches for FS and GS are the only ones that carry nontrivial base values — and modern CPUs also provide dedicated MSRs (MSR_FS_BASE, MSR_GS_BASE) as a faster alternative to loading a selector and waiting for the GDT fetch.

Note: The nonprogrammable descriptor-cache registers cannot be directly read or written by software. They are purely internal to the processor. This is why the Intel manuals call them "nonprogrammable" — the CPU manages them automatically as a performance optimization.

Segment Selectors and Descriptors

When the CPU loads a segment register (e.g., mov ds, ax or implicitly during syscall/iretq), the value being loaded is not an address — it is a selector, a 16-bit handle that tells the CPU where to find the real segment description. The selector is an index into a table (GDT or LDT) plus a few bits of privilege metadata. The table entry it points to — the descriptor — contains the actual base address, limit, type, and access-rights fields that the CPU caches in the hidden part described above. This two-level indirection (selector → descriptor → cached attributes) is the fundamental mechanism that allows the OS to control what each segment register means, while keeping the register itself small enough to fit in a 16-bit field inherited from the 8086.

A segment selector is still a 16-bit value. Its layout is unchanged from the classic x86 design:

  • bits 15:3 — Index into the descriptor table
  • bit 2 — TI (table indicator; 0 for GDT, 1 for LDT)
  • bits 1:0 — RPL (requested privilege level)

A selector therefore does not contain a base address. It contains a small index and privilege metadata. The actual segment information is in the descriptor that the selector names.

Selector layout sketch

 15                              3 2   1 0
 +--------------------------------+-----+
 |         Descriptor Index       | TI  |RPL|
 +--------------------------------+-----+---+

The descriptor format is defined in arch/x86/include/asm/desc_defs.h. The basic code/data descriptor is eight bytes:

struct desc_struct {
    u16 limit0;
    u16 base0;
    u16 base1: 8, type: 4, s: 1, dpl: 2, p: 1;
    u16 limit1: 4, avl: 1, l: 1, d: 1, g: 1, base2: 8;
} __attribute__((packed));

This structure packs the historical fields:

  • base address split across base0, base1, and base2
  • segment limit split across limit0 and limit1
  • type bits
  • the system/non-system bit s
  • descriptor privilege level dpl
  • present bit p
  • long-mode bit l
  • default operand-size bit d
  • granularity bit g

For long-mode Linux, those bits matter more for type checking than for relocation. A bad CS descriptor will still break returns or privilege transitions even if the base is zero.

System descriptors are wider

The 8-byte desc_struct works for code and data segments because long mode ignores their base and limit fields anyway. But system descriptors — specifically, the TSS descriptor and the LDT descriptor — are different. These describe structures that the CPU must locate by their full 64-bit base address (the TSS can live anywhere in physical memory, not just the low 4 GB). An 8-byte descriptor can only hold a 32-bit base, so long mode doubles system descriptors to 16 bytes, adding a second doubleword for the upper 32 bits of the base address. This means a single TSS or LDT entry occupies two adjacent slots in the GDT — a detail that matters when computing GDT indices and sizing the GDT array.

Long mode widens some system descriptors to 16 bytes. The TSS and LDT descriptor format is represented by struct ldttss_desc in the same header:

struct ldttss_desc {
    u16 limit0;
    u16 base0;
    u16 base1 : 8, type : 5, dpl : 2, p : 1;
    u16 limit1 : 4, zero0 : 3, g : 1, base2 : 8;
#ifdef CONFIG_X86_64
    u32 base3;
    u32 zero1;
#endif
} __attribute__((packed));

The extra base3 field is what allows a full 64-bit base address for the TSS or LDT. Linux still needs a TSS per CPU, not for hardware task switching, but for stack pointers, interrupt-stack-table entries, and a few legacy architectural requirements.

The Segmentation Unit

The segmentation unit is the hardware block inside the CPU that sits between the instruction decoder and the paging unit. Its job is conceptually simple: take a logical address (a selector:offset pair) and produce a linear address (a flat pointer into the virtual address space). On a pre-long-mode processor this involved real arithmetic — the segment base was added to the offset, and the result was checked against the segment limit. On x86_64 in long mode, the arithmetic is trivially 0 + offset = offset for CS/DS/ES/SS, so the unit's visible effect reduces to privilege and type checks. Nonetheless, the logic still executes on every memory reference; it is simply that its output equals its input for most segments.

Understanding the segmentation unit's algorithm matters for two reasons. First, the kernel's entry and exit paths (syscall, sysret, iretq, interrupt gates) manipulate selectors explicitly, and bugs in those paths manifest as segmentation faults or privilege escalations that only make sense if you know what the hardware checks. Second, the segmentation unit is what enforces the FS and GS base addition — the one case where the translation is not an identity function and where the result differs from the raw instruction offset.

The hardware performs the following steps every time it encounters a logical address (selector:offset pair):

  1. Examine the TI bit of the segment selector to determine which descriptor table to use. If TI = 0, the descriptor lives in the GDT and the segmentation unit reads the GDT base from the gdtr register. If TI = 1, the descriptor lives in the active LDT and the unit reads the LDT base from the ldtr register.
  2. Compute the descriptor address. The 13-bit Index field of the selector is multiplied by 8 (the size of a standard segment descriptor) and added to the base obtained in step 1. The result is the physical location of the segment descriptor in memory.
  3. Add the offset. The Base field of the segment descriptor is added to the offset component of the logical address, producing the final linear address.

Modern reality: the translation is trivial

On x86_64 long mode, this machinery still executes for every memory reference, but it produces a trivial result for the four main segments. The kernel's GDT (arch/x86/kernel/cpu/common.c) makes this explicit — every mainstream segment has base = 0 and limit = 0xfffff:

[GDT_ENTRY_KERNEL_CS]       = GDT_ENTRY_INIT(DESC_CODE64, 0, 0xfffff),
[GDT_ENTRY_KERNEL_DS]       = GDT_ENTRY_INIT(DESC_DATA64, 0, 0xfffff),
[GDT_ENTRY_DEFAULT_USER_DS] = GDT_ENTRY_INIT(DESC_DATA64 | DESC_USER, 0, 0xfffff),
[GDT_ENTRY_DEFAULT_USER_CS] = GDT_ENTRY_INIT(DESC_CODE64 | DESC_USER, 0, 0xfffff),

Because the base is zero, the linear address is simply equal to the offset:

linear_address = 0 + offset = offset = virtual_address

The segmentation unit is therefore an identity function for CS, DS, ES, and SS — it still runs on every reference, but its output always equals its input. The kernel even relies on this identity in its context-switch fast path: arch/x86/kernel/process_64.c comments that "there are no user segments in the GDT with nonzero bases other than the TLS segments," and optimises by skipping segment-base reloads when the base is already zero.

The only registers where the base contributes a non-zero value are FS and GS. Their bases are loaded via WRFSBASE/WRGSBASE instructions (on CPUs with the FSGSBASE feature) or via the MSR_FS_BASE/MSR_GS_BASE model-specific registers on older hardware. During context switch, the kernel writes the incoming thread's FS base and GS base explicitly:

/* arch/x86/kernel/process_64.c — context switch */
wrfsbase(next->fsbase);
__wrgsbase_inactive(next->gsbase);

This is exactly why the earlier section on "Memory Addresses" described segmentation as "Stage 1" of translation and noted that it is effectively dead for mainstream 64-bit code. The hardware still runs the algorithm above on every reference, but the result is always offset + 0 — except for the two registers that carry thread-local and per-CPU state.

Segmentation in Linux

The x86 segmentation hardware was designed to encourage programmers to split applications into logically separate segments — code, data, stack, and so on. Intel's original vision was a segmented memory model where each segment descriptor would define a protected, independently relocatable region. In theory, an OS could give each process different segment bases and limits, providing hardware-enforced isolation at the segment level.

Linux rejected that model from the very beginning. Instead, it uses segmentation in a very limited way — just enough to satisfy what the hardware demands, and no more. The entire per-CPU GDT on x86_64 contains only 16 entries (GDT_ENTRIES = 16 in arch/x86/include/asm/segment.h), and only a handful of those are actually populated: kernel CS/DS, user CS/DS, a 32-bit compatibility CS, three TLS entries, the TSS descriptor, and the LDT descriptor. The rest are zeroed or reserved. Compare this to the 8,192 entries the hardware supports — Linux uses less than 0.2% of the GDT's capacity.

Even the LDT (Local Descriptor Table), which the hardware provides for per-process segments, is left empty by default. The kernel only allocates an LDT when a process explicitly requests one via the modify_ldt() system call — a path used almost exclusively by legacy 16-bit/32-bit emulators, Wine, and DOSEMU. As arch/x86/kernel/ldt.c notes, "modify_ldt() is mostly used by legacy code and emulators." For the vast majority of processes, mm->context.ldt is NULL, and the hardware's ldtr register points nowhere.

Segmentation and paging are somewhat redundant: both can separate the address spaces of processes. Linux prefers paging for two fundamental reasons:

  • Simpler memory management. When all processes share the same segment register values — and therefore the same linear address space layout — the kernel's memory management code does not need to track per-process segment bases or limits. All the interesting per-process isolation is done at the paging layer instead.
  • Portability. One of the primary design goals of Linux is to run on a wide range of architectures. RISC processors such as ARM, RISC-V, and MIPS have no segmentation at all. By making segmentation a no-op, the Linux kernel can share a single memory management design across both x86 and non-x86 platforms.

Yet Linux cannot ignore segmentation entirely — the x86 hardware mandates it in several places that have no paging equivalent:

  • Privilege transitions. The MSR_STAR register, which controls syscall/sysret, embeds segment selectors directly: wrmsr(MSR_STAR, 0, (__USER32_CS << 16) | __KERNEL_CS). The CPU uses these selectors to load CS and SS atomically on every system call — there is no way to enter or leave the kernel without valid segment descriptors.
  • The TSS. The Task State Segment is required for the Interrupt Stack Table (IST), which provides dedicated stacks for critical exceptions like double faults and NMIs. Linux sets up a per-CPU TSS and loads it with load_TR_desc() during CPU initialization.
  • swapgs. On every syscall entry and interrupt from user mode, the kernel executes swapgs to exchange the user GS base with the kernel's per-CPU GS base — a segmentation operation that makes per-CPU data accessible in the very first instructions of the entry path.

As a result, Linux uses segmentation only when required by the x86 architecture — for privilege levels, task-state segments, and a few compatibility features. The mainstream address-space work is done entirely through paging.

The four main segments

All Linux processes running in User Mode use the same pair of segments to address instructions and data: the user code segment and the user data segment. Similarly, all processes running in Kernel Mode use the kernel code segment and the kernel data segment. The descriptor field values for these four segments are:

SegmentBaseLimitGSTypeDPLLD/BPSelector
Kernel code0x000000000xfffff11Execute/Read0101__KERNEL_CS
Kernel data0x000000000xfffff11Read/Write0001__KERNEL_DS
User code0x000000000xfffff11Execute/Read3101__USER_CS
User data0x000000000xfffff11Read/Write3001__USER_DS

Notice that all four segments start at address 0 and extend to the maximum addressable limit. This has two critical consequences:

  1. All processes share the same linear address range. There is no per-process segment relocation. The logical offset is the linear address, because base + offset = 0 + offset = offset.
  2. Logical addresses coincide with linear (virtual) addresses. This is why Linux kernel code never stores or manipulates segment selectors alongside pointers. A pointer value in C is always the virtual address; the segment selector is implicit and constant.

Segment register updates on privilege transitions

The Current Privilege Level (CPL) is encoded in the RPL field of the segment selector in the cs register. Whenever the CPL changes — on syscall entry, interrupt, or return to user mode — certain segment registers must be updated accordingly:

  • Kernel Mode (CPL = 0): cs holds __KERNEL_CS, and ds/ss hold __KERNEL_DS. The stack pointer refers to the kernel stack.
  • User Mode (CPL = 3): cs holds __USER_CS, and ds/ss hold __USER_DS. The stack pointer refers to the user-space stack.

Because there is only one kernel code segment and one kernel data segment, the kernel never needs to store the segment selector component of an address. When a function is called, the call instruction specifies only the offset; the segment selector in cs is implicitly the current one. The same applies to data pointers via ds and stack references via ss.

This simplification — that segment selectors are constant and need not be tracked — is a direct consequence of the flat segmentation model. It is also why Linux data structures like struct pt_regs save cs and ss only as metadata for detecting the privilege level of the interrupted context, not as address-translation inputs.

ARM64 note: ARM64 has no segmentation at all. The concept of "segments" simply does not exist. Virtual addresses are already linear addresses, and privilege is determined by the Exception Level (EL0–EL3), not by segment register contents. This is one of the primary reasons Linux's segmentation-independent design works so cleanly across architectures.

The Linux GDT in Kernel 7.0.12

The Global Descriptor Table is the central data structure that defines every segment the CPU can reference. On a uniprocessor system a single GDT would suffice, but Linux allocates a separate GDT for every CPU. The reason is the TLS (Thread-Local Storage) entries: when the scheduler context-switches to a new thread, it copies that thread's tls_array into the running CPU's GDT (arch/x86/include/asm/desc.h, native_load_tls()). If all CPUs shared one GDT, a context switch on CPU 0 would clobber the TLS descriptors that CPU 1 is currently using. Per-CPU GDTs eliminate that race entirely.

Each per-CPU GDT is page-aligned (__attribute__((aligned(PAGE_SIZE)))) so that it can be mapped independently in the page tables. This matters for Kernel Page Table Isolation (KPTI / PTI): the GDT must be accessible even when the CPU is running with the user-mode page tables, because interrupts and exceptions need valid segment descriptors to enter the kernel. Linux solves this by mapping each CPU's GDT into the CPU Entry Area — a small per-CPU fixmap region that is shared between user and kernel page tables. The mapping is read-only (PAGE_KERNEL_RO) so that even if an attacker gains write access to kernel memory, they cannot modify the GDT to escalate privileges. The kernel loads the read-only fixmap GDT via load_fixmap_gdt() during cpu_init(), and temporarily switches to the writable version only when it needs to update TLS entries or load the TSS descriptor.

The actual declaration is in arch/x86/kernel/cpu/common.c:

DEFINE_PER_CPU_PAGE_ALIGNED(struct gdt_page, gdt_page) = { .gdt = {

The 64-bit layout is defined by arch/x86/include/asm/segment.h. For Linux 7.0.12, the key indices are:

#define GDT_ENTRY_KERNEL32_CS       1
#define GDT_ENTRY_KERNEL_CS     2
#define GDT_ENTRY_KERNEL_DS     3
#define GDT_ENTRY_DEFAULT_USER32_CS 4
#define GDT_ENTRY_DEFAULT_USER_DS   5
#define GDT_ENTRY_DEFAULT_USER_CS   6
#define GDT_ENTRY_TSS           8
#define GDT_ENTRY_LDT           10
#define GDT_ENTRIES         16

The corresponding selectors are:

#define __KERNEL32_CS           (GDT_ENTRY_KERNEL32_CS*8)
#define __KERNEL_CS         (GDT_ENTRY_KERNEL_CS*8)
#define __KERNEL_DS         (GDT_ENTRY_KERNEL_DS*8)
#define __USER32_CS         (GDT_ENTRY_DEFAULT_USER32_CS*8 + 3)
#define __USER_DS           (GDT_ENTRY_DEFAULT_USER_DS*8 + 3)
#define __USER_CS           (GDT_ENTRY_DEFAULT_USER_CS*8 + 3)

The + 3 in the user selectors sets the RPL field to ring 3. That tiny arithmetic detail is the visible trace of x86 privilege levels in modern Linux.

Why the GDT order looks strange

Looking at the GDT indices, a natural question arises: why is the user data segment (index 5) sandwiched between the 32-bit user code segment (index 4) and the 64-bit user code segment (index 6)? On the kernel side, CS and DS sit next to each other (indices 2 and 3) in a logical order — why doesn't the user side do the same?

The answer is a hard constraint imposed by the SYSRET instruction. When the CPU executes sysret to return from the kernel to user mode, it does not read segment descriptors from the GDT by index. Instead, it computes the selectors arithmetically from a single value stored in the MSR_STAR register. The kernel programs this MSR at boot in syscall_init():

/* arch/x86/kernel/cpu/common.c */
wrmsr(MSR_STAR, 0, (__USER32_CS << 16) | __KERNEL_CS);

The upper 16 bits of STAR[63:48] hold the base selector for user mode (__USER32_CS = 0x23). The CPU then applies fixed arithmetic — documented in the comment in arch/x86/include/asm/segment.h:

  • If returning to 32-bit user space: cs = STAR.SYSRET_CS (index 4 → __USER32_CS)
  • If returning to 64-bit user space: cs = STAR.SYSRET_CS + 16 (index 4 + 2 = index 6 → __USER_CS)
  • In either case: ss = STAR.SYSRET_CS + 8 (index 4 + 1 = index 5 → __USER_DS)

The "+ 16" and "+ 8" are byte offsets (each GDT entry is 8 bytes), so they correspond to +2 and +1 in index terms. This forces the user DS to sit at exactly one slot after the 32-bit user CS, and the 64-bit user CS at exactly two slots after it. Linux cannot rearrange these entries without breaking every sysret on every CPU — the arithmetic is burned into the silicon.

Similarly, the SYSCALL entry direction uses STAR[47:32] (__KERNEL_CS = 0x10) and computes ss = __KERNEL_CS + 8 = __KERNEL_DS (index 3). This is why kernel CS (index 2) and kernel DS (index 3) must also be adjacent. In other words, Linux's GDT layout is shaped less by human aesthetics than by the non-negotiable arithmetic of the syscall/sysret ABI.

The initial descriptors are flat

The actual per-CPU initializers in arch/x86/kernel/cpu/common.c are explicit:

[GDT_ENTRY_KERNEL32_CS]     = GDT_ENTRY_INIT(DESC_CODE32, 0, 0xfffff),
[GDT_ENTRY_KERNEL_CS]       = GDT_ENTRY_INIT(DESC_CODE64, 0, 0xfffff),
[GDT_ENTRY_KERNEL_DS]       = GDT_ENTRY_INIT(DESC_DATA64, 0, 0xfffff),
[GDT_ENTRY_DEFAULT_USER32_CS]   = GDT_ENTRY_INIT(DESC_CODE32 | DESC_USER, 0, 0xfffff),
[GDT_ENTRY_DEFAULT_USER_DS] = GDT_ENTRY_INIT(DESC_DATA64 | DESC_USER, 0, 0xfffff),
[GDT_ENTRY_DEFAULT_USER_CS] = GDT_ENTRY_INIT(DESC_CODE64 | DESC_USER, 0, 0xfffff),

Every one of these entries has:

  • base = 0
  • limit = 0xfffff

The limit value is historical and paired with the granularity bit. In day-to-day long-mode operation, what matters is that the effective model is flat: all ordinary kernel and user code/data selectors describe the same linear address space, with privilege encoded by descriptor type and DPL rather than by different base addresses.

Each processor has its own copy of this GDT, allocated as a page-aligned per-CPU variable. The copies are mostly identical, but differ in:

  • TSS entries — each CPU has its own Task State Segment with distinct stack pointers and interrupt-stack-table entries.
  • LDT entries — may differ when the running process has a custom LDT (e.g., Wine).
  • TLS entries — updated on context switch to reflect the current thread's TLS descriptors.
Table 2-3. Main x86_64 GDT entries in Linux 7.0.12
Index Macro Purpose DPL Descriptor style
1 GDT_ENTRY_KERNEL32_CS 32-bit kernel code compatibility segment 0 DESC_CODE32
2 GDT_ENTRY_KERNEL_CS 64-bit kernel code 0 DESC_CODE64
3 GDT_ENTRY_KERNEL_DS Kernel data/stack segment 0 DESC_DATA64
4 GDT_ENTRY_DEFAULT_USER32_CS 32-bit user compatibility code 3 DESC_CODE32 | DESC_USER
5 GDT_ENTRY_DEFAULT_USER_DS User data/stack 3 DESC_DATA64 | DESC_USER
6 GDT_ENTRY_DEFAULT_USER_CS 64-bit user code 3 DESC_CODE64 | DESC_USER
8-9 GDT_ENTRY_TSS 16-byte TSS descriptor system DESC_TSS
10-11 GDT_ENTRY_LDT 16-byte LDT descriptor system DESC_LDT
12-14 GDT_ENTRY_TLS_* TLS descriptors for compatibility paths varies user-managed
15 GDT_ENTRY_CPUNODE per-CPU/node support segment 3 selector form data

The Linux LDTs

The Local Descriptor Table (LDT) is an optional, per-process descriptor table that supplements the global GDT. Intel designed it so that each process could define its own private segments — a powerful mechanism for fine-grained memory protection in a segmented environment. In practice, almost no modern software uses it. The flat memory model that Linux adopted (and that long mode enforces for CS/DS/ES/SS) makes per-process segments pointless for address translation. The LDT persists in the architecture only because legacy 32-bit and 16-bit code — and compatibility layers like Wine — occasionally need custom segment descriptors with non-zero bases to emulate the segmented memory models of older operating systems.

Linux 7.0.12 treats the LDT as an opt-in, security-sensitive exception rather than a default feature. A process starts life with no LDT at all: mm_struct.context.ldt is NULL, and the CPU's ldtr register is loaded with a null selector. No LDT allocation occurs unless a process explicitly requests one via the modify_ldt() system call. As the source code in arch/x86/kernel/ldt.c comments, "modify_ldt() is mostly used by legacy code and emulators." The kernel also enforces additional safety measures: LDT memory is page-aligned and fully zeroed on allocation (Xen requires this to prevent "evil descriptors such as call gates"), 16-bit segments are rejected unless explicitly allowed by a boot parameter, and the entire PTI aliasing mechanism (described below) exists specifically to keep LDT entries accessible from user mode without exposing kernel memory.

Unlike the old 2.6 kernel, which kept a shared default_ldt array with call gates for iBCS and Solaris/x86 binary compatibility, modern Linux 7.0.12 has no default LDT. The read_default_ldt() function in arch/x86/kernel/ldt.c simply returns zero bytes — it exists only as a stub for the modify_ldt(0, …) read path.

When LDTs are used

In practice, only a handful of scenarios trigger LDT allocation. The primary use case is Wine (and similar compatibility layers like DOSEMU2) that run segment-oriented Windows or DOS applications requiring custom selectors with non-zero bases. Some JIT compilers and sandboxing frameworks (notably Google's NaCl, now discontinued) also used LDT entries to create restricted execution environments. On a typical Linux desktop or server, the vast majority of processes never call modify_ldt(), and their mm->context.ldt remains NULL for their entire lifetime.

The modify_ldt() system call allows a process to create and populate a per-process LDT. The kernel performs several security checks in write_ldt() before accepting a descriptor:

/* arch/x86/kernel/ldt.c — write_ldt() checks */
SYSCALL_DEFINE3(modify_ldt, int, func, void __user *, ptr,
                unsigned long, bytecount)
  • func = 0 — read the current LDT (or the empty default)
  • func = 1 — write a new LDT entry (write_ldt())
  • func = 2 — read the old-style default LDT (legacy, returns empty)
  • func = 0x11 — write with old-style semantics

write_ldt() validates: the entry number must be less than LDT_ENTRIES (8192), the contents field must not be 3 (reserved), and 16-bit segments are rejected unless explicitly allowed by the allow_16bit_segments boot parameter — a security hardening measure that prevents exploitation of 16-bit addressing modes. Only after these checks does the kernel allocate, copy, map, and install the new descriptor.

The ldt_struct

Each process with a custom LDT has a struct ldt_struct in its mm_struct.context:

/* arch/x86/include/asm/mmu_context.h */
struct ldt_struct {
    struct desc_struct  *entries;
    unsigned int        nr_entries;
    int                 slot;  /* PTI alias slot, or -1 */
};
  • entries — pointer to an array of desc_struct descriptors
  • nr_entries — number of valid descriptors
  • slot — used by Page Table Isolation (PTI) to manage dual mappings

LDTs and Page Table Isolation (PTI / KPTI)

LDTs create one of the more subtle complications in the PTI (Page Table Isolation) design. Normally, PTI unmaps all kernel memory from the user-mode page tables to mitigate Meltdown — when user code is running, it cannot speculatively read kernel data because the kernel pages simply are not mapped. But when user-mode code references an LDT selector, the CPU must be able to read the LDT entries to perform the segmentation translation — and those entries live in kernel-allocated memory. If the LDT pages are unmapped during user execution, the CPU will take a fault on every LDT-based memory access.

Linux 7.0.12 solves this with a dual-slot aliasing scheme implemented in map_ldt_struct():

  1. The kernel reserves a dedicated virtual address region for LDT remaps (each CPU has a pair of slots at fixed addresses computed by ldt_slot_va()).
  2. map_ldt_struct() maps the LDT pages into one of these slots with user-accessible, read-only, no-execute permissions — just enough for the CPU's segmentation hardware to read the descriptors, but not enough for user code to execute from them or modify them.
  3. Two slots exist per mm so that when a process calls modify_ldt() to update its LDT, the new LDT is mapped in the other slot while CPUs still reference the old one. Once all CPUs have been IPIed to switch (via install_ldt()), the old slot is unmapped. This avoids a window where no LDT is accessible — a subtle race that would cause user-mode faults on any LDT-referencing instruction.
/* arch/x86/kernel/ldt.c — write_ldt() slot selection */
new_slot = old_ldt ? !old_ldt->slot : 0;  /* alternate slots */
map_ldt_struct(mm, new_ldt, new_slot);
install_ldt(mm, new_ldt);                 /* IPI all CPUs */
unmap_ldt_struct(mm, old_ldt);            /* free old slot */

This PTI interaction is entirely new compared to the 2.6-era kernel and is one of the reasons LDT handling is significantly more complex today. On context switch, load_mm_ldt() checks whether the new process has an LDT: if so, it reads the slot number and loads the ldtr register with the aliased address; if not, it calls clear_LDT().

LDTs during fork and exit

When a process forks (copy_mm()), the child must inherit the parent's LDT if one exists. The kernel calls ldt_dup_context() in arch/x86/kernel/ldt.c, which:

  1. Locks the parent's mm->context.lock to prevent concurrent modify_ldt() calls.
  2. Allocates a new ldt_struct with the same size as the parent's.
  3. Copies the parent's descriptor entries with memcpy().
  4. Calls map_ldt_struct() to create the PTI alias mapping for the child's address space.
  5. Assigns mm->context.ldt = new_ldt in the child's mm.

On process exit, destroy_context_ldt() frees the LDT memory and tears down the PTI mapping. An important invariant noted in the source is that "an mm will never go from having an LDT to not having an LDT" — once a process allocates an LDT, it keeps one (possibly modified) until exit. Individual entries can be cleared, but the ldt_struct itself persists.

Note: The kernel itself never uses LDT entries. Any segments created via modify_ldt() are purely for user-mode use. The kernel does not need to track them beyond ensuring the descriptor table is correctly mapped and inherited across fork.

Thread-Local Storage: FS and GS

Of all the segment registers, FS and GS are the only two that remain operationally important on x86_64. They are where segmentation stops being historical trivia and becomes a daily concern for kernel and toolchain developers. Their bases are the only ones that can hold non-zero values in long mode, and the kernel exploits this to solve two distinct problems without consuming a general-purpose register: FS gives each user-space thread a fast pointer to its thread-local storage block, and GS gives the kernel a fast pointer to per-CPU data on whichever CPU is currently executing.

The hardware mechanism is the same for both: when the CPU encounters an instruction with an %fs: or %gs: prefix (e.g., mov %fs:0x28, %rax), it adds the segment's base value to the offset before feeding the result to the paging unit. This is the one case where the segmentation unit performs real arithmetic in long mode — for all other segments the base is zero and the addition is a no-op. The base values are stored in MSRs (MSR_FS_BASE, MSR_GS_BASE) or, on modern CPUs with the FSGSBASE feature, can be read and written with dedicated instructions (RDFSBASE/WRFSBASE/RDGSBASE/WRGSBASE) that are faster than MSR access.

FS base in user space

User-space thread-local storage on x86_64 conventionally uses FS base. The C library, thread libraries, and language runtimes treat %fs as the thread pointer register. A memory reference such as mov %fs:0x28, %rax is a TLS load — it reads a field at offset 0x28 from the thread-local block, not an odd form of segmented addressing in the old DOS sense.

The kernel provides two interfaces for user space to set the FS base:

  • arch_prctl(ARCH_SET_FS, addr) — the native 64-bit interface. Internally, do_arch_prctl_64() in arch/x86/kernel/process_64.c calls x86_fsbase_write_cpu(), which uses wrfsbase() if the CPU supports FSGSBASE, otherwise falls back to wrmsrq(MSR_FS_BASE, addr). glibc calls this during thread creation to point %fs at the new thread's TLS block.
  • set_thread_area() — the older, 32-bit-compatible interface that writes a GDT TLS entry (indices 12–14) with a custom base and limit. This is used by 32-bit binaries running in compatibility mode, where %gs (not %fs) is traditionally the TLS register. The implementation in arch/x86/kernel/tls.c validates the request, writes the descriptor into the thread's tls_array, and reloads it if the thread is currently running.

During context switch, __switch_to() calls save_fsgs() to snapshot the outgoing thread's FS and GS bases, then x86_fsgsbase_load() to restore the incoming thread's values. On CPUs with FSGSBASE, this is a pair of fast register writes:

/* arch/x86/kernel/process_64.c — x86_fsgsbase_load() */
wrfsbase(next->fsbase);
__wrgsbase_inactive(next->gsbase);

On older CPUs without FSGSBASE, the kernel falls back to MSR writes or segment-selector loads, which are slower but functionally equivalent.

GS base in the kernel

The kernel uses GS base for a different purpose: GS points at per-CPU data while running in the kernel. During boot, switch_gdt_and_percpu_base() in arch/x86/kernel/cpu/common.c writes MSR_GS_BASE with the per-CPU area offset for that CPU. From that point on, %gs:offset is an efficient way to access CPU-local variables without first materializing a per-CPU base pointer in a general-purpose register.

Three MSRs matter here:

#define MSR_FS_BASE         0xc0000100  /* user FS base */
#define MSR_GS_BASE         0xc0000101  /* currently active GS base */
#define MSR_KERNEL_GS_BASE  0xc0000102  /* shadow, swapped by swapgs */

The slightly confusing naming is worth spelling out. MSR_GS_BASE holds whichever GS base is currently active — when the kernel is running, this is the per-CPU data pointer; when user code is running, this is the user's GS base. MSR_KERNEL_GS_BASE is the shadow: it holds the value that is not currently active. The swapgs instruction atomically exchanges these two MSRs.

In arch/x86/entry/entry_64.S, user-to-kernel transitions execute swapgs as one of the very first instructions in entry_SYSCALL_64 — before the kernel can access any per-CPU data, it must swap in the kernel GS base. On return to user mode, swapgs restores the user's GS base before sysretq or iretq. The kernel enforces a convention (documented in the entry code) that a negative GS base value indicates the kernel — this allows paranoid entry paths (NMI, machine check) to determine whether swapgs has already been executed without needing an additional flag.

This swapgs sequence is security-sensitive: a misordered or mispredicted swapgs can expose wrong per-CPU assumptions. Modern kernels include speculation barriers (FENCE_SWAPGS_USER_ENTRY, CLEAR_CPU_BUFFERS) around these paths, and CPUs with the X86_BUG_SWAPGS_FENCE erratum insert an mfence or lfence to prevent speculative use of the wrong GS base.

Why Linux kept GS instead of inventing something else

Because it is fast. The x86 architecture already offers a dedicated base register, hidden behind a segment register, with efficient addressing modes. Using GS for per-CPU data means:

  • no general-purpose register is permanently consumed,
  • per-CPU access syntax can be short,
  • entry code can switch between user and kernel views with a single architectural instruction.

In other words, segmentation became vestigial for memory protection, but remained useful as a low-overhead addressing aid.

Segmentation on ARM64

ARM64 has no segmentation. This is not merely an implementation preference; it is a fundamental architectural simplification. There are no segment selectors, no GDT, no LDT, no descriptor privilege levels in the x86 sense, and no FS/GS-style hidden bases. ARM designed the AArch64 execution state from scratch in 2011, decades after the segmentation concept had proven unnecessary for modern operating systems. Every feature that x86 shoehorns through segmentation — privilege transitions, thread-local storage, per-CPU data, user/kernel address-space split — ARM64 handles with cleaner, purpose-built mechanisms.

Privilege levels use Exception Levels (EL0–EL3) instead of segment descriptor privilege. Linux runs user code at EL0 and the kernel at EL1. The current exception level is stored in the PSTATE register, and the kernel determines whether interrupted code was user or kernel by checking (regs->pstate & PSR_MODE_MASK) == PSR_MODE_EL0t in arch/arm64/include/asm/ptrace.h. There is no swapgs equivalent because there are no segment bases to swap — entering the kernel from EL0 is simply an exception that saves SPSR_EL1 (saved process state) and ELR_EL1 (return address), then jumps to the exception vector. The entry path in arch/arm64/kernel/entry.S is dramatically simpler than x86's entry_SYSCALL_64 precisely because there are no segments to manipulate.

Thread-local storage uses the TPIDR_EL0 system register instead of an FS base. User-space reads and writes this register directly (it is accessible from EL0), and the kernel saves/restores it on context switch in __switch_to():

/* arch/arm64/kernel/process.c — tls_thread_switch() */
*task_user_tls(current) = read_sysreg(tpidr_el0);
write_sysreg(*task_user_tls(next), tpidr_el0);

For 32-bit compatibility tasks (AArch32), the kernel additionally uses TPIDRRO_EL0 (a read-only register from EL0) as the TLS pointer, matching the convention used by ARM32 glibc.

Per-CPU data uses TPIDR_EL1 (or TPIDR_EL2 under VHE/hypervisor mode) instead of a GS base. The kernel stores the per-CPU offset in this register during boot and reads it via __my_cpu_offset in arch/arm64/include/asm/percpu.h:

/* arch/arm64/include/asm/percpu.h */
static inline unsigned long __kern_my_cpu_offset(void)
{
    unsigned long off;
    asm("mrs %0, tpidr_el1" : "=r" (off));  /* or tpidr_el2 */
    return off;
}

This is functionally identical to x86's %gs:offset pattern but uses a system register instead of a segment base — no descriptor tables, no swapgs, and no MSR reads.

User/kernel address-space split uses the dual TTBR design:

  • TTBR0_EL1 — holds the page-table root for the lower (user) half of the address space. Changed on every context switch by check_and_switch_context() in arch/arm64/mm/context.c.
  • TTBR1_EL1 — holds the page-table root for the upper (kernel) half. Points to swapper_pg_dir and is never changed during normal operation — all processes share the same kernel mappings.

Bit 55 of the virtual address selects which TTBR the MMU uses. An ASID (Address Space Identifier) is embedded in TTBR0 to tag TLB entries per-process, allowing the TLB to hold entries from multiple address spaces simultaneously without full flushes on context switch — a role analogous to x86's PCID but built into the TTBR register itself.

The contrast with x86_64 is instructive. Where x86_64 says "the old segmentation hardware is still here, but Linux uses it only for a few special purposes," ARM64 says "there is no such hardware; use page tables, exception levels, and system registers directly." The result is fewer moving parts in the entry code, no GDT/LDT/TSS to manage, and no Meltdown-style PTI complications for segment descriptors — though ARM64 has its own PTI implementation for Spectre-style kernel address leaks via TTBR0 zeroing.

Table 2-4. Segmentation status on the two primary architectures
Feature x86_64 ARM64
Segment registers visible to software Yes: cs ss ds es fs gs No
General code/data base relocation Effectively no in long mode No
Special TLS/per-CPU bases Yes: FS and GS No direct equivalent; use ordinary registers or system registers
Privilege encoded in code segment Yes, through CS and descriptor metadata No; use exception level and page permissions
GDT/LDT/TSS structures Yes No
Kernel/user split foundation Paging plus vestigial segmentation rules Paging plus TTBR0/TTBR1 split

Paging in Hardware

If segmentation now lives mostly in the footnotes, paging lives at the center of the story. Paging is where large virtual address spaces become sparse, where permissions are enforced, where huge pages are introduced, where TLB behavior is shaped, and where Linux spends a large fraction of its MM bookkeeping effort. The implementation differs sharply between x86_64 and ARM64, but the design goal is the same: translate quickly, cache aggressively, and invalidate only when necessary.

The paging hardware's central data structure is the page table — a tree of arrays stored in physical memory that the MMU walks on every address translation. On x86_64, the root of this tree is pointed to by the CR3 register; writing a new physical address into CR3 switches the entire address space. On ARM64, two registers serve this purpose: TTBR0_EL1 for user-space and TTBR1_EL1 for the kernel. In both cases, changing the root pointer is the fundamental mechanism behind process context switching — the kernel writes a new CR3 or TTBR0 value, and the CPU instantly sees a different virtual-to-physical mapping.

Modern kernels do not blindly flush the TLB on every context switch. Both architectures embed a small tag in the root pointer — x86_64 calls it a PCID (Process Context Identifier), ARM64 calls it an ASID (Address Space Identifier).
