TLB entries are stamped with this tag, so entries from one process do not conflict with entries from another. The kernel builds the tagged CR3 in build_cr3() and writes it via load_new_mm_cr3() in arch/x86/mm/tlb.c; ARM64 embeds the ASID in TTBR0's upper bits via FIELD_PREP(TTBR_ASID_MASK, asid) in arch/arm64/mm/context.c. This tagging means a context switch that revisits a recently-used address space may skip the TLB flush entirely — a significant performance win on workloads with frequent scheduling.

The page table itself is a multi-level radix tree. Linux 2.6 on 32-bit x86 used two- and three-level tables (without and with PAE); kernel 7.0.12 uses four or five levels depending on the virtual-address width. Standard x86_64 uses 4-level paging (48-bit VA, 256 TB per address space), while CPUs with the LA57 feature enable 5-level paging (57-bit VA, 128 PB) — detected at boot via pgtable_l5_enabled() in arch/x86/include/asm/pgtable_64_types.h. ARM64 similarly supports 3 or 4 levels depending on CONFIG_ARM64_VA_BITS (typically 48 or 52 bits), computed by ARM64_HW_PGTABLE_LEVELS() in arch/arm64/include/asm/pgtable-hwdef.h. The deeper the tree, the more memory a single process can address — but the longer the hardware walk takes on a TLB miss.

When the walk fails — the page table entry is missing or the access violates permissions — the MMU raises a page fault. On x86_64 this is exception vector 14, handled by exc_page_fault() in arch/x86/mm/fault.c; on ARM64 it arrives as a data or instruction abort, handled by do_page_fault() in arch/arm64/mm/fault.c. The fault handler is one of the most performance-critical paths in the kernel — it decides whether to allocate a new page, load one from swap, send a SIGSEGV, or invoke copy-on-write. When translation succeeds but the TLB later needs to be invalidated (after munmap(), page migration, or permission changes), x86_64 uses the INVLPG instruction for single-page shootdowns and IPI-driven flush_tlb_mm_range() for bulk invalidations; ARM64 uses TLBI instructions that can target an individual VA, an entire ASID, or all entries.

Finally, paging hardware provides a shortcut for large mappings: huge pages. Instead of pointing to the next level of the page table, an intermediate entry can directly map a large contiguous block of physical memory. On x86_64, setting the PSE (Page Size Extension) bit in a PMD entry creates a 2 MB mapping; setting it in a PUD entry creates a 1 GB mapping. ARM64 uses block descriptors (PMD_TYPE_SECT and PUD_TYPE_SECT) for the same purpose. Huge pages dramatically reduce TLB pressure — one TLB entry covers 512× or 262,144× more memory than a standard 4 KB page — and are critical for database, HPC, and virtualization workloads. The sections that follow examine each of these mechanisms in detail.

The Paging Unit

The paging unit is the hardware circuit that translates linear addresses (also called virtual addresses on architectures without segmentation) into physical addresses. It sits between the CPU's execution pipeline and the memory bus, intercepting every instruction fetch, data read, and data write. On x86_64, the paging unit is part of the MMU inside the CPU core itself; on ARM64, the equivalent is called the translation regime managed by the MMU at EL1. In both cases, the translation is invisible to user-space code — a program issues virtual addresses and never sees physical ones.

The paging unit's two central responsibilities are:

  1. Address translation — map the linear address to the physical address of a page frame in RAM. On a TLB hit, the translation completes in a single cycle; on a TLB miss, the hardware page-table walker traverses the multi-level page table stored in main memory. This walk costs tens to hundreds of cycles depending on cache residency, making TLB coverage one of the most performance-sensitive parameters in the system.
  2. Access checking — verify that the requested access type (read, write, execute) is compatible with the protection attributes of the page. If the access is invalid, the paging unit generates a Page Fault exception. On x86_64, this is exception vector 14, handled by exc_page_fault() in arch/x86/mm/fault.c, which reads the faulting address from CR2 (or FRED event data on newer CPUs). On ARM64, a data abort or instruction abort arrives through the exception vector table, and the kernel dispatches to do_page_fault() in arch/arm64/mm/fault.c, which extracts the faulting address from the FAR_EL1 register and classifies the fault via the ESR_ELx_FSC field of the Exception Syndrome Register.

The paging unit does not operate in isolation. Every translation it produces is cached in the Translation Lookaside Buffer (TLB) — a small, fast associative cache that holds recently used virtual-to-physical mappings. When the kernel modifies a page table (e.g., during munmap(), mprotect(), or page migration), it must explicitly invalidate the affected TLB entries. On x86_64, this is done with INVLPG for a single page or IPI-driven flush_tlb_mm_range() for ranges; on ARM64, the kernel issues TLBI instructions that can target a specific VA, an entire ASID, or all entries. The TLB is covered in its own section later in this chapter.

Pages and page frames

For the sake of efficiency, linear addresses are grouped into fixed-length intervals called pages. Contiguous linear addresses within a page are mapped to contiguous physical addresses. This means the kernel only needs to specify the physical address and the access rights per page rather than per byte.

The page size is defined by PAGE_SHIFT and PAGE_SIZE:

/* include/vdso/page.h */
#define PAGE_SHIFT      CONFIG_PAGE_SHIFT
#define PAGE_SIZE       (_AC(1, UL) << PAGE_SHIFT)
#define PAGE_MASK       (~(PAGE_SIZE - 1))

On x86_64, PAGE_SHIFT is always 12 (4 KB pages). On ARM64, Linux supports three granule sizes — 4 KB (PAGE_SHIFT=12), 16 KB (PAGE_SHIFT=14), and 64 KB (PAGE_SHIFT=16) — selected at kernel build time via CONFIG_ARM64_PAGE_SHIFT. The choice of granule affects the number of page-table levels, the size of huge pages, and the TLB reach of the processor.

The paging unit thinks of all physical RAM as partitioned into fixed-length page frames (also called physical pages). Each page frame can hold one page — that is, the size of a page frame equals the size of a page.

It is important to distinguish a page from a page frame:

  • A page is a block of data that may reside in any page frame or even on disk (if it has been swapped out).
  • A page frame is a physical container in main memory — a fixed storage location identified by its physical address.

The kernel tracks every page frame with a struct page descriptor (defined in include/linux/mm_types.h). This structure is approximately 64 bytes on 64-bit systems and is allocated for every physical page frame in the system. On a machine with 256 GB of RAM, that means roughly 4 GB of memory is consumed just by struct page descriptors — about 1.6% of total RAM. The kernel converts between page frame numbers and struct page pointers using pfn_to_page() and page_to_pfn() (defined in include/asm-generic/memory_model.h), whose implementation depends on the memory model (FLATMEM, SPARSEMEM, or SPARSEMEM_VMEMMAP). Chapter 8 covers struct page and the memory models in detail.

Page tables

The data structures that map linear addresses to physical addresses are called page tables. They are stored in main memory and must be properly initialized by the kernel before the paging unit is enabled.

On x86, paging is enabled by setting the PG flag (bit 31) of control register cr0. When PG = 0, linear addresses are passed straight through as physical addresses with no translation:

/* arch/x86/include/uapi/asm/processor-flags.h */
#define X86_CR0_PG_BIT      31 /* Paging */
#define X86_CR0_PG          _BITUL(X86_CR0_PG_BIT)

On ARM64, paging is controlled by the M bit (bit 0) of the SCTLR_EL1 system register. When M = 0, all addresses are treated as physical (flat mapping).

Enabling paging at boot: the identity-map trick

Enabling paging is a delicate chicken-and-egg problem: the instruction that sets CR0.PG (or SCTLR_EL1.M) is fetched by the CPU using the current addressing mode. The moment paging is enabled, the very next instruction fetch goes through the page tables. If those tables do not map the physical address where the kernel code is running, the CPU immediately faults and the system crashes.

The solution on both architectures is an identity mapping — a page-table region where virtual address == physical address. The boot code places the paging-enable instruction within this identity-mapped window so that the instruction stream remains valid across the transition.

On x86_64, the early boot code in arch/x86/kernel/head_64.S loads early_top_pgt into CR3 — a minimal page table that identity-maps the kernel's physical location. After jumping to virtual addresses, the kernel switches to init_top_pgt and unmaps the identity range:

/* arch/x86/kernel/head_64.S — simplified boot flow */
movq    $early_top_pgt, %rax
movq    %rax, %cr3              /* load identity-mapped page table */
movl    %eax, %cr0              /* set CR0.PG — paging now active */
...
/* later, after reaching virtual addresses: */
movq    $init_top_pgt, %rax
movq    %rax, %cr3              /* switch to final page table */

On ARM64, the boot code in arch/arm64/kernel/head.S uses idmap_pg_dir as the identity-mapped page directory. The __enable_mmu function loads TTBR0 with the identity map and then sets SCTLR_EL1.M:

/* arch/arm64/kernel/head.S — __enable_mmu */
msr     ttbr0_el1, x2           /* load identity-map page table */
...
set_sctlr_el1   x0             /* enable MMU (sets SCTLR_EL1.M) */

Once the kernel is running at its link-time virtual addresses, TTBR0 is reprogrammed for user-space and TTBR1 points to swapper_pg_dir (the kernel's page table). The identity map is no longer needed and its entries are eventually cleared, except for a small reserved region used during CPU hotplug and suspend/resume — operations that require briefly returning to physical addresses.

How page tables are created and managed at runtime

The boot-time page tables described above are just the starting point. During normal operation, the kernel constantly creates, populates, and destroys page tables in response to mmap(), fork(), page faults, and process exit. Understanding this lifecycle is essential because page tables are one of the kernel's most frequently allocated data structures — a busy system may create thousands of page-table pages per second.

The on-demand allocation cascade (page fault path)

User-space page tables are created lazily: the kernel does not pre-allocate the full tree when a process calls mmap(). Instead, it waits until the process actually touches an address, which triggers a page fault. The fault handler then walks the page-table tree from the top, allocating any missing levels on the way down:

/* mm/memory.c — __handle_mm_fault() simplified */
pgd = pgd_offset(mm, address);           /* PGD always exists */
p4d = p4d_alloc(mm, pgd, address);       /* allocate P4D if needed */
vmf.pud = pud_alloc(mm, p4d, address);   /* allocate PUD if needed */
vmf.pmd = pmd_alloc(mm, vmf.pud, address); /* allocate PMD if needed */
...
return handle_pte_fault(&vmf);           /* allocate PTE + page frame */

Each *_alloc() function checks whether the entry already exists; if not, it allocates a fresh zeroed page-table page and installs it. The PGD itself is never allocated here — it is created once when the process's mm_struct is initialized (at fork() time) and persists until process exit.

Page-table page allocation

All page-table pages are allocated through a unified interface. The key GFP flags are defined in include/asm-generic/pgalloc.h:

/* include/asm-generic/pgalloc.h */
#define GFP_PGTABLE_KERNEL  (GFP_KERNEL | __GFP_ZERO)
#define GFP_PGTABLE_USER    (GFP_PGTABLE_KERNEL | __GFP_ACCOUNT)

Two aspects are notable:

  • __GFP_ZERO — every page-table page is zeroed on allocation. A zero entry means "not present," so a freshly allocated table has no valid mappings. This is a security requirement: leftover data from a previous use could leak kernel addresses or grant unintended access.
  • __GFP_ACCOUNT — user-space page tables are charged to the process's memory cgroup. This prevents a process from exhausting system memory by creating deeply nested page-table trees (e.g., by mmap()-ing millions of sparse regions).

On x86_64, pte_alloc_one() in arch/x86/mm/pgtable.c calls through to pagetable_alloc(GFP_PGTABLE_USER, 0) — allocating a single zeroed 4 KB page. The PMD pre-allocation path (preallocate_pmds()) uses the same allocator to prepare the kernel portion of the PGD at pgd_alloc() time.

On ARM64, pgd_alloc() in arch/arm64/mm/pgd.c uses GFP_PGTABLE_USER. Lower-level tables (PUD, PMD, PTE) go through the generic pagetable_alloc() path. The arch-specific *_populate() functions in arch/arm64/include/asm/pgalloc.h set ARM64-specific bits (UXN, PXN, table type) when installing entries — a step that x86 does not need because x86 table entries carry permission bits only in the leaf.

Kernel page-table creation

The kernel's own page tables are built differently from user-space tables. The kernel page directory swapper_pg_dir (on ARM64) or init_top_pgt (on x86_64) is allocated statically at compile time and populated during paging_init().

On x86_64, __kernel_physical_mapping_init() in arch/x86/mm/init_64.c builds the direct map — a linear mapping of all physical RAM into the kernel's virtual address space starting at PAGE_OFFSET. It uses 2 MB or 1 GB huge pages wherever possible to minimize TLB pressure. The PGD, PUD, PMD, and PTE pages are allocated using alloc_low_pages() from the early boot memory allocator (memblock), since the slab allocator is not yet available.

On ARM64, paging_init() in arch/arm64/mm/mmu.c maps the kernel image, the linear region (direct map), and any fixmap/vmemmap areas. It uses block descriptors (2 MB sections with 4 KB granule) for the linear map and page-level mappings for fine-grained regions like the kernel text (which needs distinct read/execute vs. read/write permissions).

A critical detail: on x86_64, every new user PGD must include the kernel mappings in its upper entries. The function pgd_ctor() in arch/x86/mm/pgtable.c clones the kernel portion of swapper_pg_dir into each newly allocated PGD, ensuring that a context switch to any process still reaches kernel code. On ARM64, this is unnecessary because TTBR1 always points to the kernel's swapper_pg_dir — user PGDs only contain user mappings.

Fork: duplicating page tables with copy-on-write

When a process calls fork(), the kernel must give the child its own copy of the parent's virtual address space. Rather than copying every mapped page, Linux uses copy-on-write (COW): it duplicates the page-table structure but marks all writable pages as read-only in both parent and child. The first write from either process triggers a page fault, and the fault handler allocates a private copy of just that page.

The duplication is performed by copy_page_range() in mm/memory.c, which walks the tree level by level:

/* mm/memory.c — fork page-table duplication chain */
copy_page_range()           /* top-level entry: per-VMA */
  → copy_p4d_range()        /* allocate child P4D if needed */
    → copy_pud_range()      /* allocate child PUD */
      → copy_pmd_range()    /* allocate child PMD */
        → copy_pte_range()  /* copy PTE entries, set COW */

At each level, the kernel allocates a fresh table page for the child if none exists, then copies entries from the parent. At the PTE level, writable pages are write-protected and their reference counts are incremented. This makes fork() fast — proportional to the number of page-table pages, not the number of mapped physical pages.

Tearing down: page-table freeing

When a process exits or unmaps a region, the kernel must free the page-table pages that are no longer needed. This is handled by free_pgtables() in mm/memory.c, which walks the VMA list and calls free_pgd_range() for each contiguous unmapped region.

Page-table pages cannot be freed immediately because other CPUs may still hold stale TLB entries pointing into them. The kernel uses RCU-protected TLB batching: page-table pages are added to a gather list via tlb_remove_table(), and the actual freeing happens only after a TLB invalidation has been issued and all CPUs have acknowledged it. This deferred freeing prevents a dangerous race where one CPU is still walking a page table that another CPU has already freed.

Regular Paging (the Classic Two-Level Model)

Before we describe the modern multi-level page tables used on x86_64 and ARM64, it is helpful to understand the classic two-level paging scheme introduced by the i386, because it illustrates the core ideas that all deeper hierarchies build upon. Every concept in this section — the directory/table/offset split, the CR3 root pointer, the Present/Dirty/Accessed flags, and large-page short-circuiting — reappears in every subsequent x86 paging mode. PAE widened the entries, x86_64 added more levels, and ARM64 replaced the whole vocabulary, but the algorithm remained the same.

Remarkably, the 32-bit two-level code path still compiles and runs in kernel 7.0.12. The header arch/x86/include/asm/pgtable-2level_types.h defines:

/* arch/x86/include/asm/pgtable-2level_types.h */
#define PGDIR_SHIFT     22
#define PTRS_PER_PGD    1024
#define PTRS_PER_PTE    1024

These constants are selected when building a CONFIG_X86_32 kernel without PAE. The fact that this path still exists — three decades after the i386 — reflects both the backwards compatibility culture of x86 and the fact that some deeply embedded deployments still run 32-bit kernels. For this book, we treat two-level paging as a teaching model, not a deployment target.

In classic 32-bit x86 paging, the 32 bits of a linear address are divided into three fields:

FieldBitsWidthIndexes into
Directory31:2210 bitsPage Directory (1024 entries)
Table21:1210 bitsPage Table (1024 entries)
Offset11:012 bitsByte within the 4 KB page

The translation is accomplished in two steps, each based on a translation table:

  1. The cr3 register holds the physical address of the Page Directory.
  2. The Directory field selects one of 1024 entries in the Page Directory. Each entry points to a Page Table.
  3. The Table field selects one of 1024 entries in the Page Table. Each entry points to a 4 KB page frame.
  4. The Offset field identifies the specific byte within that page frame.

Why multi-level?

The purpose of this two-level hierarchy is to reduce RAM consumption. If a flat one-level table were used, it would need 2²⁰ = 1,048,576 entries (4 MB) for each process — even if the process uses only a small fraction of its 4 GB address space. With two levels, Page Tables are allocated only for the virtual address ranges the process actually uses. A typical process might need only a handful of Page Tables rather than the full set of 1024.

To make this concrete: a simple "Hello, World" program touches perhaps 3–4 virtual regions (text, data, stack, shared libraries). That might require only 5–10 Page Tables (5–10 × 4 KB = 20–40 KB) plus the single 4 KB Page Directory — roughly 50 KB of page-table memory. The same program under a flat table would consume 4 MB regardless. This 80× reduction in overhead is why multi-level paging was adopted and why the same principle was extended to 3, 4, and 5 levels as address spaces grew. The cost is one extra memory access per level on a TLB miss, but TLB hits avoid the walk entirely, making the trade-off strongly favourable in practice.

Page table entry flags

Both Page Directory entries and Page Table entries share a common 32-bit format. The upper 20 bits hold the physical address of the next-level table or page frame (aligned to 4 KB, so the low 12 bits are always zero and can be reused). The lower 12 bits hold flags that control access, caching, and bookkeeping. Two of these flags — Accessed and Dirty — are hardware-managed: the CPU sets them automatically during translation, and the kernel must explicitly clear them when it wants to track new access patterns. Linux checks these bits through helper functions like pte_young() (tests Accessed) and pte_dirty() (tests Dirty) in arch/x86/include/asm/pgtable.h, and clears them with ptep_test_and_clear_young() and pte_mkclean(). These helpers are used extensively by the page-reclaim code (Chapter 8) and the working-set estimator to decide which pages are hot and which can be evicted.

The key fields include:

FlagBitMeaning
Present0If set, the page/table is in main memory. If clear, accessing it triggers a Page Fault. The remaining bits can be used by the OS (e.g., to store swap location).
Read/Write1If set, the page is read/write. If clear, it is read-only.
User/Supervisor2If set, user-mode code can access the page. If clear, only kernel mode.
PWT3Page-level write-through — controls cache write policy.
PCD4Page-level cache disable — bypasses the hardware cache.
Accessed5Set by hardware whenever the page is accessed. Must be cleared by the OS. Used by page-replacement algorithms.
Dirty6Set by hardware on writes (Page Table entries only). Must be cleared by the OS. Used to decide whether a page needs to be written back before eviction.
Page Size (PSE)7In Page Directory entries: if set, the entry maps a large page (2 MB or 4 MB) directly, skipping the Page Table level.
Global8If set, the TLB entry is not flushed on cr3 reload. Used for kernel pages mapped identically in all processes.

The remaining upper bits hold the physical address of the next-level table or page frame. Because page frames are aligned to 4 KB, the 12 least significant bits of the physical address are always zero and can be reused for the flags above.

Extended paging (large pages)

The multi-level approach optimizes for sparse address spaces, but some regions — such as the kernel's direct map of all physical RAM — are dense: every entry in a Page Table points to a valid page frame. For such regions, the intermediate Page Table level wastes both memory (1024 4-byte entries × thousands of tables) and TLB capacity (each TLB entry covers only 4 KB). Extended paging solves this by letting the hardware short-circuit the walk: a Page Directory entry can directly map a large contiguous region, skipping the Page Table level entirely.

Starting with the Pentium, x86 processors support extended paging, which maps larger page frames directly from a Page Directory entry without an intermediate Page Table. On classic 32-bit systems, this gives 4 MB pages; on PAE systems, 2 MB pages. It is enabled by setting the PSE flag in the cr4 register:

/* arch/x86/include/uapi/asm/processor-flags.h */
#define X86_CR4_PSE_BIT     4  /* enable page size extensions */
#define X86_CR4_PSE         _BITUL(X86_CR4_PSE_BIT)

When PSE is set in a Page Directory entry, the linear address is split into just two fields: the 10-bit Directory field and a 22-bit Offset, and the entry directly contains the physical base of a 4 MB page frame. Extended paging can coexist with regular 4 KB paging.

In extended paging, the Page Directory entry with the PSE bit set directly contains the physical base address of a 4 MB page frame. The intermediate Page Table level is completely bypassed. Because the Offset field is 22 bits wide, it can address any byte within the 4 MB frame (2²² = 4,194,304).

The key differences from regular paging:

  • The Page Size flag (bit 7) in the Page Directory entry must be set.
  • Only the 10 most significant bits of the 20-bit physical address field are meaningful, because the 4 MB page frame must be aligned on a 4 MB boundary (the 22 least significant bits are always zero).
  • The Dirty and Global flags, which normally apply only to Page Table entries, are honored directly in the Page Directory entry for extended pages.

Extended paging is particularly useful for mapping large contiguous regions — such as the kernel's direct-mapped physical memory — because it saves both memory (no intermediate Page Tables) and TLB entries (one TLB entry covers 4 MB instead of 4 KB).

Modern evolution: On x86_64, the same principle lives on as 2 MB huge pages (PSE at the PMD level) and 1 GB huge pages (PSE at the PUD level). The mechanism is identical — a leaf entry at a higher level of the hierarchy — just applied to deeper page-table trees with larger address spaces.

Hardware Protection Scheme

The paging unit uses a different — and in some ways simpler — protection scheme from the segmentation unit. While x86 segmentation supports four privilege levels (rings 0–3), paging recognizes only two: supervisor and user, controlled by a single bit per page. Segmentation enforced fine-grained privilege through DPL, RPL, and gate descriptors; paging replaces all of that with a flat binary question: is this a kernel page or a user page?

This simplicity is deliberate. In the original i386, both segmentation and paging could enforce access control, but they overlapped — a page marked read-only was read-only regardless of the segment's permissions. When AMD64 effectively flattened segmentation, paging became the sole hardware protection mechanism for memory access. All modern memory-protection features — NX, SMEP, SMAP, Protection Keys, CET shadow stacks — are implemented entirely within the paging unit.

The kernel never manipulates raw PTE flag bits in application code. Instead, it defines page-protection templates that combine the right flags for each use case. The key templates in arch/x86/include/asm/pgtable_types.h are:

/* arch/x86/include/asm/pgtable_types.h — page protection templates */
#define PAGE_SHARED          __pg(__PP|__RW|_USR|___A|__NX|   0|   0|   0)
#define PAGE_SHARED_EXEC     __pg(__PP|__RW|_USR|___A|   0|   0|   0|   0)
#define PAGE_READONLY        __pg(__PP|   0|_USR|___A|__NX|   0|   0|   0)
#define PAGE_READONLY_EXEC   __pg(__PP|   0|_USR|___A|   0|   0|   0|   0)
#define PAGE_KERNEL          __pgprot_mask(__PAGE_KERNEL            | _ENC)
#define PAGE_KERNEL_EXEC     __pgprot_mask(__PAGE_KERNEL_EXEC       | _ENC)

Notice the pattern: user pages always have _USR set; kernel pages omit it. Data pages have __NX; executable pages clear it. Shared pages have __RW; read-only pages clear it. The __pg() macro assembles all flags into a single pgprot_t value that the kernel can apply to any PTE.

These templates are connected to VMA permissions through a translation table in arch/x86/mm/pgprot.c:

/* arch/x86/mm/pgprot.c — VMA flags to PTE protection */
static pgprot_t protection_map[16] __ro_after_init = {
    [VM_NONE]                            = PAGE_NONE,
    [VM_READ]                            = PAGE_READONLY,
    [VM_WRITE]                           = PAGE_COPY,
    [VM_WRITE | VM_READ]                 = PAGE_COPY,
    [VM_EXEC]                            = PAGE_READONLY_EXEC,
    [VM_EXEC | VM_READ]                  = PAGE_READONLY_EXEC,
    [VM_EXEC | VM_WRITE]                 = PAGE_COPY_EXEC,
    [VM_EXEC | VM_WRITE | VM_READ]       = PAGE_COPY_EXEC,
    [VM_SHARED]                          = PAGE_NONE,
    [VM_SHARED | VM_READ]                = PAGE_READONLY,
    [VM_SHARED | VM_WRITE]               = PAGE_SHARED,
    [VM_SHARED | VM_WRITE | VM_READ]     = PAGE_SHARED,
    [VM_SHARED | VM_EXEC]                = PAGE_READONLY_EXEC,
    [VM_SHARED | VM_EXEC | VM_READ]      = PAGE_READONLY_EXEC,
    [VM_SHARED | VM_EXEC | VM_WRITE]     = PAGE_SHARED_EXEC,
    [VM_SHARED | VM_EXEC | VM_WRITE | VM_READ] = PAGE_SHARED_EXEC,
};

pgprot_t vm_get_page_prot(vm_flags_t vm_flags)
{
    unsigned long val = pgprot_val(protection_map[vm_flags &
                  (VM_READ|VM_WRITE|VM_EXEC|VM_SHARED)]);
    ...
}

When the kernel creates a page-table entry — during mmap(), page fault handling, or fork() — it calls vm_get_page_prot() with the VMA's vm_flags to obtain the correct hardware protection bits. This single function is the bridge between the software protection model (VM_READ, VM_WRITE, VM_EXEC) and the hardware flags (R/W, U/S, NX) in every PTE.

The classic two-flag model

Two flags in every page-table entry form the foundation of paging-level protection:

FlagBitEffect
User/Supervisor (U/S)2When clear (0), the page can be accessed only at CPL 0–2 (in Linux, only CPL 0 = Kernel Mode). When set (1), user-mode code (CPL 3) can also access the page.
Read/Write (R/W)1When clear (0), the page is read-only. When set (1), both read and write are allowed.

The effective permission of a page is the intersection (most restrictive) of the flags in every level of the hierarchy: if a PGD entry marks a region as read-only, no page within that region can be written, regardless of the PTE's R/W flag. In practice, Linux sets intermediate table entries (PGD, PUD, PMD) to the most permissive valid combination — _KERNPG_TABLE (Present + R/W + Accessed) — and relies on the leaf PTE to impose the actual restriction. The KPTI code in arch/x86/mm/pti.c illustrates this: when cloning kernel page tables into the user-visible copy, it consistently uses _KERNPG_TABLE for intermediate entries:

/* arch/x86/mm/pti.c — cloning intermediate page-table levels */
set_pgd(pgd, __pgd(_KERNPG_TABLE | __pa(new_p4d_page)));
set_p4d(p4d, __p4d(_KERNPG_TABLE | __pa(new_pud_page)));
set_pud(pud, __pud(_KERNPG_TABLE | __pa(new_pmd_page)));

In the original i386 design, the paging unit had no notion of execute permission — code could be executed from any readable page. This gap was closed by later hardware additions.

The NX / XD bit (Execute Disable)

Starting with the Pentium 4 (when PAE is enabled) and on all x86_64 processors, bit 63 of each page-table entry is the NX (No eXecute) bit (Intel calls it XD, Execute Disable). When set, the page cannot be executed — any instruction fetch from it triggers a page fault. The NX bit lives in bit 63 because the original 32-bit PTE had only 32 bits, and all lower bit positions were already assigned. PAE widened entries to 64 bits, making bit 63 available for the first time.

/* arch/x86/include/asm/pgtable_types.h */
#define _PAGE_BIT_NX        63
#define _PAGE_NX            (_AT(pteval_t, 1) << _PAGE_BIT_NX)

NX requires explicit enablement in the CPU's EFER (Extended Feature Enable Register) before the bit is honored. The kernel enables it very early in the boot sequence — before the first page tables are fully set up. On 64-bit kernels, arch/x86/kernel/head_64.S checks the CPUID feature flag and sets EFER.NXE:

/* arch/x86/kernel/head_64.S — early NX enablement */
movl    $0x80000001, %eax
cpuid
btl     $20, %edi               /* check NX feature bit */
jnc     1f
btsl    $_EFER_NX, %eax         /* enable EFER.NXE */
btsq    $_PAGE_BIT_NX, early_pmd_flags(%rip)   /* mark early pages NX */

After boot, the kernel function x86_configure_nx() records NX availability in __supported_pte_mask — a global mask that is ANDed with every PTE the kernel creates. If the CPU lacks NX, the mask clears bit 63 and all pages become executable by default:

/* arch/x86/kernel/setup.c */
void x86_configure_nx(void)
{
    if (boot_cpu_has(X86_FEATURE_NX))
        __supported_pte_mask |= _PAGE_NX;
    else
        __supported_pte_mask &= ~_PAGE_NX;
}

Linux sets NX on stack pages, heap pages, and data sections — essentially every page that is not part of a .text section. Referring back to the protection_map[] table above, notice that only entries with VM_EXEC clear the NX bit (by using PAGE_READONLY_EXEC or PAGE_SHARED_EXEC); all other entries include __NX. This is a critical defense against code injection attacks: even if an attacker can write shellcode into a data buffer, the CPU will refuse to execute it.

On ARM64, two separate execute-disable bits provide finer control:

/* arch/arm64/include/asm/pgtable-hwdef.h */
#define PTE_PXN     (_AT(pteval_t, 1) << 53)  /* Privileged eXecute Never */
#define PTE_UXN     (_AT(pteval_t, 1) << 54)  /* User eXecute Never */
  • PXN prevents execution at EL1 (kernel mode).
  • UXN prevents execution at EL0 (user mode).

The ARM64 page-protection templates show how these two bits are used independently. Kernel pages set both PXN and UXN by default (via PROT_NORMAL), making kernel data non-executable at every privilege level. Kernel code pages clear PXN but keep UXN, allowing execution only at EL1:

/* arch/arm64/include/asm/pgtable-prot.h */
#define PROT_NORMAL       (PROT_DEFAULT | PTE_PXN | PTE_UXN | PTE_WRITE | ...)
#define _PAGE_KERNEL      (PROT_NORMAL | PTE_DIRTY)         /* data: no exec */
#define _PAGE_KERNEL_EXEC ((PROT_NORMAL & ~PTE_PXN) | PTE_DIRTY) /* code: EL1 exec */
#define _PAGE_KERNEL_ROX  ((PROT_NORMAL & ~(PTE_WRITE|PTE_PXN)) | PTE_RDONLY | PTE_DIRTY)

This means ARM64 can mark a page as executable for the kernel but not for user space, or vice versa — a capability x86_64 achieves only indirectly through SMEP. User pages follow a similar pattern: _PAGE_SHARED_EXEC clears UXN but keeps PXN, so user code can execute but the kernel cannot run it.

Modern x86_64 protection extensions

The NX bit closed the most glaring gap — code injection into data pages — but the kernel itself remained a rich target. An attacker who could divert a kernel code pointer to a user-space page (a ret2user attack) could execute arbitrary code at ring 0. Even without code execution, a kernel bug that accidentally dereferenced a user-controlled pointer could leak or corrupt kernel data. Starting around 2011, Intel and AMD added a series of CR4 bits that harden the supervisor/user boundary far beyond what R/W + U/S alone could enforce:

FeatureCR4 bitPurpose
SMEP20Supervisor Mode Execution Prevention — prevents the kernel from executing code on user-mode pages (U/S = 1). Blocks ret2user attacks.
SMAP21Supervisor Mode Access Prevention — prevents the kernel from reading/writing user-mode pages unless explicitly allowed (via stac/clac). Blocks unintended kernel access to user data.
PKU—Protection Keys for Userspace — assigns a 4-bit "protection key" to each page. User-mode code can toggle read/write permissions per key via the PKRU register without modifying page tables.
UMIP11User-Mode Instruction Prevention — prevents user-mode code from executing SGDT, SIDT, SLDT, SMSW, and STR — instructions that leak kernel addresses.
CET23Control-flow Enforcement Technology — hardware shadow stacks and indirect-branch tracking to prevent ROP/JOP attacks.

The kernel enables each feature at boot time in arch/x86/kernel/cpu/common.c, guarded by a CPUID capability check:

/* arch/x86/kernel/cpu/common.c — boot-time SMEP/SMAP setup */
static __always_inline void setup_smep(struct cpuinfo_x86 *c)
{
    if (cpu_has(c, X86_FEATURE_SMEP))
        cr4_set_bits(X86_CR4_SMEP);
}

static __always_inline void setup_smap(struct cpuinfo_x86 *c)
{
    unsigned long eflags = native_save_fl();
    BUG_ON(eflags & X86_EFLAGS_AC);   /* AC must be clear */
    if (cpu_has(c, X86_FEATURE_SMAP))
        cr4_set_bits(X86_CR4_SMAP);
}

SMAP deserves special attention because the kernel must temporarily disable it to access user memory during system calls — copy_from_user(), copy_to_user(), and similar functions. The x86 architecture uses two single-byte instructions for this: stac (Set AC flag — temporarily allows supervisor access to user pages) and clac (Clear AC flag — re-enables SMAP protection). Every user-access function brackets the actual memory access between stac and clac, creating a narrow window where SMAP is disabled.

Protection Keys use a different mechanism entirely. Instead of a CR4 bit, PKU assigns a 4-bit key (stored in PTE bits 59–62) to each page, then lets user-space code control access via the PKRU register — a 32-bit register with two bits (access-disable, write-disable) per key, for 16 keys total. The WRPKRU instruction updates PKRU without a system call, making permission changes extremely fast:

/* arch/x86/include/asm/pgtable_types.h — protection key bits in PTE */
#define _PAGE_BIT_PKEY_BIT0    59
#define _PAGE_BIT_PKEY_BIT1    60
#define _PAGE_BIT_PKEY_BIT2    61
#define _PAGE_BIT_PKEY_BIT3    62

/* arch/x86/mm/pgprot.c — VMA pkey bits mapped to PTE */
if (vm_flags & VM_PKEY_BIT0) val |= _PAGE_PKEY_BIT0;
if (vm_flags & VM_PKEY_BIT1) val |= _PAGE_PKEY_BIT1;
if (vm_flags & VM_PKEY_BIT2) val |= _PAGE_PKEY_BIT2;
if (vm_flags & VM_PKEY_BIT3) val |= _PAGE_PKEY_BIT3;

CET (Control-flow Enforcement Technology) addresses a fundamentally different threat: code-reuse attacks like ROP (Return-Oriented Programming) and JOP (Jump-Oriented Programming). CET provides two mechanisms: a shadow stack that stores return addresses in a hardware-protected memory region (any mismatch between the shadow stack and the regular stack triggers a fault), and indirect branch tracking (IBT) that requires indirect jump/call targets to begin with an ENDBR64 instruction. The kernel sets up CET in setup_cet():

/* arch/x86/kernel/cpu/common.c — CET setup */
static __always_inline void setup_cet(struct cpuinfo_x86 *c)
{
    bool user_shstk, kernel_ibt;
    user_shstk = cpu_feature_enabled(X86_FEATURE_SHSTK) &&
                 IS_ENABLED(CONFIG_X86_USER_SHADOW_STACK);
    ...
    if (kernel_ibt)
        wrmsrq(MSR_IA32_S_CET, CET_ENDBR_EN);
    cr4_set_bits(X86_CR4_CET);
}

/* arch/x86/kernel/shstk.c — enabling user shadow stack */
wrmsrq(MSR_IA32_PL3_SSP, addr + size);   /* set shadow stack pointer */
wrmsrq(MSR_IA32_U_CET, CET_SHSTK_EN);   /* enable shadow stack */

ARM64 equivalents

ARM64 provides analogous protections through a combination of page-table descriptor bits and system register controls. The design philosophy differs from x86: where x86 added each feature as a new CR4 bit with a global on/off switch, ARM64 often integrates protection directly into the descriptor format (PXN, UXN) or the processor state (PSTATE.PAN), giving per-page or per-exception granularity from the start.

FeatureARM64 mechanismEquivalent to
PXNPTE bit 53SMEP (per-page granularity)
PANPSTATE.PANSMAP
WXNSCTLR_EL1.WXNW^X enforcement
BTISCTLR_EL1.BT0/BT1 + GP bitCET indirect-branch tracking
MTETag bits in pointers + TCR_EL1.TBIBounds checking / use-after-free detection

PAN (Privileged Access Never) is ARM64's answer to SMAP. When PSTATE.PAN is set, any kernel attempt to load from or store to a user page (one with the EL0 access flag set) triggers a permission fault. The kernel must explicitly clear PAN before accessing user memory, then re-enable it immediately after. In Linux, this is managed through uaccess_enable_privileged() and uaccess_disable_privileged():

/* arch/arm64/include/asm/uaccess.h — PAN gating for user access */
static inline void __uaccess_disable_hw_pan(void)
{
    asm(ALTERNATIVE("nop", SET_PSTATE_PAN(0), ARM64_HAS_PAN));
}

static inline void __uaccess_enable_hw_pan(void)
{
    asm(ALTERNATIVE("nop", SET_PSTATE_PAN(1), ARM64_HAS_PAN));
}

static inline void uaccess_enable_privileged(void)
{
    mte_enable_tco();
    if (uaccess_ttbr0_enable())
        return;
    __uaccess_disable_hw_pan();   /* clear PAN → allow user access */
}

Notice that uaccess_enable_privileged() also touches MTE (Memory Tagging Extension) state via mte_enable_tco() — disabling tag checking during user copies to avoid false positives from tag mismatches. This illustrates how modern protection features interact: enabling access for one subsystem requires coordinating with others.

Kernel Page Table Isolation (KPTI)

The most dramatic protection addition in recent Linux history is KPTI (Kernel Page Table Isolation), developed as a mitigation for the Meltdown vulnerability (CVE-2017-5754). Meltdown allowed user-space code on affected Intel CPUs to read kernel memory by exploiting speculative execution — even though the page-table U/S bit correctly marked kernel pages as supervisor-only, the CPU's speculative execution window could leak the data before the permission check took effect.

KPTI's solution is radical: maintain two separate sets of page tables for each process. The kernel page tables map everything (kernel + user space), while the user page tables map only user space plus a minimal kernel "trampoline" needed to handle interrupts and system calls. On every transition from user mode to kernel mode, the CPU switches from the user page tables to the kernel page tables (by reloading CR3), and switches back on return. This ensures that kernel memory is simply not mapped — and therefore not speculatively accessible — while user code runs.

/* arch/x86/mm/pti.c — cloning PGD entries for user page table */
pgd_t __pti_set_user_pgtbl(pgd_t *pgdp, pgd_t pgd)
{
    ...
    /* Store into the user-visible half of the PGD array */
    kernel_to_user_pgdp(pgdp)->pgd = pgd.pgd;

    /* Force NX on kernel entries visible to user page tables */
    if ((pgd.pgd & (_PAGE_USER|_PAGE_PRESENT)) ==
        (_PAGE_USER|_PAGE_PRESENT) &&
        (__supported_pte_mask & _PAGE_NX))
        pgd.pgd |= _PAGE_NX;
    return pgd;
}

The implementation doubles the PGD: the kernel PGD occupies the first half of an 8 KB allocation, and the user PGD occupies the second half. kernel_to_user_pgdp() simply adds an offset to reach the user copy. On each kernel entry, the trampoline code in arch/x86/entry/entry_64.S switches CR3 to the kernel PGD; on return, it switches back. The PCID (Process-Context Identifier) feature avoids flushing the TLB on these switches by assigning different PCIDs to the kernel and user page-table sets.

On ARM64, Meltdown mitigation is architecturally unnecessary for most implementations because the ARM architecture's speculative execution model does not permit the same data leakage. ARM64 naturally uses separate page tables for kernel (TTBR1_EL1) and user (TTBR0_EL1), providing KPTI-like isolation by design. However, some Cortex-A75 variants were affected, and the kernel includes an optional CONFIG_UNMAP_KERNEL_AT_EL0 mitigation that unmaps the kernel from TTBR1_EL1 when running at EL0.

Summary: protection evolution

The trend is clear: each generation adds another layer of hardware enforcement. Linux 7.0.12 enables all available protections by default when the CPU supports them, making the kernel far more resistant to exploitation than the 2.6-era kernel could ever be. Classic 32-bit x86 paging had only R/W and U/S; today's kernel uses eight distinct protection mechanisms on x86_64 and six on ARM64, all orchestrated through the same page-table entries and a handful of system registers. Understanding this protection stack is essential for anyone working on kernel security, driver development, or vulnerability analysis.

From Two Levels to Four (and Five)

The classic two-level scheme scales to 4 GB (32-bit address space). When AMD64/x86_64 extended the virtual address space to 48 bits and beyond, the same principle was extended by adding more levels. Each additional level multiplies the addressable range by the number of entries per table (512 for 64-bit PTEs in a 4 KB page), at the cost of one more memory access per TLB miss. The table below summarises every x86 and ARM64 paging mode:

Architecture eraLevelsVA bitsPage sizes
i386 (classic)2324 KB, 4 MB
i386 + PAE3364 KB, 2 MB
x86_64 (standard)4484 KB, 2 MB, 1 GB
x86_64 + LA575574 KB, 2 MB, 1 GB
ARM64 (4 KB pages)3 or 439 or 484 KB, 2 MB, 1 GB

The fundamental algorithm is unchanged: each level uses a subset of the virtual address bits as an index into a table of entries, and each entry either points to the next-level table or (at a leaf) to the final page frame. Adding more levels simply adds more index fields and extends the addressable range.

The software abstraction: a five-level model for all architectures

Supporting all these modes with a single codebase poses a software challenge: the vast majority of the VM code (page fault handling, fork(), munmap(), page reclaim) needs to walk page tables without knowing — or caring — how many levels are in use. Linux solves this with a five-level software model that always defines five page-table types, even on architectures that use fewer:

pgd_t  →  p4d_t  →  pud_t  →  pmd_t  →  pte_t
 (PGD)    (P4D)    (PUD)    (PMD)    (PTE)

On x86_64 with standard 4-level paging, the P4D level does nothing. On ARM64 with VA_BITS=39 (3 levels), both P4D and PUD do nothing. On a 32-bit kernel without PAE (2 levels), P4D, PUD, and PMD all do nothing. The trick is in folding: unused levels are defined as single-entry wrappers around the next level down, so the compiler optimizes them away entirely.

The folding mechanism uses three header files that define "no-op" levels:

/* include/asm-generic/pgtable-nop4d.h — fold P4D into PGD */
#define __PAGETABLE_P4D_FOLDED 1
typedef struct { pgd_t pgd; } p4d_t;
#define P4D_SHIFT       PGDIR_SHIFT
#define PTRS_PER_P4D    1

static inline p4d_t *p4d_offset(pgd_t *pgd, unsigned long address)
{
    return (p4d_t *)pgd;    /* P4D IS the PGD — no indirection */
}

/* include/asm-generic/pgtable-nopud.h — fold PUD into P4D */
#define __PAGETABLE_PUD_FOLDED 1
typedef struct { p4d_t p4d; } pud_t;
#define PUD_SHIFT       P4D_SHIFT
#define PTRS_PER_PUD    1

/* include/asm-generic/pgtable-nopmd.h — fold PMD into PUD */
#define __PAGETABLE_PMD_FOLDED 1
typedef struct { pud_t pud; } pmd_t;
#define PMD_SHIFT       PUD_SHIFT
#define PTRS_PER_PMD    1

When a level is folded, its PTRS_PER_* is 1 and its offset function simply casts the pointer — no memory is allocated, no table is walked. The "walk" through that level compiles to zero instructions. A consistency check in include/linux/pgtable.h verifies that CONFIG_PGTABLE_LEVELS matches the number of non-folded levels:

/* include/linux/pgtable.h — compile-time consistency check */
#if 5 - defined(__PAGETABLE_P4D_FOLDED) - defined(__PAGETABLE_PUD_FOLDED) - \
    defined(__PAGETABLE_PMD_FOLDED) != CONFIG_PGTABLE_LEVELS
#error CONFIG_PGTABLE_LEVELS is not consistent with __PAGETABLE_{P4D,PUD,PMD}_FOLDED
#endif

How CONFIG_PGTABLE_LEVELS is set

Each architecture's Kconfig determines the number of active page-table levels based on address-space size and page size. On x86, the choice is straightforward:

/* arch/x86/Kconfig */
config PGTABLE_LEVELS
    int
    default 5 if X86_64    /* always 5 in software; 4 or 5 at runtime */
    default 3 if X86_PAE
    default 2

Notice that x86_64 always sets CONFIG_PGTABLE_LEVELS=5 — even on CPUs that only support 4-level paging. This is because modern x86_64 kernels are compiled once and must run on both 4-level and 5-level (LA57) hardware. The P4D level is present in the software model either way; whether the hardware actually walks it is decided at runtime via pgtable_l5_enabled():

/* arch/x86/include/asm/pgtable_64_types.h */
extern unsigned int __pgtable_l5_enabled;

/* After boot, resolved to a CPU feature check: */
#define pgtable_l5_enabled()  cpu_feature_enabled(X86_FEATURE_LA57)

/* arch/x86/include/asm/pgtable.h — runtime P4D folding */
static inline p4d_t *p4d_offset(pgd_t *pgd, unsigned long address)
{
    if (!pgtable_l5_enabled())
        return (p4d_t *)pgd;      /* runtime fold: P4D = PGD */
    return (p4d_t *)pgd_page_vaddr(*pgd) + p4d_index(address);
}

This p4d_offset() is the heart of x86_64's runtime folding: on 4-level hardware, the P4D level is skipped with a simple cast (the if branch is resolved by static branch patching after boot, so it costs zero cycles). On 5-level hardware, the P4D is a real table with 512 entries.

ARM64's configuration is more varied because it supports three page sizes (4 KB, 16 KB, 64 KB) and multiple VA widths:

/* arch/arm64/Kconfig — levels depend on page size × VA width */
config PGTABLE_LEVELS
    int
    default 2 if ARM64_16K_PAGES && ARM64_VA_BITS_36
    default 2 if ARM64_64K_PAGES && ARM64_VA_BITS_42
    default 3 if ARM64_64K_PAGES && (ARM64_VA_BITS_48 || ARM64_VA_BITS_52)
    default 3 if ARM64_4K_PAGES  && ARM64_VA_BITS_39
    default 3 if ARM64_16K_PAGES && ARM64_VA_BITS_47
    default 4 if !ARM64_64K_PAGES && ARM64_VA_BITS_48
    default 5 if ARM64_4K_PAGES  && ARM64_VA_BITS_52

The ARM64 level count is computed from a formula: levels = (VA_BITS - page_order - 1) / table_shift. For the common case of 4 KB pages with VA_BITS=48: (48 - 12 - 1) / 9 = 3.88 → 4 levels. With VA_BITS=39: (39 - 12 - 1) / 9 = 2.88 → 3 levels. The kernel codifies this in a macro:

/* arch/arm64/include/asm/pgtable-hwdef.h */
#define ARM64_HW_PGTABLE_LEVELS(va_bits) \
    (((va_bits) - PTDESC_ORDER - 1) / PTDESC_TABLE_SHIFT)

Unlike x86_64, ARM64 typically decides the level count at compile time — a kernel built for 48-bit VA always uses 4 levels, a kernel for 39-bit VA always uses 3. The unused levels are folded away using the same pgtable-nop4d.h / pgtable-nopud.h headers.

The x86_64 shift and entry constants

With all five levels potentially active, x86_64 defines a set of shift constants that carve a virtual address into its component indices. Each 4 KB page holds 512 entries (2⁹), so every level consumes exactly 9 bits of address:

/* arch/x86/include/asm/pgtable_64_types.h — 4/5-level constants */
#define PGDIR_SHIFT     pgdir_shift   /* 48 (4-level) or 48 (5-level: see below) */
#define PTRS_PER_PGD    512

#define P4D_SHIFT       39
#define MAX_PTRS_PER_P4D 512
#define PTRS_PER_P4D    ptrs_per_p4d  /* 1 (4-level) or 512 (5-level) */

#define PUD_SHIFT       30
#define PTRS_PER_PUD    512

#define PMD_SHIFT       21
#define PTRS_PER_PMD    512

#define PTRS_PER_PTE    512

On 4-level hardware, PGDIR_SHIFT is 39 and PTRS_PER_P4D is 1 (the P4D level is folded). On 5-level LA57 hardware, PGDIR_SHIFT is 48 and PTRS_PER_P4D is 512, adding 9 more bits of virtual address space (2⁹ × 2³⁹ = 2⁴⁸ → the PGD covers a full 256 TB, and the P4D extends this to 128 PB).

The generic page-table walk

The beauty of the folding model is that every page-table walk uses the same code path, regardless of how many levels are active. The generic walker in mm/pagewalk.c iterates through all five levels using identical loop structures at each level:

/* mm/pagewalk.c — walk_pgd_range() (simplified) */
pgd = pgd_offset(walk->mm, addr);
do {
    next = pgd_addr_end(addr, end);
    if (pgd_none_or_clear_bad(pgd))
        ...
    err = walk_p4d_range(pgd, addr, next, walk);
} while (pgd++, addr = next, addr != end);

/* walk_p4d_range() */
p4d = p4d_offset(pgd, addr);
do {
    next = p4d_addr_end(addr, end);
    if (p4d_none_or_clear_bad(p4d))
        ...
    err = walk_pud_range(p4d, addr, next, walk);
} while (p4d++, addr = next, addr != end);

/* walk_pud_range() */
pud = pud_offset(p4d, addr);
do {
    next = pud_addr_end(addr, end);
    if (pud_none_or_clear_bad(pud))
        ...
    err = walk_pmd_range(pud, addr, next, walk);
} while (pud++, addr = next, addr != end);

Each level follows the same pattern: compute the offset into the current table, iterate with *_addr_end() to step through the address range, check for empty or invalid entries with *_none_or_clear_bad(), then recurse into the next level. On a 4-level x86_64 system, the P4D loop executes exactly once (because PTRS_PER_P4D = 1 and p4d_offset() returns the PGD pointer cast to p4d_t *). The compiler may even eliminate the loop entirely. On a 2-level 32-bit system, P4D, PUD, and PMD loops all collapse — the walk goes directly from PGD to PTE.

This pattern — code that is written for 5 levels but transparently works for 2, 3, 4, or 5 — is one of Linux's most elegant abstractions. It allows a single mm/memory.c to handle page faults on everything from a 32-bit embedded processor to a 5-level x86_64 server, without a single #ifdef in the walk loop.

The following sections describe the specific multi-level implementations used on x86_64 and ARM64 in Linux 7.0.12.

The Physical Address Extension (PAE) Paging Mechanism

Before diving into x86_64 paging, it is worth understanding PAE — the transitional mechanism that bridged 32-bit paging to the 64-bit page-table format used today. PAE was introduced with the Pentium Pro (1995) to allow 32-bit processors to address more than 4 GB of physical RAM (up to 64 GB with 36-bit physical addresses). By the late 1990s, high-end servers were hitting the 4 GB physical memory ceiling, but migrating to a 64-bit architecture would take years. PAE solved the immediate problem without changing the 32-bit virtual address space: programs still saw 4 GB of virtual memory, but the kernel could map different portions of a larger physical memory into that space.

PAE's lasting significance, however, is not the 36-bit physical address — it is the 64-bit page-table entry format it introduced. This format — with 512 entries per table, 9 index bits per level, and the NX bit at position 63 — was carried over directly to x86_64. Understanding PAE is therefore understanding the entry format that runs on every modern x86 processor.

PAE is activated by setting the PAE flag in the cr4 control register:

/* arch/x86/include/uapi/asm/processor-flags.h */
#define X86_CR4_PAE_BIT     5  /* enable physical address extensions */
#define X86_CR4_PAE         _BITUL(X86_CR4_PAE_BIT)

PAE changed the paging mechanism in three important ways:

  1. Page-table entries widened from 32 to 64 bits. The physical address field expanded from 20 to 24 bits (supporting 36-bit physical addresses), and the extra bits also made room for the NX flag at bit 63. Because entries doubled in size, each 4 KB page now holds 512 entries instead of 1024.
  2. A third level was introduced. A new Page Directory Pointer Table (PDPT) sits above the Page Directory. It contains just 4 entries (selected by bits 31:30 of the linear address), each pointing to a full Page Directory.
  3. Large pages became 2 MB (not 4 MB). With 9-bit Table and 21-bit Offset fields instead of the old 10+12 split, the PSE page size is 2²¹ = 2 MB.

The kernel's PAE type definitions in arch/x86/include/asm/pgtable-3level_types.h reveal the format. Note the union trick — each entry can be accessed as a single 64-bit value or as two 32-bit halves (essential for atomic updates on 32-bit CPUs that cannot write 64 bits atomically):

/* arch/x86/include/asm/pgtable-3level_types.h */
typedef u64 pteval_t;
typedef u64 pmdval_t;
typedef u64 pudval_t;

typedef union {
    struct { unsigned long pte_low, pte_high; };
    pteval_t pte;
} pte_t;

#define PGDIR_SHIFT     30
#define PTRS_PER_PGD    4       /* only 4 PDPT entries! */
#define PMD_SHIFT       21
#define PTRS_PER_PMD    512
#define PTRS_PER_PTE    512

The 32-bit kernel selects these types at compile time based on CONFIG_X86_PAE:

/* arch/x86/include/asm/pgtable_32.h */
#ifdef CONFIG_X86_PAE
# include <asm/pgtable-3level.h>   /* 3-level PAE mode */
#else
# include <asm/pgtable-2level.h>   /* classic 2-level mode */
#endif

Why PAE matters for x86_64

PAE is not just a historical curiosity — it is the direct ancestor of x86_64 paging:

PropertyPAE (32-bit)x86_64 (4-level)
Entry size64 bits64 bits
Entries per table512512
Index bits per level99
Offset bits1212
Top-level entries4 (PDPT)512 (PGD/PML4)
VA bits3248
PA bits3652

The x86_64 paging format is essentially PAE extended to more levels with a full-width top-level table. The 64-bit entry format, 512-entry tables, and 9-bit index fields carried over directly. This is why x86_64 long mode requires PAE to be enabled in cr4 — the underlying page-table entry format is the PAE format. You can see this dependency in the 64-bit boot code, which sets CR4.PAE as one of the first steps before enabling long mode:

/* arch/x86/kernel/head_64.S — CR4 setup for long mode */
movl    $(X86_CR4_PAE | X86_CR4_LA57), %edx
movq    %cr4, %rcx
andl    %edx, %ecx
btsl    $X86_CR4_PSE_BIT, %ecx    /* also enable PSE for large pages */
movq    %rcx, %cr4                /* PAE must be set before paging on */

Without CR4.PAE, the processor cannot enter long mode — the EFER.LME bit has no effect and 64-bit paging simply does not exist. In a very real sense, every x86_64 system is running PAE paging, extended to four or five levels.

The PDPT: a special top-level table

The PDPT is the most unusual page-table level in the x86 family. Unlike every other level (which occupies a full 4 KB page with 512 entries), the PAE PDPT has only 4 entries and is just 32 bytes. The hardware imposes unique constraints on it:

  • The PDPT must be 32-byte aligned (not page-aligned like other tables) — CR3 bits [11:5] encode the PDPT address, while bits [4:0] are ignored.
  • On 32-bit PAE systems, the PDPT must reside in the first 4 GB of physical memory — CR3 is still only 32 bits wide.
  • Any modification to a PDPT entry requires a full CR3 reload to take effect — the CPU caches the 4 PDPT entries internally and does not re-read them on each translation.

This last constraint has a real impact on the kernel's TLB management code:

/* arch/x86/mm/pgtable.c — PAE PDPT flush requirement */
/*
 * NOTE! For PAE, any changes to the top page-directory-pointer-table
 * entries need a full cr3 reload to flush.
 */
#ifdef CONFIG_X86_PAE
    tlb->need_flush_all = 1;
#endif

These quirks are PAE-specific — on x86_64, the top-level PGD (PML4) is a normal 4 KB page with 512 entries and no special alignment or caching constraints. This is one of the ways AMD cleaned up the architecture when designing x86_64.

PAE and the NX bit

PAE was also the mechanism that first enabled the NX bit (bit 63 of each page-table entry). Before PAE, entries were only 32 bits wide and had no room for an execute-disable flag. The kernel source makes this dependency explicit with a conditional compilation guard:

/* arch/x86/include/asm/pgtable_types.h — NX requires 64-bit entries */
#if defined(CONFIG_X86_64) || defined(CONFIG_X86_PAE)
#define _PAGE_NX    (_AT(pteval_t, 1) << _PAGE_BIT_NX)
#else
#define _PAGE_NX    (_AT(pteval_t, 0))   /* NX not available */
#endif

Without PAE (i.e., classic 32-bit two-level paging), _PAGE_NX is defined as zero — the kernel simply cannot set an NX bit because the entry isn't wide enough. This is why NX requires PAE on 32-bit systems: the wider entry format was a prerequisite for per-page execute protection.

PAE in Linux 7.0.12

On modern 64-bit Linux, PAE is always enabled (it is architecturally required for long mode). The CR4_PAE flag is set very early during boot and never cleared. The 32-bit PAE code paths still exist in the kernel for CONFIG_X86_32 builds — the Kconfig option is:

/* arch/x86/Kconfig */
config X86_PAE
    bool "PAE (Physical Address Extension)"
    depends on X86_32
    select X86_HAVE_PAE

When building a 32-bit kernel with CONFIG_X86_PAE=y, the kernel uses the 3-level types from pgtable-3level_types.h with PTRS_PER_PGD=4. The boot code in arch/x86/kernel/head_32.S builds the initial page tables with PAE-specific alignment and structure — including the initial_pg_pmd array (the early Page Directories) aligned to PGD_ALIGN:

/* arch/x86/kernel/head_32.S — PAE boot page tables */
__PAGE_ALIGNED_BSS
    .align PGD_ALIGN
#ifdef CONFIG_X86_PAE
.globl initial_pg_pmd
initial_pg_pmd:
    .fill 1024*KPMDS,4,0     /* KPMDS Page Directories (PMDs) */
#else
.globl initial_page_table
initial_page_table:
    .fill 1024,4,0            /* classic single Page Directory */
#endif

    .align PGD_ALIGN
.globl swapper_pg_dir
swapper_pg_dir:
    .fill 1024,4,0

A 64-bit kernel never uses the 3-level PAE walk — it uses the 4-level (or 5-level) walk described in the next section. But the entry format remains the same: 64-bit entries with the physical address in the upper bits, flags in the lower 12 bits, and NX at bit 63. PAE's legacy lives on in every page-table entry on every x86_64 system.

x86_64 Paging

With the transition from 32-bit to 64-bit, x86_64 adopted a deeper page-table hierarchy that extends the PAE entry format to four (and optionally five) levels. The same 64-bit entries, the same 512-entry tables, and the same 9-bit index fields — but now covering a vast 48-bit or 57-bit virtual address space. This section covers the x86_64 paging hardware in detail: the walk structure, address decomposition, canonical address enforcement, the kernel's virtual memory layout, and the complete PTE flag set used in Linux 7.0.12.

The most important difference from 32-bit paging is the canonical address split. The 64-bit architecture defines 2⁶⁴ possible virtual addresses but only implements 48 or 57 bits of them. The unused upper bits must be a sign-extension of the highest implemented bit — creating a "canonical" range. This splits the address space into a low half (user space, starting at 0x0000000000000000) and a high half (kernel space, starting at 0xFFFF800000000000 for 4-level), with a massive non-canonical "hole" in between that catches stray pointer arithmetic:

/* arch/x86/include/asm/page_64_types.h — canonical address helpers */
static __always_inline u64 __canonical_address(u64 vaddr, u8 vaddr_bits)
{
    return ((s64)vaddr << (64 - vaddr_bits)) >> (64 - vaddr_bits);
}

static __always_inline u64 __is_canonical_address(u64 vaddr, u8 vaddr_bits)
{
    return __canonical_address(vaddr, vaddr_bits) == vaddr;
}

#define __VIRTUAL_MASK_SHIFT  (pgtable_l5_enabled() ? 56 : 47)

4-Level Paging (Standard)

The standard x86_64 configuration uses four levels of translation. Intel literature names the top level PML4, but Linux uses its architecture-neutral software names:

  • PGD (Page Global Directory — maps to PML4)
  • P4D (folded away in 4-level hardware mode)
  • PUD (Page Upper Directory — maps to PDPT)
  • PMD (Page Middle Directory — maps to PD)
  • PTE (Page Table Entry — maps to PT)

For the hardware walk on a four-level system, the path is effectively:

CR3 → PGD/PML4 → PUD/PDPT → PMD/PD → PTE/PT → page

The virtual-address split for a 48-bit canonical address is:

  • bits 47:39 — PGD index
  • bits 38:30 — PUD index
  • bits 29:21 — PMD index
  • bits 20:12 — PTE index
  • bits 11:0 — page offset

Each level contributes 9 index bits, so each page-table page holds 512 entries. With 4 KB pages, that is exactly one 4 KB page per table:

512 entries × 8 bytes = 4096 bytes

arch/x86/include/asm/pgtable_64_types.h encodes this geometry directly:

#define P4D_SHIFT       39
#define MAX_PTRS_PER_P4D    512
#define PUD_SHIFT   30
#define PTRS_PER_PUD    512
#define PMD_SHIFT   21
#define PTRS_PER_PMD    512
#define PTRS_PER_PTE    512

A few consequences follow immediately. A single entry maps:

  • 4 KB at the PTE level
  • 2 MB at the PMD level if _PAGE_PSE is used
  • 1 GB at the PUD level if _PAGE_PSE is used there

Linux calls the larger mappings “huge” or “large” pages depending on context, but the core hardware idea is just leaf termination at a higher level. The _PAGE_PSE (Page Size Extension) bit in a PMD or PUD entry tells the hardware "stop walking here — this entry directly maps the physical page." The kernel provides helper functions to create these large mappings:

/* arch/x86/mm/pgtable.c — setting 2 MB and 1 GB huge pages */
int pmd_set_huge(pmd_t *pmd, phys_addr_t addr, pgprot_t prot)
{
    ...
    set_pte((pte_t *)pmd, pfn_pte(
        (u64)addr >> PAGE_SHIFT,
        __pgprot(protval_4k_2_large(pgprot_val(prot)) | _PAGE_PSE)));
    return 1;
}

int pud_set_huge(pud_t *pud, phys_addr_t addr, pgprot_t prot)
{
    ...
    set_pte((pte_t *)pud, pfn_pte(
        (u64)addr >> PAGE_SHIFT,
        __pgprot(protval_4k_2_large(pgprot_val(prot)) | _PAGE_PSE)));
    return 1;
}

Both functions check MTRR uniformity first (the memory type must be consistent across the entire large page) and then set the _PAGE_PSE bit. 2 MB pages are heavily used in the kernel's direct map — the linear mapping of all physical memory that allows the kernel to access any physical address via a simple offset. The direct map base address depends on the number of paging levels:

/* arch/x86/include/asm/page_64_types.h — direct map base */
#define __PAGE_OFFSET_BASE_L4  _AC(0xffff888000000000, UL)   /* 4-level */
#define __PAGE_OFFSET_BASE_L5  _AC(0xff11000000000000, UL)   /* 5-level */
#define __PAGE_OFFSET          page_offset_base

/* Converting between physical and virtual addresses */
#define __pa(x)   __phys_addr((unsigned long)(x))
#define __va(x)   ((void *)((unsigned long)(x) + PAGE_OFFSET))

The __va() and __pa() macros are the most frequently used address conversion functions in the kernel — they simply add or subtract PAGE_OFFSET to convert between physical addresses and the direct-map virtual addresses.

The walk is cached at multiple levels. Modern x86 processors keep paging-structure caches as well as ordinary TLBs, which is one reason Linux must invalidate carefully when changing upper-level entries. In addition to TLBs, the processor caches intermediate entries (PGD→PUD, PUD→PMD translations), so changing a PUD entry may require invalidating cached entries for all PMDs and PTEs underneath it.

The walk begins at the physical address stored in CR3. On modern CPUs with PCID (Process-Context Identifier) support, CR3 also carries a 12-bit PCID value in its low bits. Different PCIDs tag TLB entries so that a context switch can load a new CR3 without flushing TLB entries belonging to other processes:

/* arch/x86/mm/tlb.c — building CR3 with PCID */
static inline unsigned long build_cr3(pgd_t *pgd, u16 asid, unsigned long lam)
{
    unsigned long cr3 = __sme_pa(pgd) | lam;

    if (static_cpu_has(X86_FEATURE_PCID))
        cr3 |= kern_pcid(asid);

    return cr3;
}

With KPTI enabled, each process gets two PCID values — one for the kernel page tables and one for the user page tables — doubling the effective PCID usage. The kernel assigns kern_pcid(asid) for kernel mode and user_pcid(asid) for user mode, allowing the TLB to retain entries from both address spaces simultaneously.

Worked address decomposition

Consider a concrete example: the virtual address 0x00007f4a_3b6c1a80 — a typical user-space address in the lower canonical half. Stripping the sign-extension bits (63:48 are all zero), the 48-bit value 0x7f4a3b6c1a80 decomposes as:

Virtual address: 0x00007f4a3b6c1a80
Binary:          0111 1111 0100 1010 0011 1011 0110 1100 0001 1010 1000 0000

[47:39]  [38:30]  [29:21]  [20:12]  [11:0]
  254      297      475      193     0xa80

Field decomposition:
  PGD index  = VA[47:39] = 0xFE  = 254  → selects PGD entry 254 (of 512)
  PUD index  = VA[38:30] = 0x129 = 297  → selects PUD entry 297
  PMD index  = VA[29:21] = 0x1DB = 475  → selects PMD entry 475
  PTE index  = VA[20:12] = 0x0C1 = 193  → selects PTE entry 193
  Page offset = VA[11:0]  = 0xA80       → byte 2688 within the 4 KB page

The MMU walks the tree: CR3 points to the PGD; entry 254 of the PGD yields the physical address of a PUD page; entry 297 of that PUD yields a PMD page; entry 475 of that PMD yields a PTE page; and entry 193 of the PTE contains the physical page frame number. The final physical address is the page frame base OR'd with offset 0xA80.

If the PMD entry had its _PAGE_PSE bit set, the walk would terminate there and bits [20:0] (1 048 576 + 0xa80 = a 2 MB offset) would index directly into a huge page. This is exactly how Linux maps its direct-map region via 2 MB pages for performance.

5-Level Paging (LA57)

When CONFIG_X86_5LEVEL is enabled and the processor advertises X86_FEATURE_LA57, x86_64 adds a real hardware P4D/PML5 level. Now the path is:

CR3 → P4D/PML5 → PGD/PML4 → PUD/PDPT → PMD/PD → PTE/PT → page

Linux exposes the distinction through pgtable_l5_enabled() in arch/x86/include/asm/pgtable_64_types.h:

extern unsigned int __pgtable_l5_enabled;

static inline bool pgtable_l5_enabled(void)
{
    return __pgtable_l5_enabled;
}

or, in normal non-early code, through the feature test:

#define pgtable_l5_enabled() cpu_feature_enabled(X86_FEATURE_LA57)

The same header also shows the runtime-controlled top-level geometry:

extern unsigned int pgdir_shift;
extern unsigned int ptrs_per_p4d;
#define PGDIR_SHIFT pgdir_shift
#define PTRS_PER_P4D    ptrs_per_p4d

The important values are:

  • P4D_SHIFT = 39
  • PGDIR_SHIFT = 39 on four-level systems
  • PGDIR_SHIFT = 48 on five-level systems

So the top index becomes VA[56:48] on a 57-bit configuration. User space expands dramatically—from roughly 128 TB in the low half to roughly 64 PB. The kernel regions move downward accordingly, as Documentation/arch/x86/x86_64/mm.rst illustrates in its separate 4-level and 5-level layout tables.

The decision to use 4 or 5 levels is made very early in the boot sequence — even before the decompressor finishes, in arch/x86/boot/compressed/pgtable_64.c. The code checks the no5lvl command-line option and the CPUID LA57 feature bit (leaf 7, ECX bit 16), then sets the global variables that control the rest of the kernel:

/* arch/x86/boot/compressed/pgtable_64.c — early 5-level detection */
if (!cmdline_find_option_bool("no5lvl") &&
    native_cpuid_eax(0) >= 7 &&
    (native_cpuid_ecx(7) & BIT(16))) {
    l5_required = true;
    __pgtable_l5_enabled = 1;
    pgdir_shift = 48;
    ptrs_per_p4d = 512;
}

Once set, __pgtable_l5_enabled, pgdir_shift, and ptrs_per_p4d are never changed again. All subsequent code — including the P4D folding in p4d_offset() — relies on these variables (via static branch patching) to handle both 4-level and 5-level hardware from a single kernel binary.

Why Linux kept a five-level software model even on four-level hardware

As described in the "From Two Levels to Four (and Five)" section above, Linux always defines five page-table types (PGD → P4D → PUD → PMD → PTE) and folds unused levels into zero-cost no-ops. On 4-level x86_64 hardware, the P4D level is folded: p4d_offset() returns the PGD pointer cast to p4d_t*, costing nothing. This design means that generic MM code — page fault handlers, copy_page_range(), walk_page_range(), and hundreds of other functions — never needs #ifdef guards for the number of levels. For x86_64 specifically, this also made adding 5-level (LA57) support far less invasive: the P4D type was already present everywhere; all that changed was making it real on hardware that supports it.

Page Table Entry Flags (x86_64)

The main x86 page-bit definitions are in arch/x86/include/asm/pgtable_types.h. The file first gives symbolic bit positions:

#define _PAGE_BIT_PRESENT   0
#define _PAGE_BIT_RW        1
#define _PAGE_BIT_USER      2
#define _PAGE_BIT_PWT       3
#define _PAGE_BIT_PCD       4
#define _PAGE_BIT_ACCESSED  5
#define _PAGE_BIT_DIRTY     6
#define _PAGE_BIT_PSE       7
#define _PAGE_BIT_PAT       7
#define _PAGE_BIT_GLOBAL    8
#define _PAGE_BIT_PAT_LARGE 12
#define _PAGE_BIT_NX        63

It then maps them to masks:

#define _PAGE_PRESENT   (_AT(pteval_t, 1) << _PAGE_BIT_PRESENT)
#define _PAGE_RW    (_AT(pteval_t, 1) << _PAGE_BIT_RW)
#define _PAGE_USER  (_AT(pteval_t, 1) << _PAGE_BIT_USER)
#define _PAGE_PWT   (_AT(pteval_t, 1) << _PAGE_BIT_PWT)
#define _PAGE_PCD   (_AT(pteval_t, 1) << _PAGE_BIT_PCD)
#define _PAGE_ACCESSED  (_AT(pteval_t, 1) << _PAGE_BIT_ACCESSED)
#define _PAGE_DIRTY (_AT(pteval_t, 1) << _PAGE_BIT_DIRTY)
#define _PAGE_PSE   (_AT(pteval_t, 1) << _PAGE_BIT_PSE)
#define _PAGE_GLOBAL    (_AT(pteval_t, 1) << _PAGE_BIT_GLOBAL)
#define _PAGE_PAT   (_AT(pteval_t, 1) << _PAGE_BIT_PAT)
#define _PAGE_PAT_LARGE (_AT(pteval_t, 1) << _PAGE_BIT_PAT_LARGE)
#define _PAGE_NX    (_AT(pteval_t, 1) << _PAGE_BIT_NX)

The most important architectural meanings are:

  • _PAGE_PRESENT — the translation is valid.
  • _PAGE_RW — writes are permitted.
  • _PAGE_USER — user mode may access the mapping.
  • _PAGE_PWT and _PAGE_PCD — write-through and cache-disable controls.
  • _PAGE_ACCESSED — set by hardware on access.
  • _PAGE_DIRTY — set by hardware on write.
  • _PAGE_PSE — indicates a large/huge leaf at PMD or PUD level, and shares bit 7 with _PAGE_PAT at the PTE level.
  • _PAGE_GLOBAL — TLB entry survives CR3 reloads (used for kernel mappings to avoid unnecessary TLB flushes on context switch). However, when KPTI is enabled, kernel pages mapped in the user page tables must not be global — otherwise they would persist across the CR3 switch and defeat the isolation. The kernel disables _PAGE_GLOBAL on KPTI-affected entries.
  • _PAGE_NX — execution disabled when NX is supported.

Linux overlays software-defined bits on top of spare positions as well. The x86_64 PTE has several bit positions that the hardware ignores (bits 9–11, 58, 62 for non-present entries), and Linux uses them to carry kernel-private state directly in the page-table entry:

/* arch/x86/include/asm/pgtable_types.h — software-defined PTE bits */
#define _PAGE_BIT_UFFD_WP      _PAGE_BIT_SOFTW2    /* userfaultfd write-protect */
#define _PAGE_BIT_SOFT_DIRTY   _PAGE_BIT_SOFTW3    /* page write tracking */
#define _PAGE_BIT_SAVED_DIRTY  _PAGE_BIT_SOFTW5    /* dirty emulation for shadow stack */

#define _PAGE_SOFT_DIRTY   (_AT(pteval_t, 1) << _PAGE_BIT_SOFT_DIRTY)
#define _PAGE_UFFD_WP      (_AT(pteval_t, 1) << _PAGE_BIT_UFFD_WP)
#define _PAGE_SAVED_DIRTY  (_AT(pteval_t, 1) << _PAGE_BIT_SAVED_DIRTY)

These software bits serve important modern subsystems:

  • _PAGE_SOFT_DIRTY — tracks whether a page has been written since the last clear. Used by checkpoint/restore (CRIU) and live migration to identify modified pages without relying on hardware dirty bits (which are cleared by the page-reclaim scanner).
  • _PAGE_UFFD_WP — marks a page as write-protected by userfaultfd. When user code writes to such a page, the fault is delivered to a user-space handler via userfaultfd rather than the normal kernel page-fault path.
  • _PAGE_SAVED_DIRTY — preserves the dirty state when the hardware dirty bit must be cleared for other purposes (such as shadow stack dirty-bit emulation).

This is an important modern difference from the 2.6 era: the kernel no longer treats page-table entries as purely hardware-owned bitfields. They are hybrid structures that carry both architectural and Linux-private state, and understanding the software bits is as important as understanding the hardware bits for anyone working on the VM subsystem.

Table 2-5. Core x86_64 leaf-entry flags in Linux 7.0.12
Flag Bit Meaning
_PAGE_PRESENT 0 Entry is valid for translation
_PAGE_RW 1 Writable mapping
_PAGE_USER 2 User-accessible
_PAGE_PWT 3 Page write-through caching
_PAGE_PCD 4 Cache disable
_PAGE_ACCESSED 5 Accessed/reference bit
_PAGE_DIRTY 6 Dirty/write bit
_PAGE_PSE 7 Large-page leaf at PMD/PUD
_PAGE_PAT 7 on PTE PAT index bit for 4 KB page
_PAGE_GLOBAL 8 Global TLB entry
_PAGE_PAT_LARGE 12 PAT index bit for large leaf
_PAGE_NX 63 No-execute

ARM64 Translation Tables

ARM64 takes a fundamentally different approach to page-table design than x86_64. Where x86 evolved paging through a series of backward-compatible extensions (2-level → PAE → 4-level → 5-level), ARM64 was designed from scratch for a 64-bit world with no legacy constraints. The result is a cleaner architecture in several ways: separate hardware registers for kernel and user page tables (eliminating the need for KPTI), configurable page sizes (4 KB, 16 KB, 64 KB), memory attributes stored in a separate system register (MAIR_EL1) rather than packed into PTE bits, and a descriptor format that cleanly separates table entries from block/page entries. However, the underlying principle is identical: a multi-level radix tree that translates virtual addresses to physical addresses one index at a time.

Translation regime

ARM64 page translation begins with TTBR0_EL1 and TTBR1_EL1. The architecture uses the top portion of the virtual address to choose between them. Linux's documentation spells it out: bit 55 selects TTBRx. Addresses with that bit clear are looked up via TTBR0_EL1, which Linux uses for user mappings. Addresses with that bit set go through TTBR1_EL1, which Linux uses for kernel mappings.

This split is one of the cleanest architectural differences between ARM64 and x86_64. On x86_64, KPTI had to create separate shadow page tables to keep most kernel mappings out of user mode. On ARM64, the architecture already offers separate translation-table bases for the two halves of the address space. That does not eliminate all hardening issues, but it changes the baseline design.

Each TTBR carries an ASID (Address Space Identifier) in its upper 16 bits — the ARM64 equivalent of x86's PCID. When the kernel switches to a new process, it loads the new page-table base into TTBR0_EL1 and embeds the process's ASID into both TTBRs:

/* arch/arm64/mm/context.c — TTBR loading with ASID */
void cpu_do_switch_mm(phys_addr_t pgd_phys, struct mm_struct *mm)
{
    unsigned long ttbr1 = read_sysreg(ttbr1_el1);
    unsigned long asid = ASID(mm);
    unsigned long ttbr0 = phys_to_ttbr(pgd_phys);
    ...
    ttbr1 &= ~TTBR_ASID_MASK;
    ttbr1 |= FIELD_PREP(TTBR_ASID_MASK, asid);
    write_sysreg(ttbr1, ttbr1_el1);  /* ASID in TTBR1 */
    write_sysreg(ttbr0, ttbr0_el1);  /* new user page table */
}

The ASID width is either 8 bits (256 ASIDs) or 16 bits (65536 ASIDs), depending on the CPU implementation. The kernel detects this at boot from ID_AA64MMFR0_EL1. Unlike x86's 12-bit PCID, ARM64's 16-bit ASID provides a much larger pool — large enough that ASID recycling (and the associated full TLB flush) is rare on most workloads.

The TCR_EL1 (Translation Control Register) configures the translation geometry for both halves. It sets the granule (page size), the VA width (via T0SZ and T1SZ fields), inner and outer cacheability, shareability, and several feature enables. The kernel sets it up during early boot in arch/arm64/mm/proc.S:

/* arch/arm64/mm/proc.S — TCR_EL1 setup (simplified) */
mov_q  tcr, TCR_T0SZ(IDMAP_VA_BITS) | TCR_T1SZ(VA_BITS_MIN) | \
            TCR_CACHE_FLAGS | TCR_SHARED | TCR_TG_FLAGS | \
            TCR_EL1_AS | TCR_EL1_TBI0 | TCR_EL1_A1
orr    tcr, tcr, #TCR_EL1_HA    /* hardware Access flag update */
msr    tcr_el1, tcr

Note the TCR_EL1_HA bit — this enables hardware management of the Access flag (PTE_AF), so the CPU automatically sets AF on first access rather than taking a fault. This is a significant performance advantage over older ARM implementations that required the kernel to handle every first-access fault in software.

The virtual-address layout in Linux follows from the TTBR0/TTBR1 split:

/* arch/arm64/include/asm/memory.h — kernel/user VA layout */
#define VA_BITS           (CONFIG_ARM64_VA_BITS)
#define PAGE_OFFSET       (-(UL(1) << VA_BITS))    /* start of direct map */
#define MODULES_VADDR     (_PAGE_END(VA_BITS_MIN))  /* module region */
#define MODULES_VSIZE     (SZ_2G)

With VA_BITS=48, PAGE_OFFSET is 0xFFFF000000000000 — the start of the kernel's direct map of all physical memory. User space occupies the TTBR0 half from 0x0000000000000000 to 0x0000FFFFFFFFFFFF (256 TB).

The basic virtual-address-size definition lives in arch/arm64/include/asm/memory.h:

#define VA_BITS         (CONFIG_ARM64_VA_BITS)

Linux supports several page sizes, selected through CONFIG_ARM64_PAGE_SHIFT and its helper configs:

  • 4 KB — the most common configuration, matching x86_64
  • 16 KB — used by Apple Silicon (M1/M2) kernels
  • 64 KB — reduces TLB misses for memory-intensive workloads

Those page sizes change the number of levels and the span covered by each level.

Translation Table Walk

The header arch/arm64/include/asm/pgtable-hwdef.h defines the generic relationship between address width, page size, and translation depth. The key macro is:

#define ARM64_HW_PGTABLE_LEVELS(va_bits) \
    (((va_bits) - PTDESC_ORDER - 1) / PTDESC_TABLE_SHIFT)

With 4 KB pages, Linux most commonly uses:

  • 48-bit VA → 4 levels (L0 → L1 → L2 → L3)
  • 39-bit VA → 3 levels (L1 → L2 → L3)

With 64 KB pages, 52-bit VA can still be represented with only 3 levels because each table entry covers a much larger range.

The same header derives the level shifts:

#define PMD_SHIFT       ARM64_HW_PGTABLE_LEVEL_SHIFT(2)
#define PUD_SHIFT       ARM64_HW_PGTABLE_LEVEL_SHIFT(1)
