#define P4D_SHIFT       ARM64_HW_PGTABLE_LEVEL_SHIFT(0)
#define PGDIR_SHIFT     ARM64_HW_PGTABLE_LEVEL_SHIFT(4 - CONFIG_PGTABLE_LEVELS)

Linux then overlays its architecture-neutral names on top of the ARM terminology. The architecture manuals speak of levels 0 through 3, blocks, pages, and tables; Linux software speaks of pgd_t, p4d_t, pud_t, pmd_t, and pte_t.

Like x86_64's _PAGE_PSE, ARM64 supports block descriptors that terminate the walk early. A block at L1 maps 1 GB (with 4 KB granule); a block at L2 maps 2 MB. The descriptor type bits distinguish blocks from tables:

/* arch/arm64/include/asm/pgtable-hwdef.h — block descriptors */
#define PUD_TYPE_SECT   (_AT(pudval_t, 1) << 0)   /* L1 block: 1 GB */
#define PMD_TYPE_SECT   (_AT(pmdval_t, 1) << 0)   /* L2 block: 2 MB */

/* arch/arm64/include/asm/pgtable.h — creating block descriptors */
static inline pmd_t pmd_mkhuge(pmd_t pmd)
{
    pmdval_t mask = PMD_TYPE_MASK & ~PTE_VALID;
    pmdval_t val = PMD_TYPE_SECT & ~PTE_VALID;
    return __pmd((pmd_val(pmd) & ~mask) | val);
}

ARM64 also provides a contiguous bit (PTE_CONT, bit 52) that hints to the TLB that a group of adjacent entries (16 for 4 KB pages) share the same attributes and can be cached as a single large TLB entry. This gives 64 KB effective TLB coverage at the PTE level or 32 MB at the PMD level, without changing the page-table structure:

/* arch/arm64/include/asm/pgtable-hwdef.h — contiguous hint */
#define PTE_CONT    (_AT(pteval_t, 1) << 52)   /* contiguous range */
#define CONT_PTE_SIZE   (CONT_PTES * PAGE_SIZE)    /* 16 × 4 KB = 64 KB */
#define CONT_PMD_SIZE   (CONT_PMDS * PMD_SIZE)     /* 16 × 2 MB = 32 MB */

TLB maintenance on ARM64 uses ASID-tagged invalidation, which is more selective than x86's approach. Instead of flushing by virtual address alone, ARM64 can invalidate all TLB entries for a specific ASID (process) without affecting other processes:

/* arch/arm64/include/asm/tlbflush.h — ASID-based TLB flush */
static inline void flush_tlb_mm(struct mm_struct *mm)
{
    unsigned long asid;
    dsb(ishst);
    asid = __TLBI_VADDR(0, ASID(mm));
    __tlbi(aside1is, asid);         /* invalidate by ASID, inner-shareable */
    __tlbi_user(aside1is, asid);    /* also invalidate user ASID */
    __tlbi_sync_s1ish();
}

The __tlbi_user() call handles the case where a separate user ASID is in use (similar to KPTI's dual-PCID on x86). The dsb(ishst) barrier ensures all prior page-table writes are visible before the invalidation, and __tlbi_sync_s1ish() waits for the invalidation to complete across all inner-shareable CPUs.

Level count examples

A few concrete examples make the geometry intuitive:

  • With 4 KB pages, each table page contains 512 entries, and each index contributes 9 bits. A 48-bit virtual address therefore needs four table levels above the 12-bit page offset.
  • With 4 KB pages and 39-bit VA, there are only 27 translated bits above the offset, so three levels are enough.
  • With 64 KB pages, the page offset already consumes 16 bits, and each level covers more address space, so 52-bit VA can fit in three levels.

This flexibility is one reason ARM64 Linux code leans so heavily on macros. Hard-coding level numbers would make too much generic code page-size dependent.

Page Descriptor Format (ARM64)

ARM64 descriptors are always 64-bit values, but their meaning depends on the level and the descriptor type bits. arch/arm64/include/asm/pgtable-hwdef.h defines the core encodings. At the last level, PTEs use:

#define PTE_VALID       (_AT(pteval_t, 1) << 0)
#define PTE_TYPE_MASK       (_AT(pteval_t, 3) << 0)
#define PTE_TYPE_PAGE       (_AT(pteval_t, 3) << 0)
#define PTE_USER        (_AT(pteval_t, 1) << 6)
#define PTE_RDONLY      (_AT(pteval_t, 1) << 7)
#define PTE_SHARED      (_AT(pteval_t, 3) << 8)
#define PTE_AF          (_AT(pteval_t, 1) << 10)
#define PTE_NG          (_AT(pteval_t, 1) << 11)
#define PTE_PXN         (_AT(pteval_t, 1) << 53)
#define PTE_UXN         (_AT(pteval_t, 1) << 54)
#define PTE_ATTRINDX(t)     (_AT(pteval_t, (t)) << 2)

The type conventions are:

  • 0b00 — invalid descriptor
  • 0b01 — block/section descriptor at intermediate levels
  • 0b11 — table descriptor at intermediate levels, or page descriptor at the final level

Linux names the intermediate huge mappings sections in some macros and comments, but ARMv8 documentation more often uses the term block. Both refer to the same basic idea: end the walk early and map a large contiguous region.

The most important attribute bits are:

  • PTE_VALID — mapping is active.
  • PTE_USER — user access is allowed via AP[1].
  • PTE_RDONLY — write permission is restricted via AP[2].
  • PTE_AF — access flag.
  • PTE_NG — non-global entry.
  • PTE_SHARED — shareability field, typically inner-shareable for normal memory.
  • PTE_PXN — privileged execute-never.
  • PTE_UXN — user execute-never.
  • PTE_ATTRINDX(x) — chooses a byte from MAIR_EL1.

Linux also overlays software bits in the upper region of the descriptor. For example, arch/arm64/include/asm/pgtable-prot.h defines:

#define PTE_WRITE       (PTE_DBM)
#define PTE_DIRTY       (_AT(pteval_t, 1) << 55)
#define PTE_SPECIAL     (_AT(pteval_t, 1) << 56)

As on x86_64, modern Linux page-table entries are not purely hardware objects. They are hybrid encodings with architecture-defined and kernel-defined state in one 64-bit slot.

Access Flag management. The PTE_AF bit serves the same purpose as x86's Accessed bit. When TCR_EL1.HA (Hardware Access flag) is enabled — as Linux does on all modern ARMv8.1+ cores — the hardware automatically sets PTE_AF on first access, eliminating the overhead of access-flag faults that plagued older ARM implementations:

/* arch/arm64/include/asm/pgtable.h — Access flag helpers */
static inline int pte_young(pte_t pte)
{
    return !!(pte_val(pte) & PTE_AF);
}
static inline pte_t pte_mkyoung(pte_t pte)
{
    return set_pte_bit(pte, __pgprot(PTE_AF));
}

Dirty bit management. ARM64's dirty tracking is more complex than x86's single _PAGE_DIRTY bit. The architecture uses a hardware Dirty Bit Modifier (PTE_DBM) at bit 51, which the hardware clears when it makes a page dirty (effectively switching the read-only AP[2] bit). Linux layers a software PTE_DIRTY bit on top, creating a dual-tracking scheme:

/* arch/arm64/include/asm/pgtable-prot.h — dirty tracking */
#define PTE_WRITE       (PTE_DBM)           /* bit 51: hw dirty modifier */
#define PTE_DIRTY       (_AT(pteval_t, 1) << 55)  /* sw dirty bit */

/* arch/arm64/include/asm/pgtable.h — dirty detection */
static inline bool pte_dirty(pte_t pte)
{
    return pte_sw_dirty(pte) || pte_hw_dirty(pte);
}

A page is considered dirty if either the software PTE_DIRTY flag is set (by the kernel during fault handling) or the hardware has cleared PTE_RDONLY via DBM. This dual approach allows Linux to track dirty state correctly across both hardware-managed and software-managed transitions.

Table 2-6. Core ARM64 leaf-entry attributes in Linux 7.0.12
Field or flag Meaning
PTE_VALID Entry participates in translation
PTE_TYPE_PAGE Last-level page descriptor
PTE_USER EL0 access allowed
PTE_RDONLY Read-only mapping
PTE_SHARED Inner-shareable memory
PTE_AF Access flag
PTE_NG Non-global mapping
PTE_PXN Privileged execute-never
PTE_UXN User execute-never
PTE_ATTRINDX(MT_*) Selects memory type from MAIR_EL1
PTE_WRITE Linux software write/DBM helper bit
PTE_DIRTY Linux software dirty state

Memory Attributes (MAIR_EL1)

ARM64 separates address translation from memory-type selection more explicitly than x86. Instead of PCD/PWT/PAT bits alone describing cacheability, leaf descriptors carry an AttrIndx field, and that field selects one byte inside MAIR_EL1.

Linux defines the attribute bytes in arch/arm64/include/asm/sysreg.h:

#define MAIR_ATTR_DEVICE_nGnRnE     UL(0x00)
#define MAIR_ATTR_DEVICE_nGnRE      UL(0x04)
#define MAIR_ATTR_NORMAL_NC     UL(0x44)
#define MAIR_ATTR_NORMAL_TAGGED     UL(0xf0)
#define MAIR_ATTR_NORMAL        UL(0xff)
#define MAIR_ATTRIDX(attr, idx)     ((attr) << ((idx) * 8))

Linux assigns symbolic indices in arch/arm64/include/asm/memory.h:

#define MT_NORMAL       0
#define MT_NORMAL_TAGGED    1
#define MT_NORMAL_NC        2
#define MT_DEVICE_nGnRnE    3
#define MT_DEVICE_nGnRE     4

So the chapter's memory-type names correspond to these raw byte encodings:

  • MT_DEVICE_nGnRnE → 0x00
  • MT_DEVICE_nGnRE → 0x04
  • MT_NORMAL_NC → 0x44
  • MT_NORMAL → 0xff
  • MT_NORMAL_TAGGED → 0xf0

The initial MAIR setup occurs in __cpu_setup() in arch/arm64/mm/proc.S. The default constant is:

#define MAIR_EL1_SET                            \
    (MAIR_ATTRIDX(MAIR_ATTR_DEVICE_nGnRnE, MT_DEVICE_nGnRnE) |  \
     MAIR_ATTRIDX(MAIR_ATTR_DEVICE_nGnRE, MT_DEVICE_nGnRE) |    \
     MAIR_ATTRIDX(MAIR_ATTR_NORMAL_NC, MT_NORMAL_NC) |      \
     MAIR_ATTRIDX(MAIR_ATTR_NORMAL, MT_NORMAL) |            \
     MAIR_ATTRIDX(MAIR_ATTR_NORMAL, MT_NORMAL_TAGGED))

A subtle but important comment immediately above it explains the modern MTE nuance: MT_NORMAL_TAGGED is initially programmed as ordinary normal memory and later changed in mte_cpu_setup() if the CPU supports memory tagging. So the symbolic type exists unconditionally, but its final byte value is feature-dependent during boot. That is a good example of Linux separating policy names from early-boot hardware constraints.

Putting the pieces together

An ARM64 PTE says, in effect:

  • whether the mapping is valid,
  • whether user mode may reach it,
  • whether writes are allowed,
  • whether either privilege level may execute from it,
  • how hardware should cache and share it,
  • and which physical page frame it refers to.

Unlike x86, there is no separate segment privilege story layered on top. Protection, cacheability, executability, and globality all live in the page descriptor and the TTBR choice. That makes the model easier to explain, although the large number of optional architecture extensions—MTE, LPA2, TTL, DBM, PAN, BTI—means the real implementation is no less sophisticated.

The kernel combines all these pieces into protection templates that parallel x86's PAGE_KERNEL and PAGE_SHARED macros. For ARM64 these live in arch/arm64/include/asm/pgtable-prot.h:

/* arch/arm64/include/asm/pgtable-prot.h — protection templates */
#define _PAGE_KERNEL    (PTE_WRITE | PTE_DIRTY | PTE_AF | PTE_UXN | \
                         PTE_PXN | PTE_SHARED | \
                         PTE_ATTRINDX(MT_NORMAL))
#define _PAGE_KERNEL_EXEC  (_PAGE_KERNEL & ~PTE_PXN)

#define PROT_NORMAL      (PTE_TYPE_PAGE | PTE_AF | PTE_SHARED)
#define PROT_NORMAL_TAGGED  (PTE_TYPE_PAGE | PTE_AF | PTE_SHARED | \
                             PTE_ATTRINDX(MT_NORMAL_TAGGED))

Notice how _PAGE_KERNEL sets both PTE_UXN and PTE_PXN — kernel data pages are execute-never for both user and privileged mode. Only _PAGE_KERNEL_EXEC removes PTE_PXN for kernel code pages. This is the ARM64 equivalent of x86's NX-by-default policy.

When the kernel needs to flush TLB entries — for example, after unmapping a user page or changing permissions — ARM64 provides fine-grained invalidation that x86 lacks. While x86 can only invalidate by virtual address (INVLPG) or flush everything, ARM64 can invalidate by ASID, by VA+ASID, by intermediate physical address (IPA), or broadcast to all cores in the inner-shareable domain. This selective invalidation is one reason ARM64 performs well with many concurrent address spaces.

Comparison: x86_64 vs ARM64 Paging

Sections 3.6 and 3.7 presented x86_64 and ARM64 paging individually. This section puts the two architectures side by side — not merely to list differences, but to show how those differences ripple through the kernel's memory-management code. Both architectures are mature 64-bit paged MMU designs with multi-level tables, huge mappings, and rich attribute bits. They converge on the same fundamental principle: a radix-tree walk from a root pointer to a page-frame number plus attribute bits. But they diverge in the places that matter most to kernel design: how address spaces are split, how TLBs are maintained, how context switches are optimized, how memory types are encoded, and how huge pages are detected. Understanding these differences explains why the generic MM code uses so many macros and abstraction layers — it must paper over genuinely different hardware semantics.

Table 2-7. Paging comparison for Linux 7.0.12 primary architectures
Aspect x86_64 ARM64
Main translation roots CR3 — single register for the whole address space TTBR0_EL1 (user) and TTBR1_EL1 (kernel) — hardware-separated
Typical virtual-address sizes 48-bit or 57-bit (LA57) 39-bit, 48-bit, or 52-bit (LPA2)
Standard level count 4 hardware levels; 5 with LA57 3 or 4 levels with 4 KB pages; configurable with other granules
Page sizes 4 KB only (fixed) 4 KB, 16 KB, or 64 KB (build-time choice)
Large-page sizes 2 MB (PMD) and 1 GB (PUD) Depends on granule: 2 MB/1 GB (4 KB), 32 MB/1 GB (16 KB), 512 MB (64 KB)
Leaf detection _PAGE_PSE bit in PMD/PUD entry Descriptor type mask: PMD_TYPE_SECT / PUD_TYPE_SECT
Permission bits Present, R/W, User, NX, Global, cache bits Valid, AP[2:1], UXN, PXN, AF, SH, nG, AttrIndx
Memory type control PAT — 3 PTE bits index MSR_IA32_CR_PAT (8 slots) AttrIndx — 3 PTE bits index MAIR_EL1 (8 slots)
User/kernel split Canonical halves; KPTI adds shadow page tables Hardware TTBR0/TTBR1 split; no shadow tables needed
TLB tag PCID (12-bit, 4096 entries) ASID (8 or 16-bit, up to 65536 entries)
TLB invalidation INVLPG (local), IPI-driven shootdowns TLBI (broadcast by hardware to inner-shareable domain)
Dirty tracking Single hardware _PAGE_DIRTY bit Dual: hardware DBM + software PTE_DIRTY
Access flag Always hardware-managed (_PAGE_ACCESSED) Hardware-managed when TCR_EL1.HA is set (ARMv8.1+)
Segmentation legacy GDT/LDT still present, FS/GS active for TLS None

TLB invalidation models

The single most important operational difference between x86_64 and ARM64 paging is how they invalidate TLB entries. The kernel must invalidate TLB entries whenever it unmaps a page, changes permissions, or migrates a page to a different physical frame. The two architectures take fundamentally different approaches to this problem.

On x86_64, the INVLPG instruction invalidates a single TLB entry on the local CPU only. When a mapping change affects a process that might be running on other CPUs, the kernel must send Inter-Processor Interrupts (IPIs) to those CPUs and wait for each one to execute its own invalidation. This is the "TLB shootdown" pattern, and it is one of the most expensive multi-core operations in the kernel:

/* arch/x86/mm/tlb.c — IPI-based TLB shootdown (simplified) */
void flush_tlb_mm_range(struct mm_struct *mm,
                        unsigned long start, unsigned long end, ...)
{
    struct flush_tlb_info *info = ...;
    if (mm_global_asid(mm)) {
        broadcast_tlb_flush(info);        /* global ASID: broadcast */
    } else {
        flush_tlb_multi(mm_cpumask(mm), info);  /* IPI to each CPU */
    }
}

static void native_flush_tlb_multi(const struct cpumask *cpumask,
                                   const struct flush_tlb_info *info)
{
    if (info->freed_tables)
        on_each_cpu_mask(cpumask, flush_tlb_func, ...);  /* must reach all */
    else
        on_each_cpu_cond_mask(tlb_is_not_lazy, ...);     /* skip lazy CPUs */
}

On ARM64, the hardware broadcasts TLBI instructions to all CPUs in the inner-shareable domain automatically — no IPIs needed. The kernel simply issues a TLBI instruction with the appropriate operand, and the hardware ensures all cores see the invalidation:

/* arch/arm64/include/asm/tlbflush.h — hardware-broadcast TLBI */
static void __flush_tlb_range(struct vm_area_struct *vma,
                              unsigned long start, unsigned long end,
                              unsigned long stride, bool last_level, ...)
{
    dsb(ishst);                          /* barrier: page-table writes visible */
    __flush_tlb_range_op(vale1is, ...);  /* broadcast TLBI to all CPUs */
    dsb(ish);                            /* wait for invalidation to complete */
}

The practical consequences are significant. On x86_64, TLB shootdowns involve interrupt latency, IPI delivery overhead, and worst-case stalls when target CPUs are in non-interruptible states. On ARM64, the hardware broadcast completes without software intervention on remote CPUs. However, ARM64 must issue explicit DSB (Data Synchronization Barrier) instructions before and after TLBI to ensure ordering — the hardware broadcast is asynchronous until the final barrier completes.

Context switch strategies

Both architectures face the same problem during context switches: they must change the active address space from one process to another while minimizing TLB flushes. Both use tagged TLBs (PCID on x86, ASID on ARM64) to keep entries from different processes in the TLB simultaneously. But the implementation strategies differ substantially.

On x86_64, the context switch path in switch_mm_irqs_off() uses a generation counter to decide whether the TLB needs flushing. If the incoming process was recently active on this CPU, its TLB entries may still be valid:

/* arch/x86/mm/tlb.c — context switch fast path (simplified) */
void switch_mm_irqs_off(struct mm_struct *prev, struct mm_struct *next, ...)
{
    u16 prev_asid = this_cpu_read(cpu_tlbstate.loaded_mm_asid);

    if (mm_needs_global_asid(next, prev_asid))
        return;                     /* global ASID: TLB still valid */
    if (is_global_asid(prev_asid))
        return;                     /* already using global ASID */
    if (!was_lazy)
        return;                     /* same generation: no flush needed */

    /* TLB generation mismatch — mark flush needed */
    ns.need_flush = true;
    load_new_mm_cr3(next->pgd, new_asid, need_flush);
}

On ARM64, the path in check_and_switch_context() uses a global generation counter. Each mm_struct holds its ASID plus a generation stamp. If the generation matches, the switch is a fast cmpxchg — no flush needed. When the ASID pool is exhausted, the kernel bumps the generation and marks all CPUs for a deferred TLB flush:

/* arch/arm64/mm/context.c — ASID-based context switch (simplified) */
void check_and_switch_context(struct mm_struct *mm)
{
    unsigned long asid = atomic64_read(&mm->context.id);

    if (old_active_asid && asid_gen_match(asid) &&
        atomic64_cmpxchg_relaxed(&per_cpu(active_asids, cpu), ...))
        goto switch_mm_fastpath;      /* same generation: reuse ASID */

    /* ASID mismatch or rollover — allocate new ASID */
    asid = new_context(mm);

    /* If rollover happened, deferred flush on this CPU */
    if (cpumask_test_and_clear_cpu(cpu, &tlb_flush_pending))
        local_flush_tlb_all();

switch_mm_fastpath:
    cpu_do_switch_mm(virt_to_phys(mm->pgd), mm);
}

The key difference: ARM64's 16-bit ASID pool (65536 entries) means generation rollovers are far rarer than x86's 12-bit PCID pool (4096 entries). On a system with thousands of active processes, x86 must recycle PCIDs and flush TLBs much more frequently.

Huge page detection

Both architectures support early termination of the page-table walk to map large contiguous regions — 2 MB or 1 GB on x86_64, and granule-dependent sizes on ARM64. But the hardware mechanisms differ enough that the kernel needs separate leaf-detection functions.

On x86_64, a single bit (_PAGE_PSE, "Page Size Extension") marks a PMD or PUD entry as a leaf:

/* arch/x86/include/asm/pgtable.h — x86 huge page detection */
static inline int pmd_leaf(pmd_t pte)
{
    return pmd_flags(pte) & _PAGE_PSE;     /* bit 7 = leaf */
}
static inline int pud_leaf(pud_t pud)
{
    return pud_val(pud) & _PAGE_PSE;       /* same bit */
}

On ARM64, the descriptor type field (bits [1:0]) distinguishes tables from blocks. A block descriptor has type 01, while a table has type 11:

/* arch/arm64/include/asm/pgtable.h — ARM64 block/section detection */
#define pmd_sect(pmd) \
    ((pmd_val(pmd) & PMD_TYPE_MASK) == PMD_TYPE_SECT)

#define pmd_leaf(pmd)  (pmd_present(pmd) && !pmd_table(pmd))

Linux's generic page-table walkers call pmd_leaf() and pud_leaf(), which each architecture implements differently. This is a classic example of how the kernel's abstraction layer papers over hardware differences — the generic walk_pmd_range() simply calls pmd_leaf() and gets the right answer regardless of architecture.

Memory type encoding

Both architectures need to tell the CPU how to cache and order accesses to different memory regions — normal RAM should be write-back cached, device MMIO should be uncacheable, etc. Both use a 3-bit index in the page-table entry to select from 8 possible memory types. But the indirection mechanisms differ.

On x86_64, the PAT (Page Attribute Table) mechanism uses three PTE bits — _PAGE_PWT, _PAGE_PCD, and _PAGE_PAT — to form a 3-bit index into the MSR_IA32_CR_PAT model-specific register. Each of the 8 MSR slots encodes a memory type (WB, WT, UC, WC, etc.):

/* arch/x86/mm/pat/memtype.c — PAT overview (from header comment) */
 * PAT doesn't work via explicit memory ranges, but uses page table entries
 * to add a type attribute to the regular page frame address ... The encoding
 * in PAT MSR is (_PAGE_PWT, _PAGE_PCD, _PAGE_PAT) mapped to ... via
 * MSR_IA32_CR_PAT

On ARM64, the same concept exists under a different name: the AttrIndx[2:0] bits in the descriptor index into the MAIR_EL1 system register, which holds 8 byte-sized memory attribute encodings. The kernel programs MAIR_EL1 during early boot:

/* arch/arm64/mm/proc.S — MAIR_EL1 setup */
#define MAIR_EL1_SET  ...   /* 8 memory type slots */
    mov_q  mair, MAIR_EL1_SET
    msr    mair_el1, mair

The conceptual model is identical — a 3-bit PTE index into an 8-entry system register. The practical difference is naming and the details of each memory-type encoding. Linux abstracts this through architecture-specific pgprot_* helpers, so generic code never deals with PAT bits or AttrIndx directly.

Security implications of the split

The comparison table hides one of the biggest post-2018 differences between the platforms. On x86_64, Meltdown forced Linux to treat the presence of kernel mappings in a user-accessible page-table root as a hazard even if page permissions blocked actual loads. That is why KPTI became a defining part of the x86 MM story — the kernel maintains two PGDs per process (one with kernel mappings, one without) and switches between them on every user↔kernel transition, adding measurable overhead to every system call and interrupt.

On ARM64, the TTBR split and different microarchitectural behavior meant the baseline design already looked closer to what x86 wanted after KPTI. User code runs with TTBR0 pointing at user tables and TTBR1 pointing at kernel tables — two physically separate page-table trees. There is no single root that contains both user and kernel mappings.

This does not mean ARM64 has no speculation or translation hardening work. ARM64 kernels enable PAN (Privileged Access Never) to prevent the kernel from accidentally accessing user memory, and use speculative-barrier instructions (SB, CSDB) to mitigate Spectre-class attacks. But the first line of defense — address-space isolation — is part of the architecture's ordinary address-space model rather than a post-Meltdown retrofit.

Page table allocation and population

Both architectures allocate page-table pages through the same generic __pte_alloc() path in mm/memory.c. The differences appear when populating intermediate entries — writing a table descriptor that points the walk to the next level.

On x86_64, an intermediate entry is simply the physical address OR'd with _KERNPG_TABLE (Present + R/W). On ARM64, intermediate entries carry additional attribute bits that restrict what the next level can grant:

/* arch/arm64/include/asm/pgalloc.h — ARM64 table population */
static inline void __pmd_populate(pmd_t *pmdp, phys_addr_t ptep, pmdval_t prot)
{
    set_pmd(pmdp, __pmd(__phys_to_pmd_val(ptep) | prot));
}
/* Typical call: */
__pmd_populate(pmdp, page_to_phys(ptep),
               PMD_TYPE_TABLE | PMD_TABLE_AF | PMD_TABLE_PXN);

The PMD_TABLE_PXN bit in the intermediate entry means "no entry reached through this table can be executed at EL1" — a hierarchical permission that has no x86 equivalent. x86's intermediate entries carry only the union permission bits (R/W, User), and NX enforcement happens solely at the leaf level.

Performance implications

The performance story comes down to three factors: context-switch cost, TLB shootdown cost, and TLB capacity.

  • Context switches: x86_64's 12-bit PCID gives 4096 address-space tags; ARM64's 16-bit ASID gives up to 65536. Larger ASID pools mean fewer generation rollovers (which trigger full TLB flushes). On heavily multitasked systems, this difference is measurable.
  • TLB shootdowns: x86_64's IPI-based shootdowns involve interrupt latency and cross-core synchronization. ARM64's hardware-broadcast TLBI eliminates the IPI overhead but requires explicit memory barriers (DSB). In practice, ARM64's model scales better on high-core-count systems where IPI storms become expensive.
  • TLB capacity: ARM64's contiguous-bit hint (PTE_CONT) allows the TLB to coalesce 16 adjacent entries into one slot, effectively giving 64 KB coverage from 4 KB pages. x86_64 has no equivalent — huge pages are the only way to increase per-entry coverage.

Both systems converge on the same kernel strategy: avoid full-space invalidations when possible, preserve hot kernel translations when safe, batch unmap work through the mmu_gather machinery, and express geometry through macros so the generic MM code stays portable. The architecture-specific code handles the how; the generic code decides the what.

The Memory Management Unit (MMU) and System MMU (SMMU/IOMMU)

Every address translation described in the preceding sections is performed by a hardware block called the Memory Management Unit. The CPU's MMU translates virtual addresses issued by software into physical addresses that reach DRAM or MMIO. But the CPU is not the only bus master in a modern system—DMA-capable devices such as GPUs, NICs, storage controllers, and media accelerators also issue memory transactions. Without an equivalent translation unit on the device side, those transactions would bypass all of the kernel's carefully constructed page tables and protection. That device-side translation unit is the System MMU (SMMU) on ARM systems and the IOMMU on x86 systems.

This section covers four tightly connected topics. First, the CPU MMU itself — the hardware that makes paging possible, including the TLB management state the kernel must maintain. Second, the reasons that devices need their own translation units. Third, the architecture-specific IOMMU implementations: Intel VT-d and AMD-Vi on x86, ARM SMMUv2 and SMMUv3. Fourth, the generic Linux IOMMU framework that unifies all of these behind a single API, and the DMA mapping layer that most drivers actually use. The section concludes with a comparison of CPU MMU versus device-side IOMMU/SMMU, and Shared Virtual Addressing (SVA) — a feature that lets devices share a process's page tables directly.

The CPU MMU

The CPU MMU sits between the processor pipeline and the memory bus. It is not a separate chip — on all modern processors it is integrated into the core pipeline, so translation happens in parallel with execution. On every instruction fetch, load, or store, the MMU:

  1. receives the virtual address from the CPU core,
  2. checks the TLB (Translation Lookaside Buffer) for a cached translation — a hit avoids the expensive page-table walk entirely,
  3. on a TLB miss, walks the page tables in memory (PGD → P4D → PUD → PMD → PTE on Linux's five-level model),
  4. checks permissions (read, write, execute, user/supervisor, NX) at every level,
  5. produces the physical address that goes out on the memory bus and caches the translation in the TLB for next time,
  6. and raises a page fault if the translation is missing or permissions are violated.

On x86_64 the MMU is controlled through CR3 (page-table base), CR0.PG (paging enable), and CR4/EFER feature bits such as PAE, LA57, PCIDE, and NXE. On ARM64 the MMU is controlled through TTBR0_EL1/TTBR1_EL1 (table bases), TCR_EL1 (translation control), and SCTLR_EL1.M (MMU enable).

TLB state management

The TLB is the MMU's cache — without it, every memory access would require four or five memory reads to walk the page tables. The kernel must carefully manage TLB state to ensure that stale translations never cause incorrect accesses. Each architecture tracks TLB state differently.

On x86_64, the kernel maintains a per-CPU struct tlb_state that tracks which address space is currently loaded and which PCID slots are in use:

/* arch/x86/include/asm/tlbflush.h — per-CPU TLB state */
struct tlb_state {
    struct mm_struct *loaded_mm;       /* currently active mm */
    u16 loaded_mm_asid;                /* PCID slot for this mm */
    u16 next_asid;                     /* next PCID to allocate */
    bool invalidate_other;             /* must invalidate on switch */
    u64 user_pcid_flush_mask;          /* KPTI: which user PCIDs need flush */
    struct tlb_context ctxs[TLB_NR_DYN_ASIDS]; /* per-ASID generation */
};
DECLARE_PER_CPU_ALIGNED(struct tlb_state, cpu_tlbstate);

The ctxs[] array holds a generation counter for each PCID slot, allowing the kernel to detect whether the TLB entries for a given PCID are still valid without actually flushing them. When switch_mm_irqs_off() runs, it compares the stored generation against the current tlb_gen for that mm — if they match, the TLB can be reused without flushing.

On ARM64, the TLB state is simpler because the hardware ASID pool is larger (up to 16 bits = 65536 slots vs x86's 12-bit PCID = 4096 slots). The kernel tracks active ASIDs with a single per-CPU atomic variable:

/* arch/arm64/mm/context.c — per-CPU ASID tracking */
static DEFINE_PER_CPU(atomic64_t, active_asids);
static DEFINE_PER_CPU(u64, reserved_asids);

/* Generation rollover: when all ASIDs are exhausted */
static void flush_context(void)
{
    /* clear all active ASIDs across all CPUs */
    for_each_possible_cpu(i)
        atomic64_set_release(per_cpu_ptr(&active_asids, i), 0);
    /* mark all CPUs for deferred TLB flush */
}

When the ASID generation rolls over (all ASIDs exhausted), flush_context() clears every CPU's active ASID and sets a pending-flush flag. The next context switch on each CPU will see the flag and execute a local_flush_tlb_all() before loading the new ASID. This deferred-flush design avoids broadcasting an IPI to every CPU — each CPU handles the flush lazily when it next switches contexts.

The critical architectural difference is that x86_64 uses a single CR3 register for both user and kernel page tables, while ARM64 has two hardware table-base registers (TTBR0_EL1 for user and TTBR1_EL1 for kernel). That split is one reason ARM64 starts from a cleaner baseline than x86_64 for kernel/user isolation.

Why devices need address translation too

Understanding the IOMMU/SMMU requires understanding why it exists. In early PC hardware, devices performing DMA used physical addresses directly. The driver programmed a physical address into the device's DMA register, and the device read or wrote that physical memory without any translation layer. This model worked when systems had a single bus master and no memory protection, but it fails catastrophically in modern systems:

  • No isolation—a buggy or malicious device (or driver) can overwrite arbitrary physical memory, including kernel text and other processes' data. A compromised NIC driver can read encryption keys from any physical page.
  • No virtualization—when a virtual machine is assigned a physical device (passthrough), the guest's physical addresses are not the host's physical addresses. Without device-side translation, the hypervisor would need to intercept every DMA setup, destroying performance.
  • Fragmentation pressure—devices needing large contiguous DMA buffers can force early reservations or CMA regions, wasting memory when the device is idle.
  • Address-width limits—32-bit DMA engines cannot naturally reach memory above 4 GB, requiring bounce buffers that copy data through low memory (SWIOTLB).

The IOMMU/SMMU solves all of these problems by placing a translation unit between the device bus and main memory. Devices issue an IOVA (I/O virtual address), and the IOMMU translates that IOVA through device-facing page tables before memory is touched. The IOMMU can enforce per-device permissions (read-only, no-execute), provide fault reporting when a device accesses an unmapped IOVA, and present a contiguous IOVA range that maps to scattered physical pages — exactly as the CPU MMU does for processes.

IOMMU on x86: Intel VT-d and AMD-Vi

On x86 platforms, the IOMMU is implemented as Intel VT-d (Virtualization Technology for Directed I/O) or AMD-Vi (AMD I/O Virtualization Technology). Both serve the same purpose — translating device DMA addresses through per-device page tables — but use different hardware interfaces and table formats. Intel VT-d is described in ACPI DMAR (DMA Remapping) tables; AMD-Vi uses ACPI IVRS (I/O Virtualization Reporting Structure) tables.

VT-d discovery and initialization

The kernel discovers VT-d hardware by parsing the ACPI DMAR table during early boot. The initialization sequence in drivers/iommu/intel/iommu.c proceeds as:

/* drivers/iommu/intel/iommu.c — VT-d initialization (simplified) */
int __init intel_iommu_init(void)
{
    if (dmar_table_init())                  /* parse ACPI DMAR table */
        return -ENODEV;
    ...
    ret = init_dmars();                     /* initialize each IOMMU unit */
    ...
}

/* drivers/iommu/intel/dmar.c — ACPI DMAR parsing */
int __init dmar_table_init(void)
{
    /* early detection/parsing of Remapping Reporting Structure */
}

init_dmars() walks every IOMMU unit discovered from the DMAR table, enables queued invalidation (QI) for batched IOTLB flushes, and sets up the root and context tables that map PCI bus/device/function triples to their I/O page tables.

VT-d table hierarchy

VT-d uses a three-level lookup to find a device's I/O page tables. The root table has one entry per PCI bus; each root entry points to a context table with one entry per device function; each context entry points to the actual I/O page tables for that device's domain:

/* drivers/iommu/intel/iommu.h — VT-d table structures */
struct root_entry {           /* 128 bits — one per PCI bus */
    u64 lo;                   /* Context table pointer + present */
    u64 hi;                   /* Used for scalable mode */
};

struct context_entry {        /* 128 bits — one per device function */
    u64 lo;                   /* Domain ID, address width, table pointer */
    u64 hi;                   /* Fault processing, AW, etc. */
};

/* I/O page table entry permission bits */
#define DMA_PTE_READ        BIT_ULL(0)
#define DMA_PTE_WRITE       BIT_ULL(1)
#define DMA_PTE_LARGE_PAGE  BIT_ULL(7)
#define DMA_PTE_SNP         BIT_ULL(11)

The I/O page-table format is deliberately similar to the CPU's page tables — it uses the same multi-level radix-tree structure with 4 KB pages and optional 2 MB/1 GB large pages. This similarity is intentional: it allows the hardware to reuse the same page-walk logic, and it makes the code easier to understand for anyone who already knows CPU page tables.

IOTLB invalidation

Like the CPU's TLB, the IOMMU caches translations in an IOTLB. When the kernel changes an I/O page-table entry, it must invalidate the IOTLB to prevent stale translations. Intel VT-d uses queued invalidation (QI) — the kernel writes invalidation requests to a shared command queue, and the hardware processes them asynchronously:

/* drivers/iommu/intel/iommu.c — queued IOTLB invalidation */
iommu->flush.flush_iotlb = qi_flush_iotlb;

/* For device-specific invalidation: */
quirk_extra_dev_tlb_flush(info, addr, mask, PASID_RID2PASID, ...);

Queued invalidation is more efficient than register-based invalidation because the hardware can batch multiple invalidations and process them without stalling the CPU. This is analogous to how ARM64's command-queue-based SMMUv3 works (see below).

SMMU on ARM64: SMMUv2 and SMMUv3

ARM systems use the System MMU as part of the AMBA interconnect architecture. The SMMU is the ARM equivalent of Intel VT-d — it sits between DMA-capable devices and main memory, translating device-issued addresses through per-device page tables. Linux 7.0.12 supports two generations: SMMUv2 (the legacy design) and SMMUv3 (the modern redesign with command queues and two-stage translation).

SMMUv2

SMMUv2 is organized around Stream IDs and Context Banks. Each DMA-capable device is assigned a stream ID (typically from the device tree or ACPI IORT table). That stream ID selects a context bank — a set of registers that carry the TTBR, TCR, and MAIR-like state needed to walk I/O page tables. The page tables themselves are built through the common io-pgtable framework (see below).

struct arm_smmu_device {      /* drivers/iommu/arm/arm-smmu/arm-smmu.h */
    void __iomem *base;
    unsigned long pgsize_bitmap;
    u32 num_context_banks;
    u32 num_s2_context_banks;
    u32 num_mapping_groups;
};

struct arm_smmu_domain {
    struct arm_smmu_device *smmu;
    struct io_pgtable_ops *pgtbl_ops;
    enum arm_smmu_domain_stage stage;
    struct arm_smmu_cfg cfg;
};

SMMUv2 supports both Stage 1 (device VA → physical) and Stage 2 (IPA → physical) translation, but cannot easily nest both stages simultaneously. Its register-based programming model also limits scalability — each context bank switch requires MMIO register writes.

SMMUv3

SMMUv3 is a major redesign that replaces register-based programming with command queues, event queues, and PRI (Page Request Interface) support. It is the ARM equivalent of Intel VT-d's queued invalidation and scalable mode. The key improvement is full two-stage translation: Stage 1 translates a device virtual address to an Intermediate Physical Address (IPA), and Stage 2 translates that IPA to a host physical address. This makes nested virtualization and device passthrough practical without hypervisor intervention on every DMA access.

SMMUv3 discovery happens through ACPI IORT (I/O Remapping Table) or device tree. The kernel's probe function sets up the stream table, command queue, and event queue:

struct arm_smmu_device {      /* drivers/iommu/arm/arm-smmu-v3/arm-smmu-v3.h */
    void __iomem *base;
    u32 features;
    unsigned long oas;            /* output address size */
    unsigned int asid_bits;       /* 8 or 16 */
    unsigned int vmid_bits;       /* Stage 2 VM IDs */
    unsigned int ssid_bits;       /* SubstreamID (PASID) width */
    unsigned int sid_bits;        /* StreamID width */
    struct arm_smmu_strtab_cfg strtab_cfg;  /* stream table config */
    struct arm_smmu_cmdq cmdq;    /* command queue for invalidations */
    struct arm_smmu_evtq evtq;    /* event queue for fault reports */
    struct arm_smmu_priq priq;    /* PRI queue for page requests */
};

struct arm_smmu_master {
    struct arm_smmu_device *smmu;
    struct arm_smmu_stream *streams;
    struct arm_smmu_ctx_desc_cfg cd_table;  /* context descriptor table */
    unsigned int num_streams;
    unsigned int ssid_bits;
    bool ats_enabled;             /* Address Translation Service */
    bool stall_enabled;           /* stall-on-fault for recoverable faults */
};

The SMMUv3 command queue

Unlike the CPU's TLBI instructions (which are individual CPU instructions), SMMUv3 invalidations go through a shared memory command queue. The kernel writes command entries to the queue, issues a memory barrier, and updates the hardware producer pointer. The SMMU hardware processes commands asynchronously:

/* drivers/iommu/arm/arm-smmu-v3/arm-smmu-v3.c — command queue (simplified) */
static int arm_smmu_cmdq_issue_cmdlist(struct arm_smmu_device *smmu, ...)
{
    /* write command(s) to queue memory */
    /* dma_wmb() — ensure commands visible before prod update */
    /* update valid bitmap */
    /* ring hardware prod pointer */
}

This command-queue model is more efficient than SMMUv2's register-based programming for two reasons: commands can be batched, and the CPU does not stall waiting for each invalidation to complete.

SMMUv3 Stream Table Entry fields
Field Purpose
STE.Config Translation mode: bypass, Stage 1, Stage 2, or nested Stage 1 + Stage 2.
STE.S1ContextPtr Pointer to the Context Descriptor table for PASID/SubstreamID lookups.
STE.S2TTB Stage 2 translation-table base when virtualization is active.
STE.S2AA64 Selects AArch64 format for Stage 2 tables.
STE.EATS Enables Address Translation Service interactions with the device.

The Linux IOMMU Framework

Linux provides a generic IOMMU API in include/linux/iommu.h and drivers/iommu/iommu.c that abstracts Intel VT-d, AMD-Vi, ARM SMMU, and other remapping units behind a common interface. This layer is the IOMMU equivalent of the generic page-table API (pgd/pud/pmd/pte) — it lets the rest of the kernel work with I/O address spaces without knowing which hardware implementation is underneath.

Core abstractions

The key abstractions are IOMMU groups, IOMMU domains, and driver-specific iommu_ops. A group is the minimum isolation unit visible to Linux — all devices in a group share the same I/O address space because the hardware cannot distinguish between them (e.g., multi-function PCI devices behind a non-ACS bridge). A domain is an I/O address space with its own page tables. The operations tables provide the callbacks for probing, mapping, unmapping, and flushing IOTLB state.

/* include/linux/iommu.h — IOMMU domain */
struct iommu_domain {
    unsigned type;
    const struct iommu_domain_ops *ops;
    const struct iommu_ops *owner;
    unsigned long pgsize_bitmap;
    struct iommu_domain_geometry geometry;
};

Device probing

When a device is discovered (PCI enumeration, device-tree probing, or ACPI), the IOMMU core's iommu_probe_device() function attaches it to the appropriate IOMMU group and domain:

/* drivers/iommu/iommu.c — device probe path (simplified) */
static int iommu_init_device(struct device *dev, ...)
{
    ops->probe_device(dev);       /* arch-specific: find IOMMU for this device */
    ops->device_group(dev);       /* determine isolation group */
}

int iommu_probe_device(struct device *dev)
{
    mutex_lock(&iommu_probe_device_lock);
    ret = __iommu_probe_device(dev, NULL);
    mutex_unlock(&iommu_probe_device_lock);
}

The probe_device callback is where the architecture-specific driver (VT-d, SMMU) discovers which IOMMU hardware unit handles this device. The device_group callback determines whether the device can be isolated individually or must share a group.

Mapping and unmapping

The generic iommu_map() function creates an IOVA → physical mapping in a domain's I/O page tables:

/* drivers/iommu/iommu.c — generic IOMMU mapping */
int iommu_map(struct iommu_domain *domain, unsigned long iova,
              phys_addr_t paddr, size_t size, int prot, gfp_t gfp)
{
    ret = iommu_map_nosync(domain, iova, paddr, size, prot, gfp);
    iommu_sync_map(domain, iova, size);    /* flush IOTLB */
    return ret;
}

Note the iommu_sync_map() call — after modifying the I/O page tables, the kernel must flush the IOTLB to ensure the hardware sees the new mapping. This is the IOMMU equivalent of flush_tlb_* for CPU page tables.

Domain types

IOMMU domain types in Linux 7.0.12
Domain type Description
IOMMU_DOMAIN_BLOCKED All DMA blocked; used to isolate a device completely before assigning it to a VM.
IOMMU_DOMAIN_IDENTITY Pass-through mode where DMA address equals physical address. Used when the IOMMU is present but translation overhead is undesirable.
IOMMU_DOMAIN_DMA The kernel DMA API manages IOVA allocation and mapping. The most common mode for general-purpose devices.
IOMMU_DOMAIN_DMA_FQ DMA mappings plus batched flush-queue invalidation. Defers IOTLB flushes for better throughput at the cost of slightly delayed unmap visibility.
IOMMU_DOMAIN_UNMANAGED User-managed mappings, typically via VFIO for VM pass-through. The hypervisor (or userspace VFIO driver) controls the mapping directly.
IOMMU_DOMAIN_SVA Shared Virtual Addressing — a device shares a process's CPU page tables.
IOMMU_DOMAIN_NESTED Nested translation for virtualization — Stage 1 from the guest, Stage 2 from the host.

DMA API Integration

Most device drivers never call the IOMMU API directly. They use the DMA mapping API in include/linux/dma-mapping.h, and the kernel transparently chooses whether the request becomes a direct DMA address, an IOMMU-backed IOVA, or a SWIOTLB bounce-buffer mapping. This abstraction is critical: it means a driver writer can use the same dma_map_single() call whether the system has an IOMMU, a bounce buffer, or neither.

/* include/linux/dma-mapping.h — the driver-facing DMA API */
dma_addr_t dma = dma_map_single(dev, cpu_addr, size, DMA_TO_DEVICE);
/* device uses 'dma' as its DMA address for the transfer */
dma_unmap_single(dev, dma, size, DMA_TO_DEVICE);

/* For coherent (long-lived) DMA buffers: */
void *buf = dma_alloc_coherent(dev, size, &dma_handle, GFP_KERNEL);

The dma_map_single() function returns a dma_addr_t — this is an IOVA when an IOMMU domain is active, or a raw physical address when in identity/passthrough mode. The driver does not need to know which case applies.

DMA translation strategies
Strategy When used Mechanism
Direct mapping No IOMMU, or an identity domain DMA address equals physical address.
IOMMU translation IOMMU present with a DMA domain Allocate an IOVA and create IOMMU page-table entries.
Bounce buffering The device DMA mask cannot reach the target memory SWIOTLB copies through a low-memory bounce buffer.

The key decision is whether an IOMMU domain is active and, if not, whether the device's DMA mask can reach the target address. When that mask is too small, dev_use_swiotlb() steers the transfer through SWIOTLB instead of exposing unreachable memory to the device.

The io-pgtable Framework

Both SMMUv2 and SMMUv3 build their I/O page tables through a shared framework in drivers/iommu/io-pgtable-arm.c. This framework provides a generic set of page-table operations that the SMMU drivers call, abstracting the details of page-table format (LPAE, AArch64, etc.) from the IOMMU driver itself:

/* include/linux/io-pgtable.h — generic I/O page table operations */
struct io_pgtable_ops {
    int (*map_pages)(struct io_pgtable_ops *ops, unsigned long iova,
                     phys_addr_t paddr, size_t pgsize, size_t pgcount,
                     int iommu_prot, gfp_t gfp, size_t *mapped);
    size_t (*unmap_pages)(struct io_pgtable_ops *ops, unsigned long iova,
                          size_t pgsize, size_t pgcount, ...);
    phys_addr_t (*iova_to_phys)(struct io_pgtable_ops *ops, unsigned long iova);
};

The ARM LPAE implementation in drivers/iommu/io-pgtable-arm.c builds page tables that use the same descriptor format as the CPU's ARM64 page tables — the same PTE_AF, AttrIndx, and AP bits. This means the same hardware page-table walker design serves both the CPU MMU and the SMMU, which is one reason ARM SoCs can share page-table silicon between the two units.

MMU vs SMMU/IOMMU Comparison

The CPU MMU and the device-side IOMMU/SMMU solve the same fundamental problem — translating one address space to another through a multi-level page-table walk — but they serve different bus masters and have different fault models. The following table summarizes the key differences:

CPU MMU versus device-side IOMMU/SMMU
Feature CPU MMU SMMU/IOMMU
Who issues addresses CPU cores (instruction fetch, load, store) DMA-capable devices (GPU, NIC, storage controller)
Translation input Virtual address (VA) I/O virtual address (IOVA)
Translation output Physical address (PA) Physical address (PA)
Context selector CR3 or TTBRx_EL1 (one address space at a time) Device stream ID + PASID/SubstreamID (many devices, many contexts)
Page-table format Architecture-defined (x86 or ARM64 format) Usually same format as CPU (VT-d ≈ x86; SMMU ≈ ARM64 LPAE)
TLB/IOTLB invalidation INVLPG / TLBI instructions Queued invalidation (VT-d QI) or command queue (SMMUv3)
Fault model CPU page fault → kernel handler IOMMU event / PRI page request → kernel or userspace handler
Two-stage translation Used for VM guests (EPT on x86, Stage 2 on ARM64) Used for device passthrough to VMs (same nested concept)
Linux interface MM subsystem (mm_struct, page-table API) IOMMU API + DMA mapping API (iommu_domain, dma_map_*)
SVA support Always (CPU always uses process page tables) Optional — requires PASID support in device and IOMMU

The key insight is that the IOMMU/SMMU extends the same virtual-memory model that the CPU MMU provides to processes — but extends it to devices. With SVA, the boundary blurs entirely: a device can walk the same page tables as a CPU core, using the same ASID/PASID to maintain isolation. The kernel's job is to ensure that both translation units stay consistent — when a page is unmapped or migrated, both the CPU TLB and the IOTLB must be invalidated, which is why the MMU notifier infrastructure (mmu_notifier) is essential for correct SVA operation.

Shared Virtual Addressing (SVA)

One of the most powerful modern features is Shared Virtual Addressing (also called Shared Virtual Memory). SVA represents a fundamental shift in how devices interact with memory: instead of working with a separate IOVA address space, a device can share the same virtual address space as a user process. The device uses the same page tables as the process, so pointers in shared data structures are valid for both the CPU and the device.

How SVA works

The mechanism centers on PASID (Process Address Space ID) on Intel or SubstreamID on ARM. When a device supports SVA, the kernel binds the device to a process's mm_struct, allocating a PASID that the device includes in every DMA transaction. The IOMMU/SMMU uses that PASID to look up the process's page tables:

/* drivers/iommu/iommu-sva.c — binding a device to a process */
struct iommu_sva *iommu_sva_bind_device(struct device *dev, struct mm_struct *mm)
{
    /* allocate or look up mm->iommu_mm */
    /* allocate a PASID for this process */
    /* attach device to mm's page tables via the PASID */
}

On ARM SMMUv3, the SVA bind creates a Context Descriptor that points directly to the process's PGD. The SMMU hardware then walks the same page tables the CPU uses:

/* drivers/iommu/arm/arm-smmu-v3/arm-smmu-v3-sva.c — SVA context */
static void arm_smmu_make_sva_cd(struct arm_smmu_cd *target,
                                 struct mm_struct *mm, u16 asid)
{
    /* encode ASID, TTBR0 = virt_to_phys(mm->pgd), MAIR, TCR */
    /* the SMMU will walk the process's CPU page tables directly */
}

On Intel VT-d, the equivalent function installs a first-level (Stage 1) translation that uses the process's page-table root:

/* drivers/iommu/intel/svm.c — Intel SVA setup */
static int intel_svm_set_dev_pasid(...)
{
    __domain_setup_first_level(iommu, dev, __pa(mm->pgd), ...);
    /* device DMA with this PASID walks the process's page tables */
}

Benefits and requirements

  • Zero-copy acceleration: GPUs and accelerators can directly access user buffers without pinning or copying, because they share the process's virtual address space.
  • Demand paging: A missing translation generates a Page Request (via PRI on PCIe or stall-on-fault on SMMUv3). The kernel can resolve the fault — including allocating pages or paging from swap — just as it would for a CPU page fault.
  • Simplified programming: Device and CPU code can share pointers directly, reducing the complexity of accelerator libraries.

SVA requires hardware support: the device must support PASID tagging in its DMA transactions, and the IOMMU/SMMU must support per-PASID page-table lookups and fault reporting. Linux 7.0.12 exposes this through IOMMU_DOMAIN_SVA and architecture-specific support via CONFIG_ARM_SMMU_V3_SVA and CONFIG_INTEL_IOMMU_SVM.

Hardware Caches

Address translation would be too slow if every load and store ended by going straight to DRAM. The cache hierarchy absorbs most references, and its behavior is tightly entangled with page-table attributes. Linux rarely manipulates caches directly in normal code, but it constantly shapes their behavior through page flags, alignment annotations, executable permission changes, and architecture-specific maintenance operations.

This section covers the hardware cache hierarchy from the kernel developer's perspective. We start with the physical structure — multiple levels of progressively larger, slower caches — then examine cache lines, false sharing, and the alignment helpers Linux provides to control layout. We then look at how page-table attributes control caching policy (write-back vs. uncached), how coherency protocols keep multi-core caches consistent, and how the kernel performs explicit cache maintenance when the hardware cannot do it automatically. The section concludes with prefetch hints and a summary of how all these mechanisms interact.

Modern cache hierarchy

The textbook single-cache picture is far too simple for a current server or mobile SoC. Modern processors use a multi-level cache hierarchy that trades off size against latency. A typical CPU core in 2025 uses:

  • L1 instruction cache — usually 32 KB to 64 KB, private to one core, 1–4 cycle latency. Holds recently fetched instructions to avoid going to memory on every branch.
  • L1 data cache — usually 32 KB to 64 KB, private to one core, 1–4 cycle latency. Holds recently accessed data for loads and stores.
  • L2 cache — often 256 KB to 1 MB, private to one core or shared by a small cluster, 10–20 cycle latency. Catches L1 misses before they reach the slower L3.
  • L3 cache (Last-Level Cache / LLC) — often several megabytes to tens of megabytes, shared across many cores, 30–70 cycle latency. The last stop before DRAM (100–300+ cycles).

Exact sizes vary by microarchitecture — Intel's recent server cores have up to 3 MB L2 per core and 30+ MB shared L3; Apple M-series chips have 192 KB L1 per performance core; ARM Cortex-A7xx cores use 64 KB L1 and 2 MB L2 — but the structural pattern is now standard on both x86_64 and ARM64.

The important lesson for kernel developers is not a particular cache size; it is that latency increases sharply as a reference misses deeper into the hierarchy. An L1 hit costs 1–4 cycles; an L3 miss to DRAM costs 100–300 cycles — a 50–100× penalty. That is why the kernel worries so much about locality, false sharing, and TLB friendliness. Every extra cache miss in a hot path (the page allocator, the scheduler, the network stack) costs as much as dozens of instructions. Figure 2-18 shows this hierarchy and the widening latency gap at each level.

Cache lines

Caches move data in cache lines, not individual bytes. When the CPU reads a single byte, the entire cache line containing that byte is fetched from memory. On current x86_64 and ARM64 systems, 64 bytes is the standard line size. The kernel treats this as an architecture parameter rather than a universal constant — each architecture defines its own cache geometry:

/* arch/x86/include/asm/cache.h — x86 cache line size */
#define L1_CACHE_SHIFT      (CONFIG_X86_L1_CACHE_SHIFT)  /* typically 6 */
#define L1_CACHE_BYTES      (1 << L1_CACHE_SHIFT)        /* typically 64 */
#define INTERNODE_CACHE_SHIFT  CONFIG_X86_INTERNODE_CACHE_SHIFT
#define INTERNODE_CACHE_BYTES  (1 << INTERNODE_CACHE_SHIFT)

/* arch/arm64/include/asm/cache.h — ARM64 cache line size */
#define L1_CACHE_SHIFT      (6)
#define L1_CACHE_BYTES      (1 << L1_CACHE_SHIFT)        /* 64 */

Note x86's INTERNODE_CACHE_BYTES — this is a larger alignment used for data structures shared between NUMA nodes, where the cost of false sharing is even higher because cross-node traffic goes through the interconnect (QPI/UPI on Intel, Infinity Fabric on AMD).

False sharing

The cache-line granularity creates one of the most insidious performance problems in SMP kernels: false sharing. If two unrelated hot fields share the same 64-byte cache line, and one CPU writes field A while another CPU reads field B, the cache-coherency protocol bounces the entire line between cores even though the logical data is disjoint. Each bounce costs 30–100+ cycles, and in hot paths (scheduler runqueues, network queues, page allocator freelists) this can dominate overall performance.

False sharing is particularly dangerous because it is invisible in source code — two fields that happen to be adjacent in a structure definition may share a cache line, and the resulting performance degradation shows up only in hardware performance counters as excessive cache-line invalidations.

Alignment helpers in Linux

Linux provides cacheline-aware annotations so that frequently accessed fields or objects do not accidentally share the same line with unrelated data. The generic definitions in include/linux/cache.h provide the foundation:

/* include/linux/cache.h — cacheline alignment helpers */
#define __cacheline_aligned                             \
    __attribute__((__aligned__(SMP_CACHE_BYTES),         \
                   __section__(".data..cacheline_aligned")))

#define ____cacheline_aligned_in_smp  ____cacheline_aligned
#define __cacheline_aligned_in_smp    __cacheline_aligned

/* Place data in a read-mostly section to reduce cache pollution */
#define __read_mostly  __section(".data..read_mostly")

In prose, these mean: place the object at cacheline alignment, and in some cases place it in a dedicated linker section so the linker helps preserve the separation. The _in_smp variants are no-ops on uniprocessor builds, since false sharing cannot occur with only one CPU.

Real-world false sharing prevention

Three of the kernel's most performance-critical data structures show how cacheline discipline works in practice.

The scheduler runqueue (kernel/sched/sched.h) groups hot fields together and isolates the lock on its own cache line:

/* kernel/sched/sched.h — struct rq (simplified) */
struct rq {
    /* Hot fields read/written on every schedule() */
    unsigned int        nr_running;
    unsigned int        ttwu_pending;
    unsigned long       cpu_capacity;
    ...
    /* Isolate the lock and switch counter on a separate cache line */
    u64                 nr_switches ____cacheline_aligned;
    raw_spinlock_t      __lock;
};

The memory zone (include/linux/mmzone.h) uses explicit CACHELINE_PADDING macros to separate write-intensive regions:

/* include/linux/mmzone.h — struct zone (simplified) */
struct zone {
    /* Read-mostly zone metadata */
    ...
    /* Write-intensive fields used from the page allocator */
    CACHELINE_PADDING(_pad1_);
    struct free_area    free_area[NR_PAGE_ORDERS];
    ...
    /* Write-intensive fields used by compaction and vmstats */
    CACHELINE_PADDING(_pad2_);
    ...
};

The CACHELINE_PADDING macro inserts enough padding bytes to push the next field onto a fresh cache line. This prevents the page allocator's frequent updates to free_area from bouncing cache lines that hold zone metadata read by other CPUs.

The network device (include/linux/netdevice.h) uses an even more sophisticated approach — __cacheline_group_begin/__cacheline_group_end to define explicit groups of fields that should share cache lines:

/* include/linux/netdevice.h — struct net_device (simplified) */
struct net_device {
    __cacheline_group_begin(net_device_read_tx);
    /* TX-path read-only fields grouped together */
    ...
    __cacheline_group_end(net_device_read_tx);

    __cacheline_group_begin(net_device_read_txrx);
    /* Fields read by both TX and RX paths */
    ...
    __cacheline_group_end(net_device_read_txrx);

    __cacheline_group_begin(net_device_read_rx);
    /* RX-path read-only fields grouped together */
    ...
    __cacheline_group_end(net_device_read_rx);
};

This grouping ensures that the TX path and RX path — which often run on different CPUs — do not contend for the same cache lines. The network stack is one of the most cache-line-sensitive subsystems in the kernel because it processes millions of packets per second.

Write-back vs write-through

Two classic cache policies determine when modified data reaches lower levels of the memory hierarchy:

  • Write-back (WB) — the cache holds modified data and defers writing to DRAM until the line is evicted. This is the fastest policy for general-purpose memory because multiple writes to the same line only generate one memory write.
  • Write-through (WT) — every write propagates to the next level immediately. Simpler to reason about, but slower because every store generates memory traffic.
  • Write-combining (WC) — writes to the same region are combined in a buffer before being flushed as a single burst. Used for frame buffers and GPU memory.
  • Uncacheable (UC) — no caching at all. Every access goes to memory or the device. Required for MMIO registers where the device expects to see every individual access.

Ordinary DRAM-backed kernel and user memory is almost always mapped as write-back normal memory. Write-through, write-combining, and uncacheable mappings are reserved for specific hardware interactions: MMIO regions, frame buffers, persistent memory, and DMA buffers for non-coherent devices.

On x86, page-table bits such as _PAGE_PWT, _PAGE_PCD, and the PAT selection bits contribute to this decision. On ARM64, the AttrIndx field selects a MAIR_EL1 byte such as MT_DEVICE_nGnRE or MT_NORMAL_NC. So the Linux source expresses cache policy in different ways on the two architectures, but the underlying choice—normal cacheable memory versus special I/O behavior—remains the same.

Coherency protocols

A multicore system is usable only if caches stay coherent — when one core writes a memory location, other cores must not silently keep stale copies. Without hardware coherency, every shared-memory operation would need explicit software cache flushes, making SMP programming impractical.

MESI and MOESI (x86)

The classic x86 coherency story is MESI (Intel) or MOESI (AMD), where each cache line is in one of these states:

  • Modified — this core has written the line; no other core has a copy; the line is "dirty" and must be written back to memory eventually.
  • Exclusive — this core has the only copy, but it has not been modified. A write can proceed without bus traffic.
  • Shared — multiple cores have clean copies. A write must first invalidate the other copies.
  • Invalid — this line is not valid in this cache.
  • Owned (MOESI only) — this core has a modified copy and is responsible for supplying it to other cores, but other cores may have Shared copies.

The transitions between these states happen automatically via the cache-coherency interconnect (QPI, UPI on Intel; Infinity Fabric on AMD). The kernel does not explicitly manage these transitions — they are invisible to software. What the kernel does care about is the cost of transitions: moving a line from Modified on one core to Shared on another requires cross-core traffic that can take 40–100+ cycles, which is why false sharing is so expensive.

ARM64 coherency (ACE/CHI)

ARM64 systems implement coherency through interconnect protocols like ACE (AXI Coherency Extensions) or the more modern CHI (Coherent Hub Interface). These protocols serve the same purpose as MESI/MOESI — ensuring that all observers see a consistent view of memory — but use different state machines and interconnect topologies. The kernel-visible rule is identical: if one core writes a cacheable location, other cores will see the update after the appropriate barriers.

Linux normally depends on hardware coherency for all ordinary cacheable RAM and does not need explicit cache-maintenance instructions for inter-CPU communication. Explicit maintenance is required only at the boundaries:

  • DMA with non-coherent devices — some devices cannot snoop the CPU caches, so the kernel must flush or invalidate cache lines before/after DMA transfers.
  • Instruction cache synchronization — after modifying executable code (text patching, module loading, JIT compilation), the I-cache must be invalidated because most architectures do not automatically snoop the I-cache when the D-cache is written.
  • Device memory (MMIO) — mapped uncacheable; coherency protocols do not apply.
  • Architecture-specific aliasing — some older ARM implementations have cache-coloring constraints that require explicit maintenance.

Paging and caching interact

A page-table entry is not only a virtual-to-physical translation — it also specifies the caching policy for every access to that page. Both architectures use a 3-bit index in the PTE to select from 8 possible memory types, but the indirection mechanisms differ.

On x86_64: the PAT (Page Attribute Table)

Three PTE bits — _PAGE_PWT, _PAGE_PCD, and _PAGE_PAT — form a 3-bit index into the MSR_IA32_CR_PAT model-specific register. The kernel programs this MSR during boot with the desired memory-type assignments:

/* arch/x86/mm/pat/memtype.c — PAT MSR slot assignments */
/* Index  PWT PCD PAT  → Memory Type */
/*  000    0   0   0   →  WB  (Write-Back, normal DRAM)       */
/*  001    1   0   0   →  WC  (Write-Combining, frame buffers) */
/*  010    0   1   0   →  UC- (Uncacheable Minus)              */
/*  011    1   1   0   →  UC  (Uncacheable, MMIO)              */
/*  100    0   0   1   →  WB  (duplicate for compatibility)    */
/*  101    1   0   1   →  WP  (Write-Protect)                  */
/*  110    0   1   1   →  UC- (duplicate)                      */
/*  111    1   1   1   →  WT  (Write-Through)                  */

pat_msr_val = PAT_VALUE(WB, WC, UC_MINUS, UC, WB, WP, UC_MINUS, WT);
wrmsrq(MSR_IA32_CR_PAT, pat_msr_val);

Linux provides helpers that hide the PAT bit manipulation behind meaningful names:

/* arch/x86/include/asm/pgtable.h — cache policy helpers */
pgprot_noncached(prot)       /* UC- (uncacheable for MMIO) */
pgprot_writecombine(prot)    /* WC  (for frame buffers)    */
pgprot_writethrough(prot)    /* WT  (rare, specialized)    */

This is why ioremap() and fixmap code care about page protections — they must select the correct PAT index to get uncacheable or write-combining behavior for device memory.

On ARM64: MAIR_EL1

ARM64 uses a cleaner indirection: the AttrIndx[2:0] bits in the PTE select one of 8 byte-sized slots in the MAIR_EL1 system register. Each slot encodes the memory attributes for that type. The kernel programs MAIR_EL1 during early boot:

/* arch/arm64/mm/proc.S — MAIR_EL1 memory type definitions */
#define MAIR_EL1_SET                                                     \
    (MAIR_ATTRIDX(MAIR_ATTR_DEVICE_nGnRnE, MT_DEVICE_nGnRnE) |         \
     MAIR_ATTRIDX(MAIR_ATTR_DEVICE_nGnRE,  MT_DEVICE_nGnRE)  |         \
     MAIR_ATTRIDX(MAIR_ATTR_NORMAL_NC,      MT_NORMAL_NC)      |         \
     MAIR_ATTRIDX(MAIR_ATTR_NORMAL,          MT_NORMAL)          |         \
     MAIR_ATTRIDX(MAIR_ATTR_NORMAL,          MT_NORMAL_TAGGED))

/* arch/arm64/include/asm/memory.h — index constants */
#define MT_NORMAL           0    /* Write-back, read/write-allocate */
#define MT_NORMAL_TAGGED    1    /* Normal + MTE tag storage */
#define MT_NORMAL_NC        2    /* Non-cacheable normal memory */
#define MT_DEVICE_nGnRnE    3    /* Device: no Gather, no Reorder, no Early ack */
#define MT_DEVICE_nGnRE     4    /* Device: no Gather, no Reorder, Early ack */

The distinction between MT_DEVICE_nGnRnE and MT_DEVICE_nGnRE is crucial for MMIO: the former prevents the CPU from acknowledging a write until the device has received it (strongest ordering), while the latter allows early write acknowledgment (slightly weaker but sufficient for most device registers). MT_NORMAL is the default for all DRAM — it enables full caching, read/write allocation, and speculative reads.

The conceptual model is identical on both architectures: a small index in the PTE selects from a register-programmed table of memory types. The practical difference is naming and the details of each type's semantics.

Cache Maintenance Operations

Cache maintenance — flushing data caches, invalidating instruction caches, and the interaction between cache operations and TLB invalidation — is covered in detail in the Cache Maintenance subsection of the Handling the Hardware Cache and TLB section later in this chapter. That section covers CLFLUSH/CLFLUSHOPT on x86_64, flush_icache_range() on ARM64, and the scenarios where TLB and cache operations must be coordinated.

Prefetch hints

The kernel can ask the CPU to speculatively load a cache line before it is actually needed, hiding memory latency in hot paths. The generic API is defined in include/linux/prefetch.h:

/* include/linux/prefetch.h — software prefetch hints */
#define prefetch(x)   __builtin_prefetch(x)      /* prefetch for read */
#define prefetchw(x)  __builtin_prefetch(x, 1)   /* prefetch for write */

These compile to architecture-specific prefetch instructions (PREFETCHT0 on x86, PRFM on ARM64) that bring a cache line into L1 without stalling the pipeline. The kernel uses them extensively in performance-critical paths:

  • Block I/O: blk-mq.c prefetches the next request and its bio before processing the current one.
  • Networking: net/core/dev.c prefetches the next sk_buff in the receive chain before processing the current packet.
  • Memory management: mm/vmscan.c prefetches the next page in LRU lists during page reclaim scanning.

Prefetch is a hint, not a command — the CPU may ignore it if the prefetch queue is full or the cache is under pressure. Used correctly, prefetching can hide 100+ cycles of memory latency in list-traversal loops; used incorrectly, it wastes cache space and bandwidth.

Cache effects on kernel structure layout

Once a structure is shared by many CPUs, its field order can matter almost as much as its meaning. The kernel's approach to structure layout follows three principles:

  1. Group read-mostly fields together — fields that are read frequently but rarely written should share cache lines so a single load brings in all the needed data. The __read_mostly section attribute clusters such data in a separate linker section.
  2. Separate writer fields from reader fields — fields written by one CPU should not share a cache line with fields read by another CPU (to avoid false sharing).
  3. Isolate locks from their protected data — a spinlock that is contended by many cores should be on its own cache line, not packed next to the counter it protects.

The __read_mostly annotation is particularly important for kernel configuration data that is set once at boot and then read on every hot path — such as system-wide feature flags, numa topology information, and scheduler parameters. By placing these in a dedicated .data..read_mostly section, the linker ensures they do not share cache lines with frequently written data.

Table 2-8 summarizes the cache-relevant mechanisms this section has covered, together with where each is defined and what it controls.

Table 2-8. Cache-relevant mechanisms discussed in this chapter
Mechanism x86_64 ARM64 Why Linux cares
Small hot private cache L1 I/D, 1–4 cycle L1 I/D, 1–4 cycle Common-case latency
Shared last-level cache L3, 30–70 cycle LLC / cluster cache Cross-core locality
Cache line size 64 bytes (L1_CACHE_BYTES) 64 bytes (L1_CACHE_BYTES) Alignment and false sharing
Memory-type control PAT MSR (8 slots via PWT/PCD/PAT bits) MAIR_EL1 (8 slots via AttrIndx) MMIO, WC/UC, noncacheable mappings
Coherency protocol MESI/MOESI via QPI/UPI ACE/CHI via interconnect Automatic SMP cache consistency
I-cache sync Not needed (snooped); sync_core() for pipeline flush_icache_range() with IPI broadcast Text patching, module loading, JIT
D-cache maintenance Rarely needed (clflush for PMEM) flush_dcache_folio() for non-coherent DMA DMA buffer consistency
Prefetch hints PREFETCHT0 PRFM Hide memory latency in hot paths
Alignment helpers __cacheline_aligned, __read_mostly Same generic helpers Performance and contention control

Cache maintenance is mostly invisible when all participants are coherent CPUs using normal cached RAM. It becomes explicit and tricky at the boundaries: executable text patching, DMA with non-coherent devices, MMIO, and architecture-specific alias rules. When in doubt, the kernel errs on the side of flushing too much rather than too little — a redundant flush costs cycles, but a missing flush causes silent data corruption.

Translation Lookaside Buffers (TLBs)

If page-table walks had to consult memory on every access, even good caches would not save the system. The TLB is the MMU's answer: a small, extremely fast cache of recent virtual-to-physical translations and permissions. Modern Linux performance is inseparable from TLB behavior. Context-switch cost, huge-page policy, KPTI overhead, lazy MM handling, and page-unmap batching all exist partly to keep TLB maintenance from dominating the machine.

This section covers the TLB from three angles. First, the hardware structure — how TLBs are organized, what they store, and why a miss is so expensive. Second, the tagging mechanisms (PCID on x86, ASID on ARM64) that allow translations from different address spaces to coexist in the TLB. Third, the invalidation machinery — how the kernel ensures that stale translations never cause incorrect behavior, including the flush-or-individual tradeoff, range-based invalidation, and the interaction between global pages and KPTI.

TLB Structure

Most current CPUs use a multi-level TLB hierarchy that mirrors the data cache hierarchy — small and fast near the core, larger and more associative farther away:

  • L1 instruction TLB (ITLB) — typically 64–256 entries, fully associative, translates instruction-fetch addresses. Separate from the data TLB because fetch and data access patterns differ.
  • L1 data TLB (DTLB) — typically 64–256 entries, translates load/store addresses. On modern x86 cores, the DTLB has separate sections for 4 KB, 2 MB, and 1 GB pages.
  • Second-level TLB (STLB or L2 TLB) — often 1024–4096 entries, unified (handles both instruction and data misses from L1 TLBs). Set-associative rather than fully associative to keep lookup latency reasonable.
  • Paging-structure caches — x86 also caches upper-level page-table entries (PGD, PUD, PMD entries) so that a TLB miss can skip part of the walk if the intermediate entries are already cached.

The kernel can discover exact TLB sizes through CPUID on x86:

/* arch/x86/kernel/cpu/common.c — TLB size reporting at boot */
pr_info("Last level iTLB entries: 4KB %d, 2MB %d, 4MB %d\n",
        tlb_lli_4k[ENTRIES], tlb_lli_2m[ENTRIES], tlb_lli_4m[ENTRIES]);
pr_info("Last level dTLB entries: 4KB %d, 2MB %d, 4MB %d, 1GB %d\n",
        tlb_lld_4k[ENTRIES], tlb_lld_2m[ENTRIES], tlb_lld_4m[ENTRIES],
        tlb_lld_1g[ENTRIES]);

A typical Intel server core might report 64 DTLB entries for 4 KB pages, 32 for 2 MB pages, and 4 for 1 GB pages — revealing why huge pages are so valuable for TLB coverage.

What a TLB entry stores

A TLB entry conceptually stores:

  • Virtual page number — the upper bits of the virtual address
  • Physical frame number — the translation result
  • Page size — 4 KB, 2 MB, 1 GB, or other architecture-specific sizes
  • Permissions — read, write, execute, user/supervisor
  • Address-space tag — PCID (x86) or ASID (ARM64), so entries from different processes can coexist
  • Global flag — whether the entry survives address-space switches (typically kernel mappings)
  • Cacheability — memory-type information (WB, UC, etc.)

The cost of a TLB miss

The penalty for a TLB miss is substantial. On x86_64 with 4-level paging, a miss requires four dependent memory reads — one for each level of the page table (PGD → PUD → PMD → PTE) — before the original load or store can complete. With 5-level paging, it is five reads. Each read may itself miss in the data cache, compounding the latency. In the worst case, a single TLB miss can cost 200+ cycles — comparable to a full DRAM access.

This is why the kernel invests enormous engineering effort in TLB management: huge pages reduce miss rates by covering more memory per entry; PCID/ASID tags avoid unnecessary flushes on context switches; lazy TLB mode defers flushes for kernel threads that do not need user translations; and mmu_gather batching amortizes invalidation costs across multiple page unmaps.

Why the TLB matters to Linux policy

Several MM policies that look like pure software questions are really TLB questions in disguise. Understanding why these policies exist requires understanding the TLB cost model:

  • Why are huge pages useful? Because one 2 MB TLB entry covers 512× more memory than a 4 KB entry. With a DTLB that holds only 64 entries for 4 KB pages, a process can cover at most 256 KB of data before every access triggers a TLB miss. With 2 MB pages, those same 64 entries cover 128 MB.
  • Why is KPTI expensive? Because switching between user and kernel page-table roots on every syscall/interrupt can evict or bypass hot translations. Without PCID, this effectively flushes the TLB twice per syscall.
  • Why is unmapping batched? Because each TLB invalidation may trigger cross-CPU IPIs (x86) or barrier instructions (ARM64). The mmu_gather machinery collects multiple unmaps and performs one batch invalidation.
  • Why do per-CPU ASIDs or PCIDs matter? Because they let the CPU retain translations from recently-used address spaces across context switches, avoiding a full TLB flush on every schedule().
  • Why does lazy TLB mode exist? Because kernel threads run in whatever address space they inherited; they do not need user translations, so the kernel can skip TLB flushes for them entirely.

The TLB therefore sits at the junction of performance, correctness, and security. Every decision about page-table management — from the page size to the KPTI design to the context-switch fast path — is shaped by TLB behavior.

Process Context Identifiers (x86_64 PCID)

On x86, the feature is called PCID, for Process Context IDentifier. It is conceptually similar to the ASID mechanisms used on many RISC processors, but the Linux implementation on x86_64 has its own terminology and policy.

arch/x86/mm/tlb.c explains the naming carefully. The code distinguishes:

  • ASID — Linux's internal identifier for an mm
  • kPCID — the PCID written into CR3 for the kernel view of that address space
  • uPCID — the user-side PCID used when KPTI is enabled

The low bits of CR3 carry the PCID when CR4.PCIDE is enabled. The format is summarized by the code comments and helper functions:

  • CR3[11:0] carry the PCID value
  • CR3_NOFLUSH is bit 63

The macro CR3_NOFLUSH is defined in arch/x86/include/asm/processor-flags.h. The helper in arch/x86/mm/tlb.c uses it like this:

static inline unsigned long build_cr3_noflush(pgd_t *pgd, u16 asid,
                          unsigned long lam)
{
    return build_cr3(pgd, asid, lam) | CR3_NOFLUSH;
}

If the CPU accepts that bit, loading CR3 can preserve non-global translations for the same PCID instead of flushing them unconditionally. That is a major performance tool in post-KPTI kernels.

Linux's dynamic ASIDs per CPU

The x86 TLB code does not simply assign one global PCID forever to each mm and stop there. Instead, it keeps a small dynamic working set per CPU. The key constant is in arch/x86/include/asm/tlbflush.h:

#define TLB_NR_DYN_ASIDS    6
