The surrounding comment says, “6 because 6 should be plenty and struct tlb_state will fit in two cache lines.” That tiny comment captures a very Linux sort of trade-off: this is not only a naming-space design, it is also a cache-footprint design.

The conversion helpers in arch/x86/mm/tlb.c are:

static inline u16 kern_pcid(u16 asid)
static inline u16 user_pcid(u16 asid)

kern_pcid(asid) returns asid + 1, reserving PCID 0. When KPTI is enabled, user_pcid(asid) sets the special user bit as well. That lets the kernel keep paired user and kernel TLB identities for the same mm.

Why PCID matters even more after KPTI

Without PCID, frequent CR3 changes would punish every transition between user and kernel page tables — KPTI requires two CR3 writes per syscall (user→kernel on entry, kernel→user on return). Without PCID, each write would flush the entire TLB, doubling the effective TLB miss rate. PCID and the no-flush CR3 reload path recover much of the lost performance by allowing both user and kernel translations to remain tagged and reusable.

This is one of the defining design patterns of the modern x86 MM subsystem: a security mitigation (KPTI) introduces extra address-space roots, and a hardware tagging feature (PCID) rescues the steady-state performance.

The INVPCID instruction

Modern x86 processors provide the INVPCID instruction as a more flexible alternative to INVLPG. While INVLPG can only invalidate a single address in the current PCID, INVPCID supports four invalidation types:

/* arch/x86/include/asm/invpcid.h — INVPCID types */
#define INVPCID_TYPE_INDIV_ADDR       0  /* single VA in a specific PCID */
#define INVPCID_TYPE_SINGLE_CTXT      1  /* all entries for a PCID */
#define INVPCID_TYPE_ALL_INCL_GLOBAL  2  /* everything, including global */
#define INVPCID_TYPE_ALL_NON_GLOBAL   3  /* everything except global */

/* Wrappers: */
invpcid_flush_one(pcid, addr);          /* type 0: one address */
invpcid_flush_single_context(pcid);     /* type 1: one PCID */
invpcid_flush_all();                    /* type 2: flush everything */
invpcid_flush_all_nonglobals();         /* type 3: keep globals */

The kernel prefers INVPCID when available because it is considerably faster than the alternative (a pair of CR3 writes to achieve the same effect). The selection logic in arch/x86/mm/tlb.c checks X86_FEATURE_INVPCID at boot:

/* arch/x86/mm/tlb.c — choosing INVPCID vs CR3 pair */
if (static_cpu_has(X86_FEATURE_INVPCID))
    invpcid_flush_all();    /* fast single instruction */
else
    /* fall back to CR3 write pair */

Global pages and KPTI

x86 defines a _PAGE_GLOBAL bit that marks TLB entries as "global" — they survive CR3 reloads and are shared across all PCIDs. This is ideal for kernel text and data, which are mapped identically in every process. However, KPTI fundamentally conflicts with global pages:

/* arch/x86/mm/tlb.c — global pages disabled under KPTI */
/* If PTI is on, then the kernel is mapped with non-global PTEs,
   and we use per-process kern_pcid/user_pcid pairs instead. */

When KPTI is enabled, the kernel disables _PAGE_GLOBAL for kernel mappings because global entries would be visible in the user page tables — defeating the entire purpose of KPTI. Instead, the kernel relies on PCID tagging to preserve kernel TLB entries across user↔kernel transitions. When KPTI is disabled (on CPUs not vulnerable to Meltdown), global pages remain active and provide better TLB utilization.

Linux 7.0.12 also supports global ASIDs — a mechanism where frequently-used address spaces (such as large databases or VMs) are assigned a system-wide PCID that is recognized across all CPUs. This avoids per-CPU ASID allocation overhead and uses hardware broadcast invalidation (INVLPGB on AMD) instead of IPI-based shootdowns:

/* arch/x86/mm/tlb.c — global ASID pool */
/* x86 has 4k ASIDs (2k when compiled with KPTI)...
   global ASIDs are handed out to frequently-used mm's */

Address Space Identifiers (ARM64 ASID)

ARM64 uses the more traditional name: ASID (Address Space Identifier). Where x86's PCID is limited to 12 bits (4096 values, halved to 2048 under KPTI), ARM64 supports 8-bit or 16-bit ASIDs depending on the CPU implementation — up to 65536 unique address-space tags. This larger pool dramatically reduces the frequency of ASID recycling (and the associated full TLB flush).

In Linux 7.0.12, arch/arm64/include/asm/mmu.h defines:

#define TTBR_ASID_MASK  (UL(0xffff) << 48)
#define ASID(mm)    (atomic64_read(&(mm)->context.id) & 0xffff)

The ASID is embedded in TTBR1 during context switch (as shown in the earlier cpu_do_switch_mm() code), allowing the hardware to distinguish TLB entries from different processes without flushing.

Unlike x86's per-CPU PCID pool of 6 dynamic slots, ARM64's ASID allocation is system-wide with a generation counter. Each mm_struct stores its ASID plus the current generation. When a context switch finds that the stored generation matches the global generation, the switch is a fast cmpxchg — no ASID reallocation or TLB flush needed. When the ASID pool is exhausted, the global generation is bumped and every CPU receives a deferred full-TLB-flush flag.

TLBI instructions

ARM64 invalidates translations with explicit TLBI (TLB Invalidate) instructions. Unlike x86's single INVLPG instruction, ARM64 provides a rich family of TLBI variants that control exactly what gets invalidated — by VA, by ASID, by shareability domain, and by level:

  • tlbi vmalle1is — invalidate all EL1 entries in the inner-shareable domain (equivalent to a full TLB flush across all CPUs)
  • tlbi aside1is — invalidate all entries for a specific ASID (flush one process)
  • tlbi vale1is — invalidate by VA + ASID, leaf entries only (flush one page)
  • tlbi vaae1is — invalidate by VA, all ASIDs (flush one page across all processes)

The kernel wraps these in inline helpers:

/* arch/arm64/include/asm/tlbflush.h — TLBI wrappers */
static inline void flush_tlb_mm(struct mm_struct *mm)
{
    dsb(ishst);
    asid = __TLBI_VADDR(0, ASID(mm));
    __tlbi(aside1is, asid);           /* by-ASID invalidation */
    __tlbi_user(aside1is, asid);      /* also user ASID if separate */
    __tlbi_sync_s1ish();              /* barrier: wait for completion */
}

static inline void flush_tlb_page(struct vm_area_struct *vma,
                                  unsigned long uaddr)
{
    flush_tlb_page_nosync(vma, uaddr);
    __tlbi_sync_s1ish();
}

The __tlbi_user() call is the ARM64 equivalent of flushing the KPTI user-side PCID on x86 — it handles the case where a separate user ASID is in use.

The key difference from x86 is that ARM64 TLBI instructions are hardware-broadcast within the inner-shareable domain. No IPIs are needed — the hardware ensures that all CPUs in the domain see the invalidation. However, explicit memory barriers (DSB ISHST before the TLBI and DSB ISH after) are required to ensure ordering.

Range-based TLBI with TTL hints

Modern ARM64 CPUs (ARMv8.4+) add FEAT_TLBIRANGE — range-based invalidation that can invalidate a contiguous range of virtual addresses in a single instruction, rather than looping over individual pages. This is the ARM64 equivalent of a loop of INVLPG instructions on x86, but implemented in hardware for much better efficiency.

The kernel detects this feature at boot through CPUID:

/* arch/arm64/kernel/cpufeature.c — TLBIRANGE detection */
{
    .desc = "TLB range maintenance instructions",
    .capability = ARM64_HAS_TLB_RANGE,
    .matches = has_cpuid_feature,
    .sys_reg = SYS_ID_AA64ISAR0_EL1,
    ...
}

/* arch/arm64/include/asm/cpufeature.h — runtime check */
static inline bool system_supports_tlb_range(void)
{
    return alternative_has_cap_unlikely(ARM64_HAS_TLB_RANGE);
}

When FEAT_TLBIRANGE is available, the range TLBI instruction encodes:

/* arch/arm64/include/asm/tlbflush.h — range TLBI encoding fields */
TLBIR_ASID   — target ASID
TLBIR_TG     — translation granule (4K/16K/64K)
TLBIR_SCALE  — scale factor for range size
TLBIR_NUM    — number of pages in the range
TLBIR_TTL    — translation table level hint
TLBIR_BADDR  — base virtual address

The TTL (Translation Table Level) field is an optimization hint that tells the hardware which page-table level the invalidation applies to. When Linux knows the exact level (e.g., PMD-level for a 2 MB mapping), it passes the level hint so the hardware can target only the relevant TLB entries:

  • TLBI_TTL_TG_4K, TLBI_TTL_TG_16K, TLBI_TTL_TG_64K — granule encodings
  • TLBI_TTL_UNKNOWN — when the level cannot be determined; performs a broader (but safe) invalidation

If the range is too large (exceeds MAX_TLBI_RANGE_PAGES) or FEAT_TLBIRANGE is not supported, the kernel falls back to a full flush_tlb_mm() — a safe but expensive all-ASID flush.

Flush coalescing: individual vs. full flush

Both architectures face the same tradeoff: when unmapping N pages, is it faster to invalidate each one individually, or to do a single full flush?

On x86_64, the kernel uses a configurable threshold:

/* arch/x86/mm/tlb.c — flush threshold */
unsigned long tlb_single_page_flush_ceiling __read_mostly = 33;

/* If more than 33 pages need invalidation, do a full flush
   instead of 33+ individual INVLPG instructions */

The threshold of 33 is a heuristic — tuned through benchmarking to balance the cost of individual INVLPG instructions against the cost of flushing the entire TLB (which evicts all cached translations, not just the ones being unmapped). The value is exposed via /sys/kernel/debug/x86/tlb_single_page_flush_ceiling for runtime tuning.

On ARM64, the equivalent decision is between range TLBI (when FEAT_TLBIRANGE is available) and full aside1is flush. The range TLBI can handle large ranges efficiently, so the threshold is much higher than x86's 33-page cutoff.

Batched TLB flush during reclaim

The mmu_gather batching described above amortizes invalidation within a single address space (an munmap() or process exit). Page reclaim poses a harder problem: when the kernel reclaims memory under pressure, try_to_unmap() may unmap pages belonging to many different processes in quick succession. Issuing a separate TLB shootdown for each unmapped page would flood the system with IPIs. To avoid this, both architectures select CONFIG_ARCH_WANT_BATCHED_UNMAP_TLB_FLUSH and defer the flush: reclaim records each pending invalidation with arch_tlbbatch_add_pending() and performs a single consolidated flush at the end with arch_tlbbatch_flush().

The two architectures exploit the deferral differently, reflecting their invalidation models:

  • x86_64 accumulates a cpumask of every CPU that has run one of the affected mms, but issues no shootdown yet. At the end, arch_tlbbatch_flush() sends one full-flush IPI to that whole set (flush_tlb_multi() with TLB_FLUSH_ALL) — or, on AMD CPUs with INVLPGB, a single broadcast invlpgb_flush_all_nonglobals() that needs no IPI at all. One flush replaces potentially thousands of per-page shootdowns.
  • ARM64 works the opposite way. Because TLBI is hardware-broadcast, arch_tlbbatch_add_pending() already issues the per-page TLBI immediately — it simply omits the trailing DSB that would wait for completion. arch_tlbbatch_flush() then issues a single DSB to wait for all of the deferred invalidations at once, saving one barrier per page.
Table 2-9. TLB tagging and invalidation mechanisms
Feature x86_64 ARM64
Tag name PCID (12-bit, 4096 values; 2048 under KPTI) ASID (8 or 16-bit, up to 65536 values)
Tag location CR3 bits [11:0] TTBR1 bits [63:48]
Per-CPU pool 6 dynamic ASIDs (TLB_NR_DYN_ASIDS) System-wide with generation counter
No-flush switch CR3_NOFLUSH (bit 63) Always no-flush when ASID generation matches
Global pages _PAGE_GLOBAL (disabled under KPTI) nG bit in PTE; kernel always via TTBR1
Single-address invalidation INVLPG TLBI vale1is
Per-PCID/ASID invalidation INVPCID type 1 TLBI aside1is
Range invalidation Loop of INVLPG (no hardware range) FEAT_TLBIRANGE (single instruction for a range)
Broadcast mechanism IPI-based shootdowns Hardware broadcast (inner-shareable domain)
Flush threshold tlb_single_page_flush_ceiling = 33 MAX_TLBI_RANGE_PAGES
KPTI interaction Dual PCID (kern_pcid + user_pcid) Natural TTBR0/TTBR1 split; __tlbi_user()
Linux helpers kern_pcid(), user_pcid(), build_cr3_noflush() ASID(mm), flush_tlb_mm(), flush_tlb_range()

Handling the Hardware Cache and TLB

Once the kernel changes a page table, the job is not finished. Old translations may still sit in the local TLB, in remote CPUs' TLBs, or in paging-structure caches. Executable mappings may still exist in the instruction cache. Modern Linux wraps hardware maintenance in a layered API: architecture-neutral entry points at the top, architecture-specific shootdown and barrier logic underneath, and batching mechanisms that keep large unmap operations from degenerating into storms of tiny invalidations.

This section covers:

  1. The generic TLB flushing API — the architecture-neutral surface.
  2. x86_64 TLB implementation — generations, IPIs, INVPCID, INVLPGB, and PCID management.
  3. ARM64 TLB implementation — TLBI instructions, range invalidation, barriers, and ASID.
  4. Lazy TLB mode — how kernel threads avoid unnecessary TLB maintenance.
  5. TLB batching (mmu_gather) — deferred invalidation and page-table freeing.
  6. INVPCID and broadcast invalidation — advanced x86 invalidation instructions.
  7. Cache maintenance — instruction-cache coherence, CLFLUSH, and DMA concerns.

The Generic TLB Flushing API

The generic MM code expresses invalidation through a small, architecture-neutral API surface. Each architecture provides its own implementation, but the call sites in generic code (mm/mmap.c, mm/memory.c, mm/rmap.c) use only these functions:

Table 2-10. Generic TLB flushing API
FunctionScopeWhen used
flush_tlb_all()All entries on all CPUs Kernel page-table changes (vmalloc, module load)
flush_tlb_mm(mm)All user entries for mm exec, address-space teardown
flush_tlb_range(vma, start, end)Range within a VMA munmap, mprotect, mremap
flush_tlb_page(vma, addr)Single page COW fault, single-page unmap
flush_tlb_kernel_range(start, end)Kernel address range vmalloc/vfree, set_memory_*

The API is deliberately minimal. The architecture implementations decide internally whether to use per-page invalidation, full flush, IPI-based shootdown, or broadcast instructions — the generic code does not need to know.

x86_64 TLB Implementation

The x86_64 TLB subsystem in arch/x86/mm/tlb.c is one of the most sophisticated pieces of architecture-specific code in the kernel. It manages PCID allocation, TLB generation tracking, lazy-mode optimization, IPI targeting, and (on newer AMD CPUs) broadcast invalidation via INVLPGB — all to minimize the cost of keeping TLBs coherent across a many-core system.

Per-CPU TLB state

Each CPU maintains a struct tlb_state that tracks what address space is currently loaded, which PCID slots are in use, and whether the CPU is in lazy mode:

/* arch/x86/include/asm/tlbflush.h — simplified struct tlb_state */
struct tlb_state {
    struct mm_struct *loaded_mm;        /* which mm is in CR3 */
    u16 loaded_mm_asid;                 /* which PCID slot */
    u16 next_asid;                      /* round-robin counter */

    /* Per-ASID context tracking */
    struct tlb_context ctxs[TLB_NR_DYN_ASIDS];  /* 6 slots */

    /* Generation number: detects stale entries */
    u64 tlb_gen;                        /* last-seen generation */
    ...
};
DECLARE_PER_CPU_ALIGNED(struct tlb_state, cpu_tlbstate);

The TLB_NR_DYN_ASIDS = 6 constant limits the number of concurrent address spaces whose TLB entries can coexist on a single CPU. When a 7th address space is loaded, the oldest slot is evicted and the incoming context gets a fresh PCID.

TLB generation tracking

The generation mechanism is the key innovation that lets x86 Linux avoid unnecessary TLB flushes. Each mm_struct has a monotonically increasing generation counter:

/* arch/x86/include/asm/tlbflush.h */
static inline u64 inc_mm_tlb_gen(struct mm_struct *mm)
{
    /* Atomic increment returns the new generation.
     * The implicit full barrier ensures all PTE updates
     * are visible before the generation becomes visible. */
    return atomic64_inc_return(&mm->context.tlb_gen);
}

When the kernel modifies page tables for an mm, it bumps the generation. Each CPU records the last generation it has seen. On context switch or IPI, the CPU compares its local generation against the mm's current generation — if they match, no flush is needed. If they differ, the CPU knows its TLB entries are stale.

The flush dispatcher: flush_tlb_mm_range()

This is the central function that most TLB flush operations funnel through:

/* arch/x86/mm/tlb.c — simplified flush_tlb_mm_range() */
void flush_tlb_mm_range(struct mm_struct *mm, unsigned long start,
                        unsigned long end, unsigned int stride_shift,
                        bool freed_tables)
{
    u64 new_tlb_gen = inc_mm_tlb_gen(mm);

    struct flush_tlb_info info = {
        .mm          = mm,
        .start       = start,
        .end         = end,
        .stride_shift = stride_shift,
        .freed_tables = freed_tables,
        .new_tlb_gen  = new_tlb_gen,
    };

    /* Try broadcast invalidation first (AMD INVLPGB) */
    if (broadcast_tlb_flush(mm, &info))
        return;

    /* Send IPIs only to CPUs that actually need the flush */
    flush_tlb_multi(mm_cpumask(mm), &info);
}

The decision tree is:

  1. Broadcast path (AMD INVLPGB): If available and the mm has a global ASID, broadcast the invalidation to all CPUs without IPIs.
  2. IPI path: Send IPIs to CPUs in the mm's cpu_bitmap. The should_flush_tlb() function filters out CPUs that don't need the flush (lazy mode, different mm loaded).
  3. Local-only path: If only the current CPU runs this mm, flush locally without any IPI.

The IPI handler: flush_tlb_func()

When a CPU receives a TLB shootdown IPI, flush_tlb_func() runs and decides what to do. This function is the most algorithmically dense part of the TLB subsystem:

/* arch/x86/mm/tlb.c — simplified flush_tlb_func() logic */
static void flush_tlb_func(void *info)
{
    struct flush_tlb_info *f = info;

    /* 1. If this CPU is in lazy mode, just switch to init_mm
     *    and return — the real flush will happen on next switch_mm */
    if (cpu_tlbstate_shared.is_lazy) {
        switch_mm_irqs_off(NULL, &init_mm, NULL);
        return;
    }

    /* 2. Check if the loaded mm matches the target mm */
    if (loaded_mm != f->mm)
        return;  /* wrong mm, nothing to do */

    /* 3. Compare generations: if local is already up-to-date, skip */
    local_gen = this_cpu_read(cpu_tlbstate.ctxs[loaded_asid].tlb_gen);
    if (local_gen == f->new_tlb_gen)
        return;  /* already flushed */

    /* 4. Decide: partial flush (INVLPG per page) or full flush (CR3 reload) */
    if (f->end == TLB_FLUSH_ALL || f->freed_tables ||
        (f->end - f->start) >> f->stride_shift > tlb_single_page_flush_ceiling) {
        /* Full flush: reload CR3 with the same PCID */
        local_flush_tlb();
    } else {
        /* Partial flush: INVLPG for each page in the range */
        for (addr = f->start; addr < f->end; addr += stride)
            invlpg(addr);
    }

    /* 5. Update local generation to mark as current */
    this_cpu_write(cpu_tlbstate.ctxs[loaded_asid].tlb_gen, f->new_tlb_gen);
}

The tlb_single_page_flush_ceiling tunable (default varies by microarchitecture) controls the crossover point. For small ranges, per-page INVLPG is cheaper than a full CR3 reload; for large ranges, a full flush avoids thousands of individual INVLPG instructions.

IPI targeting: should_flush_tlb()

Before sending an IPI to a remote CPU, the kernel checks whether the flush is actually needed:

/* arch/x86/mm/tlb.c — simplified should_flush_tlb() */
static bool should_flush_tlb(int cpu, void *data)
{
    struct flush_tlb_info *info = data;

    /* Skip lazy CPUs — they'll catch up on next switch_mm */
    if (per_cpu(cpu_tlbstate_shared.is_lazy, cpu))
        return !info->freed_tables;  /* except when freeing PT pages */

    /* Skip if this CPU has a different mm loaded */
    if (per_cpu(cpu_tlbstate.loaded_mm, cpu) != info->mm)
        return false;

    return true;
}

The exception for freed_tables is critical for correctness: when page-table pages are being freed, even lazy CPUs must be forced to stop using them, because speculative page-table walks could access freed memory.

Local flush mechanics

The actual hardware flush on x86 can take several forms:

Table 2-11. x86_64 TLB invalidation methods
MethodInstructionScopeWhen used
Single addressINVLPG addrOne TLB entry, current PCID Partial flush (small ranges)
Full PCID flushMOV CR3 (without NOFLUSH)All entries for one PCID Full mm flush, context switch
All non-globalMOV CR3 (any PCID)All non-global entries Full local flush
All including globalINVPCID type 2Every TLB entry flush_tlb_all(), or CR4.PGE toggle fallback
Single-context allINVPCID type 1All entries for one PCID Flush specific address space
BroadcastINVLPGB + TLBSYNC All CPUs, specified ASID/rangeAMD broadcast flush (no IPI needed)

The native_flush_tlb_global() function prefers invpcid_flush_all() when INVPCID is available; otherwise it falls back to the classic CR4.PGE toggle (__native_tlb_flush_global()) which clears and restores the Global Enable bit to flush all entries including globals.

ARM64 TLB Implementation

ARM64 uses dedicated TLBI (TLB Invalidate) instructions rather than register reloads. These instructions are more expressive than x86's INVLPG — they can target specific ASIDs, address ranges, page-table levels, and shareability domains in a single instruction.

The canonical barrier sequence

Every TLB invalidation on ARM64 follows a strict barrier sequence documented in arch/arm64/include/asm/tlbflush.h:

/* ARM64 TLB invalidation sequence:
 *
 * 1. DSB ISHST  — ensure all prior page-table stores are visible
 *                 to the table walker before invalidation
 * 2. TLBI ...   — invalidate the specified translation(s)
 * 3. DSB ISH    — ensure the invalidation completes across the
 *                 inner-shareable domain (all cores in the cluster)
 * 4. ISB        — (only for kernel executable mappings) synchronize
 *                 the instruction stream
 */

The DSB (Data Synchronization Barrier) instructions ensure ordering: the first DSB makes page-table writes visible before the TLBI executes; the second DSB ensures the TLBI has completed on all cores before execution continues. The ISB (Instruction Synchronization Barrier) is only needed when executable mappings are changed.

TLBI instruction variants

ARM64 provides a rich set of TLBI instructions. The kernel uses different variants depending on the flush scope:

Table 2-12. Key ARM64 TLBI instructions used by Linux
InstructionMeaningUsed by
tlbi vmalle1isInvalidate all EL1 entries, inner-shareable flush_tlb_all()
tlbi aside1is, XtInvalidate by ASID, inner-shareable flush_tlb_mm()
tlbi vae1is, XtInvalidate by VA + ASID, inner-shareable flush_tlb_page()
tlbi vale1is, XtInvalidate by VA + ASID, last-level only flush_tlb_range() (leaf PTEs)
tlbi vaale1is, XtInvalidate by VA, all ASIDs, inner-shareable flush_tlb_kernel_range()
tlbi vaae1is, XtInvalidate by VA, all ASIDs (all levels) __flush_tlb_kernel_pgtable()
tlbi rvae1is, XtRange-based invalidation by VA + ASID flush_tlb_range() with FEAT_TLBIRANGE

The last-level hint (vale1is vs vae1is) is significant: when the kernel knows it is only changing leaf PTEs (not intermediate page-table entries), it uses the last-level variant. This allows the hardware to skip invalidating cached intermediate translations (walk-cache entries), which is faster.

Range-based TLBI (FEAT_TLBIRANGE)

Modern ARM64 CPUs support range-based TLBI instructions that can invalidate a contiguous range of addresses in a single instruction, eliminating the need to loop over individual pages. The kernel's __flush_tlb_range_op() function encodes the range parameters into the TLBI operand:

/* arch/arm64/include/asm/tlbflush.h — simplified range TLBI */
#define __flush_tlb_range_op(op, start, pages, stride, asid, tlb_level, ...)  \
    /* Encode range: base VA, number of pages, scale, TTL level */           \
    __TLBI_VADDR_RANGE(addr, pages, scale, ...);                            \
    __tlbi(r##op, ...)  /* e.g., rvae1is for range VA+ASID */

The MAX_TLBI_RANGE_PAGES constant limits the maximum range that can be expressed in a single instruction. For ranges exceeding this limit, the kernel falls back to a loop of individual TLBI instructions or a full ASID flush.

Translation Table Level (TTL) hints

ARM64 also supports TTL hints in TLBI instructions, telling the hardware which page-table level the invalidated entry belongs to. The kernel tracks this via TLBI_TTL_* constants and passes the level through the __flush_tlb_range_nosync() helper. This allows the hardware to invalidate only the relevant walk-cache level rather than all levels, improving performance on implementations with multi-level TLB caches.

Comparison: x86_64 vs ARM64 TLB invalidation

Table 2-13. TLB invalidation comparison
Aspectx86_64ARM64
Single-page invalidationINVLPG addr tlbi vae1is, Xt
Full address-space flushCR3 reload (same PCID, no NOFLUSH) tlbi aside1is
All-CPU broadcastIPI + per-CPU INVLPG/CR3 (or AMD INVLPGB) Built-in: is suffix = inner-shareable (all cores)
Range invalidationLoop of INVLPG (no native range) tlbi rvae1is (FEAT_TLBIRANGE)
Last-level hintNot available vale1is vs vae1is
Level hint (TTL)Not available TTL field in TLBI operand
ASID-taggedPCID in INVPCID operand ASID encoded in TLBI Xt register
Barrier requirementImplicit (serializing instructions) Explicit DSB ISHST / DSB ISH / ISB

The most significant difference: ARM64's TLBI instructions with the is (inner-shareable) suffix are inherently broadcast — they invalidate the specified entries on all cores in the coherency domain without requiring IPIs. x86 must use IPIs for remote invalidation (except on AMD CPUs with INVLPGB support). This makes ARM64 TLB shootdowns architecturally cheaper — no interrupt overhead, no IPI latency, no per-CPU handler dispatch.

Lazy TLB Mode

When a CPU is running a kernel thread (which has no user-space mm_struct), it does not need user-space TLB entries. Rather than flushing immediately, Linux enters lazy TLB mode — the CPU keeps whatever user-space TLB entries happen to be loaded but marks itself as "lazy" so that TLB shootdown IPIs can be deferred.

x86_64 lazy mode

On x86, lazy mode is tracked via a per-CPU shared flag:

/* arch/x86/mm/tlb.c */
void enter_lazy_tlb(struct mm_struct *mm, struct task_struct *tsk)
{
    if (this_cpu_read(cpu_tlbstate.loaded_mm) == &init_mm)
        return;

    this_cpu_write(cpu_tlbstate_shared.is_lazy, true);
}

When a lazy CPU receives a TLB shootdown IPI, the flush_tlb_func() handler takes a shortcut: instead of performing the requested flush, it switches the CPU to init_mm and returns. The stale TLB entries are abandoned — the CPU will load fresh entries when it next switches to a real user mm:

/* flush_tlb_func() lazy handling */
if (this_cpu_read(cpu_tlbstate_shared.is_lazy)) {
    /* Don't flush — just switch to init_mm.
     * Next switch_mm will load correct entries. */
    switch_mm_irqs_off(NULL, &init_mm, NULL);
    return;
}

The paired leave_mm() function exits lazy mode when a CPU in lazy state needs to do real work:

/* arch/x86/mm/tlb.c */
void leave_mm(void)
{
    struct mm_struct *loaded_mm = this_cpu_read(cpu_tlbstate.loaded_mm);

    if (loaded_mm == &init_mm)
        return;

    WARN_ON(!this_cpu_read(cpu_tlbstate_shared.is_lazy));
    switch_mm(NULL, &init_mm, NULL);
}

Stale lazy state on context switch

When a CPU returns from lazy mode to a real user mm, switch_mm_irqs_off() detects that the cached TLB generation may be stale. It performs a memory barrier and compares the local generation against the mm's current generation. If they differ, a full TLB flush is performed to ensure no stale translations survive:

/* arch/x86/mm/tlb.c — switch_mm_irqs_off() stale lazy check */
if (was_lazy) {
    smp_mb();  /* see above comment about lazy + flush_tlb_func() race */
    if (ctxs[prev_asid].tlb_gen != next->context.tlb_gen)
        need_flush = true;
}

The freed_tables exception

Lazy mode optimization has one important exception: when freed_tables is set in the flush info, even lazy CPUs must be flushed. This is because speculative page-table walks can access freed page-table pages — if the memory is reused for a different purpose, the CPU could construct corrupted translations. The should_flush_tlb() function encodes this rule:

/* should_flush_tlb() — lazy skip with freed_tables exception */
if (per_cpu(cpu_tlbstate_shared.is_lazy, cpu))
    return !info->freed_tables;  /* force flush if freeing PT pages */

ARM64 lazy mode

ARM64 implements enter_lazy_tlb() in arch/arm64/include/asm/mmu_context.h. Rather than using a flag, it points TTBR0 to the reserved zero-page table via update_saved_ttbr0(tsk, &init_mm). This is simpler than x86's approach because ARM64's broadcast TLBI instructions eliminate the IPI-based shootdown complexity — there is no need for a "should I skip this CPU?" filter.

TLB Batching (mmu_gather)

Unmapping a large range one page at a time would be disastrous if each cleared PTE triggered an immediate TLB shootdown. Consider a munmap() of a 1 GB region with 4 KB pages: that's 262,144 pages. Without batching, this would require 262,144 individual IPI rounds — each one interrupting all CPUs running the target process.

Linux solves this with struct mmu_gather, a transaction-like abstraction that batches TLB invalidation and deferred page freeing:

The mmu_gather structure

/* include/asm-generic/tlb.h — key fields of struct mmu_gather */
struct mmu_gather {
    struct mm_struct    *mm;             /* target address space */

    /* Range tracking */
    unsigned long       start;           /* lowest flushed address */
    unsigned long       end;             /* highest flushed address */

    /* What changed? (flags) */
    unsigned int        fullmm    : 1;   /* entire mm being torn down */
    unsigned int        need_flush_all : 1;
    unsigned int        freed_tables : 1; /* page-table pages were freed */
    unsigned int        delayed_rmap : 1; /* rmap removal deferred */

    /* Page-size tracking for stride optimization */
    unsigned int        cleared_ptes  : 1;  /* 4 KB PTEs cleared */
    unsigned int        cleared_pmds  : 1;  /* 2 MB PMDs cleared */
    unsigned int        cleared_puds  : 1;  /* 1 GB PUDs cleared */

    /* Batch of freed pages */
    unsigned int        batch_count;
    struct mmu_gather_batch *active;     /* current batch */
    struct mmu_gather_batch local;       /* inline first batch */
    struct page         *__pages[MMU_GATHER_BUNDLE];  /* inline page array */

    ...
};

The structure accumulates pages to free, tracks the address range that was modified, and records which page-table levels were affected (for stride calculation during the flush).

The gather/flush/free lifecycle

The typical usage pattern looks like a transaction:

/* Typical mmu_gather usage in mm/memory.c */
struct mmu_gather tlb;

/* 1. Begin the transaction */
tlb_gather_mmu(&tlb, mm);          /* or tlb_gather_mmu_fullmm() for exit */

/* 2. For each VMA in the range: */
tlb_start_vma(&tlb, vma);
    /* Clear PTEs, queue pages for freeing */
    ptep_get_and_clear(mm, addr, pte);
    tlb_remove_page_size(&tlb, page, PAGE_SIZE);
    /* Or free page-table pages */
    tlb_remove_table(&tlb, ptdesc);
tlb_end_vma(&tlb, vma);

/* 3. Finish: flush TLB + free all queued pages */
tlb_finish_mmu(&tlb);

The key operations:

  • tlb_gather_mmu(): Initializes the mmu_gather, sets mm, clears flags. For full-mm teardown (process exit), tlb_gather_mmu_fullmm() sets the fullmm flag, which enables optimizations (no per-page tracking needed since the entire address space is being destroyed).
  • tlb_remove_page_size(): Queues a freed page into the batch. Pages accumulate in a linked list of struct mmu_gather_batch arrays, each holding up to MAX_GATHER_BATCH pages.
  • tlb_remove_table(): Queues a freed page-table page (struct ptdesc) for RCU-deferred freeing. Page-table pages cannot be freed immediately because lockless software walkers — chiefly gup_fast() — may be traversing them on another CPU without holding any lock. They are collected in a struct mmu_table_batch and freed via an RCU callback.
  • tlb_change_page_size(): Called when the page size changes (e.g., from 4 KB PTEs to 2 MB PMDs). Sets the appropriate cleared_* flag so the final flush knows the stride.
  • tlb_finish_mmu(): The commit step. Calls tlb_flush_mmu() which performs the actual TLB invalidation (via the architecture's tlb_flush() hook), then frees all queued pages and page-table pages.

Batch limits and overflow

The batching system has limits to prevent unbounded memory consumption:

/* include/asm-generic/tlb.h */
#define MAX_GATHER_BATCH      (512 / sizeof(void *))  /* pages per batch */
#define MAX_GATHER_BATCH_COUNT (10000UL / MAX_GATHER_BATCH)  /* max batches */
#define MAX_TABLE_BATCH        (256)  /* page-table pages per table batch */

When the batch fills up (exceeds MAX_GATHER_BATCH_COUNT), an intermediate flush is performed via tlb_flush_mmu() — the accumulated pages are freed, and the gather restarts. This prevents a single huge munmap() from consuming excessive memory in the batch buffers.

RCU-deferred page-table freeing

Page-table pages require special treatment because they may be walked locklessly by concurrent software page-table walkers. The most important is gup_fast() (get_user_pages_fast()), which walks the page tables with interrupts disabled and without taking the page-table lock; hardware speculative walks add a second reason. Both x86_64 and ARM64 therefore select CONFIG_MMU_GATHER_RCU_TABLE_FREE and defer the freeing through RCU:

/* mm/mmu_gather.c — simplified tlb_remove_table() */
void tlb_remove_table(struct mmu_gather *tlb, void *table)
{
    struct mmu_table_batch **batch = &tlb->batch;

    tlb->freed_tables = 1;   /* ensure TLB flush before freeing */
    tlb->delayed_rmap = 1;

    if (*batch == NULL) {
        *batch = alloc mmu_table_batch;
        (*batch)->nr = 0;
    }

    (*batch)->tables[(*batch)->nr++] = table;

    if ((*batch)->nr == MAX_TABLE_BATCH)
        tlb_table_flush(tlb);  /* flush + schedule RCU callback */
}

The RCU callback (tlb_remove_table_rcu()) frees the page-table pages only after all CPUs have passed through an RCU grace period. Because gup_fast() disables IRQs across its walk, that grace period cannot elapse until every in-flight lockless walk has finished — guaranteeing no walker is still dereferencing the freed page.

x86 flush hook

The architecture-specific tlb_flush() function bridges mmu_gather to the x86 TLB flush engine:

/* arch/x86/include/asm/tlb.h */
static inline void tlb_flush(struct mmu_gather *tlb)
{
    unsigned long start = tlb->start;
    unsigned long end = tlb->end;
    unsigned int stride_shift = tlb_get_unmap_shift(tlb);

    flush_tlb_mm_range(tlb->mm, start, end,
                       stride_shift, tlb->freed_tables);
}

The tlb_get_unmap_shift() helper computes the stride from the cleared_ptes/cleared_pmds/cleared_puds flags — if only 2 MB PMDs were cleared, the flush can use a 2 MB stride instead of walking every 4 KB address.

INVPCID and Broadcast Invalidation

Modern x86 CPUs provide dedicated invalidation instructions beyond the basic INVLPG:

INVPCID

The INVPCID instruction (Intel, since Haswell) provides four invalidation types:

/* arch/x86/include/asm/invpcid.h */
#define INVPCID_TYPE_INDIV_ADDR         0  /* individual address + PCID */
#define INVPCID_TYPE_SINGLE_CTXT        1  /* all entries for one PCID */
#define INVPCID_TYPE_ALL_INCL_GLOBAL    2  /* everything, including global */
#define INVPCID_TYPE_ALL_NON_GLOBAL     3  /* everything except global */

Linux uses invpcid_flush_all() (type 2) as the preferred implementation of native_flush_tlb_global() — it's faster than the legacy CR4.PGE toggle method. Type 1 (invpcid_flush_single_context()) can flush all entries for a specific PCID without affecting other address spaces.

INVLPGB (AMD broadcast invalidation)

AMD CPUs with the INVLPGB feature can broadcast TLB invalidations to all CPUs in the system without IPIs. This is a significant performance advantage for workloads with frequent TLB shootdowns:

/* arch/x86/include/asm/tlb.h — simplified INVLPGB usage */
/* INVLPGB: broadcast invalidation, weakly ordered */
__invlpgb(asid, pcid, addr, nr_pages, flags);
/* TLBSYNC: wait for all INVLPGB operations to complete */
__tlbsync();

The kernel's broadcast_tlb_flush() function in arch/x86/mm/tlb.c uses INVLPGB when available. The mm must have a global ASID — a system-wide ASID that all CPUs agree on — for broadcast invalidation to work. The global ASID space is managed separately from the per-CPU dynamic PCID pool.

After issuing INVLPGB, the kernel must execute TLBSYNC to wait for completion. INVLPGB is weakly ordered — without TLBSYNC, subsequent memory accesses might proceed before the invalidation has been applied on all CPUs.

Cache Maintenance

TLB invalidation handles the translation cache, but the data and instruction caches present their own coherence requirements. Changing page attributes that affect executability or memory type requires additional cache maintenance beyond TLB flushing.

x86_64 cache maintenance

x86_64 maintains hardware-coherent data caches — normal stores are automatically visible to all CPUs without explicit cache flushes. However, explicit cache control is needed for:

  • Non-temporal stores / persistent memory: The CLFLUSH, CLFLUSHOPT, and CLWB instructions flush or write-back cache lines to memory. Linux wraps these in clflush_cache_range() from arch/x86/mm/pat/set_memory.c:
/* arch/x86/mm/pat/set_memory.c */
void clflush_cache_range(void *vaddr, unsigned int size)
{
    const unsigned long clflush_size = boot_cpu_data.x86_clflush_size;
    void *p = (void *)((unsigned long)vaddr & ~(clflush_size - 1));
    void *vend = vaddr + size;

    for (; p < vend; p += clflush_size)
        clflushopt(p);
    mb();
}
  • Instruction-cache coherence: On x86, instruction caches are automatically coherent with data caches — stores to executable memory are immediately visible to the instruction fetch unit. This is not the case on ARM64, which is why x86 text-patching code does not need explicit I-cache maintenance.

ARM64 cache maintenance

ARM64 does not guarantee instruction-cache coherence with the data cache. When the kernel modifies executable code (module loading, ftrace patching, kprobes), it must explicitly clean the data cache and invalidate the instruction cache:

/* arch/arm64/include/asm/cacheflush.h — simplified flush_icache_range() */
void flush_icache_range(unsigned long start, unsigned long end)
{
    /* Clean the D-cache to Point of Unification (PoU)
     * and invalidate the I-cache */
    caches_clean_inval_pou(start, end);

    /* Force all CPUs to see the updated instructions */
    kick_all_cpus_sync();
}

The kick_all_cpus_sync() call sends a synchronization event to all CPUs because the I-cache invalidation is local on ARM64 — each CPU has its own instruction cache that must be separately invalidated. The icache_inval_all_pou() function uses ic ialluis (Invalidate I-cache, Inner Shareable) for system-wide invalidation.

For DMA, ARM64 uses explicit D-cache maintenance to ensure that data written by the CPU is visible to DMA devices (clean) and that data written by DMA devices is visible to the CPU (invalidate). This is handled by the DMA mapping layer rather than the TLB subsystem, but it uses the same underlying cache maintenance instructions.

Cache-TLB interaction

The interplay between caches and TLBs matters in several kernel paths:

Table 2-14. Cache-TLB interaction scenarios
ScenarioTLB actionCache actionPlatform notes
Change PTE permissions (mprotect)Flush TLB for rangeNone Both architectures
Change memory type (PAT/MAIR)Flush TLB for range CLFLUSH on x86, D-cache clean on ARM64 Required to prevent stale cached data with wrong attributes
Text patching (ftrace, kprobes)Flush TLB if mapping changed None on x86, I-cache invalidate on ARM64 ARM64 requires explicit I/D coherence
Module load (new executable pages)New mapping, no flush needed None on x86, I-cache invalidate on ARM64 New pages have no stale TLB entries
DMA buffer setupNone (physical access) D-cache clean/invalidate on ARM64 x86 DMA is cache-coherent; ARM64 often is not

Paging in Linux

Hardware page walkers are architecture-specific, but Linux tries hard to present one software model to the rest of the kernel. That model is now five levels deep whether or not the hardware really is. Once one understands the folding rules, the wrapper types, and the accessor macros, the generic MM code becomes much easier to read. The abstraction is not cosmetic; it is what allows the same core VM logic to support x86_64, ARM64, RISC-V, s390, and many other architectures without forking the entire memory-management subsystem.

This section traces the entire software page-table layer from top to bottom: the five-level model and its folding rules, the typed wrappers and address-field macros, the accessor and mutator vocabulary, the generic page-table walk framework, entry validity and presence semantics, allocation and deallocation through the ptdesc infrastructure, and finally the VMA-to-PTE protection mapping that connects user-space permissions to hardware page-table bits. Every subsection is grounded in kernel 7.0.12 source code so the reader can trace each abstraction back to a specific file and function.

Compile-time consistency guarantee

Before any of this machinery runs, the kernel verifies at compile time that CONFIG_PGTABLE_LEVELS agrees with the folding headers actually included. The check in include/linux/pgtable.h is:

#if 5 - defined(__PAGETABLE_P4D_FOLDED) - defined(__PAGETABLE_PUD_FOLDED) - \
        defined(__PAGETABLE_PMD_FOLDED) != CONFIG_PGTABLE_LEVELS
#error CONFIG_PGTABLE_LEVELS is not consistent with __PAGETABLE_{P4D,PUD,PMD}_FOLDED
#endif

The arithmetic is elegant: five levels minus one for each folded level must equal the configured level count. If an architecture header mistakenly folds the wrong level, the build fails immediately with a clear error message. This prevents silent misconfigurations that could produce subtly corrupt page tables at runtime.

On x86_64, the number of real paging levels can change at boot time (4-level vs. 5-level LA57), so the kernel provides a runtime check as well:

/* arch/x86/include/asm/pgtable_64_types.h */
#define pgtable_l5_enabled() cpu_feature_enabled(X86_FEATURE_LA57)

This allows a single x86_64 kernel binary to boot on both 4-level and 5-level hardware. The PGDIR_SHIFT and PTRS_PER_P4D values are resolved to their runtime variants (pgdir_shift and ptrs_per_p4d) via static keys, making the level detection essentially zero-cost on the hot path.

The Five-Level Paging Model

Since Linux 4.11, the generic software page-table vocabulary is:

  • PGD — Page Global Directory
  • P4D — Page 4th-level Directory
  • PUD — Page Upper Directory
  • PMD — Page Middle Directory
  • PTE — Page Table Entry

The key design idea: every architecture walks through all five levels in the source code, but folded levels compile down to nothing — the compiler eliminates the single-entry indirection entirely. This means the same pgd_offset() → p4d_offset() → pud_offset() → pmd_offset() → pte_offset() chain works on a 2-level i386 and a 5-level x86_64 without any #ifdef.

This is true even on systems whose hardware has fewer levels. The “missing” levels are represented by folded headers. The three generic fold implementations are:

  • include/asm-generic/pgtable-nop4d.h
  • include/asm-generic/pgtable-nopud.h
  • include/asm-generic/pgtable-nopmd.h

The folding logic is intentionally mechanical. For example, pgtable-nop4d.h says:

#define __PAGETABLE_P4D_FOLDED 1

typedef struct { pgd_t pgd; } p4d_t;

#define P4D_SHIFT       PGDIR_SHIFT
#define PTRS_PER_P4D        1

and then turns set_pgd() into set_p4d():

#define set_pgd(pgdptr, pgdval) set_p4d((p4d_t *)(pgdptr), (p4d_t) { pgdval })

Likewise, pgtable-nopud.h makes PTRS_PER_PUD equal 1 and maps set_p4d() onto set_pud(), and pgtable-nopmd.h maps set_pud() onto set_pmd().

This means generic code can always write:

pgd = pgd_offset(mm, addr);
p4d = p4d_offset(pgd, addr);
pud = pud_offset(p4d, addr);
pmd = pmd_offset(pud, addr);

without caring how many real hardware tables exist on the current architecture. If a level is folded, its offset helper simply returns the containing object.

Why folding matters to readability

Without folding, generic MM code would need architecture-dependent #ifdef cascades in every page-table walk. With folding, all the control flow can stay in one place, and only the type wrappers and helper macros differ by architecture. This is one of the quiet triumphs of modern Linux MM design. It turns hardware diversity into local header complexity instead of system-wide source fragmentation.

Page Table Types

Linux wraps raw page-table values in small structs for type safety. On x86_64, arch/x86/include/asm/pgtable_64_types.h defines:

typedef unsigned long   pteval_t;
typedef unsigned long   pmdval_t;
typedef unsigned long   pudval_t;
typedef unsigned long   p4dval_t;
typedef unsigned long   pgdval_t;
typedef unsigned long   pgprotval_t;

typedef struct { pteval_t pte; } pte_t;
typedef struct { pmdval_t pmd; } pmd_t;

and similarly for the higher levels.

On ARM64, arch/arm64/include/asm/pgtable-types.h defines a unified 64-bit descriptor type:

typedef u64 ptdesc_t;
typedef ptdesc_t pteval_t;
typedef ptdesc_t pmdval_t;
typedef ptdesc_t pudval_t;
typedef ptdesc_t p4dval_t;
typedef ptdesc_t pgdval_t;

then wraps them the same way:

typedef struct { pteval_t pte; } pte_t;
#define pte_val(x)  ((x).pte)
#define __pte(x)    ((pte_t) { (x) } )

The common pattern is therefore:

  • pte_t, pmd_t, pud_t, p4d_t, pgd_t — typed wrappers around raw integer values
  • pgprot_t — wrapper around protection bits

The conversion helpers follow the same naming scheme everywhere:

  • constructors: __pte(), __pmd(), __pud(), __p4d(), __pgd(), __pgprot()
  • extractors: pte_val(), pmd_val(), pud_val(), p4d_val(), pgd_val(), pgprot_val()

This is not mere style. It prevents many accidental type mismatches and makes generic inline helpers possible without using raw integers everywhere.

Linear Address Fields

The following macros simplify page-table handling by providing the shift amounts, sizes, masks, and entry counts for each level of the paging hierarchy. Understanding them is essential for reading any MM code in the kernel.

PAGE_SHIFT, PAGE_SIZE, PAGE_MASK

PAGE_SHIFT specifies the length in bits of the Offset field — the portion of a linear address that selects a byte within a page. On both x86_64 and ARM64 (with 4 KB pages), it yields the value 12.

Because all byte addresses within a page must fit in the Offset field, the size of a page is 2PAGE_SHIFT. PAGE_SIZE computes this directly:

#define PAGE_SHIFT  12
#define PAGE_SIZE   (_AC(1, UL) << PAGE_SHIFT)   /* = 4096 */
#define PAGE_MASK   (~(PAGE_SIZE - 1))            /* = 0xfffff000 */

PAGE_MASK is used to mask out the Offset bits, leaving only the page-aligned portion of an address. On ARM64, the page size is configurable — PAGE_SHIFT can be 14 (16 KB pages) or 16 (64 KB pages), and all dependent geometry shifts accordingly.

PMD_SHIFT, PMD_SIZE, PMD_MASK

PMD_SHIFT is the total number of bits in the Offset + PTE index fields combined — the logarithm of the size of the area that a single Page Middle Directory entry can map (i.e., the size of one Page Table's coverage).

/* arch/x86/include/asm/pgtable_64_types.h */
#define PMD_SHIFT   21
  • PMD_SIZE = 1 << 21 = 2 MB — the area mapped by one PMD entry (one Page Table).
  • PMD_MASK = ~(PMD_SIZE - 1) — masks the Offset and PTE index fields.

This is also the size of a 2 MB huge page when the PSE/huge-page bit is set at the PMD level.

PUD_SHIFT, PUD_SIZE, PUD_MASK

PUD_SHIFT is the logarithm of the size of the area a single Page Upper Directory entry can map (the total coverage of one PMD table).

#define PUD_SHIFT   30
  • PUD_SIZE = 1 << 30 = 1 GB — the area mapped by one PUD entry.
  • PUD_MASK = ~(PUD_SIZE - 1) — masks the Offset, PTE, and PMD fields.

On x86_64, a 1 GB huge page is a leaf entry at this level.

P4D_SHIFT

P4D_SHIFT is the logarithm of the size of the area a single P4D entry can map.

#define P4D_SHIFT   39
  • P4D_SIZE = 1 << 39 = 512 GB per P4D entry.

On 4-level x86_64 systems, the P4D level is folded (PTRS_PER_P4D = 1), so P4D_SHIFT equals PGDIR_SHIFT and the P4D is transparent. On 5-level systems (LA57), the P4D is a real table with 512 entries.

PGDIR_SHIFT, PGDIR_SIZE, PGDIR_MASK

PGDIR_SHIFT is the logarithm of the area that a single Page Global Directory entry can map — the widest span in the hierarchy.

/* x86_64: runtime-dependent */
#define PGDIR_SHIFT pgdir_shift
  • On 4-level x86_64: PGDIR_SHIFT = 39, each PGD entry covers 512 GB.
  • On 5-level x86_64: PGDIR_SHIFT = 48, each PGD entry covers 256 TB.

The crucial point is that PGDIR_SHIFT is runtime-dependent on x86_64 because Linux supports both 4-level and 5-level hardware with a single kernel binary (determined at boot by pgtable_l5_enabled()).

PTRS_PER_PTE, PTRS_PER_PMD, PTRS_PER_PUD, PTRS_PER_P4D, PTRS_PER_PGD

These macros compute the number of entries in each level of page table:

MacroValueMeaning
PTRS_PER_PTE512Entries in a Page Table
PTRS_PER_PMD512Entries in a Page Middle Directory
PTRS_PER_PUD512Entries in a Page Upper Directory
PTRS_PER_P4D512 or 1512 on 5-level; 1 (folded) on 4-level
PTRS_PER_PGD512Entries in the Page Global Directory

Each entry is 8 bytes (64-bit), so each table page is 512 × 8 = 4096 bytes — exactly one page. This is the fundamental design constraint that makes 9-bit index fields natural for 4 KB pages.

ARM64 geometry

ARM64 computes the same macros from PAGE_SHIFT, CONFIG_PGTABLE_LEVELS, and VA_BITS. For a common 4 KB / 48-bit configuration:

  • PAGE_SHIFT = 12, PMD_SHIFT = 21, PUD_SHIFT = 30, P4D_SHIFT = 39 (if four levels)

That parallelism with x86_64 is convenient, but changing the granule to 16 KB or 64 KB moves all the shifts. ARM64 code uses ARM64_HW_PGTABLE_LEVEL_SHIFT() instead of hard-coding values.

Table 2-15. Important address-field macros
Macrox86_64ARM64 (4 KB/48-bit)Meaning
PAGE_SHIFT1212log₂ of page size; length of Offset field
PAGE_SIZE40964096base page size in bytes
PAGE_MASK0xf…f0000xf…f000masks Offset bits to zero
PMD_SHIFT2121log₂ of area mapped by one PMD entry
PMD_SIZE2 MB2 MBarea per PMD entry; huge page size
PMD_MASK~(PMD_SIZE-1)~(PMD_SIZE-1)masks Offset + PTE fields
PUD_SHIFT3030log₂ of area mapped by one PUD entry
PUD_SIZE1 GB1 GBarea per PUD entry; 1 GB huge page
P4D_SHIFT3939 if presentlog₂ of area mapped by one P4D entry
PGDIR_SHIFT39 or 48derivedlog₂ of area mapped by one PGD entry
PTRS_PER_PTE512512entries per Page Table
PTRS_PER_PMD512512entries per PMD
PTRS_PER_PUD512512entries per PUD
PTRS_PER_P4D512 or 11 or 512entries per P4D (1 if folded)
PTRS_PER_PGD512512entries per PGD

Page Table Entry Accessors

Once raw values are wrapped in types, Linux builds a large vocabulary of accessors and mutators on top. These helpers are what make page-table code readable.

Read helpers

On x86_64, arch/x86/include/asm/pgtable.h defines representative helpers such as:

static inline bool pte_dirty(pte_t pte)
static inline int pte_young(pte_t pte)
static inline int pte_write(pte_t pte)
static inline int pte_exec(pte_t pte)
static inline bool pmd_leaf(pmd_t pte)
static inline int pte_none(pte_t pte)
static inline int pte_same(pte_t a, pte_t b)
static inline int pte_present(pte_t a)
static inline int pmd_none(pmd_t pmd)

These map almost directly to the chapter's conceptual questions:

  • Is the mapping present?
  • Is it writable?
  • Was it accessed?
  • Was it dirtied?
  • Is it executable?
  • Is a PMD entry a leaf huge-page mapping or a pointer to another table?
  • Is this entry empty?
  • Are two entries bitwise identical?

ARM64 defines analogous helpers in arch/arm64/include/asm/pgtable.h:

#define pte_present(pte)    (pte_valid(pte) || pte_present_invalid(pte))
#define pte_young(pte)      (!!(pte_val(pte) & PTE_AF))
#define pte_write(pte)      (!!(pte_val(pte) & PTE_WRITE))
#define pte_rdonly(pte)     (!!(pte_val(pte) & PTE_RDONLY))
#define pte_user(pte)       (!!(pte_val(pte) & PTE_USER))
#define pte_none(pte)       (!pte_val(pte))

The exact bit meanings differ, but the software questions are the same. That is precisely why the generic VM code can express operations at the accessor level rather than in raw bit tests.

Write helpers

The mutators are equally important. Representative x86 helpers include:

  • pte_mkwrite()
  • pte_mkdirty()
  • pte_mkyoung()
  • pte_wrprotect()
  • pte_mkold()
  • set_pte()
  • set_pmd()
  • set_pud()
  • set_p4d()
  • set_pgd()
  • pte_clear()
  • pmd_clear()

From arch/x86/include/asm/pgtable.h:

static inline pte_t pte_wrprotect(pte_t pte)
static inline pte_t pte_mkold(pte_t pte)
static inline pte_t pte_mkdirty(pte_t pte)
static inline pte_t pte_mkyoung(pte_t pte)
pte_t pte_mkwrite(pte_t pte, struct vm_area_struct *vma);

These do not all mean “flip a single bit.” For example, modern x86 shadow-stack semantics complicate what writable versus dirty means, so helpers may need to move state between hardware and software bits to preserve invariants. That is why Linux insists that MM code use accessors instead of open-coding bit operations.

Atomic pointer operations

Several helpers operate on a pointer to a page-table entry (pte_t *) rather than on the entry value itself. They perform read-modify-write updates atomically, which is critical because hardware may set Accessed or Dirty bits concurrently:

FunctionDescription
ptep_get_and_clear(mm, addr, ptep) Atomically reads a PTE, clears it, and returns the old value. Essential before unmapping a page so the kernel can inspect Dirty/Accessed state.
ptep_set_wrprotect(mm, addr, ptep) Atomically clears the R/W bit. On x86_64 this uses cmpxchg() to avoid racing with hardware Dirty-bit updates.
ptep_set_access_flags(vma, addr, ptep, entry, dirty) If the Dirty flag is set, updates the PTE to the new entry value and flushes the TLB for addr. Used by the page-fault path to upgrade permissions without a full unmap/remap cycle.
pte_modify(pte, newprot) Replaces the protection bits in pte with newprot while preserving the page-frame address. On modern x86, this must also handle shadow-stack and dirty-write invariants.

From arch/x86/include/asm/pgtable.h:

static inline pte_t ptep_get_and_clear(struct mm_struct *mm,
                                       unsigned long addr, pte_t *ptep)
{
    pte_t pte = native_ptep_get_and_clear(ptep);
    return pte;
}

static inline void ptep_set_wrprotect(struct mm_struct *mm,
                                      unsigned long addr, pte_t *ptep)
{
    /*
     * Avoid accidentally creating shadow-stack PTEs
     * (Write=0, Dirty=1). Must use cmpxchg to race
     * safely against hardware setting the Dirty bit.
     */
    pte_t old_pte, new_pte;
    old_pte = ptep_get(ptep);
    do {
        new_pte = pte_wrprotect(old_pte);
    } while (!try_cmpxchg(&ptep->pte, &old_pte.pte, new_pte.pte));
}

The cmpxchg loop in ptep_set_wrprotect() is a notable change from Linux 2.6, where a simple clear_bit() sufficed. Modern x86 shadow-stack support (CET) gives the combination Write=0 + Dirty=1 a special meaning ("shadow-stack page"), so the kernel must clear both bits together atomically.

Constructing a leaf mapping

A common helper from include/linux/mm.h is:

static inline pte_t mk_pte(const struct page *page, pgprot_t pgprot)
{
    return pfn_pte(page_to_pfn(page), pgprot);
}

This captures a very common pattern:

  1. take a struct page *
  2. convert it to a PFN
  3. combine that PFN with a protection template
  4. obtain a typed pte_t

The caller can then install it with set_pte() or set_pte_at().

Page Table Walk Functions

Linux provides page-table walking at two levels: low-level offset helpers that descend one level at a time (used by hand-written walks in hot paths), and a high-level generic walk framework (walk_page_range()) that abstracts the entire PGD→P4D→PUD→PMD→PTE cascade behind a callback-driven API. Both are essential vocabulary.

Low-level offset helpers

The Linux walking API is layered from the top down. The generic helpers in include/linux/pgtable.h are representative:

#define pgd_offset(mm, address)     pgd_offset_pgd((mm)->pgd, (address))
#define pgd_offset_k(address)       pgd_offset(&init_mm, (address))
static inline pud_t *pud_offset(p4d_t *p4d, unsigned long address)
static inline pmd_t *pmd_offset(pud_t *pud, unsigned long address)
static inline pte_t *pte_offset_kernel(pmd_t *pmd, unsigned long address)

There is also pte_offset_map() in include/linux/mm.h for the case where the PTE page may need a temporary mapping on architectures with highmem-style constraints:

static inline pte_t *pte_offset_map(pmd_t *pmd, unsigned long addr)
{
    return __pte_offset_map(pmd, addr, NULL);
}

On 64-bit systems without highmem complications, pte_offset_map() often collapses to the same address as pte_offset_kernel(), but generic code uses the abstract helper when it intends the full map/unmap discipline.

Walk example

A canonical kernel page-table walk looks like this:

pgd_t *pgd;
p4d_t *p4d;
pud_t *pud;
pmd_t *pmd;
pte_t *pte;

pgd = pgd_offset(mm, addr);
if (pgd_none(*pgd) || pgd_bad(*pgd))
    return NULL;

p4d = p4d_offset(pgd, addr);
if (p4d_none(*p4d) || p4d_bad(*p4d))
    return NULL;

pud = pud_offset(p4d, addr);
if (pud_none(*pud) || pud_bad(*pud))
    return NULL;

pmd = pmd_offset(pud, addr);
if (pmd_none(*pmd))
    return NULL;
if (pmd_leaf(*pmd))
    /* huge page case */;

pte = pte_offset_map(pmd, addr);
if (!pte)
    return NULL;

A real kernel path may take locks, handle folded levels, inspect huge-page cases, and coordinate with RCU or the fault path, but this skeleton captures the structure. The generic API names each table level explicitly and lets the architecture-specific headers provide the details.

Kernel versus user walks

pgd_offset_k(addr) uses &init_mm and therefore walks the kernel page tables. That distinction matters in code that inspects:

  • the direct map,
  • vmalloc space,
  • fixmap entries,
  • vmemmap,
  • or KPTI shadow structures.

A surprising amount of architecture code in Linux 7.0.12 is really just a set of specialized page-table walkers for those regions. arch/x86/mm/pti.c, for example, contains helper walks that descend through the shadow user copy of the kernel page tables in order to clone safe top-level mappings.

The generic walk framework: walk_page_range()

The hand-written offset walk above is fine for simple one-off lookups, but many kernel subsystems need to iterate over an entire range of virtual addresses, calling back at each level for inspection or modification. Rather than duplicating the five-level cascade everywhere, Linux provides a generic callback-driven walk framework in mm/pagewalk.c.

The caller fills in an mm_walk_ops structure with per-level callbacks:

/* include/linux/pagewalk.h */
struct mm_walk_ops {
    int (*pgd_entry)(pgd_t *pgd, unsigned long addr,
                     unsigned long next, struct mm_walk *walk);
    int (*p4d_entry)(p4d_t *p4d, unsigned long addr,
                     unsigned long next, struct mm_walk *walk);
    int (*pud_entry)(pud_t *pud, unsigned long addr,
                     unsigned long next, struct mm_walk *walk);
    int (*pmd_entry)(pmd_t *pmd, unsigned long addr,
                     unsigned long next, struct mm_walk *walk);
    int (*pte_entry)(pte_t *pte, unsigned long addr,
                     unsigned long next, struct mm_walk *walk);
    int (*pte_hole)(unsigned long addr, unsigned long next,
                    int depth, struct mm_walk *walk);
    int (*hugetlb_entry)(pte_t *pte, unsigned long hmask,
                         unsigned long addr, unsigned long next,
                         struct mm_walk *walk);
    int (*pre_vma)(unsigned long start, unsigned long end,
                   struct mm_walk *walk);
    void (*post_vma)(struct mm_walk *walk);
    int (*install_pte)(unsigned long addr, unsigned long next,
                       pte_t *ptep, struct mm_walk *walk);
    enum page_walk_lock walk_lock;
};

The entry point is walk_page_range(mm, start, end, ops, private). It:

  1. Iterates over VMAs in the [start, end) range.
  2. For each VMA, descends through the page-table levels: walk_pgd_range() → walk_p4d_range() → walk_pud_range() → walk_pmd_range() → walk_pte_range().
  3. At each level, calls the corresponding callback if it is non-NULL.
  4. If no callback is provided for a level, it simply descends to the next level.
  5. If a callback returns a non-zero value, the walk stops early.
  6. The pte_hole callback is invoked for unmapped ranges (gaps between VMAs or unpopulated page-table entries).

Callers must hold mm->mmap_lock (at least for reading) during the walk. The walk_lock field specifies additional per-VMA locking:

walk_lock valueVMA lock held during walk
PGWALK_RDLOCKVMA read lock (allows concurrent faults)
PGWALK_WRLOCKVMA write lock (exclusive access)
PGWALK_WRLOCK_VERIFYVerify caller already holds VMA write lock

This framework is used throughout the MM subsystem:

  • /proc/PID/smaps — iterates page tables to gather per-VMA RSS, PSS, referenced, and swap statistics.
  • madvise(MADV_COLD/PAGEOUT) — walks page tables to deactivate or page out entries.
  • NUMA balancing — scans page tables to set PROT_NONE on selected pages for fault-driven migration.
  • mincore() — walks page tables to report which pages are in memory.
  • KSM — scans page tables to find identical pages for merging.

The existence of this framework means that most subsystems never need to write their own five-level descent loops. It handles huge pages (via hugetlb_entry), transparent huge pages (via pmd_entry checking pmd_leaf()), and memory holes, all in one place.

Validity Checks: the *_bad() Macros

The walk example above tests pgd_bad(), p4d_bad(), pud_bad(), and pmd_bad() at each level. These macros detect malformed page-table entries — entries that claim to point to a subordinate table but contain illegal bit combinations.

On x86_64 (arch/x86/include/asm/pgtable.h), pmd_bad() yields 1 if the entry contains flag bits that should not be set in a non-leaf PMD. The kernel defines a mask of "allowed" flags and checks:

static inline int pmd_bad(pmd_t pmd)
{
    return (pmd_flags(pmd) & ~(_PAGE_USER | _PAGE_RW |
                   _PAGE_ACCESSED | _PAGE_DIRTY |
                   _PAGE_NX | ...)) != _KERNPG_TABLE;
}

In practice, a bad entry at any intermediate level means at least one of:

  • The Present flag is cleared (the subordinate table is not in memory).
  • Unexpected flags are set (hardware corruption, stale entries).
  • The entry is a leaf (huge page) when the walker expected a table pointer.

There is no pte_bad() macro because a leaf PTE is allowed to be not-present (swapped out), not-writable (COW), or not-accessible (PROT_NONE). Those are normal states, not errors.

The pte_present() Subtlety

The pte_present() macro does not simply test the hardware Present bit. On x86_64:

static inline int pte_present(pte_t a)
{
    return pte_flags(a) & (_PAGE_PRESENT | _PAGE_PROTNONE);
}

A page mapped with PROT_NONE (no read, write, or execute permission) has _PAGE_PRESENT cleared — so hardware will fault on access — but _PAGE_PROTNONE is set in a software-defined bit. This tells the kernel that the page is in memory; the fault handler should enforce the PROT_NONE semantics rather than treating it as a missing page.

On ARM64, the same concept exists with PTE_PROT_NONE:

#define pte_present(pte)  (pte_valid(pte) || pte_present_invalid(pte))

where pte_present_invalid() checks a software-defined "present-invalid" state that mirrors x86's PROTNONE logic.

This design has practical consequences:

  • pte_present() returns 1 for both normal pages and PROT_NONE pages.
  • Code that tests only the hardware Present bit would incorrectly treat PROT_NONE pages as swapped-out or unmapped.
  • The distinction matters heavily in the page-fault path (Chapter 9) and in mprotect() / madvise() handling.

Page Table Allocation and Deallocation

Creating and destroying page tables is the final piece of the page-table handling API. In the old Linux 2.6 era, page-table pages were ordinary page frames allocated with get_zeroed_page(). In kernel 7.0.12, they are managed through the ptdesc infrastructure (described in the next section), allocated via pagetable_alloc(), and freed through pagetable_dtor_free() with RCU-safe, mmu-gather-batched teardown.

Allocation functions

FunctionDescription
pgd_alloc(mm) Allocates a new Page Global Directory for the process described by mm. On x86_64, this also clones the kernel portion of the PGD from init_mm and, if KPTI is enabled, allocates a paired user-visible shadow PGD.
p4d_alloc(mm, pgd, addr) Returns the P4D entry for addr. If the PGD entry is empty, allocates a new P4D table page. On 4-level systems this is a no-op (the P4D is folded into the PGD).
pud_alloc(mm, p4d, addr) Returns the PUD entry for addr. Allocates a new PUD table page if the P4D entry is empty.
pmd_alloc(mm, pud, addr) Returns the PMD entry for addr. Allocates a new PMD table page if the PUD entry is empty.
pte_alloc_map(mm, pmd, addr) Returns a mapped pointer to the PTE for addr. If the PMD entry is empty, allocates a new page-table page via pte_alloc_one(), zeroes it, installs the PMD entry, and then maps the PTE page.
pte_alloc_kernel(pmd, addr) Like pte_alloc_map() but for kernel page tables (uses init_mm). No map/unmap discipline needed since kernel PTEs are always directly accessible.
pte_alloc_one(mm) The low-level allocator: obtains a zeroed page via pagetable_alloc(), runs the page-table constructor (pagetable_ctor()), and returns a pgtable_t.

The allocation chain for user-space page tables is therefore:

pte_alloc_map()
  → __pte_alloc()
    → pte_alloc_one()
      → pagetable_alloc()     /* allocate a ptdesc-backed page */
      → pagetable_ctor()       /* initialize spinlock, memcg accounting */
    → set_pmd()                /* install the new PTE table in the PMD */
  → pte_offset_map()          /* return a pointer into the new table */

Per-level constructors and destructors

Not all page-table levels need the same initialization. The kernel provides level-specific constructor functions, each defined in include/linux/mm.h:

/* PTE-level: needs a per-page spinlock for split page-table lock */
static inline bool pagetable_pte_ctor(struct mm_struct *mm, struct ptdesc *ptdesc)
{
    if (mm != &init_mm && !ptlock_init(ptdesc))
        return false;
    __pagetable_ctor(ptdesc);
    return true;
}

/* PMD-level: also needs a lock, plus huge_pte tracking */
static inline bool pagetable_pmd_ctor(struct mm_struct *mm, struct ptdesc *ptdesc)
{
    if (mm != &init_mm && !pmd_ptlock_init(ptdesc))
        return false;
    ptdesc_pmd_pts_init(ptdesc);
    __pagetable_ctor(ptdesc);
    return true;
}

/* Higher levels: just the common ctor (no per-page lock needed) */
static inline void pagetable_pud_ctor(struct ptdesc *ptdesc) { __pagetable_ctor(ptdesc); }
static inline void pagetable_p4d_ctor(struct ptdesc *ptdesc) { __pagetable_ctor(ptdesc); }
static inline void pagetable_pgd_ctor(struct ptdesc *ptdesc) { __pagetable_ctor(ptdesc); }

The design reveals an important invariant: split page-table locking applies only at the PTE and PMD levels, where concurrent page faults from different threads are common. Higher levels (PUD, P4D, PGD) are protected by coarser locks (mm->page_table_lock or mmap_lock), so they need only the common constructor which sets the __folio_set_pgtable() flag and updates the NR_PAGETABLE memory statistic. Kernel page tables (&init_mm) skip the per-page lock entirely since they are protected by the global init_mm.page_table_lock.

The common constructor and destructor pair handle accounting:

static inline void __pagetable_ctor(struct ptdesc *ptdesc)
{
    struct folio *folio = ptdesc_folio(ptdesc);
    __folio_set_pgtable(folio);
    lruvec_stat_add_folio(folio, NR_PAGETABLE);
}

static inline void pagetable_dtor(struct ptdesc *ptdesc)
{
    struct folio *folio = ptdesc_folio(ptdesc);
    ptlock_free(ptdesc);
    __folio_clear_pgtable(folio);
    lruvec_stat_sub_folio(folio, NR_PAGETABLE);
}

The NR_PAGETABLE accounting means the kernel can report exactly how much memory is consumed by page tables (visible via /proc/meminfo as PageTables). The folio pgtable flag distinguishes page-table pages from regular data pages, allowing the allocator and debuggers to identify them.

Deallocation functions

FunctionDescription
pte_free(mm, pgtable) Releases a page-table page. Calls pagetable_dtor_free() which runs the destructor (releases locks, memcg uncharge) and frees the page.
pmd_free(mm, pmd)Releases a PMD table page.
pud_free(mm, pud)Releases a PUD table page.
p4d_free(mm, p4d)Releases a P4D table page. No-op on 4-level systems.
pgd_free(mm, pgd) Releases a PGD, including the KPTI shadow copy on x86_64.

Batched deallocation via mmu_gather

Linux 2.6.11 used clear_page_range() for bulk page-table teardown. That function no longer exists. Modern Linux uses the mmu_gather framework (include/asm-generic/tlb.h) to batch page-table freeing:

tlb_remove_table(tlb, table);

This queues the table page for deferred freeing after the TLB has been flushed for the corresponding address range. The actual free happens in tlb_table_flush() or at tlb_finish_mmu() time. This is critical because:

  1. A page-table page cannot be freed while any CPU might still have cached TLB entries pointing through it.
  2. Batching the frees reduces the number of expensive TLB shootdown IPIs.
  3. On some architectures, RCU-based freeing (call_rcu() on the ptdesc->pt_rcu_head) provides the safety window instead of explicit IPI synchronization.

The x86 TLB-free helpers in arch/x86/mm/pgtable.c show this pattern:

void ___pte_free_tlb(struct mmu_gather *tlb, struct page *pte)
{
    tlb_remove_table(tlb, page_ptdesc(pte));
}

void ___pmd_free_tlb(struct mmu_gather *tlb, pmd_t *pmd)
{
    tlb_remove_table(tlb, virt_to_ptdesc(pmd));
}

The tlb_remove_table() call links the ptdesc into the mmu_gather batch; actual deallocation is deferred until it is safe.

Page Table Descriptor (struct ptdesc)

A recent Linux addition, merged in 6.6, is struct ptdesc. This structure overlays struct page for pages that are themselves page tables. The definition in include/linux/mm_types.h begins:

struct ptdesc {
    memdesc_flags_t pt_flags;
    union {
        struct rcu_head pt_rcu_head;
        struct list_head pt_list;
        struct {
            unsigned long _pt_pad_1;
            pgtable_t pmd_huge_pte;
        };
    };
    unsigned long __page_mapping;
    ...
};

The comment above it is worth paraphrasing carefully. A ptdesc tracks page-table-specific metadata such as:

  • RCU free state for deferred teardown
  • list linkage for architectures that keep page-table lists
  • the owning mm for x86 PGDs
  • page-table lock placement
  • memcg accounting for page tables
  • flags that distinguish kernel page tables from user page tables

The generic MM helpers in include/linux/mm.h then provide conversions such as:

static inline struct ptdesc *virt_to_ptdesc(const void *x)
static inline void *ptdesc_address(const struct ptdesc *pt)
static inline void ptdesc_set_kernel(struct ptdesc *ptdesc)
static inline bool ptdesc_test_kernel(const struct ptdesc *ptdesc)

This is a major modernization relative to the Linux 2.6 era. Page-table pages are no longer treated as anonymous pages that happen to contain PTE arrays. They carry their own first-class metadata, which lets the MM subsystem batch freeing, account memory, and annotate special cases more cleanly.

Split Page-Table Locks

Placing page-table metadata in struct ptdesc enables one of the most important MM scalability features: the split page-table lock. In the Linux 2.6 era, every page-table modification for a process serialized on a single lock, mm->page_table_lock. On a many-core machine, threads faulting different regions of the same address space contended heavily on that one lock. Modern Linux instead stores a dedicated spinlock inside each PTE and PMD page's ptdesc, so faults touching different page-table pages proceed in parallel.

The lock lives in ptdesc->ptl. When the lock is small enough it is embedded in the ptdesc directly; otherwise it is allocated separately and pointed to. This is exactly what the constructors shown earlier set up — ptlock_init() at the PTE level and pmd_ptlock_init() at the PMD level. The feature is controlled by CONFIG_SPLIT_PTE_PTLOCKS and CONFIG_SPLIT_PMD_PTLOCKS; when disabled (or for kernel page tables), the code falls back to mm->page_table_lock.

Generic MM code never touches the lock directly. Instead it uses helpers that resolve the correct per-page lock, take it, and hand back a mapped pointer to the entry:

/* include/linux/mm.h — take the PTE page's lock, map the entry */
pte_t *pte_offset_map_lock(struct mm_struct *mm, pmd_t *pmd,
                           unsigned long addr, spinlock_t **ptlp);

#define pte_unmap_unlock(pte, ptl)  do {  \
    spin_unlock(ptl);                     \
    pte_unmap(pte);                       \
} while (0)

/* PMD-level lock (for THP, page-table installation) */
static inline spinlock_t *pmd_lock(struct mm_struct *mm, pmd_t *pmd);

The canonical fault-path idiom is therefore:

pte = pte_offset_map_lock(mm, pmd, addr, &ptl);
if (!pte)
    return VM_FAULT_RETRY;   /* the PMD changed under us — retry */
/* ... inspect or update *pte while holding ptl ... */
pte_unmap_unlock(pte, ptl);

Two subtleties make this more than a simple lock-and-map. First, since Linux 6.5, pte_offset_map_lock() can fail and return NULL: a concurrent operation (for example, khugepaged collapsing the range into a transparent huge page, or the PMD being cleared) may invalidate the PTE page after the caller read the PMD. Callers must check for NULL and retry — this is why the walk skeletons in this chapter test if (!pte). Second, the lock is keyed to the page-table page, not the address, so two addresses in the same PTE page share a lock while addresses in different PTE pages do not — which is precisely what makes concurrent faults across a large mapping scale.

Why the generic interface matters for later chapters

Chapter 8 will use many of these wrapper types and helpers when discussing allocation, reclaim, and folios. Chapter 9 will use them in the context of mmap(), page faults, and copy-on-write. If this chapter feels accessor-heavy, that is intentional: these macros are the vocabulary the rest of the modern MM subsystem speaks.

Table 2-16. Linux software page-table vocabulary
Concept Representative API
Top-level lookup pgd_offset(mm, addr)
Kernel top-level lookup pgd_offset_k(addr)
Next level p4d_offset(), pud_offset(), pmd_offset()
Leaf lookup pte_offset_kernel(), pte_offset_map()
Presence test pte_present(), pmd_present()
Empty test pte_none(), pmd_none()
Validity test pmd_bad(), pud_bad(), p4d_bad(), pgd_bad()
Huge-page test pmd_leaf(), pud_leaf()
Permission tests pte_write(), pte_dirty(), pte_young(), pte_exec()
Permission changes pte_mkwrite(), pte_mkdirty(), pte_mkyoung(), pte_wrprotect(), pte_mkold()
Atomic pointer ops ptep_get_and_clear(), ptep_set_wrprotect(), ptep_set_access_flags(), pte_modify()
Entry construction mk_pte(), pfn_pte()
Entry installation set_pte(), set_pmd(), set_pud(), set_p4d(), set_pgd()
Table allocation pgd_alloc(), pud_alloc(), pmd_alloc(), pte_alloc_map(), pte_alloc_kernel()
Table deallocation pgd_free(), pte_free(), pmd_free(), tlb_remove_table()
Split page-table locking pte_offset_map_lock(), pte_unmap_unlock(), pmd_lock()
Page-table metadata struct ptdesc
Generic walk framework walk_page_range(), struct mm_walk_ops
VMA→PTE protection vm_get_page_prot(), protection_map[16]

VMA-to-PTE Protection Mapping

When the kernel creates or modifies a page-table entry for a user-space mapping, it needs to translate the VMA's permission flags (VM_READ, VM_WRITE, VM_EXEC, VM_SHARED) into a hardware pgprot_t value that the MMU can enforce. This translation is performed by vm_get_page_prot() via a 16-entry lookup table called protection_map.

The protection_map table

The table is indexed by the 4-bit combination of VM_READ | VM_WRITE | VM_EXEC | VM_SHARED. On x86_64 (arch/x86/mm/pgprot.c):

static pgprot_t protection_map[16] __ro_after_init = {
    [VM_NONE]                                   = PAGE_NONE,
    [VM_READ]                                   = PAGE_READONLY,
    [VM_WRITE]                                  = PAGE_COPY,
    [VM_WRITE | VM_READ]                        = PAGE_COPY,
    [VM_EXEC]                                   = PAGE_READONLY_EXEC,
    [VM_EXEC | VM_READ]                         = PAGE_READONLY_EXEC,
    [VM_EXEC | VM_WRITE]                        = PAGE_COPY_EXEC,
    [VM_EXEC | VM_WRITE | VM_READ]              = PAGE_COPY_EXEC,
    [VM_SHARED]                                 = PAGE_NONE,
    [VM_SHARED | VM_READ]                       = PAGE_READONLY,
    [VM_SHARED | VM_WRITE]                      = PAGE_SHARED,
    [VM_SHARED | VM_WRITE | VM_READ]            = PAGE_SHARED,
    [VM_SHARED | VM_EXEC]                       = PAGE_READONLY_EXEC,
    [VM_SHARED | VM_EXEC | VM_READ]             = PAGE_READONLY_EXEC,
    [VM_SHARED | VM_EXEC | VM_WRITE]            = PAGE_SHARED_EXEC,
    [VM_SHARED | VM_EXEC | VM_WRITE | VM_READ]  = PAGE_SHARED_EXEC
};

Several observations:

  • Write-only is impossible: entries for VM_WRITE without VM_READ map to PAGE_COPY — the hardware does not support write-only pages, so the kernel upgrades write to copy-on-write (read+write after fault).
  • Private writable maps to COPY: a private writable mapping starts as read-only (PAGE_COPY) and gets upgraded to writable only after a copy-on-write fault. This is the basis of fork()'s COW mechanism.
  • Shared writable maps to SHARED: a shared writable mapping is immediately writable since there is no COW — writes must be visible to all mappers.
  • The table is __ro_after_init: it is writable during early boot (allowing architecture-specific patches) but becomes read-only before user space starts.

vm_get_page_prot()

The function that uses this table adds architecture-specific adjustments:

/* arch/x86/mm/pgprot.c */
pgprot_t vm_get_page_prot(vm_flags_t vm_flags)
{
    unsigned long val = pgprot_val(protection_map[vm_flags &
                        (VM_READ|VM_WRITE|VM_EXEC|VM_SHARED)]);

    /* Apply SME encryption mask if enabled */
    val = __sme_set(val);

    /* Clear unsupported hardware bits */
    if (val & _PAGE_PRESENT)
        val &= __supported_pte_mask;

    return __pgprot(val);
}

The __sme_set() call enables AMD Secure Memory Encryption (SME) for user pages when the feature is active — a modern concern that did not exist in Linux 2.6. The __supported_pte_mask filter ensures that bits not supported by the current CPU (e.g., NX on older processors) are cleared rather than causing faults.

How VMA protection flows to the PTE
