The complete path from user-space mmap() to hardware PTE is:

mmap(addr, len, PROT_READ|PROT_WRITE, MAP_PRIVATE, fd, off)
  → do_mmap()
    → vm_flags = calc_vm_prot_bits(prot) | calc_vm_flag_bits(flags)
    → vma->vm_page_prot = vm_get_page_prot(vm_flags)     /* protection_map lookup */
  → (later, on page fault)
    → handle_mm_fault()
      → mk_pte(page, vma->vm_page_prot)                  /* combine PFN + pgprot_t */
      → set_pte_at(mm, addr, ptep, pte)                   /* install into page table */

The vma->vm_page_prot field caches the translated pgprot_t so it does not need to be recomputed on every fault. If permissions change later (via mprotect()), the kernel calls vma_set_page_prot() to update the cached value and then walks the affected page-table entries to apply the new permissions via pte_modify().

Physical Memory Model

A virtual-address chapter would be incomplete if it stopped at page tables. Linux also needs a stable model of physical memory itself: how to represent every PFN, how to split RAM into NUMA nodes and zones, and how to translate between a PFN and its metadata without wasting absurd amounts of memory. The modern answer is SPARSEMEM with VMEMMAP and a metadata layer built around struct page, struct folio, and now struct ptdesc.

This section covers five layers of the physical-memory model, each building on the one below:

  1. Physical Memory Layout — how the kernel discovers RAM via firmware tables (E820, Device Tree) and manages early allocations through memblock.
  2. SPARSEMEM and VMEMMAP — how physical memory is divided into sections and how struct page metadata is mapped into a contiguous virtual array.
  3. NUMA Nodes and Memory Zones — how the kernel groups physical memory by proximity (nodes) and capability (zones), with watermarks controlling allocation pressure.
  4. Page Flags, struct page, and struct folio — the per-page metadata structures, their union-heavy layout, and the folio abstraction that is replacing raw page pointers throughout the MM subsystem.
  5. Memory Hotplug and Device Memory — how modern Linux adds and removes physical memory at runtime, and how ZONE_DEVICE extends the model to non-RAM memory.

Together, these layers answer the fundamental question that every allocator must solve: given a physical page frame number (PFN), where is its metadata, which NUMA node does it belong to, what zone constrains its use, and is it even valid?

Physical Memory Layout

Before the kernel can manage memory, it must discover what physical memory exists, which ranges are usable RAM, and which are reserved for firmware, MMIO, or special purposes. The old Linux 2.6 text described this in terms of the i386 BIOS memory map, the ISA hole (640 KB–1 MB), and a small set of global variables (num_physpages, max_pfn, max_low_pfn, etc.). On modern 64-bit systems the story is richer: the firmware memory map is more complex, the kernel image itself lives at different addresses, and the early allocator (memblock) replaces the old bootmem allocator entirely.

Discovering physical memory

x86_64: the E820 table

On x86_64, firmware provides the physical memory map through the E820 BIOS interface (interrupt 15h, function E820h). The kernel stores this map in a table defined in arch/x86/include/asm/e820/types.h:

struct e820_entry {
    u64 addr;
    u64 size;
    enum e820_type type;
};

struct e820_table {
    __u32 nr_entries;
    struct e820_entry entries[E820_MAX_ENTRIES];
};

The kernel maintains three copies of this table (arch/x86/kernel/e820.c):

TablePurpose
e820_tableThe working copy, sanitized and sorted by the kernel
e820_table_kexecPreserved for kexec to pass to a crash kernel
e820_table_firmwareThe unmodified original from firmware

Each entry is tagged with a type. The modern types defined in enum e820_type are:

TypeValueMeaning
E820_TYPE_RAM1Usable system RAM
E820_TYPE_RESERVED2Reserved by firmware (MMIO, ROM, etc.)
E820_TYPE_ACPI3ACPI reclaimable — usable after ACPI data is copied
E820_TYPE_NVS4ACPI Non-Volatile Storage — must not be touched
E820_TYPE_UNUSABLE5Defective or otherwise unusable RAM
E820_TYPE_PMEM7Persistent memory (NVDIMM)
E820_TYPE_PRAM12Persistent RAM (legacy alias)
E820_TYPE_SOFT_RESERVED0xefffffffSoft-reserved (DAX-only, not for general use)

During early boot, e820__memory_setup_default() copies the firmware table, sanitizes overlapping entries, and sorts them. The result is then passed to memblock: every E820_TYPE_RAM range becomes a memblock.memory entry, and reserved ranges are marked accordingly.

A typical E820 output on a modern x86_64 system (visible via dmesg | grep e820 or /sys/firmware/memmap/) might look like:

BIOS-e820: [mem 0x0000000000000000-0x000000000009fbff] usable
BIOS-e820: [mem 0x000000000009fc00-0x000000000009ffff] reserved
BIOS-e820: [mem 0x00000000000f0000-0x00000000000fffff] reserved
BIOS-e820: [mem 0x0000000000100000-0x00000000bffeffff] usable
BIOS-e820: [mem 0x00000000bfff0000-0x00000000bfffffff] ACPI data
BIOS-e820: [mem 0x00000000fec00000-0x00000000fec00fff] reserved
BIOS-e820: [mem 0x0000000100000000-0x000000023fffffff] usable

Notice that on x86_64 the physical address space can extend well beyond 4 GB (the last range above reaches 9 GB). The old ISA hole at 640 KB–1 MB still exists (the range 0xa0000–0xfffff is reserved), a legacy of the original IBM PC architecture that persists even on modern servers.

ARM64: Device Tree and ACPI

ARM64 has no BIOS. Physical memory is described by either a Device Tree Blob (DTB) or ACPI tables, depending on the platform:

  • Device Tree: early_init_dt_scan_memory() in drivers/of/fdt.c parses /memory nodes from the flattened device tree. Each node specifies a reg property containing (base, size) pairs of usable RAM. The function calls memblock_add() for each range.
  • ACPI: On server platforms (e.g., ARM servers with UEFI), the SRAT (System Resource Affinity Table) and EFI memory map provide the physical layout.

ARM64 also reserves regions from the device tree: early_init_fdt_scan_reserved_mem() processes /memreserve/ entries and the reserved-memory node. The DTB blob itself is reserved by early_init_fdt_reserve_self().

The ARM64 memory setup then continues in arm64_memblock_init() (arch/arm64/mm/init.c), which:

  1. Clamps physical memory to the addressable range
  2. Reserves the kernel image (_text to _end)
  3. Reserves the initial ramdisk (initrd) if present
  4. Calls the Device Tree reserved-memory scanner
  5. Reserves the crashkernel region if configured

The memblock allocator

Both architectures feed their firmware memory maps into memblock, the early boot memory allocator that has completely replaced the Linux 2.6 bootmem allocator. The structure is defined in include/linux/memblock.h:

struct memblock {
    bool bottom_up;
    phys_addr_t current_limit;
    struct memblock_type memory;     /* all usable RAM */
    struct memblock_type reserved;   /* allocated / reserved ranges */
};

The design is simple and elegant:

  • memblock.memory tracks all physical RAM ranges (from E820 or DT).
  • memblock.reserved tracks ranges that have been claimed — kernel image, page tables, DTB, initrd, ACPI tables, early allocations, etc.
  • An allocation request (memblock_alloc()) finds a free range within memory that does not overlap reserved, then adds it to reserved.

Key functions:

FunctionPurpose
memblock_add(base, size)Add a usable RAM range
memblock_reserve(base, size)Mark a range as reserved
memblock_remove(base, size)Remove a range from usable RAM
memblock_alloc(size, align)Allocate memory (returns virtual address)
memblock_phys_alloc(size, align)Allocate memory (returns physical address)
memblock_free(ptr, size)Free a previously allocated range

memblock operates only during early boot. Once the buddy allocator is initialized (see Chapter 8), memblock hands off all unallocated memory to the page allocator and is itself discarded (its code is marked __init).

Kernel image layout in RAM

The kernel image is loaded into RAM as a contiguous block. The linker script (arch/x86/kernel/vmlinux.lds.S on x86_64, arch/arm64/kernel/vmlinux.lds.S on ARM64) defines symbol boundaries that delimit each section. The generic declarations are in include/asm-generic/sections.h:

extern char _text[], _stext[], _etext[];
extern char _sdata[], _edata[];
extern char __bss_start[], __bss_stop[];
extern char _end[];
extern char __init_begin[], __init_end[];

On x86_64, the kernel is typically loaded at physical address 0x1000000 (16 MB) by the boot loader, though KASLR randomizes this. The virtual address is 0xffffffff81000000 (in the top-2 GB region). On ARM64, the virtual base is KIMAGE_VADDR and the physical base is determined by the boot loader, with KASLR providing randomization.

The layout within the image follows this order:

Reserved regions in modern kernels

The following regions are typically reserved via memblock_reserve() during early boot:

RegionReserving code
Kernel image (_text to _end) memblock_reserve(__pa_symbol(_text), _end - _text)
Initial ramdisk (initrd) memblock_reserve(initrd_start, initrd_end - initrd_start)
Device tree blob (ARM64) early_init_fdt_reserve_self()
ACPI tablesACPI subsystem during early init
Crashkernel region reserve_crashkernel() if crashkernel= is on the command line
Early page tablesBuilt by head code before memblock is available
E820 reserved ranges (x86)Marked via e820__reserve_resources()

Key variables describing physical memory

Linux 2.6 described physical memory with variables like num_physpages, totalram_pages, min_low_pfn, max_pfn, max_low_pfn, totalhigh_pages, etc. In kernel 7.0.12, some have changed:

Old variable (2.6)Modern equivalent (7.0.12)Notes
num_physpagesremovedNo longer exists; use totalram_pages()
totalram_pagestotalram_pages() Now an inline function over atomic_long_t _totalram_pages, not a raw variable. Thread-safe for hotplug.
min_low_pfnmin_low_pfn Still exists; PFN of first usable page frame after kernel image
max_pfnmax_pfnStill exists; highest usable PFN
max_low_pfnmax_low_pfn On x86_64, equals max_pfn (no highmem on 64-bit)
totalhigh_pageseffectively 0 Highmem does not exist on x86_64 or ARM64
highstart_pfn / highend_pfnremoved Irrelevant on 64-bit

On x86_64, max_pfn is set during E820 parsing. On ARM64, arm64_memblock_init() sets both max_pfn and max_low_pfn to the same value (since there is no highmem distinction on 64-bit).

The disappearance of highmem is one of the most significant simplifications in the 64-bit transition. 32-bit Linux 2.6 had to manage the lowmem/highmem split at PAGE_OFFSET and the 896 MB direct-map limit. On x86_64 and ARM64, the entire physical address space can be direct-mapped, so all of RAM is "low memory" in the old terminology.

SPARSEMEM and VMEMMAP

The dominant memory model on x86_64 and ARM64 is SPARSEMEM with VMEMMAP, configured by CONFIG_SPARSEMEM_VMEMMAP. Instead of assuming that physical memory is one perfectly contiguous block, Linux represents memory in sections and keeps metadata only for sections that exist. This design scales to terabyte machines with sparse physical address spaces (holes between NUMA nodes, MMIO gaps, hot-plugged DIMMs) without wasting memory on struct page descriptors for non-existent physical ranges.

Section geometry

The section size is architecture-dependent and defined by SECTION_SIZE_BITS:

ArchitectureSECTION_SIZE_BITSSection sizePages per section
x86_6427128 MB32,768
ARM64 (4 KB / 16 KB pages)27128 MB32,768
ARM64 (64 KB pages)29512 MB8,192

Each section is further divided into subsections of 2 MB (SUBSECTION_SHIFT = 21), giving 64 subsections per 128 MB section. The subsection granularity allows partial population within a section — important for memory hotplug and device memory, where new memory may arrive in units smaller than a full section.

The total number of possible sections is NR_MEM_SECTIONS = 1 << SECTIONS_SHIFT, where SECTIONS_SHIFT is computed from the maximum addressable physical bits minus the section size bits. On a 52-bit physical-address x86_64 system, this yields 225 = 33 million possible sections — though the vast majority are absent.

The central section structures live in include/linux/mmzone.h:

struct mem_section_usage {
    struct rcu_head rcu;
#ifdef CONFIG_SPARSEMEM_VMEMMAP
    DECLARE_BITMAP(subsection_map, SUBSECTIONS_PER_SECTION);
#endif
    unsigned long pageblock_flags[0];
};

struct mem_section {
    unsigned long section_mem_map;
    struct mem_section_usage *usage;
    ...
};

The comment on section_mem_map is essential. It is “logically a pointer to an array of struct page,” but Linux stores a little extra state in the low bits. That state indicates whether the section is present, has a mem_map, is online, and so on. The helpers immediately below it decode those bits:

  • present_section()
  • valid_section()
  • online_section()
  • __section_mem_map_addr()

The global section array itself is provided by mm/sparse.c:

#ifndef CONFIG_SPARSEMEM_EXTREME
struct mem_section mem_section[NR_SECTION_ROOTS][SECTIONS_PER_ROOT]
    ____cacheline_internodealigned_in_smp;
#endif

That internode cacheline alignment is a reminder that even metadata arrays are tuned for NUMA behavior.

Why VMEMMAP helps

VMEMMAP means that the array of struct page objects is itself mapped into a regular kernel virtual range, rather than being reached through special-case arithmetic or permanently lowmem-only assumptions. That makes pfn_to_page() and page_to_pfn() scalable on very large machines.

On x86_64, arch/x86/include/asm/pgtable_64.h simply defines:

#define vmemmap ((struct page *)VMEMMAP_START)

On ARM64, arch/arm64/include/asm/memory.h shows the same concept with a little more arithmetic:

#define virt_to_page(x) ({
    u64 __idx = (__tag_reset((u64)x) - PAGE_OFFSET) / PAGE_SIZE;
    u64 __addr = VMEMMAP_START + (__idx * sizeof(struct page));
    (struct page *)__addr;
})

and the reverse:

#define page_to_virt(x) ({
    u64 __idx = ((u64)__page - VMEMMAP_START) / sizeof(struct page);
    u64 __addr = PAGE_OFFSET + (__idx * PAGE_SIZE);
    (void *)__tag_set((const void *)__addr, page_kasan_tag(__page));
})

These macros make the same big idea concrete: physical pages and their metadata occupy parallel virtual ranges.

Subsections and sparse validity

A modern refinement is subsection validity. mmzone.h and mm/sparse.c support sparse populations inside a section through a subsection_map bitmap. That is why pfn_valid() in modern Linux is more nuanced than "PFN lies inside the maximum RAM range."

How pfn_valid() works

The function checks three things in sequence (include/linux/mmzone.h):

static inline int pfn_valid(unsigned long pfn)
{
    struct mem_section *ms;

    /* 1. Out of addressable range? */
    if (pfn >= max_possible_pfn)
        return 0;

    /* 2. Section exists? */
    ms = __pfn_to_section(pfn);
    if (!valid_section(ms))
        return 0;

    /* 3. Subsection populated? */
    return early_section(ms) || pfn_section_valid(ms, pfn);
}

The pfn_section_valid() helper tests the subsection bitmap:

static inline int pfn_section_valid(struct mem_section *ms, unsigned long pfn)
{
    int idx = subsection_map_index(pfn);
    struct mem_section_usage *usage = READ_ONCE(ms->usage);

    if (!usage)
        return 0;
    return test_bit(idx, usage->subsection_map);
}

This three-level check means a PFN may have metadata (struct page exists in vmemmap), may belong to a present section, and still not refer to ordinary allocatable RAM. That distinction matters in several contexts:

  • Memory hotplug: a section may be partially populated — some subsections contain live DIMMs, others are empty slots.
  • Device memory: ZONE_DEVICE pages exist in the section map but are not managed by the buddy allocator.
  • Persistent memory (PMEM): DAX-mapped NVDIMM ranges are valid PFNs but follow different allocation and lifetime rules.
  • Crash analysis: pfn_valid() is the first check any memory-walking tool (e.g., /proc/kcore reader, kdump) performs to avoid accessing non-existent metadata.

NUMA Nodes and Memory Zones

Linux groups physical memory first by NUMA node (proximity domain) and then by zone (capability class). This two-level hierarchy lets the allocator prefer memory that is close to the requesting CPU while also respecting device DMA constraints. On UMA (single-node) systems, the node layer collapses to a single pg_data_t; on large NUMA servers, each node may contain hundreds of gigabytes with different access latencies.

The per-node structure is pg_data_t, which is simply a typedef for struct pglist_data in include/linux/mmzone.h. The beginning of the structure is:

typedef struct pglist_data {
    struct zone node_zones[MAX_NR_ZONES];
    int nr_zones;
    ...
    unsigned long node_start_pfn;
    unsigned long node_present_pages;
    unsigned long node_spanned_pages;
    int node_id;
    ...
} pg_data_t;

The addressing-relevant fields are:

  • node_zones[MAX_NR_ZONES] — the actual zones belonging to this NUMA node
  • node_start_pfn — first PFN in the node
  • node_present_pages — actual present pages
  • node_spanned_pages — size including holes
  • node_id — NUMA node number

The allocator-facing fields — node_zonelists[] (fallback ordering), kswapd/kcompactd wait queues, and per-node statistics — are covered in Chapter 8: NUMA Nodes and the Memory Model.

Zone types

The zone enum in include/linux/mmzone.h is now:

enum zone_type {
#ifdef CONFIG_ZONE_DMA
    ZONE_DMA,
#endif
#ifdef CONFIG_ZONE_DMA32
    ZONE_DMA32,
#endif
    ZONE_NORMAL,
#ifdef CONFIG_HIGHMEM
    ZONE_HIGHMEM,
#endif
    ZONE_MOVABLE,
#ifdef CONFIG_ZONE_DEVICE
    ZONE_DEVICE,
#endif
    __MAX_NR_ZONES
};

On x86_64 and ARM64, ZONE_HIGHMEM is normally absent. The practically important modern zones are:

  • ZONE_DMA — low DMA-reachable memory; historically the first 16 MB on x86
  • ZONE_DMA32 — memory below 4 GB for devices with 32-bit DMA masks
  • ZONE_NORMAL — regular directly addressable RAM
  • ZONE_MOVABLE — pages intended to remain migratable
  • ZONE_DEVICE — device-managed memory, such as certain pmem or accelerator ranges

The old i386 highmem split was central to earlier Linux kernels. On 64-bit primary architectures it has mostly disappeared from the mainstream path, replaced by NUMA, movable zones, and device memory distinctions.

The struct zone view

Each zone has its own allocator state, watermarks, free lists, and statistics. The definition in include/linux/mmzone.h begins:

struct zone {
    unsigned long _watermark[NR_WMARK];
    ...
    unsigned long zone_start_pfn;
    atomic_long_t managed_pages;
    unsigned long spanned_pages;
    unsigned long present_pages;
    const char *name;
    ...
    struct free_area free_area[NR_PAGE_ORDERS];
    spinlock_t lock;
    ...
};

The addressing-relevant fields are:

  • zone_start_pfn — the first PFN in this zone, defining where it begins in the physical address space.
  • spanned_pages — total PFN range including holes.
  • present_pages — physical pages that actually exist (may change with hotplug).
  • managed_pages — pages under buddy control (present minus boot-time reservations).

The allocator-specific fields — watermarks, free lists, per-CPU page caches, compaction state, lowmem_reserve, and the fallback zonelist mechanism — are covered in detail in Chapter 8: Memory Zones and The Zoned Page Frame Allocator.

Table 2-17. Modern zone purposes
Zone Typical role in 64-bit Linux
ZONE_DMA legacy or highly constrained DMA-capable memory
ZONE_DMA32 RAM below 4 GB for 32-bit DMA devices
ZONE_NORMAL ordinary allocatable RAM
ZONE_MOVABLE mostly migratable pages to ease compaction and hot-remove
ZONE_DEVICE device-managed or nonstandard memory types

Page Flags, struct page, and struct folio

Every physical page frame has a metadata object. The basic type is still struct page, defined in include/linux/mm_types.h. The structure is 64 bytes on 64-bit architectures and heavily unionized — the same bytes serve as buddy-allocator state, page-cache LRU links, slab metadata, or device-memory pointers depending on the page's current role.

From the addressing perspective, the fields that matter most are:

  • flags — status bits (PG_locked, PG_dirty, etc.) in the lower bits; zone number, NUMA node ID, and memory section packed in the upper bits. This encoding is how page_zone(), page_to_nid(), and page_to_section() work — they extract fields from flags without any additional lookup.
  • _mapcount — tracks how many page-table entries (PTEs) point to this page. Essential for reverse mapping: the reclaim scanner uses this to find and unmap all PTEs referencing a page before reclaiming it.
  • _refcount — ownership reference count. A freshly allocated page starts with refcount 1; get_page()/put_page() manage additional references.
  • page_type — encodes the page's role for non-user-mappable pages: PG_buddy, PG_slab, PG_table (page-table pages, linking to struct ptdesc), etc.

The full field-by-field breakdown of struct page — including the allocator-specific fields (buddy_list, pcp_list, mapping, private, compound_head) and the slab overlay — is covered in Chapter 8: Page Descriptors, which discusses them in the context of the allocator that manages each field.

The folio abstraction

Since Linux 5.16, the kernel has progressively adopted struct folio as the preferred way to refer to a unit of memory above the allocator level. A folio represents a power-of-two group of physically contiguous pages — at minimum one base page (order 0, 4 KB), but potentially order 2 (16 KB), order 4 (64 KB), or order 9 (2 MB huge page). A folio is always identified by its head page: the first struct page in the compound group.

The motivation for folios is eliminating the ambiguity that plagued the old page-based APIs. With struct page *, a function could never be sure whether it held the head of a compound page, one of its tail pages, or a standalone base page. A struct folio * is, by type contract, always the head — tail pages are never passed around as folios.

Internally, struct folio is defined as a union with struct page. Beyond the first page's worth of data, a large folio extends into a second cacheline (the "second tail page" region) that stores folio-specific fields:

/* include/linux/mm_types.h — folio extension fields (second cacheline) */
struct folio {
    /* First 64 bytes — overlays struct page exactly */
    ...
    /* Second cacheline — folio-specific extensions */
    atomic_t _large_mapcount;   /* mapcount for multi-page folios */
    atomic_t _nr_pages_mapped;  /* pages with at least one PTE */
    atomic_t _entire_mapcount;  /* mappings of the entire folio */
    atomic_t _pincount;         /* GUP fast-pin count */
    unsigned int _nr_pages;     /* how many pages in this folio */
    unsigned int _folio_order;  /* log2 of _nr_pages */
    ...
};

The key conversion APIs are:

page_folio(page) → folio        /* any page → its folio head */
folio_page(folio, n) → page     /* folio + index → nth page */
folio_nr_pages(folio) → count   /* number of pages in folio */
folio_order(folio) → order      /* log2 of page count */

The detailed allocator-level treatment of folios — including page-cache integration, reclaim behavior, compound-page mechanics, and the full struct page union layout — is in Chapter 8: Page Descriptors.

Modern Linux overlays and groups pages through folios. A folio does not replace page tables, but it changes how the kernel thinks about many memory-management operations. Page cache, reclaim, writeback, and large mappings are increasingly folio-centric. That is why later chapters will speak in folio terms more often than older literature did.

Page flags: the status bits

The lower bits of page->flags encode per-page status information. The flags are defined as an enum in include/linux/page-flags.h. The most important ones are:

FlagMeaning
PG_lockedPage is locked for I/O or state transition. Waiters sleep on the page's wait queue.
PG_referencedPage was recently accessed. Used by reclaim to implement the second-chance algorithm.
PG_uptodatePage contents match the backing store. Set after successful read I/O.
PG_dirtyPage has been modified since last writeback. Triggers eventual write to disk.
PG_lruPage is on one of the LRU lists (active/inactive, anon/file).
PG_activePage is on the active LRU list (recently used, harder to reclaim).
PG_workingsetPage was recently evicted and refaulted — indicates thrashing.
PG_reservedPage is reserved and should never be freed to the buddy allocator (firmware tables, kernel text, etc.).
PG_swapbackedPage is backed by swap rather than a file. Distinguishes anonymous from file-backed pages.
PG_mlockedPage is in a VM_LOCKED VMA (mlock()). Exempt from reclaim.
PG_hwpoisonPage has an uncorrectable hardware memory error. The kernel avoids using it.
PG_headThis is the head page of a compound allocation (folio).
PG_writebackPage is currently being written back to disk.
PG_reclaimPage has been selected for reclaim and is being written back.

The total number of defined flags (__NR_PAGEFLAGS) is approximately 26 on a typical 64-bit kernel. The remaining upper bits of the unsigned long flags field are used for topology encoding (zone, node, section) as described below. Accessor macros like PageDirty(page), SetPageDirty(page), and ClearPageDirty(page) are auto-generated by the PAGEFLAG() macro family, ensuring consistent atomic bit manipulation.

Page Flags: Encoding Zone, Node, and Section

The page->flags field is far more than a list of status bits like PG_locked, PG_dirty, or PG_uptodate. Linux packs physical placement metadata — the page's zone number, NUMA node ID, and (on SPARSEMEM configurations) its memory section number — into the upper bits of the same unsigned long word. This design means that given any struct page *, the kernel can determine which zone, which NUMA node, and which memory section the page belongs to in O(1) time, without any lookup table or pointer dereference.

The bit layout is computed at compile time by include/linux/page-flags-layout.h. The lower bits hold NR_PAGEFLAGS status flags (currently around 26 bits), and the remaining upper bits are partitioned among topology fields:

/* include/linux/page-flags-layout.h — bit layout of page->flags */
/* From MSB to LSB: */
/*   [SECTIONS_WIDTH] [NODES_WIDTH] [ZONES_WIDTH] [LRU_GEN_WIDTH] ... [NR_PAGEFLAGS status bits] */

/* Helper macros extract each field: */
#define ZONES_PGSHIFT     (NR_PAGEFLAGS)
#define NODES_PGSHIFT     (ZONES_PGSHIFT + ZONES_WIDTH)
#define SECTIONS_PGSHIFT  (NODES_PGSHIFT + NODES_WIDTH)

The kernel provides inline helpers in include/linux/mmzone.h to extract this information efficiently:

static inline enum zone_type page_zonenum(const struct page *page)
{
    return memdesc_zonenum(page->flags);
}

static inline int page_to_nid(const struct page *page)
{
    return (page->flags >> NODES_PGSHIFT) & NODES_MASK;
}

static inline unsigned long page_to_section(const struct page *page)
{
    return (page->flags >> SECTIONS_PGSHIFT) & SECTIONS_MASK;
}

/* Folio equivalents — same underlying flags word */
static inline enum zone_type folio_zonenum(const struct folio *folio)
{
    return memdesc_zonenum(folio->flags);
}

This packing is critical for performance: the page allocator's fast path calls page_zonenum() and page_to_nid() on every allocation and free to check watermarks and enforce NUMA locality. If these required pointer chases or memory lookups, the allocator's hot path would be significantly slower. The trade-off is that the number of available status-flag bits is limited — on a 64-bit kernel with many NUMA nodes and memory sections, the upper-bit fields can consume 10–15 bits, leaving the lower ~50 bits for status flags and LRU generation tracking.

This density is also why struct page metadata design is so delicate: the structure exists once per physical page frame, so every added byte multiplies across the entire machine. On a 256 GB server, there are over 67 million page descriptors — a single extra byte per descriptor would cost 64 MB of non-reclaimable kernel memory.

Memory Hotplug and Device Memory

Linux 2.6 treated physical memory as a static resource discovered at boot. Modern Linux supports memory hotplug — adding and removing physical memory at runtime — and device memory — memory regions managed by hardware devices (persistent memory, GPU memory, CXL-attached memory) that need struct page metadata but follow different allocation rules.

Adding and removing memory

The hotplug lifecycle has two phases:

  1. Physical add (add_memory()): creates struct page metadata for the new PFN range, populates the mem_section entries and subsection bitmap, and maps the vmemmap pages. The memory exists but is not yet usable.
  2. Online (online_pages()): moves the pages into a zone (typically ZONE_NORMAL or ZONE_MOVABLE) and hands them to the buddy allocator. The pages are now available for allocation.

Removal is the reverse: offline_pages() migrates all in-use pages out of the range (which is why ZONE_MOVABLE exists — it guarantees pages can be migrated), then remove_memory() tears down the metadata. Chapter 17 walks through the offline sequence and the online policies that decide which zone new memory joins (see Memory Hot-Remove and ZONE_MOVABLE).

A notable modern feature is MHP_MEMMAP_ON_MEMORY (mm/memory_hotplug.c): instead of allocating vmemmap pages from existing memory, the kernel places the struct page metadata on the hotplugged memory itself. This is essential for CXL and large DIMM hotplug where the system may not have enough existing memory to hold metadata for hundreds of gigabytes of new RAM.

ZONE_DEVICE and struct dev_pagemap

ZONE_DEVICE extends the page metadata model to memory that is not managed by the buddy allocator. These pages have struct page descriptors (so they can participate in get_user_pages(), DMA mapping, and reverse mapping) but are allocated and freed through device-specific mechanisms.

The kernel defines several device memory types in include/linux/memremap.h:

TypeDescription
MEMORY_DEVICE_PRIVATE GPU or accelerator memory not CPU-accessible. Used by HMM (Heterogeneous Memory Management) for GPU page migration.
MEMORY_DEVICE_COHERENT Device memory that is CPU-coherent (e.g., CXL Type-2 devices). Can be directly accessed by both CPU and device.
MEMORY_DEVICE_FS_DAX Persistent memory (NVDIMM) accessed via DAX (Direct Access). Bypasses the page cache for direct load/store to storage.
MEMORY_DEVICE_GENERIC Generic device memory for drivers that need struct page but manage their own allocation.
MEMORY_DEVICE_PCI_P2PDMA PCI BAR memory used for peer-to-peer DMA between PCI devices without CPU involvement.

The struct dev_pagemap structure ties a physical range to a device driver:

/* include/linux/memremap.h */
struct dev_pagemap {
    struct dev_pagemap_ops *ops;   /* device callbacks for fault, free, etc. */
    enum memory_type type;         /* PRIVATE, COHERENT, FS_DAX, etc. */
    struct range range;            /* physical address range */
    struct range internal_range;   /* internal metadata range */
    ...
};

The setup path memremap_pages() in mm/memremap.c creates the section metadata, maps the vmemmap pages, and associates each struct page with the dev_pagemap. When a device page's reference count drops to zero, the free_devmap_managed_folio() callback notifies the device driver instead of returning the page to the buddy allocator.

This infrastructure is what allows Linux to present a unified struct page interface for all physical memory — whether it sits in a DIMM, on a GPU, in an NVDIMM, or behind a CXL link. The page allocator never touches ZONE_DEVICE pages, but the rest of the MM subsystem (reverse mapping, migration, GUP) can work with them through the standard page metadata.

Why this differs from the 2.6-era explanation

The old Linux 2.6 story emphasized:

  • low memory versus high memory,
  • a more page-centric API vocabulary,
  • and less dynamic hotplug/device-memory sophistication.

Linux 7.0.12 instead emphasizes:

  • SPARSEMEM and subsection validity,
  • VMEMMAP as the normal metadata mapping mechanism,
  • folios as a first-class abstraction,
  • and richer zone taxonomy, especially ZONE_MOVABLE and ZONE_DEVICE.

The core idea is still recognizable: PFN metadata must exist, and the allocator must know what each page can be used for. But the scale and flexibility of today's systems have made the metadata model much more explicit.

Process Page Tables

Every user-space process has its own set of page tables, rooted in a Page Global Directory pointed to by mm_struct->pgd. The lower portion of the PGD (mapping user-space addresses) is private to the process and changes with every fork() or exec(). The upper portion (mapping kernel-space addresses) is shared — it mirrors the master kernel PGD so that every process can reach kernel code and data when entering kernel mode.

Process page tables are the mechanism by which the kernel gives each process the illusion of a private, flat, contiguous address space — even though the underlying physical memory is fragmented, shared, and non-contiguous. When a process dereferences a pointer, the CPU's MMU walks the process's page tables to translate the virtual address to a physical frame. If the translation fails (page fault), the kernel steps in to allocate a frame, read data from disk, enforce copy-on-write semantics, or deliver a segmentation fault.

This section covers five aspects of process page tables:

  1. The mm_struct — the central data structure tying a process to its address space, its VMA tree, and its PGD.
  2. User/kernel split — how the PGD is partitioned between per-process user mappings and shared kernel mappings, and how the two architectures differ.
  3. Context switching — how the kernel loads a different process's page tables into the hardware translation register (CR3 / TTBR0), including PCID and ASID optimizations.
  4. Kernel-mapping propagation — how x86_64 pre-allocates the top-level vmalloc page tables at boot so every process's PGD shares them without per-process synchronization.
  5. Lifecycle — how page tables are created at fork(), replaced at exec(), and torn down at exit(), including the copy-on-write mechanism.

The lifecycle of a process's page tables is tightly coupled to the process lifecycle itself. At fork(), the child receives a copy of the parent's PGD with copy-on-write mappings. At exec(), the old address space is torn down and a fresh one is built for the new binary. At exit(), all page-table pages and their associated physical frames are released. Between these events, the page tables grow and shrink dynamically as the process calls mmap(), brk(), munmap(), or triggers demand-paging faults.

The two architectures handle the user/kernel split differently. On x86_64, the PGD is a single 512-entry table: entries 0–255 map user space and entries 256–511 map kernel space, with the kernel entries cloned from the master swapper_pg_dir. On ARM64, the hardware provides two separate translation-table base registers — TTBR0_EL1 for user space and TTBR1_EL1 for kernel space — so the user and kernel page tables are completely independent structures. Figure 2-24 illustrates this architecture on both platforms.

The mm_struct and its PGD

The central data structure tying a process to its address space is struct mm_struct, defined in include/linux/mm_types.h. It is allocated dynamically (via mm_alloc() in kernel/fork.c) and its size includes a fixed portion plus variable-length fields for CPU masks and concurrency IDs.

The key fields for understanding process page tables are:

struct mm_struct {
    struct maple_tree mm_mt;     /* VMA tree (replaced old rb_tree) */
    unsigned long mmap_base;     /* base of mmap region */
    unsigned long task_size;     /* size of user address space */
    pgd_t *pgd;                  /* Page Global Directory */

    atomic_t mm_users;           /* threads sharing this mm */
    atomic_t mm_count;           /* references (including lazy TLB) */

    struct rw_semaphore mmap_lock;   /* protects VMA tree and page tables */
    spinlock_t page_table_lock;      /* protects PGD-level changes */

    struct list_head mmlist;     /* list of all mm_structs */

    unsigned long total_vm;      /* total pages mapped */
    unsigned long locked_vm;     /* pages locked via mlock() */
    unsigned long pinned_vm;     /* pages pinned via GUP */
    unsigned long data_vm;       /* data + stack pages */
    unsigned long exec_vm;       /* executable code pages */
    unsigned long stack_vm;      /* stack pages */

    struct task_struct __rcu *owner;  /* main thread (for OOM, etc.) */
    ...
};
Table 2-18. Key fields of mm_struct
FieldDescription
pgdPointer to the process's Page Global Directory. Loaded into CR3 (x86_64) or TTBR0_EL1 (ARM64) during context switch.
mm_mtThe maple tree indexing all VMAs. Replaced the old red-black tree (mm_rb) in Linux 6.1 for better cache behavior and range queries.
mmap_lockA reader-writer semaphore protecting the VMA tree and page tables. Read-locked during page faults; write-locked during mmap()/munmap(). Historically the most contended lock in the MM subsystem — which is why the fault fast path now bypasses it with per-VMA locking.
page_table_lockA spinlock protecting top-level PGD modifications. On architectures with split page-table locks, lower levels use per-page locks instead.
mm_usersNumber of threads sharing this mm. Incremented by clone(CLONE_VM); when it drops to zero, the address space is torn down.
mm_countReference count including lazy TLB references. Prevents the mm_struct from being freed while any CPU still references it.
task_sizeMaximum user-space virtual address. On x86_64: 0x00007fffffffffff (128 TB); on ARM64: up to 0x0000ffffffffffff (256 TB).
total_vmTotal pages mapped (sum of all VMA sizes). Checked against RLIMIT_AS.
ownerRCU-protected pointer to the main thread. Used by the OOM killer to select which task to kill.

The VMA tree: from red-black to maple

Linux 2.6 organized VMAs in a red-black tree (mm->mm_rb) plus a linked list (mm->mmap). In modern Linux (since 6.1), both have been replaced by a single maple tree (mm->mm_mt), which is a B-tree variant optimized for range lookups. The maple tree stores VMAs indexed by their start address and provides O(log n) lookup, insertion, and deletion with better cache locality than the old rb-tree.

The find_vma() function now uses the maple tree directly:

/* mm/mmap.c */
struct vm_area_struct *find_vma(struct mm_struct *mm, unsigned long addr)
{
    unsigned long index = addr;
    ...
    return mt_find(&mm->mm_mt, &index, ULONG_MAX);
}

Each VMA (struct vm_area_struct) describes a contiguous range of virtual addresses with uniform permissions and backing. Key fields include:

struct vm_area_struct {
    unsigned long vm_start;          /* first byte of the region */
    unsigned long vm_end;            /* first byte after the region */
    struct mm_struct *vm_mm;         /* owning mm_struct */
    vm_flags_t vm_flags;             /* VM_READ|VM_WRITE|VM_EXEC|VM_SHARED|... */
    struct anon_vma *anon_vma;       /* reverse map for anonymous pages */
    const struct vm_operations_struct *vm_ops;  /* fault/map/close callbacks */
    unsigned long vm_pgoff;          /* offset within vm_file (in pages) */
    struct file *vm_file;            /* backing file (NULL for anonymous) */
    void *vm_private_data;           /* driver-specific data */
};

The vm_flags field is the bridge between user-space permissions (from mmap()'s prot argument) and hardware page-table bits (via vm_get_page_prot() as described in the previous section). When a page fault occurs, the fault handler reads vma->vm_flags to determine whether the access is legal before installing a PTE.

Per-VMA locking

Table 2-18 calls mmap_lock the most contended lock in the MM subsystem, and for a long time it was: every page fault took it for reading, so a multithreaded process faulting on many unrelated addresses serialized on a single per-mm semaphore. Since Linux 6.4, the fault fast path avoids mmap_lock entirely by taking a lock on just the one VMA being faulted. The architecture fault handlers (arch/x86/mm/fault.c, arch/arm64/mm/fault.c) begin with:

/* arch/x86/mm/fault.c — VMA-lock fast path (simplified) */
vma = lock_vma_under_rcu(mm, address);   /* find + read-lock one VMA under RCU */
if (!vma)
    goto lock_mmap;                       /* fall back to mmap_lock */

fault = handle_mm_fault(vma, address, flags | FAULT_FLAG_VMA_LOCK, regs);
if (!(fault & (VM_FAULT_RETRY | VM_FAULT_COMPLETED)))
    vma_end_read(vma);                    /* release the per-VMA lock */

The mechanism works as follows:

  • lock_vma_under_rcu() looks up the VMA in the maple tree under RCU (no mmap_lock) and takes a lightweight read reference on it (the VMA carries its own vm_refcnt and a vm_lock_seq compared against mm->mm_lock_seq).
  • If it succeeds, handle_mm_fault() runs with FAULT_FLAG_VMA_LOCK set, and faults on different VMAs proceed fully in parallel.
  • If the VMA cannot be locked — a concurrent writer holds it, the address is not in any VMA, or the fault needs an operation not supported under the VMA lock — the handler retries under the full mmap_lock (the lock_mmap fallback), preserving correctness.

The feature is controlled by CONFIG_PER_VMA_LOCK (depends on ARCH_SUPPORTS_PER_VMA_LOCK && MMU && SMP); both x86_64 and ARM64 select it. In practice it removes almost all mmap_lock read traffic from the fault path, which is a large scalability win for heavily multithreaded workloads (databases, JVMs, browsers).

When the CPU runs a process, the hardware translation-table base register is loaded with the physical address of mm->pgd:

  • On x86_64: the value is written to CR3 (plus PCID bits).
  • On ARM64: the value is written to TTBR0_EL1 (user-space translations).

A context switch between two processes means switching mm->pgd — the fundamental operation that changes the visible user-space address space.

User/kernel split in the PGD

On x86_64 with four-level paging, the 512 entries of the PGD map 256 TB each. By convention:

PGD entriesVirtual rangeOwned by
0–2550x0000000000000000–0x00007fffffffffffUser space (per-process)
256–5110xffff800000000000–0xffffffffffffffffKernel space (shared)

The first 256 entries are unique to each process and are populated by the page-fault handler, mmap(), and related MM paths. The upper 256 entries must be identical across all processes so that kernel code, the direct map, vmalloc space, vmemmap, and all other kernel regions are always accessible in kernel mode.

On ARM64, the split is even cleaner: user-space addresses go through TTBR0_EL1 (the process's own page tables) and kernel-space addresses go through TTBR1_EL1 (the kernel's page tables). There is no need to share PGD entries — the two translation-table base registers provide a hardware-enforced split.

How kernel entries are shared on x86_64

When a new process is created via fork(), the kernel allocates a fresh PGD through pgd_alloc() in arch/x86/mm/pgtable.c. This function:

  1. Allocates a page for the new PGD.
  2. Copies the kernel portion of swapper_pg_dir (entries 256–511) into the new PGD, so the new process immediately sees all kernel mappings.
  3. If KPTI is enabled, allocates a paired user-visible shadow PGD and populates it with the minimal kernel entries needed for entry/exit trampolines.
/* arch/x86/mm/pgtable.c — simplified */
pgd_t *pgd_alloc(struct mm_struct *mm)
{
    pgd_t *pgd = _pgd_alloc();
    if (!pgd)
        return NULL;

    mm->pgd = pgd;

    /* Clone kernel entries from the master kernel PGD */
    pgd_prepopulate_pmd(mm, pgd, ...);

    if (boot_cpu_has(X86_FEATURE_PTI)) {
        /* Allocate and populate the KPTI user-visible shadow */
        ...
    }
    return pgd;
}

Context switching: loading a new address space

When the scheduler decides to run a different process, it must load that process's page tables into the hardware. This happens in switch_mm_irqs_off() on x86_64 and check_and_switch_context() on ARM64.

x86_64: CR3 and PCID

On x86_64, the context switch writes a new value to CR3. With PCID (Process-Context IDentifiers) enabled, the kernel avoids flushing the entire TLB on every switch by tagging TLB entries with a 12-bit PCID. The key function in arch/x86/mm/tlb.c is:

/* arch/x86/mm/tlb.c — simplified */
void switch_mm_irqs_off(struct mm_struct *prev, struct mm_struct *next,
                        struct task_struct *tsk)
{
    /* Choose an ASID (PCID) for the new mm */
    unsigned short new_asid;
    bool need_flush;

    choose_new_asid(next, &new_asid, &need_flush);

    if (need_flush) {
        /* First use of this ASID — must flush stale entries */
        load_new_mm_cr3(next->pgd, new_asid, ...);
    } else {
        /* ASID still valid — use CR3_NOFLUSH to preserve TLB */
        load_new_mm_cr3(next->pgd, new_asid | CR3_NOFLUSH, ...);
    }

    /* Update per-CPU state */
    this_cpu_write(cpu_tlbstate.loaded_mm, next);
    this_cpu_write(cpu_tlbstate.loaded_mm_asid, new_asid);
}

The CR3_NOFLUSH bit (bit 63) tells the CPU to not invalidate TLB entries tagged with the new PCID. This is the key optimization: if the process was recently running on this CPU, its TLB entries may still be valid, and preserving them avoids expensive TLB misses after the switch. The kernel maintains a small pool of ASIDs per CPU (TLB_NR_DYN_ASIDS = 6) and tracks which are still valid.

ARM64: TTBR0 and ASID

On ARM64, the context switch writes the new PGD address into TTBR0_EL1. The ASID (Address Space IDentifier) is embedded in the TTBR itself (upper bits). The switch function in arch/arm64/mm/context.c is:

/* arch/arm64/mm/context.c — simplified */
void check_and_switch_context(struct mm_struct *mm)
{
    unsigned long flags;
    unsigned int cpu = smp_processor_id();

    /* If the mm's ASID generation matches the current generation,
     * the ASID is still valid — just switch. */
    if (atomic64_read(&mm->context.id) >> asid_bits == asid_generation)
        goto switch_mm_fastpath;

    /* Otherwise, allocate a new ASID */
    raw_spin_lock_irqsave(&cpu_asid_lock, flags);
    new_context(mm);    /* assigns a fresh ASID */
    raw_spin_unlock_irqrestore(&cpu_asid_lock, flags);

switch_mm_fastpath:
    cpu_do_switch_mm(virt_to_phys(mm->pgd), mm);
}

ARM64's ASID space is wider (8 or 16 bits, giving 256 or 65,536 ASIDs) than x86_64's PCID (12 bits, 4,096 values), and the generation-based allocation ensures that ASID reuse triggers a full TLB flush only when the generation counter wraps — a rare event on systems with fewer than 65,536 active address spaces.

A critical difference: on ARM64, the kernel page tables (TTBR1_EL1) are never switched. Only the user page table register (TTBR0_EL1) changes on context switch, which means kernel TLB entries are never invalidated by a user-space context switch.

Kernel-Mapping Propagation (vmalloc region)

Kernel virtual mappings such as the vmalloc region must be reachable from every process's page tables. On x86_64 this is guaranteed by pre-allocating the top-level vmalloc page-table pages at boot, so every process's cloned PGD already shares them — no per-process synchronization or fault is required. (Only 32-bit x86 still uses a lazy vmalloc_fault() handler.) This mechanism, and why ARM64 needs nothing at all, is covered in detail in the vmalloc Mapping Propagation subsection of Kernel Page Tables.

Page tables during fork, exec, and exit

OperationWhat happens to page tables
fork() / clone() dup_mm() in kernel/fork.c → mm_alloc() → pgd_alloc() creates a new PGD with kernel entries cloned. Then dup_mmap() walks the parent's VMAs and creates COW (copy-on-write) mappings — the actual page-table entries are installed lazily through faults.
exec() exec_mmap() replaces the process's mm_struct entirely. The old mm is released (PGD freed), and a new empty mm with a fresh PGD is installed.
exit() exit_mm() → mmput() → exit_mmap() tears down all VMAs and page tables, then pgd_free() releases the PGD.
clone(CLONE_VM) (threads) Threads share mm_struct — no new PGD is allocated. The mm_users count is incremented. All threads in a process see the same page tables.

Copy-on-write during fork

When fork() creates a child process, the kernel does not copy every physical page. Instead, dup_mmap() in mm/mmap.c duplicates the VMA tree (using __mt_dup() on the maple tree) and then calls copy_page_range() to replicate the page-table entries. For private writable mappings, the critical step is write-protecting both the parent's and child's PTEs:

/* mm/memory.c — copy_pte_range(), simplified */
if (is_cow_mapping(src_vma->vm_flags) && pte_write(pte)) {
    /* Write-protect the parent's PTE */
    wrprotect_ptes(src_mm, addr, src_pte, nr);
    /* Copy as write-protected */
    pte = pte_wrprotect(pte);
}
/* Increment the page's reference count for the new mapping */
folio_ref_add(folio, nr);

After fork(), both parent and child share the same physical pages, but all private writable pages are marked read-only. When either process writes to such a page, the CPU triggers a write-protection fault. The page-fault handler (Chapter 9) then:

  1. Allocates a new physical page.
  2. Copies the old page's contents to the new page.
  3. Installs the new page with write permission in the faulting process's page table.
  4. Decrements the reference count on the original page.

This is copy-on-write (COW): the actual data copy is deferred until the moment a write occurs, and pages that are only read (common after fork() followed by exec()) are never copied at all.

exec: replacing the address space

When a process calls execve(), the exec path in fs/exec.c replaces the entire address space:

/* fs/exec.c — exec_mmap(), simplified */
static int exec_mmap(struct mm_struct *mm)
{
    struct mm_struct *old_mm = current->mm;

    /* Release signals, keys, and other mm-tied resources */
    exec_mm_release(tsk, old_mm);

    /* Install the new mm as the process's address space */
    task_lock(tsk);
    activate_mm(old_mm, mm);   /* loads mm->pgd into CR3/TTBR0 */
    task_unlock(tsk);

    /* Drop the reference on the old mm — tears it down if last user */
    mmput(old_mm);
    return 0;
}

The new mm was pre-allocated earlier in the exec path with an empty VMA tree and a fresh PGD. The binary loader (load_elf_binary() for ELF) then populates it with mappings for the executable's text, data, BSS, stack, and any shared libraries.

exit: tearing down page tables

When the last thread of a process exits, exit_mmap() in mm/mmap.c performs the final cleanup:

/* mm/mmap.c — exit_mmap(), simplified */
void exit_mmap(struct mm_struct *mm)
{
    struct mmu_gather tlb;

    /* Set up mmu_gather for batched TLB flush and page-table freeing */
    tlb_gather_mmu_fullmm(&tlb, mm);

    /* Unmap all VMAs — removes PTEs, PMDs, PUDs */
    unmap_vmas(&tlb, mm, ...);

    /* Free the page-table pages themselves */
    free_pgtables(&tlb, mm, ...);

    /* Flush any remaining batched TLB entries */
    tlb_finish_mmu(&tlb);

    /* Destroy the VMA tree */
    tear_down_vmas(mm);
}

The mmu_gather batching is critical here: rather than flushing the TLB and freeing page-table pages one at a time, the kernel accumulates them and performs a single bulk operation. Since this is the last user of the address space, tlb_gather_mmu_fullmm() signals that the entire mm is being torn down, which allows aggressive optimizations (e.g., skipping per-page TLB invalidation entirely on some architectures since the ASID will be recycled).

Kernel Page Tables

The kernel maintains its own master set of page tables, rooted in swapper_pg_dir and owned by the special init_mm memory descriptor. These tables map the direct-map region, vmalloc space, vmemmap, the kernel text image, fixmaps, and all other kernel virtual regions. They are built in phases during boot — first a minimal set by assembly code in head.S, then the full mapping by C initialization code — and are continually maintained at runtime for vmalloc, module loading, and permission enforcement.

This section covers the complete lifecycle of kernel page tables in Linux 7.0.12:

  1. The master kernel PGD — init_mm, swapper_pg_dir, and how kernel threads borrow address spaces.
  2. Boot-time initialization — assembly provisional tables and the C-level paging_init() that builds the final mapping.
  3. The direct map — the linear physical-to-virtual translation and its helpers.
  4. Kernel virtual memory layout — the complete address-space map on x86_64 and ARM64.
  5. Fixmaps — compile-time virtual addresses for early boot and special mappings.
  6. Permission enforcement — CONFIG_STRICT_KERNEL_RWX, mark_rodata_ro(), and the set_memory_* API.
  7. vmalloc mapping propagation — how x86_64 pre-allocates kernel mappings so every process shares them.
  8. Architecture comparison — key differences between x86_64 and ARM64 kernel tables.

The Master Kernel PGD: init_mm and swapper_pg_dir

Every process has a struct mm_struct that owns its page tables. The kernel itself is no exception — the global init_mm descriptor owns the master kernel page tables and serves as the reference for all kernel virtual mappings.

The init_mm descriptor

Defined in mm/init-mm.c, init_mm is the only mm_struct that is statically allocated (all others are created dynamically by mm_alloc()):

/* mm/init-mm.c */
struct mm_struct init_mm = {
    .mm_mt            = MTREE_INIT_EXT(mm_mt, MM_MT_FLAGS,
                                       init_mm.mmap_lock),
    .pgd              = swapper_pg_dir,
    .mm_users         = ATOMIC_INIT(2),
    .mm_count         = ATOMIC_INIT(1),
    .write_protect_seq = SEQCNT_ZERO(init_mm.write_protect_seq),
    MMAP_LOCK_INITIALIZER(init_mm)
    .page_table_lock  = __SPIN_LOCK_UNLOCKED(init_mm.page_table_lock),
    .arg_lock         = __SPIN_LOCK_UNLOCKED(init_mm.arg_lock),
    .mmlist           = LIST_HEAD_INIT(init_mm.mmlist),
    .user_ns          = &init_user_ns,
    INIT_MM_CONTEXT(init_mm)
};

Key fields deserve explanation:

Table 2-19. init_mm key fields
FieldInitial valuePurpose
.pgdswapper_pg_dir Points to the master kernel Page Global Directory
.mm_usersATOMIC_INIT(2) Prevents the descriptor from ever being freed — no user tasks own this mm
.mm_countATOMIC_INIT(1) Reference count for the mm_struct itself
.page_table_lockspin lock Protects page table modifications for kernel mappings
INIT_MM_CONTEXTarch-specific Architecture-specific context initialization (e.g., LDT on x86, ASID on ARM64)

swapper_pg_dir — the architecture alias

swapper_pg_dir is defined differently on each architecture, but always refers to the top-level page table array that maps the kernel address space:

/* x86_64: arch/x86/include/asm/pgtable_64.h */
extern pgd_t init_top_pgt[];
#define swapper_pg_dir init_top_pgt

/* ARM64: defined in the linker script as a page-aligned array */
extern pgd_t swapper_pg_dir[PTRS_PER_PGD];

On x86_64, swapper_pg_dir is an alias for init_top_pgt, the table loaded into CR3 during late boot. On ARM64, it is a standalone array loaded into TTBR1_EL1. In both cases, pgd_offset_k(addr) is shorthand for pgd_offset(&init_mm, addr), which indexes directly into swapper_pg_dir.

Kernel threads and active_mm

Kernel threads have no user address space (tsk->mm == NULL), but the CPU still needs a valid set of page tables in its control register. The scheduler solves this by borrowing the previous task's mm_struct as the kernel thread's active_mm. This technique, called lazy TLB mode, avoids a costly page-table switch when the kernel thread only accesses kernel addresses (which are the same in every mm_struct).

When a kernel thread genuinely needs to access user pages (e.g., a kernel worker performing direct I/O), it calls kthread_use_mm():

/* kernel/kthread.c */
void kthread_use_mm(struct mm_struct *mm)
{
    struct mm_struct *active_mm;
    struct task_struct *tsk = current;

    mmgrab(mm);
    task_lock(tsk);
    active_mm = tsk->active_mm;
    tsk->active_mm = mm;
    tsk->mm = mm;
    membarrier_update_current_mm(mm);
    task_unlock(tsk);

    switch_mm_irqs_off(active_mm, mm, tsk);
    mmdrop_lazy_tlb(active_mm);
}

This increments the mm's reference count, performs a full page-table switch (CR3 load on x86_64, TTBR0 update on ARM64), and drops the borrowed active_mm. The companion kthread_unuse_mm() reverses the operation.

Boot-Time Page Table Initialization

Page table creation during boot happens in two phases, as in Linux 2.6 — but the details have changed significantly for 64-bit kernels. The challenge is a bootstrapping problem: the kernel must enable paging before it can run the C code that builds proper page tables, so it needs minimal "provisional" tables created by assembly.

Phase 1: Assembly-level provisional tables

The CPU starts with paging disabled (or with firmware-provided identity mapping on ARM64/UEFI). The first task is to enable paging with minimal tables that let the kernel continue executing at its linked virtual address.

x86_64 (arch/x86/kernel/head_64.S and arch/x86/boot/startup/map_kernel.c):

  1. The boot loader or decompressor starts the kernel in 64-bit long mode.
  2. startup_64 loads early_top_pgt into CR3 — a statically allocated PGD that provides an identity mapping of the kernel's physical load address and a kernel-text mapping at __START_KERNEL_map (0xffffffff80000000).
  3. The __startup_64() function in C (called via position-independent code) fixups the provisional tables by adding the physical load delta to each entry, then creates 2 MB PMD entries covering the kernel image:
/* arch/x86/boot/startup/map_kernel.c — simplified */
unsigned long __init __startup_64(unsigned long p2v_offset,
                                  struct boot_params *bp)
{
    pgd_t *pgd = rip_rel_ptr(early_top_pgt);
    /* Fix up the PGD entry for __START_KERNEL_map */
    pgd[pgd_index(__START_KERNEL_map)] += load_delta;

    /* Fix up PUD entries */
    level3_kernel_pgt[PTRS_PER_PUD - 2].pud += load_delta;

    /* Create 2 MB PMD entries covering the kernel image */
    pmd_entry = __PAGE_KERNEL_LARGE_EXEC & ~_PAGE_GLOBAL;
    for (i = 0; i < DIV_ROUND_UP(va_end - va_text, PMD_SIZE); i++)
        pmd[idx % PTRS_PER_PMD] = pmd_entry + i * PMD_SIZE;
    ...
}
  1. After basic initialization, the kernel switches to init_top_pgt (which becomes swapper_pg_dir) and removes the identity mapping.

ARM64 (arch/arm64/kernel/head.S):

ARM64 boot is more structured, using three separate page-table arrays:

/* arch/arm64/kernel/head.S — simplified */
SYM_CODE_START(primary_entry)
    bl      record_mmu_state
    bl      preserve_boot_args

    adrp    x0, __pi_init_idmap_pg_dir
    mov     x1, xzr
    bl      __pi_create_init_idmap     /* build identity map */

    bl      __primary_switch           /* enable MMU, jump to kernel */
SYM_CODE_END(primary_entry)
  1. __pi_create_init_idmap() builds idmap_pg_dir — an identity map where VA == PA, covering only the kernel image and the page tables themselves.
  2. Separately, init_pg_dir (a temporary page table) maps the kernel image at KIMAGE_VADDR.
  3. __primary_switch loads TTBR0 with idmap_pg_dir and TTBR1 with init_pg_dir, then enables the MMU.
  4. Later, paging_init() builds the final swapper_pg_dir and switches TTBR1 to it.

Both architectures need the identity mapping only briefly — just long enough to survive the transition to virtual addressing. It is removed (or restricted to CPU hotplug use) as soon as the kernel's proper virtual mapping is active.

Phase 2: C-level final page tables (paging_init)

Once the kernel is running in C with basic virtual addressing, it builds the complete page tables for all kernel regions. The two architectures take notably different approaches.

x86_64 (arch/x86/mm/init.c and arch/x86/mm/init_64.c):

On x86_64, most work is done by init_memory_mapping() before paging_init() is called:

  1. init_memory_mapping(0, end_of_RAM) — builds the direct map by creating page-table entries that map physical RAM at page_offset_base.
  2. The function __kernel_physical_mapping_init() performs the actual PTE creation, choosing page sizes based on alignment and CPU capabilities:
/* arch/x86/mm/init_64.c — page size selection */
static unsigned long __meminit
__kernel_physical_mapping_init(unsigned long paddr_start, ...)
{
    /* probe_page_size_mask() has already checked:
     *   X86_FEATURE_GBPAGES → allow 1 GB pages
     *   X86_FEATURE_PSE     → allow 2 MB pages  */
    if (page_size_mask & (1 << PG_LEVEL_1G)) {
        /* Create 1 GB PUD entry if aligned */
        ...
    } else if (page_size_mask & (1 << PG_LEVEL_2M)) {
        /* Create 2 MB PMD entry if aligned */
        ...
    } else {
        /* Fall back to 4 KB PTE entries */
        ...
    }
}
  1. The kernel text is mapped separately at __START_KERNEL_map.
  2. vmalloc, vmemmap, and fixmap regions have their top-level PGD entries pre-allocated but are populated on demand.
  3. paging_init() itself mainly calls zone_sizes_init() to set up the zone and node data structures.

ARM64 (arch/arm64/mm/mmu.c):

paging_init() does more heavy lifting on ARM64 because the provisional init_pg_dir is truly minimal:

/* arch/arm64/mm/mmu.c */
void __init paging_init(void)
{
    map_mem(swapper_pg_dir);       /* build complete direct map */
    memblock_allow_resize();
    create_idmap();                /* rebuild identity map for hotplug */
    ...
    /* Switch TTBR1 from init_pg_dir to swapper_pg_dir */
    cpu_replace_ttbr1(lm_alias(swapper_pg_dir), init_pg_dir);
    init_mm.pgd = swapper_pg_dir;
    memblock_phys_free(__pa_symbol(init_pg_dir), ...);  /* free temp tables */
}

The map_mem() function walks all memblock regions and calls __create_pgd_mapping() for each one. The mapping engine descends through alloc_init_p4d → alloc_init_pud → alloc_init_cont_pmd → alloc_init_cont_pte, using block entries (2 MB or 1 GB) wherever alignment permits.

  1. The kernel image is mapped with section-specific permissions: .text → read-only + executable, .rodata → read-only + PXN/UXN, .data/.bss → read-write + PXN/UXN.
  2. After the switch, init_pg_dir pages are freed back to the page allocator.

The Direct Map

The direct map (also called the linear map or physmap) is the fundamental kernel mapping — a contiguous one-to-one translation from virtual to physical addresses that covers all of physical RAM. It allows the kernel to access any physical page by a simple address arithmetic, without needing to set up temporary mappings.

Direct map geometry

Table 2-20. Direct map parameters
ArchitectureDirect map baseFormulaMaximum size
x86_64 (4-level)0xffff888000000000 virt = phys + page_offset_base64 TB
x86_64 (5-level)0xff11000000000000 same formula32 PB
ARM64PAGE_OFFSET virt = phys + PAGE_OFFSET - PHYS_OFFSETdepends on VA_BITS

On x86_64, page_offset_base is a variable (not a compile-time constant) because KASLR randomizes it at boot. The default values come from architecture headers:

/* arch/x86/include/asm/page_64_types.h */
#define __PAGE_OFFSET_BASE_L4   _AC(0xffff888000000000, UL)
#define __PAGE_OFFSET_BASE_L5   _AC(0xff11000000000000, UL)

/* arch/x86/include/asm/page_64.h */
extern unsigned long page_offset_base;
#define __PAGE_OFFSET           page_offset_base

Address conversion helpers

The kernel provides a family of macros and inline functions to convert between physical addresses, virtual addresses, page frame numbers, and struct page pointers:

Table 2-21. Address conversion helpers
Macro / functionConversionImplementation sketch
__va(phys)PA → VA (void *)(phys + PAGE_OFFSET)
__pa(virt)VA → PA virt - PAGE_OFFSET (with overflow check on debug kernels)
phys_to_virt(pa)PA → VA Wrapper around __va()
virt_to_phys(va)VA → PA Wrapper around __pa()
virt_to_page(va)VA → struct page * pfn_to_page(virt_to_pfn(va))
page_to_virt(page)struct page * → VA __va(PFN_PHYS(page_to_pfn(page)))
pfn_to_page(pfn)PFN → struct page * SPARSEMEM: index into vmemmap[]
page_to_pfn(page)struct page * → PFN SPARSEMEM: (page - vmemmap_base)

On x86_64, __pa() is more complex than a simple subtraction because the kernel image at __START_KERNEL_map overlaps the direct map range. The actual implementation handles both regions:

/* arch/x86/include/asm/page_64.h */
static __always_inline unsigned long __phys_addr_nodebug(unsigned long x)
{
    unsigned long y = x - __START_KERNEL_map;
    /* If x > y, the address is in __START_KERNEL_map region;
     * otherwise it is in the direct map (PAGE_OFFSET region) */
    x = y + ((x > y) ? phys_base
                      : (__START_KERNEL_map - PAGE_OFFSET));
    return x;
}

Large-page optimization

On both architectures, the direct map uses large pages (1 GB or 2 MB) wherever physical memory alignment permits. This is one of the biggest performance advantages of 64-bit: the entire physical RAM is direct-mapped with large pages, dramatically reducing TLB pressure. A 256 GB server mapped with 1 GB pages needs only 256 TLB entries to cover all of RAM, compared to over 65 million entries with 4 KB pages.

On x86_64, probe_page_size_mask() checks for X86_FEATURE_GBPAGES and X86_FEATURE_PSE to determine which large page sizes are available. On ARM64, the mapping code in map_mem() uses block descriptors at the PUD level (1 GB) or PMD level (2 MB) based on alignment and contiguous-hint availability.

Kernel Virtual Memory Layout

The detailed kernel virtual memory layout — including the direct map, vmalloc region, vmemmap, module space, CPU entry area, KASAN shadow, and fix-mapped addresses — is covered in its own dedicated section: Kernel Virtual Memory Layout. That section provides per-region deep dives with KASLR randomization details for both x86_64 and ARM64.

Fixmaps

Fixmaps are compile-time constant virtual addresses that can be mapped to arbitrary physical pages at runtime. They solve the chicken-and-egg problem: early boot code needs to access specific hardware (APIC, device tree, early console) before the vmalloc allocator is available.

The fixed_addresses enum

Each fixmap slot is defined by an entry in an architecture-specific enum. The virtual address for each slot is computed backward from FIXADDR_TOP:

/* arch/x86/include/asm/fixmap.h — selected entries */
enum fixed_addresses {
    ...
    FIX_APIC_BASE,              /* local APIC MMIO */
    FIX_IO_APIC_BASE_0,        /* I/O APIC */
    ...
    __end_of_permanent_fixed_addresses,
    /* Boot-time temporary mappings (early_ioremap) */
    FIX_BTMAP_END = __end_of_permanent_fixed_addresses + 512 - 1,
    FIX_BTMAP_BEGIN = FIX_BTMAP_END + NR_FIX_BTMAPS * FIX_BTMAPS_SLOTS - 1,
    __end_of_fixed_addresses
};

/* Virtual address of a fixmap slot: grows downward from FIXADDR_TOP */
#define __fix_to_virt(x)  (FIXADDR_TOP - ((x) << PAGE_SHIFT))
#define FIXADDR_START     (FIXADDR_TOP - FIXADDR_SIZE)

ARM64 uses a similar enum with platform-specific slots:

/* arch/arm64/include/asm/fixmap.h — selected entries */
enum fixed_addresses {
    FIX_HOLE,
    /* Device tree blob — may span multiple pages */
    FIX_FDT_END,
    FIX_FDT = FIX_FDT_END + FIX_FDT_SIZE / PAGE_SIZE - 1,
    FIX_EARLYCON_MEM_BASE,      /* early serial console */
    /* Temporary page table manipulation slots */
    FIX_PTE,
    FIX_PMD,
    FIX_PUD,
    FIX_P4D,
    FIX_PGD,
    __end_of_fixed_addresses
};

ARM64's fixmap includes FIX_PTE through FIX_PGD — these are used by early boot page-table code to temporarily map page table pages before the full memory allocator is ready.

Using fixmaps: set_fixmap and early ioremap

set_fixmap(idx, phys) installs a PTE mapping for the fixmap slot idx at the given physical address. set_fixmap_nocache(idx, phys) does the same with uncacheable attributes (for MMIO devices). The operation is trivial — it simply writes a PTE into the pre-allocated page table at the known virtual address.

Before vmalloc/ioremap are available, drivers use early_ioremap(phys, size) which consumes slots from the FIX_BTMAP_* range. These temporary mappings are released with early_iounmap() and must not be used after the vmalloc subsystem initializes.

Permission Enforcement: W^X and mark_rodata_ro()

Modern kernels enforce the W^X invariant: no page should be simultaneously writable and executable. This is a critical defense against kernel exploits that attempt to inject and execute code. The feature is controlled by CONFIG_STRICT_KERNEL_RWX (enabled by default on all major distributions).

Kernel memory permission map

Table 2-22. Kernel region permissions under CONFIG_STRICT_KERNEL_RWX
RegionPermissionsRationale
.text (kernel code)Read + Execute Code must execute but must not be modified at runtime
.rodataRead-only, NX Constant data must be neither writable nor executable
.data / .bssRead-Write, NX Mutable data must not be executable
Direct mapRead-Write, NX Data access only; code runs from the separate .text mapping
vmallocRead-Write, NX (default) Exception: loaded module .text sections are mapped Read + Execute
FixmapPer-entry Some entries are device MMIO (uncacheable), others are read-only

mark_rodata_ro() — locking down at boot

Permissions are initially broad during boot (everything is read-write-execute to simplify initialization). Once the kernel has finished setting up, mark_rodata_ro() tightens permissions on all kernel sections. On x86_64:

/* arch/x86/mm/init_64.c */
void mark_rodata_ro(void)
{
    unsigned long start = PFN_ALIGN(_text);
    unsigned long rodata_start = PFN_ALIGN(__start_rodata);
    unsigned long end = (unsigned long)__end_rodata_hpage_align;
    unsigned long text_end = PFN_ALIGN(_etext);
    unsigned long all_end = PFN_ALIGN(_end);

    /* Make kernel text read-only (already executable) */
    set_memory_ro(start, (end - start) >> PAGE_SHIFT);

    /* Make everything after text non-executable */
    set_memory_nx(text_end, (all_end - text_end) >> PAGE_SHIFT);

    /* Free the freed-init-text range */
    free_kernel_image_pages("unused kernel text",
                            (void *)text_end, (void *)rodata_start);
}

On ARM64, the same effect is achieved through the update_mapping_prot() function, which walks the kernel page tables and changes block/page descriptors to enforce read-only or execute-never attributes.

The set_memory_* API

The kernel provides fine-grained runtime permission control through a family of functions in arch/x86/mm/pat/set_memory.c:

/* arch/x86/mm/pat/set_memory.c */
int set_memory_ro(unsigned long addr, int numpages)
{
    return change_page_attr_clear(&addr, numpages,
                                  __pgprot(_PAGE_RW | _PAGE_DIRTY), 0);
}
int set_memory_rw(unsigned long addr, int numpages)
{
    return change_page_attr_set(&addr, numpages,
                                __pgprot(_PAGE_RW), 0);
}
int set_memory_nx(unsigned long addr, int numpages) { ... }
int set_memory_x(unsigned long addr, int numpages)  { ... }

These functions are used by module loading (to make .text executable after copying), BPF JIT (to mark JIT-compiled code executable), and runtime patching infrastructure. Internally they call change_page_attr_set/clear(), which walks the page tables and may need to split large pages into 4 KB pages to change permissions on a sub-region.

vmalloc Mapping Propagation

User processes do not get their own copy of the kernel page tables. On x86_64, when a new process is created, pgd_alloc() copies the kernel half of swapper_pg_dir (the top-level entries 256–511) into the process's PGD. Those top-level entries are pointers to shared lower-level tables, so the question is: what happens when the kernel later grows the vmalloc region and needs a page-table page that did not exist when a process was forked?

The modern x86_64 answer is eager pre-allocation, not fault-time synchronization. During boot, preallocate_vmalloc_pages() (arch/x86/mm/init_64.c) walks the entire vmalloc/vmemmap range and allocates every top-level page-table page up front, into init_mm:

/* arch/x86/mm/init_64.c — preallocate_vmalloc_pages() */
for (addr = VMALLOC_START; addr <= VMEMORY_END; addr = ALIGN(addr + 1, PGDIR_SIZE)) {
    pgd_t *pgd = pgd_offset_k(addr);
    p4d_alloc(&init_mm, pgd, addr);          /* 5-level: allocate P4D */
    if (pgtable_l5_enabled())
        continue;
    pud_alloc(&init_mm, p4d, addr);          /* 4-level: allocate PUD too */
}
/* "The pages have to be there now or they will be missing
 *  in process page-tables later." */

Because these top-level tables exist in swapper_pg_dir before any user process is forked, every process's cloned PGD already points at the same shared lower-level tables. A subsequent vmalloc() only writes into those shared PMD/PTE levels — changes that are instantly visible through every process's PGD. The top-level PGD/P4D/PUD entries never change after boot, so there is nothing to synchronize per process and no vmalloc fault is needed on x86_64. On the rare occasions the kernel does add a fresh top-level entry, it is broadcast eagerly to all PGDs via arch_sync_kernel_mappings() → sync_global_pgds().

Historically, x86 did handle this lazily with a vmalloc_fault() handler that copied a top-level entry on demand at fault time. That path was removed on x86_64 and now exists only on 32-bit x86, where it closes a narrow mapping/unmapping race (arch/x86/mm/fault.c, under #ifdef CONFIG_X86_32). The in-tree comment is explicit: "On x86-64 the race does not exist as the vmalloc mappings don't need to be synchronized there."

ARM64 likewise needs no vmalloc fault. Kernel mappings live in swapper_pg_dir loaded into TTBR1_EL1 directly (never copied into per-process tables), so any change to the kernel page tables is inherently visible to every process once the TLB is invalidated.

Architecture Comparison: x86_64 vs ARM64

Table 2-23. Kernel page table comparison
Aspectx86_64ARM64
Master PGDswapper_pg_dir (alias init_top_pgt) in CR3 swapper_pg_dir in TTBR1_EL1
User/kernel separationSame PGD — upper 256 entries = kernel Separate TTBR0 (user) / TTBR1 (kernel)
Provisional tablesearly_top_pgt → init_top_pgt idmap_pg_dir + init_pg_dir → swapper_pg_dir
vmalloc mapping syncTop-level vmalloc PGD entries pre-allocated at boot; no fault needed (32-bit x86 uses vmalloc_fault()) Not needed — TTBR1 is shared directly
Direct map page size1 GB preferred (X86_FEATURE_GBPAGES), 2 MB fallback Block entries: 1 GB (PUD) or 2 MB (PMD)
KASLR impactRandomizes page_offset_base, vmalloc_base, vmemmap_base Randomizes KIMAGE_VADDR offset
KPTI implementationShadow PGD with only trampoline entries for user mode TTBR0 invalidated or pointed to trampoline PGD on kernel entry
Permission enforcementset_memory_ro/rw/nx/x() via change_page_attr update_mapping_prot() walks and modifies descriptors
Fixmap locationTop of address space (0xffffffffff000000) Below vmemmap (FIXADDR_TOP = -SZ_8M)
Identity map lifetimeRemoved after init_top_pgt switch Kept in idmap_pg_dir for CPU hotplug and suspend/resume

Virtualization and Two-Stage Address Translation

In a virtualized system the guest operating system believes it manages physical memory, but the hardware actually interposes a second translation layer controlled by the hypervisor. Linux 7.0.12's KVM subsystem leverages hardware-assisted two-stage translation on both x86_64 (Intel EPT / AMD NPT) and ARM64 (Stage-2 page tables) to run guest VMs with near-native performance. This section dissects the hardware mechanisms, the KVM data structures that manage them, and the fault handling paths that populate Stage-2 mappings on demand. The hypervisor that owns these tables—the vCPU run loop, VM exits, memory slots, and guest_memfd—is the subject of Chapter 28.

The topics covered are:

  1. Why two stages — the problem two-stage translation solves and the address terminology (GVA, GPA/IPA, HPA).
  2. Intel EPT and AMD NPT — the x86_64 hardware mechanism, SPTE format, and KVM's TDP MMU.
  3. ARM64 Stage-2 — VTTBR_EL2, the Stage-2 page table walker, and permission attributes.
  4. TLB tagging — VPID and VMID hardware tags that avoid full flushes on VM switches.
  5. Shadow vs. hardware page tables — why hardware two-stage replaced the older software approach.
  6. Stage-2 fault handling — the complete fault-to-mapping path on both architectures.
  7. MMU notifiers — how host memory-management changes are propagated into the Stage-2 tables to keep them coherent.
  8. Dirty page tracking — how KVM tracks modified pages for live migration.
  9. Nested virtualization — running a hypervisor inside a guest.

Why Two Stages?

Without virtualization, the MMU performs a single translation: Virtual Address (VA) → Physical Address (PA). When a hypervisor runs guest operating systems, each guest has its own page tables and believes it controls physical memory. But the addresses produced by guest page tables are not real physical addresses — they are Intermediate Physical Addresses (IPAs) on ARM64, or Guest Physical Addresses (GPAs) on x86_64.

This creates a three-layer address hierarchy:

Table 2-24. Address types in a virtualized system
Address typex86_64 nameARM64 nameManaged by
Guest virtualGVA (Guest Virtual Address)VAGuest OS
Guest "physical"GPA (Guest Physical Address)IPA (Intermediate Physical Address)Guest OS (layout), hypervisor (backing)
Host physicalHPA (Host Physical Address)PA (Physical Address)Hypervisor / host OS

The hardware MMU performs two translations in sequence:

StageTranslationControlled byPage tables
Stage 1GVA → GPA (IPA)Guest OS Guest page tables (CR3 on x86_64, TTBR0/TTBR1 on ARM64)
Stage 2GPA (IPA) → HPA (PA)Hypervisor (KVM) EPT/NPT tables (x86_64), Stage-2 tables (ARM64)

When a guest process accesses a virtual address, the hardware first walks the guest's Stage-1 tables to produce a GPA. Then, before completing the memory access, the MMU walks the hypervisor's Stage-2 tables to translate that GPA into the real physical address. This happens transparently in hardware — the guest OS is completely unaware of Stage 2.

The translation cost is significant: a 4-level Stage-1 walk requires 4 memory accesses, and each of those accesses is itself a GPA that must be translated through Stage 2 (another 4 accesses). In the worst case, a single guest page table walk requires up to 4 × 4 + 4 = 20 memory accesses (each of the 4 Stage-1 accesses needs a full 4-level Stage-2 walk, plus 4 final Stage-2 accesses for the data page). TLB caching hides most of this cost in practice, but it explains why VPID/VMID tagging (Section 11.4) is critical.

Intel EPT and AMD NPT on x86_64

Intel's Extended Page Tables (EPT) and AMD's Nested Page Tables (NPT) provide the hardware Stage-2 mechanism on x86_64. Both use a 4-level page table structure mirroring the regular x86_64 format (PML4 → PDPT → PD → PT), with 512 entries per level (9 bits per level). KVM unifies both behind a single abstraction called the TDP MMU (Two-Dimensional Paging MMU).

Enabling EPT and the EPTP

EPT is enabled by default on all supporting CPUs. KVM constructs the EPT Pointer (EPTP) — a 64-bit value loaded into the VMCS that tells the hardware where the Stage-2 root table is and how to walk it:

/* arch/x86/kvm/vmx/vmx.c */
bool __read_mostly enable_ept = 1;

/* The EPTP encodes the root HPA, walk length, and memory type */
static u64 construct_eptp(struct kvm_vcpu *vcpu, hpa_t root_hpa,
                           int root_level)
{
    u64 eptp = VMX_EPTP_MT_WB |          /* write-back caching */
               VMX_EPTP_PWL_4 |          /* 4-level walk */
               root_hpa;                 /* physical addr of PML4 */
    if (enable_ept_ad_bits)
        eptp |= VMX_EPTP_AD_ENABLE;      /* hardware dirty/access bits */
    return eptp;
}
