The EPTP is written into the VMCS on every VM entry. When the guest accesses a GPA, the CPU reads the EPT root from the VMCS and performs the Stage-2 walk using this pointer.

The SPTE format

Each entry in KVM's TDP page tables is a Shadow Page Table Entry (SPTE). The format mirrors Intel/AMD hardware EPT/NPT entries but includes KVM-specific software bits in the upper reserved region:

/* arch/x86/kvm/mmu/spte.h */
#define SPTE_EPT_READABLE_MASK    0x1ull    /* bit 0: read allowed */
#define SPTE_EPT_EXECUTABLE_MASK  0x4ull    /* bit 2: execute allowed */
#define SPTE_MMU_PRESENT_MASK     BIT_ULL(11) /* KVM software "present" bit */
#define SPTE_LEVEL_BITS           9
#define SPTE_ENT_PER_PAGE         (1 << SPTE_LEVEL_BITS) /* 512 */

EPT entries differ from regular x86_64 PTEs in permission semantics: read, write, and execute permissions are independent (there is no implicit read-implies-execute rule). A non-readable but executable page is valid in EPT, which enables features like execute-only memory for code integrity.

The TDP MMU and struct kvm_mmu

KVM's MMU abstraction sits in struct kvm_mmu, which contains function pointers for page fault handling, GVA→GPA translation, and the root table information:

/* arch/x86/include/asm/kvm_host.h */
struct kvm_mmu {
    unsigned long (*get_guest_pgd)(struct kvm_vcpu *vcpu);
    int (*page_fault)(struct kvm_vcpu *vcpu,
                      struct kvm_page_fault *fault);
    void (*inject_page_fault)(struct kvm_vcpu *vcpu,
                              struct x86_exception *fault);
    gpa_t (*gva_to_gpa)(struct kvm_vcpu *vcpu, struct kvm_mmu *mmu,
                        gpa_t gva_or_gpa, u64 access,
                        struct x86_exception *exception);
    struct kvm_mmu_root_info root;
    union kvm_cpu_role cpu_role;
    union kvm_mmu_page_role root_role;
    ...
};

During vCPU initialization, kvm_init_mmu() decides between the TDP path and the legacy shadow path:

/* arch/x86/kvm/mmu/mmu.c — simplified */
void kvm_init_mmu(struct kvm_vcpu *vcpu)
{
    bool is_tdp = tdp_mmu_enabled && mmu->root_role.direct;
    if (is_tdp) {
        init_kvm_tdp_mmu(vcpu, cpu_role);
        context->page_fault = kvm_tdp_page_fault;
    } else {
        init_kvm_shadow_mmu(vcpu, cpu_role);
        context->page_fault = kvm_shadow_page_fault;
    }
}

On any modern CPU with EPT or NPT, the TDP path is always chosen. The shadow path remains only for legacy scenarios (32-bit guests without hardware two-stage support).

Huge pages in EPT

KVM uses large EPT mappings (2 MB at the PD level, 1 GB at the PDPT level) wherever possible to reduce TLB pressure and page table memory. The TDP MMU checks alignment and host transparent hugepage status when handling a fault. If the guest fault covers a 2 MB-aligned GPA range and the host has a transparent hugepage backing it, KVM installs a single 2 MB SPTE instead of 512 individual 4 KB entries. When dirty logging or fine-grained permissions require it, KVM splits these huge SPTEs back into smaller pages via tdp_mmu_split_huge_page().

ARM64 Stage-2 Translation

ARM64 provides Stage-2 translation through dedicated EL2 (hypervisor exception level) registers and a page table format that is similar to — but distinct from — the Stage-1 format.

Hardware registers

Table 2-25. ARM64 Stage-2 hardware registers
RegisterPurpose
VTTBR_EL2 Holds the physical address of the Stage-2 PGD plus the VMID in bits [63:48]
VTCR_EL2 Configures IPA size (T0SZ), granule size, starting level, and shareability attributes for Stage-2 walks
HCR_EL2 Hypervisor Configuration Register — bit HCR_VM enables Stage-2 translation
HPFAR_EL2 On a Stage-2 fault, holds the faulting IPA (shifted right by 4 bits)

KVM Stage-2 data structures

KVM on ARM64 manages Stage-2 through struct kvm_s2_mmu, which holds all per-VM Stage-2 state:

/* arch/arm64/include/asm/kvm_host.h */
struct kvm_s2_mmu {
    struct kvm_vmid vmid;             /* VMID for TLB tagging */
    phys_addr_t     pgd_phys;        /* Physical address of Stage-2 PGD */
    struct kvm_pgtable *pgt;          /* Page table walker context */
    u64             vtcr;             /* VTCR_EL2 value for this VM */
    int __percpu    *last_vcpu_ran;   /* Track last vCPU per physical CPU */
    struct kvm_mmu_memory_cache split_page_cache;
    u64             tlb_vttbr;        /* Cached VTTBR for TLB invalidation */
    u64             tlb_vtcr;
    bool            nested_stage2_enabled;
    ...
};

The page table itself is described by struct kvm_pgtable:

/* arch/arm64/include/asm/kvm_pgtable.h */
struct kvm_pgtable {
    u32                         ia_bits;      /* Input address (IPA) size */
    s8                          start_level;  /* Walk starts at this level */
    kvm_pteref_t                pgd;          /* Top-level entries */
    struct kvm_pgtable_mm_ops   *mm_ops;      /* Memory allocator ops */
    enum kvm_pgtable_stage2_flags flags;
    struct kvm_s2_mmu           *mmu;
};

The IPA size is determined at boot from the CPU's ID_AA64MMFR0_EL1.PARange field and stored in kvm_ipa_limit (arch/arm64/kvm/reset.c). Typical values are 40 bits (1 TB) or 48 bits (256 TB) of guest "physical" address space.

Stage-2 permission attributes

Stage-2 page table entries use a different permission model from Stage-1. Instead of the AP[2:1] bits used in Stage-1, Stage-2 uses S2AP (Stage-2 Access Permission) bits and separate execute-never controls:

/* arch/arm64/kvm/hyp/pgtable.c */
static void stage2_set_prot_attr(struct kvm_pgtable_visit_ctx *ctx,
                                  kvm_pte_t *new, enum kvm_pgtable_prot prot)
{
    kvm_pte_t attr = 0;
    if (prot & KVM_PGTABLE_PROT_R)
        attr |= KVM_PTE_LEAF_ATTR_LO_S2_S2AP_R;  /* read access */
    if (prot & KVM_PGTABLE_PROT_W)
        attr |= KVM_PTE_LEAF_ATTR_LO_S2_S2AP_W;  /* write access */
    *new |= attr;
}

static void stage2_set_xn_attr(kvm_pte_t *new, enum kvm_pgtable_prot prot)
{
    /* XN[1:0]: 00=exec both EL0/EL1, 01=EL1 only, 10=none, 11=EL0 only */
    u64 xn = KVM_PTE_LEAF_ATTR_HI_S2_XN;  /* default: no execute */
    if (prot & KVM_PGTABLE_PROT_X)
        xn = 0;  /* allow execution */
    *new |= xn;
}

This independent R/W/X model means the hypervisor can create execute-only pages (readable by the CPU instruction fetch but not by data loads), which is used for guest code integrity protection.

The Stage-2 page table walker API

KVM provides a clean API for manipulating Stage-2 mappings:

Table 2-26. ARM64 KVM Stage-2 page table API
FunctionPurpose
kvm_pgtable_stage2_init() Allocate and initialize the Stage-2 page table for a VM
kvm_pgtable_stage2_map() Install a mapping: IPA → PA with given permissions and size
kvm_pgtable_stage2_unmap() Remove a mapping for an IPA range
kvm_pgtable_stage2_flush() Clean Stage-2 entries to the point of coherence (for DMA)
kvm_pgtable_stage2_split() Split block (huge) mappings into page-level mappings
kvm_pgtable_stage2_relax_perms() Widen permissions on an existing mapping (e.g., add write after COW fault)
kvm_pgtable_stage2_wrprotect() Write-protect a range (for dirty tracking)

Internally, these functions use a generic page-table walker (kvm_pgtable_walk()) with callback functions for each level. The map operation calls stage2_map_walker_try_leaf() at each level to determine whether a block mapping (2 MB or 1 GB) can be installed or whether the walk must descend further.

TLB Tagging: VPID and VMID

Without TLB tagging, every VM entry and exit would require a full TLB flush — devastating for performance given that a single guest page walk can require up to 20 memory accesses through the two-stage hierarchy. Both architectures provide hardware tags that let TLB entries from different VMs and vCPUs coexist in the TLB simultaneously.

Comparison

Table 2-27. VPID vs VMID comparison
Featurex86_64 (VPID)ARM64 (VMID)
Full nameVirtual Processor IDVirtual Machine ID
Width16 bits (65,535 usable IDs)8 or 16 bits (configurable)
ScopePer-vCPU (unique per virtual processor) Per-VM (all vCPUs of a VM share one VMID)
StorageVMCS field (VIRTUAL_PROCESSOR_ID) VTTBR_EL2[63:48]
Selective flushINVVPID (by VPID) / INVEPT (by EPT) TLBI VMALLS12E1IS (all Stage 1+2 for VMID)
AllocationSimple bitmap + atomic counter Generation-based allocator

x86_64 VPID allocation

KVM allocates a unique VPID for each vCPU using a simple atomic counter:

/* arch/x86/kvm/vmx/vmx.c */
static int allocate_vpid(void)
{
    int vpid;
    if (!enable_vpid)
        return 0;
    spin_lock(&vmx_vpid_lock);
    vpid = find_first_zero_bit(vmx_vpid_bitmap, VMX_NR_VPIDS);
    if (vpid < VMX_NR_VPIDS)
        __set_bit(vpid, vmx_vpid_bitmap);
    else
        vpid = 0;  /* fall back to VPID 0 = flush on every switch */
    spin_unlock(&vmx_vpid_lock);
    return vpid;
}

VPID 0 is reserved for the host — if a vCPU gets VPID 0, TLB entries are not tagged and every VM entry/exit flushes the TLB (a graceful degradation). When a full EPT invalidation is needed (e.g., all of a VM's Stage-2 mappings change), KVM uses the INVEPT instruction which invalidates all EPT-derived TLB entries for a given EPTP.

ARM64 generation-based VMID allocator

ARM64's VMID space is smaller (typically 256 with 8-bit VMIDs), so KVM uses a generation-based allocator that recycles VMIDs efficiently:

/* arch/arm64/kvm/vmid.c */
void kvm_arm_vmid_update(struct kvm_vmid *kvm_vmid)
{
    unsigned long flags;
    u64 vmid, old_vmid;

    /* Fast path: generation matches, VMID is still valid */
    vmid = atomic64_read(&kvm_vmid->id);
    if (READ_ONCE(vmid_generation) == (vmid & ~VMID_MASK))
        return;  /* no update needed */

    /* Slow path: need a new VMID */
    raw_spin_lock_irqsave(&cpu_vmid_lock, flags);
    vmid = new_vmid(kvm_vmid);
    raw_spin_unlock_irqrestore(&cpu_vmid_lock, flags);
}

static u64 new_vmid(struct kvm_vmid *kvm_vmid)
{
    ...
    vmid = find_next_zero_bit(vmid_map, num_vmids, vmid_counter);
    if (vmid >= num_vmids) {
        /* VMID space exhausted — start a new generation */
        generation = atomic64_add_return_relaxed(
                         VMID_FIRST_VERSION, &vmid_generation);
        flush_context();   /* invalidate all Stage-2 TLBs */
        vmid = find_next_zero_bit(vmid_map, num_vmids, 1);
    }
    __set_bit(vmid, vmid_map);
    return idx2vmid(vmid) | generation;
}

The generation trick is elegant: when all VMIDs are exhausted, KVM bumps the generation counter, flushes all Stage-2 TLB entries, and starts reissuing VMIDs from the beginning. Each kvm_vmid stores its generation alongside its VMID — if the stored generation doesn't match the current one, the VM knows it needs a fresh VMID before entering the guest. This is the same pattern used for ARM64 ASIDs in the non-virtualization context (Section 9.4).

Hardware-Assisted vs. Shadow Page Tables

Before EPT/NPT existed (pre-2008 on Intel, pre-2007 on AMD) and before ARM VHE, hypervisors used shadow page tables — software-maintained copies of the guest's page tables that combined Stage-1 and Stage-2 translations into a single set of tables the hardware could walk natively.

How shadow page tables work

  1. Intercept guest page-table writes — KVM write-protects all guest page-table pages so that any modification triggers a page fault (VM exit).
  2. Synchronize on each trap — when the guest writes a PTE, KVM catches the fault, reads the new guest PTE, combines it with the Stage-2 translation (GPA → HPA), and writes the result into the shadow page table.
  3. Load the shadow table into CR3 — the hardware sees only the shadow table, which maps GVA → HPA directly in one stage.

This approach produced heavy overhead, especially for workloads with frequent page table updates (e.g., fork()'s COW write-protection, mmap(), munmap()). Every guest page-table modification caused a VM exit.

Comparison

Table 2-28. Shadow vs. hardware two-stage page tables
AspectShadow Page TablesHardware Two-Stage (TDP)
Guest PT writesTrapped via write-protection → VM exit Native, no traps needed
Translation depthSingle stage (GVA → HPA) Two stages (GVA → GPA → HPA)
TLB usageOne set of entries per shadow table Tagged entries, no flush on VM switch
Memory overheadShadow copy of every guest page table One Stage-2 table per VM
VM exit frequencyHigh (every guest PT modification) Low (only on genuine unmapped-page faults)
Page walk cost4 memory accesses (single stage) Up to 20 memory accesses (nested walks)
KVM implementation arch/x86/kvm/mmu/mmu.c (shadow path) arch/x86/kvm/mmu/tdp_mmu.c (TDP path)

KVM retains shadow page table support for legacy scenarios (32-bit guests on 64-bit hosts, or rare CPUs without EPT/NPT), but the TDP path is used by default on any modern CPU. The shadow path is also used within nested virtualization (Section 11.8) in certain configurations.

Stage-2 Page Fault Handling

Stage-2 page tables are populated on demand: when a VM is first created, its Stage-2 table is empty. As the guest accesses memory, each unmapped GPA triggers a Stage-2 fault (EPT violation on x86_64, Stage-2 abort on ARM64) that causes a VM exit. KVM's fault handler resolves the fault by installing the appropriate Stage-2 mapping.

x86_64 EPT violation path

The fault handling chain on x86_64:

  1. VM exit — the CPU reports an EPT violation in the VMCS exit reason field, with the faulting GPA in the VMCS guest-physical-address field.
  2. handle_ept_violation() (arch/x86/kvm/vmx/vmx.c) — reads the exit qualification bits to determine whether the fault was a read, write, or instruction fetch violation.
  3. kvm_mmu_page_fault() → kvm_tdp_page_fault() (arch/x86/kvm/mmu/mmu.c) — the generic fault entry point dispatches to the TDP fault handler.
  4. GPA → HVA resolution — KVM looks up the GPA in the VM's memory slots (struct kvm_memory_slot) to find the corresponding host virtual address (HVA).
  5. HVA → HPA resolution — kvm_faultin_pfn() calls get_user_pages() to pin the host page and obtain the physical frame number (PFN).
  6. kvm_tdp_mmu_map() (arch/x86/kvm/mmu/tdp_mmu.c) — walks the TDP page table from the root, allocating intermediate levels as needed, and installs the leaf SPTE mapping GPA → HPA with the appropriate permissions.
  7. VM resume — control returns to the guest, and the re-executed instruction now succeeds.

ARM64 Stage-2 abort path

The ARM64 fault path is analogous but uses different entry points:

  1. Exception to EL2 — the Stage-2 abort is captured as a data/instruction abort from a lower exception level. The faulting IPA is in HPFAR_EL2.
  2. kvm_handle_guest_abort() (arch/arm64/kvm/mmu.c) — determines whether the fault is a translation fault (unmapped page) or a permission fault (e.g., write to read-only).
  3. user_mem_abort() — the main fault handler that resolves the IPA to a host PFN and installs the Stage-2 mapping:
/* arch/arm64/kvm/mmu.c — simplified */
static int user_mem_abort(struct kvm_vcpu *vcpu,
                          phys_addr_t fault_ipa, ...)
{
    /* Resolve IPA → host PFN */
    pfn = __kvm_faultin_pfn(memslot, gfn,
                            write_fault ? FOLL_WRITE : 0, ...);

    /* Permission fault on existing mapping? Just relax permissions */
    if (fault_is_perm && vma_pagesize == fault_granule) {
        ret = kvm_pgtable_stage2_relax_perms(pgt, fault_ipa, prot);
    } else {
        /* Translation fault: install new mapping */
        ret = kvm_pgtable_stage2_map(pgt, fault_ipa,
                                     vma_pagesize, __pfn_to_phys(pfn),
                                     prot, memcache, flags);
    }
    return ret;
}

Mapping size selection

Both architectures attempt to install the largest possible mapping to minimize future faults and TLB pressure:

  • If the host VMA uses transparent hugepages and the GPA is 2 MB-aligned, a 2 MB block/PMD mapping is installed.
  • On ARM64, transparent_hugepage_adjust() is called to check whether the host backing is a hugepage and can be mapped at the PMD (2 MB) or PUD (1 GB) level.
  • If dirty logging is active, huge mappings may be forced to 4 KB granularity to enable per-page tracking.

Keeping Stage-2 Coherent: MMU Notifiers

The fault handlers above only populate Stage-2 mappings. But guest memory is backed by ordinary host user pages (obtained through __kvm_faultin_pfn() → get_user_pages()), and the host MM subsystem is free to move, reclaim, or free those pages at any time: page reclaim under memory pressure, NUMA migration, swap-out, transparent-hugepage collapse by khugepaged, KSM merging, or a plain munmap() of the guest memory region. If the host silently repurposed such a page while a stale Stage-2 entry still pointed at the old frame, the guest would read or corrupt memory that no longer belongs to it. The mmu_notifier infrastructure closes this gap.

KVM registers a set of notifier callbacks (virt/kvm/kvm_main.c) that the core MM calls whenever it is about to change a mapping that might back guest memory:

/* virt/kvm/kvm_main.c — KVM's MMU-notifier callbacks */
static const struct mmu_notifier_ops kvm_mmu_notifier_ops = {
    .invalidate_range_start = kvm_mmu_notifier_invalidate_range_start,
    .invalidate_range_end   = kvm_mmu_notifier_invalidate_range_end,
    .clear_flush_young      = kvm_mmu_notifier_clear_flush_young,
    .clear_young            = kvm_mmu_notifier_clear_young,
    .test_young             = kvm_mmu_notifier_test_young,
    .release                = kvm_mmu_notifier_release,
};

Two families of callback matter for address translation:

  • Invalidation — before the host unmaps or moves a range of host virtual addresses, it calls invalidate_range_start. KVM translates the affected HVA range back to guest frame numbers and calls kvm_unmap_gfn_range(), which zaps the corresponding Stage-2 entries (kvm_tdp_mmu_unmap_gfn_range() on x86_64, kvm_pgtable_stage2_unmap() on ARM64) and flushes the Stage-2 TLB by VPID/VMID. The guest simply re-faults later and KVM re-installs a fresh, correct mapping. A generation counter (mmu_invalidate_seq) lets an in-flight fault detect that an invalidation raced with it and retry rather than install a stale PFN.
  • Aging — when host reclaim walks the LRU and tests whether a page is still hot, clear_flush_young/test_young reach into the Stage-2 tables via kvm_age_gfn()/kvm_test_age_gfn() to read and clear the Stage-2 Accessed bit. This makes guest accesses visible to the host's page-replacement policy, so a page actively used by the guest is not mistakenly reclaimed.

MMU notifiers are what make guest memory a first-class participant in normal host memory management: the same reclaim, migration, THP, and KSM machinery described earlier in this chapter operates on VM-backing pages, with the notifier callbacks keeping the second translation stage in lock-step. The same infrastructure underpins IOMMU/SVA coherence (discussed in the DMA section) — any hardware that caches host translations registers as an mmu_notifier to be told when those translations change.

Dirty Page Tracking for Live Migration

Live migration moves a running VM from one physical host to another. To do this, the hypervisor needs to know which guest pages have been modified (dirtied) since the last transfer iteration. KVM uses the Stage-2 page tables to track dirty pages efficiently.

The write-protection approach

The basic mechanism is the same on both architectures:

  1. Write-protect all Stage-2 mappings — when dirty logging is enabled for a memory region, KVM removes write permission from all Stage-2 PTEs in that region.
  2. Trap on write — when the guest writes to a page, the Stage-2 permission fault triggers a VM exit.
  3. Mark dirty and re-enable — KVM marks the page as dirty in a bitmap, restores write permission in the Stage-2 PTE, and resumes the guest.
  4. Harvest the bitmap — the migration thread periodically reads and clears the dirty bitmap, sending modified pages to the destination host.

Implementation

On ARM64, kvm_mmu_wp_memory_region() write-protects a memory slot by calling kvm_stage2_wp_range():

/* arch/arm64/kvm/mmu.c */
void kvm_mmu_wp_memory_region(struct kvm *kvm, int slot)
{
    ...
    kvm_stage2_wp_range(&kvm->arch.mmu, start, end);
    /* For manual-protect mode, pre-split huge pages to avoid
     * expensive splitting on the write-fault path */
    if (kvm_dirty_log_manual_protect_and_init_set(kvm))
        kvm_mmu_split_huge_pages(kvm, start, end);
}

Splitting huge pages before dirty logging begins is important: if a 2 MB block mapping were left intact, a single write would dirty 512 pages (2 MB) at once, making migration less efficient. Pre-splitting ensures each 4 KB page can be tracked independently.

On x86_64, the equivalent function is kvm_tdp_mmu_wrprot_slot() in arch/x86/kvm/mmu/tdp_mmu.c, which walks the TDP tree and clears the write bit on all SPTEs. Intel EPT also supports hardware accessed/dirty (A/D) bits (enable_ept_ad_bits), which let the CPU set the dirty bit directly in the SPTE without causing a VM exit — significantly reducing dirty-tracking overhead for write-heavy workloads.

Nested Virtualization

Nested virtualization allows running a hypervisor inside a guest VM — for example, running KVM inside a KVM guest. This creates three levels of address translation:

Table 2-29. Nested virtualization address layers
LevelRolePage tables
L0Host hypervisor (bare metal KVM) Host Stage-2 (EPT/NPT or ARM64 Stage-2)
L1Guest hypervisor (KVM in the guest) Guest's Stage-2 (L1 thinks it controls Stage-2)
L2Nested guest (VM inside the L1 guest) L2's page tables (managed by L1)

The challenge is that L1's Stage-2 tables map L2's GPAs to L1's GPAs — but these L1 GPAs are themselves virtual from L0's perspective. The hardware only supports two physical translation stages, so L0 must compose L1's Stage-2 with its own Stage-2 to create a combined "shadow" Stage-2 that maps L2 GPAs directly to real HPAs.

On x86_64, nested EPT is handled in arch/x86/kvm/vmx/nested.c, which manages nested VMCS entries and emulates INVEPT/INVVPID instructions issued by the L1 hypervisor.

On ARM64, nested Stage-2 support is indicated by the nested_stage2_enabled field in struct kvm_s2_mmu:

/* arch/arm64/kvm/nested.c */
s2_mmu->nested_stage2_enabled =
    vcpu_read_sys_reg(vcpu, HCR_EL2) & HCR_VM;

When this flag is set, KVM creates a shadow Stage-2 table that merges L1's virtual Stage-2 mappings with L0's real Stage-2 mappings, allowing L2 to run with near-native performance despite the extra translation layer.

Kernel Virtual Memory Layout

Up to this point we have focused on generic translation mechanisms. The kernel also carves its virtual address space into named, fixed-purpose regions: the direct map, vmalloc, vmemmap, module space, fixmaps, CPU entry areas, and KASAN shadow memory. Understanding this layout is essential for kernel debugging, crash analysis, and comprehending how the various memory subsystems coexist without colliding.

This section covers:

  1. x86_64 virtual address space — the canonical split, kernel-half regions, and compile-time constants.
  2. KASLR randomization — how kernel_randomize_memory() shuffles region bases.
  3. ARM64 virtual address space — the TTBR0/TTBR1 split, VA_BITS, and formula-driven layout.
  4. Per-region deep dives — direct map, vmalloc, vmemmap, module space, CPU entry area, and KASAN shadow.
  5. Fix-mapped addresses — compile-time slots for early boot and special mappings.

x86_64 Virtual Address Space

The authoritative overview for x86_64 is Documentation/arch/x86/x86_64/mm.rst. For four-level paging (48-bit VA), the 64-bit canonical address space is split into:

  • Low-half user space: 0x0000000000000000 → 0x00007fffffffffff (128 TB)
  • Non-canonical hole: the vast gap where bits [63:48] are neither all-zeros nor all-ones — any access faults immediately.
  • High-half kernel space: 0xffff800000000000 → 0xffffffffffffffff (128 TB)

The kernel-half region map

The kernel half is subdivided into purpose-specific regions. The following table summarizes the 4-level layout with sizes derived from Documentation/arch/x86/x86_64/mm.rst:

Table 2-30. x86_64 kernel virtual regions (4-level, 48-bit VA)
Start addressEnd addressSizeRegion
ffff800000000000ffff87ffffffffff 8 TBGuard hole / hypervisor reserve
ffff880000000000ffff887fffffffff 0.5 TBLDT remap for PTI
ffff888000000000ffffc87fffffffff 64 TBDirect map (page_offset_base)
ffffc90000000000ffffe8ffffffffff 32 TBvmalloc / ioremap
ffffea0000000000ffffeaffffffffff 1 TBvmemmap (struct page array)
ffffeb0000000000ffffebffffffffff 1 TBGuard hole
ffffec0000000000fffffbffffffffff 16 TBKASAN shadow
fffffe0000000000fffffe7fffffffff 0.5 TBcpu_entry_area
ffffff0000000000ffffff7fffffffff 0.5 TB%esp fixup stacks
ffffffff80000000ffffffff9fffffff 512 MBKernel text (__START_KERNEL_map)
ffffffffa0000000fffffffffeffffff ~1.5 GBModules
ffffffffff000000ffffffffff5fffff ~6 MBFixmap
ffffffffff600000ffffffffff600fff 4 KBLegacy vsyscall page

Compile-time constants

These boundaries come from architecture headers. The key definitions:

/* arch/x86/include/asm/pgtable_64_types.h */
#define __VMALLOC_BASE_L4   0xffffc90000000000UL
#define __VMALLOC_BASE_L5   0xffa0000000000000UL
#define __VMEMMAP_BASE_L4   0xffffea0000000000UL
#define __VMEMMAP_BASE_L5   0xffd4000000000000UL

extern unsigned long vmalloc_base;
extern unsigned long vmemmap_base;

#define VMALLOC_START       vmalloc_base
#define VMEMMAP_START       vmemmap_base
#define VMALLOC_END         (VMEMORY_END)

/* arch/x86/include/asm/page_64_types.h */
#define __PAGE_OFFSET_BASE_L4   _AC(0xffff888000000000, UL)
#define __PAGE_OFFSET_BASE_L5   _AC(0xff11000000000000, UL)
#define __START_KERNEL_map      _AC(0xffffffff80000000, UL)

Note that VMALLOC_START, VMEMMAP_START, and page_offset_base are all variables, not compile-time constants. This is because KASLR randomizes them at boot (Section 12.2).

5-level paging adjustments

With 5-level paging (LA57, 57-bit VA), the same relative ordering is preserved, but all bases shift to accommodate the much larger 128 PB address space:

Table 2-31. 4-level vs 5-level base addresses
Region4-level base5-level base
Direct map0xffff888000000000 0xff11000000000000
vmalloc0xffffc90000000000 0xffa0000000000000
vmemmap0xffffea0000000000 0xffd4000000000000

Linux preserves the shape of the kernel's virtual layout even as the address space expands, which keeps many code assumptions stable across paging modes.

KASLR Address Randomization

Kernel Address Space Layout Randomization (KASLR) shifts the bases of major kernel regions by a random offset at each boot, making it harder for attackers to predict virtual addresses. On x86_64, kernel_randomize_memory() randomizes three key bases:

/* arch/x86/mm/kaslr.c — simplified */
void __init kernel_randomize_memory(void)
{
    unsigned long vaddr_end = CPU_ENTRY_AREA_BASE;
    unsigned long vaddr = vaddr_start;

    /* Process each region in order: direct map, vmalloc, vmemmap */
    for (i = 0; i < ARRAY_SIZE(kaslr_regions); i++) {
        unsigned long entropy = get_padding(&kaslr_regions[i]);
        vaddr += entropy;           /* random gap before this region */
        *kaslr_regions[i].base = vaddr;  /* set the new base */
        vaddr += kaslr_regions[i].size_tb << TB_SHIFT;
    }
    /* The three randomized bases are:
     *   page_offset_base  — direct map
     *   vmalloc_base      — vmalloc / ioremap
     *   vmemmap_base      — vmemmap
     */
}

The randomization has constraints: each region must still fit between its predecessor and CPU_ENTRY_AREA_BASE, and alignment must be maintained for TLB efficiency. The entropy comes from the boot randomness pool (RDRAND, RDSEED, or boot parameters). On each reboot, the direct map, vmalloc, and vmemmap all start at different addresses.

On ARM64, KASLR randomizes KIMAGE_VADDR — the virtual address of the kernel image. The linear map base (PAGE_OFFSET) is a compile-time constant derived from VA_BITS, but the physical-to-virtual offset (PHYS_OFFSET) varies based on where the bootloader placed the kernel in physical memory.

ARM64 Virtual Address Space

ARM64's layout is documented in Documentation/arch/arm64/memory.rst and parameterized in arch/arm64/include/asm/memory.h. The most important conceptual difference from x86_64 is that the user and kernel halves are architecturally separated by different translation-table base registers:

  • TTBR0_EL1 — translates the lower half (user space): addresses from 0 to (1 << VA_BITS) - 1
  • TTBR1_EL1 — translates the upper half (kernel space): addresses from PAGE_OFFSET to 0xffffffffffffffff

Bit 55 of the virtual address steers the MMU to the correct translation base register. This hardware-level split is the foundation for ARM64's simpler KPTI implementation (Section 13).

VA_BITS and VA_BITS_MIN

ARM64 supports configurable virtual address widths (typically 39, 42, 48, or 52 bits). The key macros are:

/* arch/arm64/include/asm/memory.h */
#define VA_BITS         (CONFIG_ARM64_VA_BITS)
#define VA_BITS_MIN     (VA_BITS > 48 ? 48 : VA_BITS)

#define PAGE_OFFSET     (_PAGE_OFFSET(VA_BITS))
/* _PAGE_OFFSET(va) = (-(1UL << (va))) */

#define MODULES_VADDR   (_PAGE_END(VA_BITS_MIN))
#define MODULES_END     (MODULES_VADDR + SZ_2G)
#define KIMAGE_VADDR    (MODULES_END)
#define VMEMMAP_START   (VMEMMAP_END - VMEMMAP_SIZE)
#define FIXADDR_TOP     (-UL(SZ_8M))

#define PHYS_OFFSET     ({ VM_BUG_ON(memstart_addr & 1); memstart_addr; })

VA_BITS_MIN handles the Large Virtual Address (LVA) case: when VA_BITS = 52, the kernel may fall back to 48-bit addressing on CPUs that don't support 52-bit VAs (the vabits_actual variable holds the runtime value). Module and vmalloc addresses are placed relative to VA_BITS_MIN to ensure they work on both 48-bit and 52-bit CPUs.

ARM64 kernel region layout

Table 2-32. ARM64 kernel virtual regions (48-bit VA, 4 KB pages)
MacroRegionTypical range
PAGE_OFFSETLinear map (direct map) ffff000000000000 → ~128 TB
VMALLOC_STARTvmalloc / ioremap After modules, up to VMEMMAP_START
VMEMMAP_STARTvmemmap Before PCI I/O
PCI_IO_STARTPCI I/O aperture 16 MB window after vmemmap
FIXADDR_TOPFixmap Top 8 MB of address space, growing down
MODULES_VADDRModules 2 GB window at _PAGE_END(VA_BITS_MIN)
KIMAGE_VADDRKernel image Immediately after MODULES_END

Unlike x86_64 where many boundaries are literal hex constants, ARM64 expresses everything relative to VA_BITS. The same kernel binary adapts to 39-bit through 52-bit address spaces through these formula-driven macros.

/* arch/arm64/include/asm/pgtable.h */
#define VMALLOC_START   (MODULES_END)
#define VMALLOC_END     (VMEMMAP_START - SZ_8M)

The Direct Map

The direct map (also called the linear map) is the largest kernel region — a contiguous mapping of all physical RAM at a fixed offset. It allows the kernel to access any physical page through simple address arithmetic.

On 4-level x86_64, the direct map spans 64 TB starting at page_offset_base (0xffff888000000000 by default). On ARM64, it starts at PAGE_OFFSET and covers the entire physical address range. The conversion macros __va(phys) and __pa(virt) perform the translation (see Section 10.3 for implementation details).

The direct map uses large pages (1 GB or 2 MB) wherever alignment permits, minimizing TLB pressure. A 256 GB server mapped with 1 GB pages needs only 256 TLB entries to cover all of RAM. This is one of the biggest advantages of 64-bit kernels — the entire physical memory is permanently mapped, eliminating the highmem complexity that plagued 32-bit Linux 2.6 kernels.

vmalloc and ioremap

The vmalloc/ioremap region provides virtually contiguous mappings backed by physically discontiguous pages. Unlike the direct map, pages in this region can come from anywhere in physical memory, making it ideal for:

  • Large kernel buffers — vmalloc() when kmalloc() can’t find contiguous physical memory.
  • Device MMIO — ioremap() maps device registers into kernel virtual space.
  • Vmap stacks — kernel thread stacks backed by vmalloc’d pages with guard pages for overflow detection.
  • BPF JIT code — JIT-compiled BPF programs are allocated in vmalloc space.

The vmalloc allocator uses an augmented red-black tree of struct vmap_area nodes to track free virtual ranges, enabling O(log n) best-fit allocation. When vmalloc() needs a new range, it finds a gap in the tree, allocates page frames from the buddy allocator, then populates page tables level by level. An address can be checked against the vmalloc region with is_vmalloc_addr() in mm/vmalloc.c.

The full vmalloc allocator internals — the vmap_area tree, lazy TLB flushing, vmap_pud_range() page-table population, and the purge/reclaim mechanism — are covered in Chapter 8: Noncontiguous Memory Area Management.

vmemmap

The vmemmap region holds the virtual mapping of the struct page array. In the SPARSEMEM_VMEMMAP model (the default on both x86_64 and ARM64), every physical page frame has a corresponding struct page descriptor, and these descriptors are laid out in a contiguous virtual array starting at VMEMMAP_START.

The conversion between page frame numbers and struct page pointers becomes trivial pointer arithmetic:

/* With SPARSEMEM_VMEMMAP: */
#define pfn_to_page(pfn)    (vmemmap + (pfn))
#define page_to_pfn(page)   ((page) - vmemmap)

The backing physical pages for vmemmap are allocated by vmemmap_populate() in mm/sparse-vmemmap.c. This function is called during memory hotplug and boot-time SPARSEMEM initialization. It uses huge pages (2 MB PMD entries) wherever possible to reduce TLB overhead — since the vmemmap is accessed extremely frequently (every pfn_to_page() and page_to_pfn() call), using 4 KB pages for it would be wasteful.

On 4-level x86_64, vmemmap occupies a 1 TB slot at 0xffffea0000000000. Since each struct page is 64 bytes and each page frame covers 4 KB, the ratio is 64 / 4096 = 1/64 — one byte of vmemmap metadata per 64 bytes of RAM. For 64 TB of direct-mapped RAM, vmemmap needs 1 TB of virtual space, which exactly matches the allocated slot.

Module Space

Loadable kernel modules are placed in a dedicated region near the kernel text. The proximity constraint is architectural: on x86_64, modules must be within 2 GB of the kernel text because the kernel is compiled with -mcmodel=kernel, which uses RIP-relative addressing with 32-bit signed displacements. If a module were placed farther away, calls and data references between the kernel and the module would overflow.

/* x86_64: arch/x86/include/asm/pgtable_64_types.h */
#define MODULES_VADDR   (__START_KERNEL_map + KERNEL_IMAGE_SIZE)
#define MODULES_END     _AC(0xffffffffff000000, UL)
#define MODULES_LEN     (MODULES_END - MODULES_VADDR)
/* ~1.5 GB between kernel image end and fixmap start */

/* ARM64: arch/arm64/include/asm/memory.h */
#define MODULES_VADDR   (_PAGE_END(VA_BITS_MIN))
#define MODULES_END     (MODULES_VADDR + SZ_2G)
/* 2 GB module window, also for RIP-relative equivalent (ADRP ±4 GB) */

On ARM64, modules have a 2 GB window starting at _PAGE_END(VA_BITS_MIN). The kernel image itself (KIMAGE_VADDR) is placed immediately after MODULES_END, which ensures modules are within the ±4 GB range of ARM64's ADRP instruction. If the relocation overflow check in arch/x86/kernel/module.c triggers, the kernel warns: "likely not compiled with -mcmodel=kernel" — a clear indication that the module was built for the wrong memory model.

CPU Entry Area (x86_64)

The CPU entry area is a per-CPU region that holds critical data structures needed during the earliest moments of kernel entry — before the full kernel page tables may be accessible. It was introduced alongside KPTI to ensure that interrupt and exception entry code has a guaranteed safe mapping even when user-mode page tables are active.

/* arch/x86/include/asm/cpu_entry_area.h */
struct cpu_entry_area {
    char gdt[PAGE_SIZE];                  /* Global Descriptor Table */
    struct entry_stack_page entry_stack_page; /* Entry stack for interrupts */
    struct tss_struct tss;                /* Task State Segment */
    struct cea_exception_stacks estacks;  /* NMI, #DF, #MC stacks (x86_64) */
    struct debug_store cpu_debug_store;
    struct debug_store_buffers cpu_debug_buffers;
};

Each CPU gets its own cpu_entry_area instance, mapped at a fixed per-CPU virtual address derived from CPU_ENTRY_AREA_PER_CPU. The mapping is installed by cea_set_pte() which marks the pages as global (present in both user and kernel page tables), ensuring they survive the KPTI page-table switch. The get_cpu_entry_area(cpu) helper returns the virtual address for a given CPU.

This region doesn't exist on ARM64 because TTBR1 (kernel tables) is always available — there's no need for a special "safe" mapping during kernel entry.

KASAN Shadow Memory

The Kernel Address Sanitizer (KASAN) instruments memory accesses to detect use-after-free, buffer overflows, and other memory bugs. It works by maintaining a shadow memory region where each byte tracks the validity of 8 bytes of real memory. The shadow address is computed by a simple formula:

shadow_addr = (addr >> KASAN_SHADOW_SCALE_SHIFT) + KASAN_SHADOW_OFFSET
/* KASAN_SHADOW_SCALE_SHIFT = 3 (each shadow byte covers 8 real bytes) */

This means the shadow region requires 1/8 of the total kernel virtual space it monitors. On 4-level x86_64, KASAN shadow occupies 16 TB at 0xffffec0000000000, which is enough to shadow the entire 128 TB kernel half. The shadow memory is populated lazily — only pages that are actually accessed get backing physical memory.

On ARM64, the shadow is defined in arch/arm64/include/asm/memory.h:

/* arch/arm64/include/asm/memory.h */
#define KASAN_SHADOW_START  (KASAN_SHADOW_END - KASAN_SHADOW_SIZE)
#define KASAN_SHADOW_END    (_PAGE_END(VA_BITS_MIN))

/* When KASAN is enabled, PAGE_END is pushed down to make room: */
#define PAGE_END            (KASAN_SHADOW_START)

KASAN is typically disabled in production kernels due to its ~2x memory and ~3x CPU overhead, but it is invaluable during development and CI testing.

The 1/8 shadow just described is generic KASAN (CONFIG_KASAN_GENERIC), but it is only one of three modes, and the choice changes the virtual-memory layout:

  • Generic (CONFIG_KASAN_GENERIC, both architectures) — a shadow region covering 1/8 of the monitored space (KASAN_SHADOW_SCALE_SHIFT = 3).
  • Software tag-based (CONFIG_KASAN_SW_TAGS, ARM64 only) — stores an 8-bit tag in the top byte of each pointer (relying on ARM64 Top Byte Ignore) and uses a smaller ~1/16 shadow (scale shift 4).
  • Hardware tag-based (CONFIG_KASAN_HW_TAGS, ARMv8.5+ with the Memory Tagging Extension) — the hardware stores tags directly (~1/32 of RAM) and there is no KASAN shadow VA region at all. Its low overhead makes it the only KASAN mode intended to be usable in production.

In other words, the dedicated shadow region in the kernel layout exists only for the generic and software-tag modes; on an MTE-capable ARM64 system running HW_TAGS, that slice of the address space is simply not reserved.

Fix-Mapped Addresses

Both architectures reserve a small, compile-time-addressed region called the fixmap. Fixmaps are compile-time constant virtual addresses that can be mapped to arbitrary physical pages at runtime. They solve the chicken-and-egg problem: early boot code needs to access specific hardware (APIC, device tree, early console) before the vmalloc allocator is available.

The generic interface

The fixmap API is in include/asm-generic/fixmap.h:

/* Virtual address of fixmap slot idx — grows downward from FIXADDR_TOP */
#define __fix_to_virt(x)    (FIXADDR_TOP - ((x) << PAGE_SHIFT))
#define __virt_to_fix(x)    ((FIXADDR_TOP - ((x)&PAGE_MASK)) >> PAGE_SHIFT)

/* Map a physical page to a fixmap slot */
#define set_fixmap(idx, phys)         __set_fixmap(idx, phys, FIXMAP_PAGE_NORMAL)
#define set_fixmap_nocache(idx, phys) __set_fixmap(idx, phys, FIXMAP_PAGE_NOCACHE)

x86_64 fixmap entries

arch/x86/include/asm/fixmap.h defines the x86 fixed-address enum. Key entries include:

  • FIX_APIC_BASE — local APIC MMIO mapping
  • FIX_IO_APIC_BASE_0 — I/O APIC
  • FIX_EARLYCON_MEM_BASE — early serial console
  • FIX_DBGP_BASE — USB debug port
  • FIX_BTMAP_BEGIN…FIX_BTMAP_END — early ioremap temporary slots

The x86_64 FIXADDR_TOP is constrained by the legacy vsyscall page — a historical ABI requirement that forces it to a specific address. This is an example of legacy constraints leaking into an otherwise internal kernel layout.

ARM64 fixmap entries

arch/arm64/include/asm/fixmap.h provides the ARM64 enum:

  • FIX_FDT — Flattened Device Tree blob (may span multiple pages)
  • FIX_EARLYCON_MEM_BASE — early serial console
  • FIX_TEXT_POKE0 — runtime code patching
  • FIX_ENTRY_TRAMP_TEXT1…FIX_ENTRY_TRAMP_TEXT4 — KPTI entry trampolines (when CONFIG_UNMAP_KERNEL_AT_EL0 is active)
  • FIX_PTE, FIX_PMD, FIX_PUD, FIX_P4D, FIX_PGD — temporary page-table manipulation slots for early boot

The page-table fixmap slots (FIX_PTE through FIX_PGD) are particularly interesting: ARM64 uses them during early boot to temporarily map page-table pages that it's constructing, before the full memory allocator is ready. This is necessary because the kernel can't access arbitrary physical pages through the direct map until the direct map itself is built.

Why fixmap still matters

The fixmap is a reminder that not all mappings are discovered by generic allocators at runtime. Some addresses must be known before the general virtual-memory machinery is fully operational. The fixmap gives the kernel a small island of deterministic virtual addresses that can bootstrap the rest of the system. Even on modern kernels with abundant virtual address space, the fixmap remains essential for early boot, APIC access, and KPTI trampolines.

Kernel Page Table Isolation (KPTI)

Kernel Page Table Isolation is the clearest example of how microarchitectural attacks changed mainstream memory management design. Before Meltdown (disclosed January 2018), x86 kernels were comfortable leaving all kernel mappings present in the active user-mode page tables, trusting hardware privilege checks to block access. After Meltdown demonstrated that speculative execution could bypass those checks, Linux responded by splitting the user and kernel views into separate page tables, exposing only a tiny kernel stub to user mode.

This section covers:

  1. The Meltdown problem — why shared kernel mappings became unsafe.
  2. The dual page-table architecture — how the shadow PGD is allocated and structured.
  3. CR3 switching and PCID — the entry/exit assembly that switches between user and kernel tables.
  4. Cloning the minimal shared state — what kernel code is visible from user mode.
  5. Initialization and policy — how Linux decides whether to enable KPTI.
  6. ARM64: CONFIG_UNMAP_KERNEL_AT_EL0 — the ARM64 equivalent using trampoline tables.
  7. Performance cost and PCID mitigation — how Linux minimizes the TLB overhead.

The Problem: Meltdown

Meltdown (CVE-2017-5754) exploited out-of-order execution on Intel CPUs to transiently read kernel-mapped data from user mode, then recover the result through cache-timing side channels. The ordinary page-permission check was still enforced architecturally (the instruction ultimately faulted), but it happened after the speculative data access had already left observable microarchitectural side effects (cache line loads).

The key lesson for memory management design was severe: it is no longer safe for user mode to share active page tables that contain kernel mappings, even if those mappings are marked supervisor-only. The hardware permission bits were necessary but not sufficient — an attacker could speculatively read past them.

This was primarily an x86 problem. ARM64 systems were largely unaffected because:

  • The architecture already splits user and kernel translations between TTBR0 and TTBR1 — the kernel can simply invalidate TTBR0 or point it to an empty table on kernel entry.
  • Most ARM64 implementations do not speculatively bypass privilege checks in the same way affected Intel CPUs did.

However, some ARM64 cores (notably Cortex-A75) were found vulnerable to similar attacks, leading ARM64 Linux to implement CONFIG_UNMAP_KERNEL_AT_EL0 as a defense (Section 13.6).

The Dual Page-Table Architecture

KPTI's core idea is simple: maintain two sets of top-level page tables for each process — one for kernel mode and one for user mode. The user-mode table contains only the minimal kernel mappings needed to enter the kernel (the entry trampoline); the full kernel mapping is only present in the kernel-mode table.

The 8 KB paired PGD allocation

When KPTI is enabled, pgd_alloc() allocates an order-1 (8 KB) page instead of the usual 4 KB page. The two halves form the kernel and user PGDs:

/* arch/x86/include/asm/pgalloc.h */
static inline int pgd_allocation_order(void)
{
    if (boot_cpu_has(X86_FEATURE_PTI))
        return 1;  /* order-1 = 8 KB = two 4 KB pages */
    return 0;      /* order-0 = 4 KB = single PGD */
}

The kernel PGD occupies the first 4 KB, and the user (shadow) PGD occupies the second 4 KB. A simple bit flip on the pointer switches between them:

/* arch/x86/include/asm/pgtable.h */
#define PTI_PGTABLE_SWITCH_BIT  PAGE_SHIFT  /* bit 12 */

static inline pgd_t *kernel_to_user_pgdp(pgd_t *pgdp)
{
    return ptr_set_bit(pgdp, PTI_PGTABLE_SWITCH_BIT);
}
static inline pgd_t *user_to_kernel_pgdp(pgd_t *pgdp)
{
    return ptr_clear_bit(pgdp, PTI_PGTABLE_SWITCH_BIT);
}

Because the 8 KB allocation is naturally aligned, toggling bit 12 of the physical address in CR3 flips between the kernel and user PGD without any table lookup — this is extremely fast and is the foundation of the entry/exit CR3 switching.

What goes into each PGD

Table 2-33. Contents of kernel vs user PGD under KPTI
PGD halfContainsLoaded when
Kernel PGD (offset +0) Full kernel mappings: direct map, vmalloc, vmemmap, kernel text, modules, fixmap. User mappings with NX enforced. Kernel mode (after syscall/interrupt entry)
User PGD (offset +4 KB) Full user mappings. Minimal kernel: CPU entry area, entry trampoline text, vsyscall, espfix stubs only. User mode (before syscall/interrupt entry)

When creating a new process, pgd_ctor() clones the kernel upper-half PGD entries from swapper_pg_dir into both the kernel and user halves:

/* arch/x86/mm/pgtable.c */
clone_pgd_range(pgd + KERNEL_PGD_BOUNDARY,
                swapper_pg_dir + KERNEL_PGD_BOUNDARY,
                KERNEL_PGD_PTRS);

Additionally, the kernel PGD marks user-space entries with NX (No Execute) — if the kernel were ever to accidentally execute user code with the kernel CR3 loaded, the NX bit would cause an immediate fault rather than allowing exploitation (a defense-in-depth measure).

CR3 Switching and PCID

On every syscall, interrupt, and exception, the CPU must switch from the user PGD to the kernel PGD (and back on return). This is implemented in assembly macros in arch/x86/entry/calling.h:

Entry: switching to kernel CR3

/* arch/x86/entry/calling.h — simplified SWITCH_TO_KERNEL_CR3 */
.macro SWITCH_TO_KERNEL_CR3 scratch_reg:req
    mov     %cr3, \scratch_reg
    /* Clear the user PCID bit and the PTI PGD-switch bit */
    andq    $(~(X86_CR3_PTI_PCID_USER_BIT | PTI_USER_PGTABLE_MASK)), \scratch_reg
    /* Set CR3_NOFLUSH to avoid TLB flush (PCID preserves entries) */
    orq     $CR3_NOFLUSH, \scratch_reg
    mov     \scratch_reg, %cr3
.endm

The key operations:

  1. Read current CR3 (which contains the user PGD physical address + user PCID).
  2. Clear bit 12 (PTI_USER_PGTABLE_MASK) — flips from user PGD to kernel PGD.
  3. Clear bit 11 (X86_CR3_PTI_PCID_USER_BIT) — switches from user PCID to kernel PCID.
  4. Set bit 63 (CR3_NOFLUSH) — tells the CPU not to flush TLB entries for the new PCID.
  5. Write the new CR3 — now running with kernel page tables.

Exit: switching to user CR3

/* arch/x86/entry/calling.h — simplified SWITCH_TO_USER_CR3 */
.macro SWITCH_TO_USER_CR3 scratch_reg:req scratch_reg2:req
    mov     %cr3, \scratch_reg
    /* Set the user PCID bit and PGD-switch bit */
    orq     $(PTI_USER_PGTABLE_MASK | PTI_USER_PCID_MASK), \scratch_reg
    /* Check if we need to flush the user PCID entries */
    bt      $X86_CR3_PTI_PCID_USER_BIT, \
            THIS_CPU_user_pcid_flush_mask
    jnc     .Lnoflush_\@
    /* Flush needed: don't set CR3_NOFLUSH */
    btr     $X86_CR3_PTI_PCID_USER_BIT, \
            THIS_CPU_user_pcid_flush_mask
    jmp     .Ldone_\@
.Lnoflush_\@:
    orq     $CR3_NOFLUSH, \scratch_reg
.Ldone_\@:
    mov     \scratch_reg, %cr3
.endm

The user-return path checks the per-CPU user_pcid_flush_mask to decide whether the user's TLB entries need invalidation. If another CPU has performed a TLB shootdown affecting this process, the mask bit is set, and the return path omits CR3_NOFLUSH — causing a full TLB flush for that PCID. Otherwise, CR3_NOFLUSH preserves existing user-PCID TLB entries.

Cloning the Minimal Shared Kernel State

The user PGD must contain just enough kernel mappings to handle the initial entry into the kernel — the first few instructions that execute before SWITCH_TO_KERNEL_CR3 runs. This is implemented by several cloning functions in arch/x86/mm/pti.c:

pti_clone_user_shared()

Clones the CPU entry area into the user PGD. This region contains the GDT, TSS, entry stack, and exception stacks that the CPU needs to access immediately on interrupt/syscall entry — before any software CR3 switch can occur:

/* arch/x86/mm/pti.c — simplified */
static void __init pti_clone_user_shared(void)
{
    /* Clone the entire CPU entry area base */
    pti_clone_pgtable((unsigned long)CPU_ENTRY_AREA_BASE,
                      (unsigned long)CPU_ENTRY_AREA_BASE + CPU_ENTRY_AREA_TOTAL_SIZE,
                      PTI_CLONE_PMD);
    /* Clone per-CPU TSS scratch pages */
    ...
}

pti_clone_entry_text()

Clones the kernel entry/exit trampoline text (the code between __entry_text_start and __entry_text_end) into the user PGD. This is the assembly code that runs with the user CR3 still loaded — it must be mapped in the user tables to avoid an immediate page fault on interrupt entry.

pti_clone_pgtable()

The general cloning helper that walks the kernel page tables and copies PMD or PTE entries into the user shadow. It explicitly avoids large-page mappings in the user shadow (using PTE-level entries instead) to simplify security auditing and prevent accidentally exposing more kernel data than intended.

The result: from user mode, the only kernel addresses that are mapped are:

  • The entry/exit trampoline code (~a few pages)
  • The CPU entry area (GDT, TSS, entry stacks) — read-only to user, no sensitive data
  • Fixmap stubs (vsyscall page, espfix)

The entire direct map, vmalloc, vmemmap, and kernel text are invisible from user mode — completely eliminating the Meltdown attack surface.

Initialization and Policy

KPTI is not blindly enabled on every x86 system. Linux uses boot-time policy detection to decide whether the mitigation is needed:

/* arch/x86/mm/pti.c — simplified */
void __init pti_check_boottime_disable(void)
{
    /* Xen PV guests cannot use PTI (paravirt CR3 handling) */
    if (hypervisor_is_type(X86_HYPER_XEN_PV)) {
        pti_mode = PTI_FORCE_OFF;
        return;
    }
    /* Command-line overrides: pti=off, pti=on, nopti */
    if (cmdline_find_option_bool("nopti") || pti_mode == PTI_FORCE_OFF)
        return;

    /* Auto mode: enable only if the CPU has the Meltdown bug */
    if (pti_mode == PTI_AUTO && !boot_cpu_has_bug(X86_BUG_CPU_MELTDOWN))
        return;

    setup_force_cpu_cap(X86_FEATURE_PTI);
}

The initialization sequence:

  1. pti_check_boottime_disable() — determines whether to enable KPTI based on CPU vulnerability, hypervisor type, and command-line options.
  2. pti_init() — clones user-shared mappings, entry text, espfix, and vsyscall into the user PGD. Clears global bits from init page tables.
  3. pti_finalize() — called after free_initmem() and mark_rodata_ro(), re-clones entry text with final permissions.

On CPUs that are not vulnerable to Meltdown (most AMD processors, Intel CPUs with hardware fixes), KPTI remains disabled in auto mode — avoiding the performance overhead entirely. This is a recurring pattern in modern kernel mitigations: make the defense available, then use boot-time CPU feature detection to select it only where needed.

ARM64: CONFIG_UNMAP_KERNEL_AT_EL0

ARM64's TTBR0/TTBR1 split means user mode never sees kernel mappings in TTBR1 — the hardware routes kernel addresses (top bit set) to TTBR1 automatically. However, some ARM64 cores are vulnerable to speculative attacks that can bypass this separation. Linux defends against these with CONFIG_UNMAP_KERNEL_AT_EL0, which takes the additional step of pointing TTBR1 to a minimal trampoline table while running at EL0 (user mode).

The trampoline mechanism

ARM64 uses a pair of assembly macros in arch/arm64/kernel/entry.S to switch TTBR1 between the real kernel tables and a trampoline table on exception entry/exit:

/* arch/arm64/kernel/entry.S */
.macro tramp_map_kernel, tmp
    mrs     \tmp, ttbr1_el1
    add     \tmp, \tmp, #TRAMP_SWAPPER_OFFSET   /* point to real swapper */
    bic     \tmp, \tmp, #USER_ASID_FLAG         /* clear user ASID bit */
    msr     ttbr1_el1, \tmp                     /* load full kernel tables */
.endm

.macro tramp_unmap_kernel, tmp
    mrs     \tmp, ttbr1_el1
    sub     \tmp, \tmp, #TRAMP_SWAPPER_OFFSET   /* point to trampoline */
    orr     \tmp, \tmp, #USER_ASID_FLAG         /* set user ASID bit */
    msr     ttbr1_el1, \tmp                     /* unmap kernel */
.endm

The trampoline vectors (.entry.tramp.text) are mapped into a fixmap slot (FIX_ENTRY_TRAMP_TEXT) — this small piece of code is visible even when TTBR1 points to the trampoline table. On exception entry, the trampoline code immediately calls tramp_map_kernel to restore the real kernel tables before proceeding with the handler. On return to user, tramp_unmap_kernel hides the kernel again.

Comparison with x86_64 KPTI

Table 2-34. KPTI: x86_64 vs ARM64
Aspectx86_64ARM64
Baseline isolationNone (shared PGD) Hardware TTBR0/TTBR1 split
KPTI mechanismShadow user PGD at +4 KB, CR3 switch on entry/exit TTBR1 pointed to trampoline table at EL0
Switch triggerEvery syscall, interrupt, exception Every exception to/from EL0
Config optionCONFIG_MITIGATION_PAGE_TABLE_ISOLATION CONFIG_UNMAP_KERNEL_AT_EL0
Minimal visible kernelCPU entry area + entry text (~few pages) Trampoline vectors in fixmap (~1-4 pages)
TLB optimizationPCID: separate user/kernel PCID, CR3_NOFLUSH ASID: USER_ASID_FLAG differentiates user/kernel TLB entries
Vulnerable CPUsMost Intel before Ice Lake Cortex-A75, some Neoverse cores
Default behaviorAuto: enabled only if X86_BUG_CPU_MELTDOWN set Enabled per-CPU based on ARM64_UNMAP_KERNEL_AT_EL0 capability

Performance Cost and PCID Mitigation

KPTI doubles the number of effective address-space switches: every syscall, interrupt, and exception now requires two CR3 writes (entry and exit). Without PCID, each CR3 write would flush all TLB entries, causing catastrophic performance regression (measured at 5–30% overhead for syscall-heavy workloads at initial deployment).

PCID optimization

Process Context IDentifiers (PCIDs) let the CPU maintain TLB entries from multiple address spaces simultaneously. Linux assigns separate PCIDs for the user and kernel views:

Table 2-35. PCID usage under KPTI
PCID typeIdentifiesTLB entries contain
Kernel PCID (kPCID)Kernel page tables for process N Kernel mappings + user mappings (with NX)
User PCID (uPCID)User page tables for process N User mappings + minimal kernel stubs

The CR3_NOFLUSH bit (bit 63) tells the CPU: "I'm switching CR3 but don't flush TLB entries for the target PCID." This means switching between user and kernel tables is essentially free from a TLB perspective — existing entries for both PCIDs remain valid.

Linux manages PCIDs from a per-CPU pool of TLB_NR_DYN_ASIDS = 6 entries (see Section 9.4). Each dynamic ASID becomes a pair of PCIDs (kernel + user variant with bit 11 flipped). When a process's PCID is evicted from the pool, the user_pcid_flush_mask is set so the next return-to-user will flush stale entries.

Net performance impact

With PCID active (which it is on all CPUs affected by Meltdown), the overhead of KPTI is typically 1–5% for most workloads — far less than the 30% worst-case without PCID. The remaining cost comes from:

  • Two MOV-to-CR3 instructions per kernel entry/exit (even with NOFLUSH, the write has latency).
  • Reduced effective TLB capacity (entries are split between kernel and user PCIDs).
  • Inability to use global mappings for kernel text (global entries would leak into the user view, defeating KPTI).

The code in arch/x86/mm/pti.c explicitly discusses this trade-off: pti_kernel_image_global_ok() disables global kernel text mappings on PCID systems because the security downside outweighs the small TLB benefit.

Advanced Address-Space Hardening (x86_64)

Linux 7.0.12 supports several x86 hardware features that extend the address-space security model beyond what page-table permissions alone provide. None existed in Linux 2.6; all were introduced after version 5.x to counter increasingly sophisticated attacks. These features operate at different layers — some modify address translation before the page-table walk even begins, others add new PTE encodings, and one introduces an entirely new hardware stack that the kernel must integrate with the existing memory management subsystem.

This section covers:

  1. Linear Address Masking (LAM) — embedding metadata in pointer bits without faulting.
  2. LASS (Linear Address Space Separation) — hardware enforcement of the user/kernel address boundary.
  3. CET Shadow Stacks — hardware-protected return-address stacks and their page-table encoding.
  4. Indirect Branch Tracking (IBT) — forward-edge control-flow integrity.
  5. Feature interactions and the defense-in-depth model — how these features layer with SMAP, SMEP, and KPTI.

Linear Address Masking (LAM)

Linear Address Masking allows user-space programs to embed metadata — pointer tags, memory-safety coloring, type identifiers — in the upper bits of virtual addresses without causing a canonical-address fault. The CPU ignores ("masks out") the tagged bits before the page-table walk, so the hardware translation sees a clean canonical address while the software retains its metadata in the pointer itself.

LAM modes and tag widths

x86_64 defines two LAM modes, controlled by bits in CR3:

Table 2-36. LAM modes
ModeCR3 bitMasked bitsTag widthNotes
LAM_U57Bit 6162:576 bits Compatible with 5-level paging (LA57). Default Linux mode.
LAM_U48Bit 6262:4815 bits More tag bits, but requires 4-level paging only. Ignored if LAM_U57 is also set.

The CR3 bit definitions come directly from the processor flags header:

/* arch/x86/include/uapi/asm/processor-flags.h */
#define X86_CR3_LAM_U57_BIT  61  /* bits 62:57 masked */
#define X86_CR3_LAM_U48_BIT  62  /* bits 62:48 masked */

Linux currently implements only LAM_U57, which provides 6 tag bits. The LAM_U57_BITS constant defines this limit:

/* arch/x86/kernel/process_64.c */
#define LAM_U57_BITS  6

Enabling LAM

A process enables LAM via arch_prctl(ARCH_ENABLE_TAGGED_ADDR, nr_bits). The kernel validates the request and then configures the per-mm LAM state:

/* arch/x86/kernel/process_64.c — simplified prctl_enable_tagged_addr() */
long prctl_enable_tagged_addr(struct mm_struct *mm, unsigned long nr_bits)
{
    if (!cpu_feature_enabled(X86_FEATURE_LAM))
        return -ENODEV;
    if (!nr_bits || nr_bits > LAM_U57_BITS)
        return -EINVAL;
    /* LAM can only be enabled while single-threaded */
    if (test_bit(MM_CONTEXT_LOCK_LAM, &mm->context.flags))
        return -EBUSY;
    ...
    mm_enable_lam(mm);
    return 0;
}

The actual enablement function sets up the CR3 mask and the untag mask that will be applied to every user-space pointer before the kernel touches it:

/* arch/x86/kernel/process_64.c */
static void mm_enable_lam(struct mm_struct *mm)
{
    mm->context.lam_cr3_mask = X86_CR3_LAM_U57;
    mm->context.untag_mask  = ~GENMASK(62, 57);

    /* Propagate to all CPUs running this mm */
    on_each_cpu_mask(mm_cpumask(mm), enable_lam_func, mm, true);
    /* Lock: cannot be disabled or changed once enabled */
    set_bit(MM_CONTEXT_LOCK_LAM, &mm->context.flags);
}

How LAM interacts with paging

LAM does not modify page-table entries. Instead, it operates entirely through CR3 bits and software pointer untagging:

  • CR3 integration: The build_cr3() function in arch/x86/mm/tlb.c ORs the LAM mask into CR3 alongside the PGD physical address, PCID, and KPTI bits. On every context switch, switch_mm_irqs_off() reads mm_lam_cr3_mask() and passes it to build_cr3():
/* arch/x86/mm/tlb.c — during switch_mm_irqs_off() */
unsigned long new_lam = mm_lam_cr3_mask(next);
...
build_cr3(next->pgd, new_asid, new_lam);
  • User-access untagging: Before any copy_from_user(), get_user(), or similar access, the kernel must strip tag bits so the page-table walker sees a canonical address. This is done via the __untagged_addr() macro in arch/x86/include/asm/uaccess_64.h:
/* arch/x86/include/asm/uaccess_64.h */
#define __untagged_addr(addr)  ({                             \
    unsigned long __addr = (__force unsigned long)(addr);      \
    /* When LAM is active, AND with per-cpu untag mask */      \
    ALTERNATIVE("", "and " __percpu_arg([mask]) ", %[addr]",  \
                X86_FEATURE_LAM);                             \
    __addr;                                                   \
})

The ALTERNATIVE macro patches in the AND instruction only on LAM-capable CPUs — on non-LAM systems, the untagging is a zero-cost no-op. For remote mm access (e.g., ptrace), __untagged_addr_remote() uses mm->context.untag_mask instead of the per-CPU value.

  • Single-threaded lock: LAM can only be enabled while the process is single-threaded, enforced by MM_CONTEXT_LOCK_LAM. Once set, it cannot be changed — this avoids race conditions where one thread enables LAM while another is mid-syscall with untagged pointer assumptions.
  • Fork inheritance: dup_lam() in arch/x86/include/asm/mmu_context.h copies both lam_cr3_mask and untag_mask to the child mm during fork().

ARM64 equivalent: Top Byte Ignore (TBI)

ARM64 has had Top Byte Ignore since ARMv8.0, which unconditionally ignores bits 63:56 of user addresses — giving 8 tag bits without any kernel opt-in. Linux exposes TBI via prctl(PR_SET_TAGGED_ADDR_CTRL). LAM is x86's belated answer to TBI, but with a configurable mask width and an explicit CR3-based enable mechanism. The Memory Tagging Extension (MTE) on ARMv8.5+ goes further, adding hardware-checked 4-bit tags in bits 59:56 with per-granule tag storage in physical memory.

LASS (Linear Address Space Separation)

Linear Address Space Separation adds a hardware enforcement layer for the boundary between user and kernel address spaces, based purely on bit 63 of the linear address. This check happens before the page-table walk — it is a pre-translation gate that complements the post-translation checks of SMAP/SMEP and the table-level isolation of KPTI.

The enforcement rule

LASS divides the 64-bit virtual address space into two halves based on the sign bit:

  • Kernel mode accessing lower half (bit 63 = 0): triggers a #GP fault, unless the AC (Alignment Check) flag is set via lass_stac(). This prevents kernel bugs from accidentally dereferencing user pointers.
  • User mode accessing upper half (bit 63 = 1): triggers a #GP fault unconditionally — even instruction fetches are blocked. There is no bypass mechanism for user mode.

The critical distinction from SMAP: LASS cannot be bypassed for instruction fetches, even with AC set. The kernel source documents this explicitly:

/* arch/x86/include/asm/smap.h */
/*
 * LASS enforcement is based on bit 63 of the virtual address. The
 * kernel is not allowed to touch memory in the lower half of the
 * virtual address space.
 *
 * Use lass_stac()/lass_clac() to toggle the AC bit for kernel data
 * accesses (!_PAGE_USER) that are blocked by LASS, but not by SMAP.
 *
 * Even with the AC bit set, LASS will continue to block instruction
 * fetches from the user half of the address space.
 */

Enabling LASS

LASS is enabled by setting bit 27 of CR4 (X86_CR4_LASS). The kernel performs this during CPU initialization:

/* arch/x86/kernel/cpu/common.c — simplified setup_lass() */
static __always_inline void setup_lass(void)
{
    if (!cpu_feature_enabled(X86_FEATURE_LASS))
        return;

    /* LASS is incompatible with legacy vsyscall emulation */
    if (IS_ENABLED(CONFIG_X86_VSYSCALL_EMULATION)) {
        clear_cpu_cap(&boot_cpu_data, X86_FEATURE_LASS);
        return;
    }
    /* LASS is incompatible with EFI runtime services (lower-half mappings) */
    if (IS_ENABLED(CONFIG_EFI)) {
        clear_cpu_cap(&boot_cpu_data, X86_FEATURE_LASS);
        return;
    }

    cr4_set_bits(X86_CR4_LASS);
}

The two exclusions reveal important design constraints:

  • Vsyscall emulation: The legacy vsyscall page lives at a fixed address in the lower half (0xFFFFFFFFFF600000 is actually in the "negative" range, but the vsyscall emulation mechanism maps pages that LASS would fault on). Systems using vsyscall emulation cannot enable LASS.
  • EFI runtime services: EFI may require 1:1 identity mappings in the lower half of the address space. LASS would block kernel access to these regions.

LASS fault handling

When LASS blocks an access, it generates a #GP (General Protection) fault, not a page fault. The kernel's GP handler in arch/x86/kernel/traps.c classifies LASS violations as a specific fault type:

/* arch/x86/kernel/traps.c — GP fault classification */
GP_LASS_VIOLATION  /* probably LASS violation for address */

This classification allows the kernel to distinguish LASS faults from other GP causes (segment violations, privilege errors) and produce meaningful diagnostic messages.

LASS/SMAP toggle helpers

The lass_stac() and lass_clac() macros mirror the existing stac()/clac() pair for SMAP, but they are patched in using ALTERNATIVE based on X86_FEATURE_LASS:

/* arch/x86/include/asm/smap.h */
static __always_inline void lass_stac(void)
{
    alternative("", __stringify(stac), X86_FEATURE_LASS);
}
static __always_inline void lass_clac(void)
{
    alternative("", __stringify(clac), X86_FEATURE_LASS);
}

These are used in kernel paths that legitimately need to access user-space data (e.g., copy_from_user()) — the kernel sets AC before the access and clears it afterward, just as with SMAP. The difference is that LASS uses the same AC flag but checks the address range rather than the PTE's _PAGE_USER bit.

The defense-in-depth stack

LASS does not replace any existing protection — it adds another layer:

Table 2-37. Layered address-space protections on x86_64
FeatureCheck pointWhat it checksWhat it prevents Kernel bypass
SMEPPage-table walk_PAGE_USER + CPL Kernel executing user pagesNone
SMAPPage-table walk_PAGE_USER + CPL + AC Kernel reading/writing user pagesstac() sets AC
KPTICR3 / page tablesSeparate PGD per mode User seeing kernel mappingsN/A (architectural)
LASSPre-walk (address)Bit 63 of linear address Cross-half access lass_stac() for data; fetch always blocked
CET Shadow StackPTE encodingW=0, D=1 ROP attacks via return-address tamperingN/A (hardware stack)

An attacker would need to defeat all of these layers simultaneously to exploit a kernel-to-user or user-to-kernel access vulnerability — each layer catches a different class of attack and operates at a different point in the address translation pipeline.

CET Shadow Stacks and Page-Table Encoding

Intel Control-flow Enforcement Technology (CET) includes two components: shadow stacks (backward-edge protection) and Indirect Branch Tracking (forward-edge protection, covered in Section 14.4). Shadow stacks are the component most deeply connected to the paging subsystem — they require a new PTE encoding that the kernel must carefully manage.

How shadow stacks work

A shadow stack is a second, hardware-protected stack that runs in parallel with the normal stack:

  • On CALL: the CPU pushes the return address onto both the normal stack and the shadow stack.
  • On RET: the CPU pops from both stacks and compares the return addresses. If they differ, the CPU raises a #CP (Control Protection) exception.

This detects ROP (Return-Oriented Programming) attacks, where an attacker overwrites return addresses on the normal stack to chain together gadgets. The shadow stack is mapped with special permissions that prevent normal stores from modifying it — only CALL/RET and the privileged WRSS instruction can write to it.

The page-table encoding: W=0, D=1

Shadow-stack memory uses a PTE encoding that would be self-contradictory under normal circumstances:

Shadow stack PTE:  Write = 0,  Dirty = 1   /* hardware requirement */
Normal dirty RO:   Write = 0,  SavedDirty = 1,  Dirty = 0

This combination (_PAGE_RW = 0, _PAGE_DIRTY = 1) is normally impossible because the CPU only sets the Dirty bit when it writes to a page, and it can only write to pages with _PAGE_RW = 1. Shadow stacks repurpose this "impossible" combination to mean: "this page is writable only by CALL/RET/WRSS instructions, not by normal MOV stores."

The _PAGE_SAVED_DIRTY bit

The introduction of shadow stacks created a problem: the kernel has legitimate reasons to create read-only PTEs with the Dirty bit set. For example, during fork(), both the parent and child get read-only copies of previously writable (dirty) pages for COW. Similarly, mprotect() can make a dirty page read-only, and soft-dirty tracking sets the dirty bit for bookkeeping.

Without shadow stacks, these W=0/D=1 entries were harmless — the hardware never checked for that combination. With shadow stacks, they would be misinterpreted as shadow-stack pages, causing incorrect behavior. Linux solves this by introducing _PAGE_SAVED_DIRTY:

/* arch/x86/include/asm/pgtable_types.h */
/*
 * The hardware requires shadow stack to be Write=0, Dirty=1. However,
 * there are valid cases where the kernel might create read-only PTEs
 * that are dirty (e.g., fork(), mprotect(), uffd-wp(), soft-dirty
 * tracking). In this case, the _PAGE_SAVED_DIRTY bit is used instead
 * of the HW-dirty bit, to avoid creating wrong "shadow stack" PTEs.
 */
#define _PAGE_BIT_SAVED_DIRTY  57  /* _PAGE_BIT_SOFTW5 */
#define _PAGE_SAVED_DIRTY      (_AT(pteval_t, 1) << _PAGE_BIT_SAVED_DIRTY)
#define _PAGE_DIRTY_BITS       (_PAGE_DIRTY | _PAGE_SAVED_DIRTY)

The rule is:

  • When creating a normal read-only dirty PTE (COW, mprotect, etc.): clear _PAGE_DIRTY, set _PAGE_SAVED_DIRTY instead. The hardware dirty bit stays clear, so the CPU does not see a shadow-stack encoding.
  • When creating a shadow-stack PTE: set _PAGE_DIRTY, clear _PAGE_RW. The saved-dirty bit is not used.

PTE helpers for shadow stacks

Several helpers in arch/x86/include/asm/pgtable.h implement the shadow-stack PTE logic:

/* arch/x86/include/asm/pgtable.h */

/* Detect shadow-stack PTE: RW=0 and HW Dirty=1 */
static inline bool pte_shstk(pte_t pte)
{
    return (pte_flags(pte) & (_PAGE_RW | _PAGE_DIRTY)) == _PAGE_DIRTY;
}

/* Create a shadow-stack PTE: clear RW, set Dirty */
static inline pte_t pte_mkwrite_shstk(pte_t pte)
{
    pte = pte_clear_flags(pte, _PAGE_RW);
    pte = pte_set_flags(pte, _PAGE_DIRTY);
    return pte;
}

/* Logical write check: shadow-stack pages are "writable" by CALL/RET */
static inline int pte_write(pte_t pte)
{
    return (pte_flags(pte) & _PAGE_RW) || pte_shstk(pte);
}

/* Write-protect: move dirty → saved-dirty to avoid shadow-stack confusion */
static inline pte_t pte_wrprotect(pte_t pte)
{
    pte = pte_clear_flags(pte, _PAGE_RW);
    pte = pte_mksaveddirty(pte);  /* D→SavedDirty */
    return pte;
}

The pte_wrprotect() function is particularly important: whenever the kernel write-protects a page (COW, mprotect), it must move the dirty state from the hardware bit to the saved-dirty bit. Without this, a write-protected dirty page would look like a shadow-stack page to the CPU.

Shadow stack lifecycle

The kernel manages shadow stacks through their entire lifecycle in arch/x86/kernel/shstk.c:

Allocation (alloc_shstk()): Creates a shadow-stack VMA using do_mmap() with special flags:

/* arch/x86/kernel/shstk.c — simplified alloc_shstk() */
addr = do_mmap(NULL, addr, size, PROT_READ,
               MAP_ANONYMOUS | MAP_PRIVATE, VM_SHADOW_STACK | VM_WRITE,
               ...);

Setup (shstk_setup()): Called when a process enables its shadow stack. Allocates the stack, sets the Shadow Stack Pointer (SSP) MSR, and enables CET in the user CET MSR:

/* arch/x86/kernel/shstk.c — simplified shstk_setup() */
int shstk_setup(void)
{
    if (!cpu_feature_enabled(X86_FEATURE_USER_SHSTK))
        return -EOPNOTSUPP;

    addr = alloc_shstk(0, ss_size, 0, false);
    ...
    /* SSP points to the top of the shadow stack */
    fpregs_lock_and_load();
    wrmsrl(MSR_IA32_PL3_SSP, addr + ss_size);
    wrmsrl(MSR_IA32_U_CET, CET_SHSTK_EN);
    fpregs_unlock();
    return 0;
}

Thread creation (shstk_alloc_thread_stack()): On clone()/fork(), the kernel allocates a new shadow stack for the child. The function handles special cases like CLONE_VFORK (shares the parent's shadow stack temporarily) and !CLONE_VM (full fork — COW semantics apply to shadow-stack pages).

Signal handling: setup_signal_shadow_stack() pushes a restore token onto the shadow stack before switching to the signal handler, and restore_signal_shadow_stack() validates and pops the token on signal return. This prevents attackers from using signal frames to pivot the shadow stack.

The map_shadow_stack syscall: Linux provides a dedicated system call for explicit shadow-stack allocation:

/* arch/x86/kernel/shstk.c */
SYSCALL_DEFINE3(map_shadow_stack, unsigned long, addr,
                unsigned long, size, unsigned int, flags)
{
    /* Validate flags, size, address alignment */
    ...
    return alloc_shstk(addr, size, flags, true);
}

CET MSR definitions

Table 2-38. CET-related MSRs
MSRAddressPurposeKey bits
MSR_IA32_U_CET0x6A0User-mode CET control Bit 0: CET_SHSTK_EN (enable shadow stack)
Bit 1: CET_WRSS_EN (enable WRSS instruction)
MSR_IA32_PL3_SSP0x6A7Ring 3 Shadow Stack Pointer Points to current shadow stack top

Control Protection (#CP) faults

When a shadow-stack mismatch is detected (CALL return address ≠ shadow stack return address), the CPU raises a #CP exception. The kernel handles this in arch/x86/kernel/cet.c:

/* arch/x86/kernel/cet.c — simplified */
/* User-mode #CP: shadow stack violation */
if (user_mode(regs) && cpu_feature_enabled(X86_FEATURE_USER_SHSTK))
    do_user_cp_fault(regs, error_code);  /* sends SIGSEGV */

/* Kernel-mode #CP: IBT violation (forward-edge) */
if (!user_mode(regs) && cpu_feature_enabled(X86_FEATURE_IBT))
    do_kernel_cp_fault(regs, error_code);

For user-mode violations, the kernel delivers SIGSEGV with SEGV_CPERR — the application can catch this for debugging but cannot suppress the protection.

Indirect Branch Tracking (IBT)

While shadow stacks protect the backward edge (return addresses), Indirect Branch Tracking (IBT) protects the forward edge — indirect calls and jumps. IBT requires that every valid indirect branch target begins with an ENDBR64 instruction. If an indirect JMP or CALL lands on anything else, the CPU raises #CP.

Kernel IBT

Linux supports kernel-mode IBT via CONFIG_X86_KERNEL_IBT. When enabled, the compiler inserts ENDBR64 at the start of every function that could be an indirect call target. The kernel's #CP handler (do_kernel_cp_fault()) catches forward-edge violations in kernel code — these typically indicate a corrupted function pointer.

Relationship to paging

IBT does not directly use page-table bits. However, it interacts with the execute-permission model: a page must be both executable (_PAGE_NX clear) and contain valid ENDBR64 landing pads for indirect branches to succeed. This creates a two-layer defense:

  1. W^X prevents an attacker from writing new code into executable pages.
  2. IBT prevents an attacker from jumping into the middle of existing code (gadgets).

Kconfig and hardening config

Both shadow stacks and IBT are listed in the kernel's recommended hardening configuration:

/* arch/x86/configs/hardening.config */
CONFIG_X86_KERNEL_IBT=y
CONFIG_X86_USER_SHADOW_STACK=y
