Worked Examples and Reference Tables

The previous sections explained the concepts. This reference section revisits them in a denser, more mechanical form. It is meant as a place for working kernel programmers to pause, line up the bit fields, and verify what the hardware and the source tree are really doing.

Worked Example 1: x86_64 4-level address walk

Consider a canonical low-half user virtual address on a four-level system. Suppose, for the sake of an example, that the address has already passed the canonicality check. The processor then interprets the bits as follows:

[47:39] [38:30] [29:21] [20:12] [11:0]
 PGD      PUD      PMD      PTE     offset

The walk proceeds step by step.

  1. CR3 supplies the physical base of the top-level table.
  2. The PGD index selects one 64-bit PGD entry.
  3. If that PGD entry is not present, the walk faults.
  4. Otherwise, its physical address bits point to the next table page.
  5. The PUD index selects one 64-bit PUD entry.
  6. If the PUD entry is a leaf with _PAGE_PSE, the translation stops and a 1 GB mapping is formed.
  7. Otherwise, the PUD points to a PMD page.
  8. The PMD index selects one 64-bit PMD entry.
  9. If the PMD entry is a leaf with _PAGE_PSE, the translation stops and a 2 MB mapping is formed.
  10. Otherwise, the PMD points to a PTE page.
  11. The PTE index selects one 64-bit PTE.
  12. If the PTE is present, the physical-frame number is concatenated with the low 12-bit offset.
  13. If the PTE is not present, a page fault occurs.

Linux expresses the same logic in software with:

pgd = pgd_offset(mm, addr);
p4d = p4d_offset(pgd, addr);
pud = pud_offset(p4d, addr);
pmd = pmd_offset(pud, addr);
pte = pte_offset_map(pmd, addr);

On four-level x86, p4d_offset() is usually a folded or trivial step in software terms, but keeping it visible preserves the architecture-neutral API.

Worked Example 2: x86_64 5-level walk

On a five-level system, one more top slice is inserted:

[56:48] [47:39] [38:30] [29:21] [20:12] [11:0]
  PGD      P4D      PUD      PMD      PTE    offset

Linux reflects this by making:

  • pgdir_shift = 48
  • ptrs_per_p4d = 512

through the runtime variables declared in arch/x86/include/asm/pgtable_64_types.h.

The software walk looks identical to generic code, but on this machine the p4d_t level is real rather than folded. That is why Linux can support LA57 without rewriting large portions of the core MM subsystem.

Worked Example 3: ARM64 4 KB, 48-bit walk

A common ARM64 server configuration uses 4 KB pages and a 48-bit virtual address space. In that case the levels are usually described as L0 → L1 → L2 → L3. Linux maps those levels onto:

  • pgd_t
  • p4d_t
  • pud_t
  • pmd_t
  • pte_t

with one of the software names folding away depending on CONFIG_PGTABLE_LEVELS.

The last-level descriptor carries:

  • PTE_VALID
  • PTE_USER
  • PTE_RDONLY
  • PTE_SHARED
  • PTE_AF
  • PTE_NG
  • PTE_PXN
  • PTE_UXN
  • PTE_ATTRINDX()
  • physical address bits

If the address is in the TTBR0 half, Linux expects it to be associated with a user mm and an ASID. If it is in the TTBR1 half, Linux expects it to use the shared kernel tables rooted at swapper_pg_dir.

Worked Example 4: ARM64 TLB invalidation of one user page

When Linux invalidates a single user page on ARM64, the sequence is conceptually:

  1. update the PTE
  2. ensure the store reaches visibility for the table walker
  3. create a TLBI operand that combines VA and ASID
  4. issue tlbi vale1is
  5. optionally issue the user-ASID form as well
  6. execute dsb ish
  7. continue once the invalidation has completed

The inline helper in arch/arm64/include/asm/tlbflush.h embodies exactly that flow. The point is not the specific mnemonic. The point is that Linux always couples page-table writes with the barrier protocol required by the architecture.

Worked Example 5: two-stage translation inside a guest

Now consider the same 4-level walk performed by a process running inside a virtual machine. Every address the guest page tables produce is a Guest Physical Address (GPA on x86_64, IPA on ARM64), which is not a real physical address — it must itself be translated by the hypervisor's Stage-2 (EPT/NPT or ARM64 Stage-2) tables before memory can be touched.

The crucial consequence is that Stage 2 is applied not only to the final data address but to every intermediate guest-table pointer the walker dereferences. Walking the guest's four levels therefore expands like this:

guest CR3/TTBR (a GPA)          → Stage-2 walk (4 accesses) → HPA of guest PGD
  guest PGD entry → guest PUD base (a GPA) → Stage-2 walk (4) → HPA of guest PUD
  guest PUD entry → guest PMD base (a GPA) → Stage-2 walk (4) → HPA of guest PMD
  guest PMD entry → guest PTE base (a GPA) → Stage-2 walk (4) → HPA of guest PTE
  guest PTE entry → data page   (a GPA) → Stage-2 walk (4) → final HPA

That is 5 × 4 = 20 memory accesses in the worst case for a single guest translation, versus 4 for the non-virtualized walk in Worked Example 1. Two design elements keep this affordable in practice:

  • The TLB caches the fully-composed GVA→HPA translation, so the 20-access walk happens only on a miss. VPID (x86_64) and VMID (ARM64) tags let those entries survive VM switches without a flush (see Table 2-27).
  • Large Stage-2 mappings (2 MB / 1 GB blocks) shorten each nested walk, which is why KVM installs the largest Stage-2 mapping the host backing and alignment allow.

The mapping is populated lazily: the first guest access to an unmapped GPA causes a Stage-2 fault (EPT violation or Stage-2 abort), KVM resolves the GPA to a host PFN via __kvm_faultin_pfn(), and installs the leaf Stage-2 entry — after which the re-executed guest instruction completes. Should the host later reclaim or migrate that backing page, an mmu_notifier callback zaps the Stage-2 entry so the stale mapping can never be used.

Reference Table 2-40. Key x86_64 segment selectors in Linux 7.0.12
Selector macro Numeric form Meaning Source
__KERNEL32_CS GDT_ENTRY_KERNEL32_CS * 8 32-bit kernel compat code selector arch/x86/include/asm/segment.h
__KERNEL_CS GDT_ENTRY_KERNEL_CS * 8 64-bit kernel code selector arch/x86/include/asm/segment.h
__KERNEL_DS GDT_ENTRY_KERNEL_DS * 8 kernel data/stack selector arch/x86/include/asm/segment.h
__USER32_CS GDT_ENTRY_DEFAULT_USER32_CS * 8 + 3 32-bit user compat selector arch/x86/include/asm/segment.h

__USER_CS GDT_ENTRY_DEFAULT_USER_CS * 8 + 3 64-bit user code selector arch/x86/include/asm/segment.h

Practical consequences:

  • selectors are multiples of 8 because descriptors are 8-byte aligned in the GDT view
  • user selectors add RPL 3 through the low two bits
  • kernel selectors leave the low two bits clear
  • the selector value itself is not a base address
  • the corresponding descriptor carries type, DPL, presence, long-mode, and base/limit fields

  • _PAGE_SOFT_DIRTY
  • _PAGE_UFFD_WP
  • _PAGE_SAVED_DIRTY
  • _PAGE_SPECIAL
  • _PAGE_KERNEL_4K

That is why reading a raw PTE dump requires more than just the Intel manuals. One must also know Linux's private encoding choices.

Chapter Checkpoint: questions to ask when reading MM code

When reading a line of memory-management code in Linux 7.0.12, these questions usually reveal what the line is really about.

  1. Is the code reasoning about a logical, virtual, or physical address?
  2. On x86_64, does segmentation still matter here, or is this purely paging?
  3. Is the current address in user space, direct map, vmalloc, vmemmap, module space, or fixmap?
  4. Is a page-table level real on this architecture, or folded?
  5. Does this path care about huge pages or only base pages?
  6. Are the entry bits hardware-defined, Linux-defined, or both?
  7. Is the mapping global, user, kernel, or non-global?
  8. Will the change require TLB invalidation?
  9. If yes, is it local or remote?
  10. Is batching through mmu_gather in effect?
  11. Does instruction fetch care, or only data access?
  12. Are memory-type bits relevant, for example in ioremap or device mappings?
  13. Is a per-CPU or TLS base hiding behind GS or FS on x86?
  14. Is the code looking at a struct page, a folio, or a ptdesc?
  15. Is NUMA placement or zone restriction part of the decision?

Mini-glossary

Access flag A hardware-visible bit that records whether a translation has been used. On ARM64 this is PTE_AF. On x86 the analogous accessed/reference information is _PAGE_ACCESSED.

ASID Address-space identifier. On ARM64 the ASID is carried in TTBR state. Linux also uses the word internally for x86's PCID-related logic in arch/x86/mm/tlb.c.

Canonical address An address whose unused top bits are copies of the top implemented bit. Required on x86_64 for 48-bit and 57-bit modes.

CR3 The x86 control register that points to the top page table and optionally carries PCID bits.

Descriptor An x86 segment or system-segment entry. Also used generically for an ARM64 page descriptor. Context determines the meaning.

Direct map A kernel virtual region that linearly maps physical memory. Called the linear map on ARM64 and often page_offset_base or direct map on x86_64.

Fixmap A compile-time fixed virtual-address region whose slots can be bound to physical pages at boot or runtime.

Folio A modern Linux memory-management abstraction representing a physically contiguous set of bytes, often more useful than reasoning only in base pages.

FS base The effective base used for %fs references on x86_64. Commonly used for user-space TLS.

GS base The effective base used for %gs references on x86_64. Commonly used for kernel per-CPU data.

Huge page A mapping that ends the page walk early and covers a larger region than a base page. Examples are 2 MB and 1 GB pages on x86_64.

KPTI Kernel Page Table Isolation. An x86 mitigation against Meltdown that separates user-visible and full kernel page tables.

Linear address Intel's preferred term for the address after segmentation and before paging. Usually synonymous with virtual address in long-mode Linux discussions.

MAIR_EL1 ARM64 Memory Attribute Indirection Register. Maps AttrIndx values in descriptors to cacheability and device-memory semantics.

MMU Memory Management Unit. The hardware that translates addresses and enforces access permissions.

MMU gather Linux batching mechanism for deferred TLB invalidation and page-table freeing during unmap operations.

PCID Process Context IDentifier. The x86 tag that lets TLB entries survive CR3 reloads across address spaces.

PTE Page Table Entry. Also the name of the final software page-table level in Linux's generic model.

PTDESC The page-table descriptor object that overlays struct page for page-table pages in modern Linux.

Sparsemem Linux memory model that tracks memory in sections, allowing sparse physical address spaces and hotplug support.

Swapgs The x86 instruction that swaps GS_BASE with KERNEL_GS_BASE, used heavily on kernel entry and exit.

TLB Translation Lookaside Buffer. The fast cache of recent address translations.

TLBI ARM64 family of TLB invalidation instructions.

TTBR0_EL1 / TTBR1_EL1 ARM64 translation-table base registers for the user and kernel halves of the address space.

VMEMMAP A virtual region containing the array of struct page metadata for physical memory.

VMALLOC A kernel virtual-memory allocator for virtually contiguous, physically noncontiguous memory.

Quick cross-reference to later chapters

  • Chapter 3 will use CS, GS, per-CPU state, and page-table-root changes while discussing context switches.
  • Chapter 8 will return to struct zone, pg_data_t, buddy allocation, watermarks, ZONE_MOVABLE, and folios.
  • Chapter 9 will use pgd_offset(), pte_t, flush_tlb_range(), and the page-fault path while explaining VMAs, maple trees, and copy-on-write.

Memory-addressing patterns worth recognizing in code review

When reviewing or debugging kernel memory-management code, these patterns recur constantly:

  • a page-table walk followed by pte_present() and pte_write() checks
  • a PTE change followed by some form of flush_tlb_*()
  • a kernel virtual address translated through direct-map arithmetic
  • a fixmap slot used before the general allocator is available
  • an x86 entry path using swapgs
  • an ARM64 context-switch path updating TTBR and ASID fields
  • page-table freeing deferred through mmu_gather
  • huge-page logic checking pmd_leaf() or pud_leaf()
  • memory-type selection through pgprot_*() or PTE_ATTRINDX()
  • struct page to folio or struct page to ptdesc reinterpretations

Recognizing those patterns speeds up reading dramatically. A large amount of MM code looks different on the surface, but reduces to those recurring idioms underneath.

Common debugging scenarios

Scenario 1: a stale user mapping after mprotect()

The first question is whether the PTE actually changed. If it did, ask whether:

  • the architecture-specific mutator helper was used,
  • the relevant flush_tlb_range() or flush_tlb_page() call executed,
  • the target CPUs were in lazy mode,
  • and a race with page-table teardown or huge-page splitting is possible.

On x86, read arch/x86/mm/tlb.c with flush_tlb_mm_range() and flush_tlb_func() in view. On ARM64, read arch/arm64/include/asm/tlbflush.h and inspect the dsb/tlbi/isb sequence.

Scenario 2: a kernel pointer lands outside the direct map

Ask which kernel region the pointer belongs to:

  • direct map
  • vmalloc
  • vmemmap
  • module space
  • fixmap
  • cpu entry area

On x86_64, Documentation/arch/x86/x86_64/mm.rst is usually the fastest map. On ARM64, consult arch/arm64/include/asm/memory.h and arch/arm64/include/asm/pgtable.h. Not every valid kernel pointer has direct-map arithmetic behind it.

Scenario 3: x86 entry code crashes after switching GS

Check:

  • whether the path expected to come from user mode or kernel mode,
  • whether swapgs ran in the right place,
  • whether speculation barriers around conditional swapgs paths matter,
  • and whether the saved cs really indicates ring 3 on entry.

The relevant files are not in the generic MM tree at all. They are arch/x86/entry/entry_64.S, arch/x86/include/asm/processor.h, and the MSR definitions in arch/x86/include/asm/msr-index.h. This is a good reminder that memory addressing and entry code are deeply entangled on x86_64.

Scenario 4: device MMIO behaves incorrectly on ARM64

Suspect the memory type first. Was the region mapped as:

  • MT_DEVICE_nGnRnE
  • MT_DEVICE_nGnRE
  • or ordinary MT_NORMAL memory by mistake?

The answer lives in the combination of:

  • PTE_ATTRINDX()
  • the pgprot template chosen by the mapping path
  • and MAIR_EL1 initialization in arch/arm64/mm/proc.S

Cache and ordering bugs around MMIO are often memory-type bugs in disguise.

Scenario 5: large unmap operations cause excessive CPU time in shootdowns

Look for batching. Does the path use:

  • tlb_gather_mmu()
  • tlb_finish_mmu()
  • deferred page-table free helpers
  • and architecture-specific range invalidation support?

On ARM64, range TLBI support may matter. On x86, the single-page flush ceiling and generation-tracking logic may determine whether the operation collapses into a full flush. The relevant code again lives in arch/x86/mm/tlb.c and arch/arm64/include/asm/tlbflush.h.

Additional comparison matrix

Table 2-48. Design choices revisited
Question x86_64 answer in Linux 7.0.12 ARM64 answer in Linux 7.0.12
Does segmentation relocate ordinary pointers? No, except effectively via FS/GS special bases No segmentation exists
Is user/kernel split part of ordinary hardware translation design? Partly; canonical halves yes, but KPTI still needed on vulnerable systems Yes; TTBR0/TTBR1 split is foundational
Are top-level page-table depths fixed at compile time? Software model fixed at 5, hardware depth may be 4 or 5 at runtime Driven by CONFIG_PGTABLE_LEVELS, page granule, and VA width
Does the kernel store page-table metadata separately from struct page? Modern answer is struct ptdesc overlay Same generic ptdesc model
Is struct folio part of the modern story? Yes Yes
Are memory types part of the PTE contract? Yes, through cache bits and PAT Yes, through AttrIndx and MAIR
Is TLB tagging essential to acceptable performance? Yes, PCID is especially important post-KPTI Yes, ASIDs are part of normal context-switch design
Are remote shootdowns a major scalability concern? Yes Yes

Additional reference bullets for Chapter 2 review

If a reader wants to verify specific claims in this chapter against the Linux 7.0.12 tree, these are good anchor points.

  • Verify x86 GDT entry numbers in arch/x86/include/asm/segment.h.
  • Verify flat descriptor initialization in arch/x86/kernel/cpu/common.c.
  • Verify selector and descriptor bit layouts in arch/x86/include/asm/desc_defs.h.
  • Verify MSR numbers for FS/GS/LSTAR in arch/x86/include/asm/msr-index.h.
  • Verify x86 page-bit numbers in arch/x86/include/asm/pgtable_types.h.
  • Verify runtime 5-level paging support in arch/x86/include/asm/pgtable_64_types.h.
  • Verify x86 PCID state and dynamic ASID count in arch/x86/include/asm/tlbflush.h and arch/x86/mm/tlb.c.
  • Verify x86 KPTI logic in arch/x86/mm/pti.c.
  • Verify ARM64 level macros in arch/arm64/include/asm/pgtable-hwdef.h.
  • Verify ARM64 page-type wrappers in arch/arm64/include/asm/pgtable-types.h.
  • Verify ARM64 PTE permission and software bits in arch/arm64/include/asm/pgtable-prot.h and arch/arm64/include/asm/pgtable.h.
  • Verify ARM64 MAIR values in arch/arm64/include/asm/sysreg.h and arch/arm64/mm/proc.S.
  • Verify ARM64 ASID handling in arch/arm64/include/asm/mmu.h and arch/arm64/mm/context.c.
  • Verify ARM64 TLBI helpers in arch/arm64/include/asm/tlbflush.h.
  • Verify sparsemem and mem_section semantics in include/linux/mmzone.h and mm/sparse.c.
  • Verify struct page, struct folio, and struct ptdesc in include/linux/mm_types.h.
  • Verify fixmap helpers in include/asm-generic/fixmap.h, plus architecture enums in arch/x86/include/asm/fixmap.h and arch/arm64/include/asm/fixmap.h.
  • Verify generic page-table walk helpers in include/linux/pgtable.h and include/linux/mm.h.

32-bit Linux 2.6 assumptions that no longer hold

Several assumptions that held for 32-bit Linux 2.6 are wrong for x86_64 and ARM64 on 7.0.12.

  • Do not start by asking where high memory begins. On the primary 64-bit architectures, that is no longer the first-order question.
  • Do not expect segmentation to explain ordinary kernel pointers on x86_64. It usually will not.
  • Do not assume page tables are a simple tree of anonymous pages. Modern Linux tracks page-table pages as ptdesc objects with explicit metadata.
  • Do not read struct page in isolation. Folios are part of the mainstream model now.
  • Do not treat TLB flushing as a simple “one instruction after each change” problem. Generations, ASIDs, PCIDs, ranges, batching, and remote CPUs all matter.
  • Do not think of KPTI as an optional curiosity. It changed the x86 MM architecture in durable ways.
  • Do not assume all 64-bit systems share x86's layout or entry conventions. ARM64 deliberately solves some of the same problems with different architectural tools.

Practical mnemonics

These short mnemonics are not part of the kernel source, but they summarize recurring ideas accurately.

  • FS is for threads; GS is for CPUs.
  • CR3 chooses x86 roots; TTBRs choose ARM64 roots.
  • PCID and ASID save hot translations from needless flushing.
  • Fixmap is where the kernel keeps addresses it needs before normal VM is comfortable.
  • VMEMMAP is the metadata mirror of physical memory.
  • Folios group pages; ptdesc describes page tables.
  • KPTI made x86 page-table roots a security topic, not just a performance topic.
  • The Linux page-table API is five levels deep even when the hardware is not.

Compact checklist: identifying a kernel virtual address on x86_64

If an address is:

  • between 0xffff888000000000 and 0xffffc87fffffffff, suspect the direct map
  • between 0xffffc90000000000 and 0xffffe8ffffffffff, suspect vmalloc or ioremap
  • between 0xffffea0000000000 and 0xffffeaffffffffff, suspect vmemmap
  • near 0xffffffff80000000, suspect core kernel text/data
  • near 0xffffffffa0000000, suspect modules
  • near the very top of the address space, suspect fixmap, vsyscall, or other special regions

That heuristic comes directly from Documentation/arch/x86/x86_64/mm.rst.

Compact checklist: identifying a kernel virtual address on ARM64

If an address is:

  • in the TTBR0 half, it is generally user space
  • in the TTBR1 half beginning at PAGE_OFFSET, it is kernel space
  • near MODULES_VADDR, suspect modules or nearby vmalloc start
  • near VMEMMAP_START, suspect struct page metadata
  • near PCI_IO_START, suspect PCI I/O aperture
  • descending from FIXADDR_TOP, suspect fixmap or trampolines

The actual numeric values depend on VA_BITS and page size, so the formula macros in arch/arm64/include/asm/memory.h are the best source of truth.

One last source-oriented observation

Modern Linux memory addressing is no longer cleanly separable into “hardware chapter” and “software chapter.” The source tree itself reflects this blend. One may begin in:

  • arch/x86/include/asm/pgtable_types.h

and end up needing:

  • arch/x86/mm/tlb.c
  • arch/x86/mm/pti.c
  • include/linux/mm_types.h
  • include/asm-generic/tlb.h

or begin in:

  • arch/arm64/include/asm/pgtable-hwdef.h

and end up needing:

  • arch/arm64/include/asm/tlbflush.h
  • arch/arm64/mm/context.c
  • arch/arm64/mm/proc.S
  • include/linux/mmzone.h

That is not accidental. It reflects the reality that address translation, metadata, security, and performance have become one intertwined subsystem.

Summary

Memory addressing in Linux 7.0.12 is both simpler and more sophisticated than it was in Linux 2.6. Simpler, because the mainstream 64-bit architectures no longer center the design around 32-bit segmentation tricks and highmem workarounds. More sophisticated, because page-table structure, metadata, TLB tagging, isolation, hotplug support, and security mitigations have all become richer. The chapter's unifying idea is that the kernel now treats address translation as a multi-layer system: hardware page walks, software page-table wrappers, metadata overlays, and invalidation algorithms all matter at once.

The main points to carry forward are these:

  • Linux still uses the classic distinction between logical, virtual, and physical addresses, but on x86_64 logical and virtual addresses are usually the same because long mode uses a flat segment model.
  • Segmentation survives on x86_64 mostly for privilege, compatibility, TLS, and per-CPU addressing. ARM64 has no segmentation at all.
  • Paging is now the central architectural mechanism on both x86_64 and ARM64. x86_64 uses four or five levels; ARM64 uses a level count determined by granule size and VA width.
  • Linux presents a uniform five-level software page-table model using folded levels when the hardware is shallower.
  • Page-table entries are hybrid objects combining hardware-defined permission and translation bits with Linux-defined software state.
  • The physical memory model is built around SPARSEMEM, VMEMMAP, struct page, struct folio, and struct ptdesc.
  • Kernel virtual memory layouts are architecture-specific but now contain familiar modern regions such as the direct map, vmalloc, vmemmap, modules, and fixmap.
  • KPTI changed x86_64 MM design profoundly after Meltdown, while ARM64's TTBR split started from a more naturally isolated architecture.
  • TLB maintenance is a major subsystem in its own right, with PCID/ASID tagging, lazy modes, generation tracking, range invalidation, and batching through mmu_gather.
  • Under virtualization the hardware adds a second translation stage (Intel EPT / AMD NPT, ARM64 Stage-2), and KVM keeps those tables coherent with host memory management through MMU notifiers — so guest pages remain first-class participants in reclaim and migration.
  • The address space itself has become a security surface: LAM tags pointers before the walk, LASS gates the user/kernel boundary, and CET shadow stacks and IBT add control-flow integrity that reaches down into PTE encoding.
  • Fault-path scalability now rests on finer-grained locking — the maple-tree VMA index, per-VMA locks that bypass mmap_lock, and split per-page-table-page spinlocks.
Table 2-49. What changed since Linux 2.6?
Topic Linux 2.6-era emphasis Linux 7.0.12 emphasis
Primary architecture i386 x86_64 and ARM64
Segmentation central on x86 mostly vestigial on x86_64; none on ARM64
Paging depth 2-level, PAE, early 64-bit variants standard 4-level, optional 5-level, portable 5-level software model
Page metadata struct page centric struct page + folios + struct ptdesc
Physical memory model highmem/lowmem still central SPARSEMEM_VMEMMAP, NUMA, MOVABLE, DEVICE memory
Kernel isolation permissions assumed enough on x86 KPTI required on vulnerable x86
TLB management simpler flush model PCID/ASID, generation tracking, range TLBI, lazy mode, batching
Virtualization shadow page tables, heavy VM-exit overhead hardware two-stage (EPT/NPT, Stage-2), VPID/VMID tags, MMU notifiers
Address-space security page permissions + NX KPTI, SMEP/SMAP, LAM, LASS, CET shadow stacks, IBT
Fault-path locking single mmap_sem + rb-tree of VMAs maple-tree VMAs, per-VMA locks, split page-table locks
Architecture comparison mostly x86-only explicit x86_64 vs ARM64 contrasts
User/kernel split on ARM64 no arm64 port (merged in 3.7) TTBR0/TTBR1 split is foundational

In the next chapter, we will shift focus from addresses to the objects that own them: processes. There we will see how task state, context switching, and kernel stacks interact with the memory-addressing machinery described here. The abstractions introduced in this chapter—page tables, per-CPU state, address-space tags, and direct versus indirect mappings—will recur constantly in Chapters 3, 8, and 9.

The kernel source files this chapter works from are collected by topic in Source Trails.
